# The Light on Hatha Yoga
<note>original: Haṭhayogapradīpikā; a foundational 15th-century manual on the physical and spiritual practices of Hatha Yoga.</note>

# Light on Hatha Yoga
<note>original: "Haṭhayogapradīpikā." Literally, "A Small Lamp on the Yoga of Force." This is the seminal manual on Hatha Yoga, detailing physical postures, breath control, and the awakening of spiritual energy.</note>

->And it is<-

->**Composed by Svatmarama, King of Yogis,**<-
->**the wish-fulfilling jewel of the lineage of Sahajananda—**<-
<note>original: "Sahajānanda-santāna-cintāmaṇi-svātmārāma-yogīndra." The author, Svatmarama (c. 15th century), identifies himself as part of a spiritual lineage (sampradaya) descending from Sahajananda.</note>

->Adorned with the commentary named "The Moonlight," composed by Brahmananda,<-
<note>original: "Jyotsnā." This is a famous 19th-century Sanskrit commentary that clarifies the technical yogic instructions of the main text.</note>
->and accompanied by a vernacular commentary called "The Heart’s Desire,"<-
<note>original: "Manobhilāṣiṇī bhāṣāvyākhyā." A "Bhasha" commentary typically refers to a translation or explanation in a local language (likely Marathi or Hindi in this Mumbai publication) rather than Sanskrit.</note>
->written by the late Shridhara, born of the Dadhicha family.<-

---

->In the City of Mumbai<-
->At the printing press known as the "Ocean of Awakening"<-
<note>original: "Prabodha-ratnākara." A common metaphorical name for a publishing house, suggesting it is a source of jewels of knowledge.</note>

->In the year 1817 of the Shalivahana Era<-
<note>original: "muni 7 vasudhā 1 purāṇa 18... Śālīvāhana-śake 1817." This corresponds to 1895 CE. The text uses a traditional system where words represent numbers: Munis (sages) are 7, Vasudha (earth) is 1, and Puranas (ancient texts) are 18.</note>
->By the lawful wife of Shridhara, son of the late Jatashankara,<-

## Named Tarabai

->Printed and published.<-
->Third Edition.<-
->Price: Two Rupees (2).<-

<summary>The title page of an 1895 edition of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, featuring commentaries by Brahmananda and Shridhara. It was published in Mumbai by Tarabai, the widow of the translator Shridhara.</summary>

## In Mumbai

At the "Yaduvanshi Library" opposite Anantvadi Lane in Bhuleshwar Chakla. In the shop of Pandit Jyestharam Mukundji and Hariprasad Bhagirathji near Ramvadi. It will [also] be available on Kalika Devi Road at the shop of Ramdas Kashidas Modi’s company.
<note>The areas mentioned—Bhuleshwar and Ramvadi—were (and remain) major commercial and cultural hubs in South Mumbai. During the 19th century, these neighborhoods were the center of the city's indigenous printing and publishing industry.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the commercial publication list of Shri Gathulalji to a formal preface of a seminal yoga text, the Hatha Pradipika. It establishes the lineage of the teaching from divine figures to the author Swatmarama.</meta>

# Preface.

In the subject of Yoga, the *Hatha Pradipika* is extremely beautiful. The great yogi Swatmarama, for the easy understanding of the people of this time, has described the occasion where Lord Shiva instructed Parvati in the science of Hatha; this episode is described in the *Mahakala Yoga Shastra*. And even the supreme Great Brahma has practiced this science of Hatha. Regarding this, the *Yogi Yajnavalkya Smriti* states: "Hiranyagarbha <note>original: "हिरण्यगर्भो योगस्य वक्ता नान्यः पुरातनः" — 'The Golden Womb' or Creator Brahma is the speaker of Yoga; there is no other ancient authority.'</note> is the speaker of Yoga; there is no other ancient authority." And Shri Krishna taught Yoga to Arjuna in the *Gita*, and in the *Shrimad Bhagavatam* he taught it to Uddhava. And Shiva is famous as the Lord of Yogis. Thus, this supreme science was practiced by Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. Therefore, this science was practiced by Narada and others, Shri Shuka and others, and Yajnavalkya and others, who are the foremost among the wise. And Matsyendranath heard the Yoga from Lord Shiva. Matsyendranath and Gorakhnath both became knowers of the science of Hatha; and by the grace of Gorakhnath, the great yogi Swatmarama obtained the science of Hatha. This Swatmarama composed the Yoga text named *Hatha Pradipika* for the benefit of seekers of liberation <term>mumukshu: one who intensely desires spiritual liberation</term>. In which there are 4 (four) chapters <note>original: "upadesha"</note>. Among them, in the first chapter, the section on postures <term>asana</term> along with restraints and observances <term>yama and niyama</term> is told. Posture is the first limb of Hatha Yoga; therefore, postures are taught first. And these postures provide stability by destroying the "active quality" <term>rajo-dharma: the quality of passion, movement, and restlessness</term> which manifests as fickleness of the body and mind; for this reason, postures are taught first.

In the second chapter, the section on breath control <term>pranayama</term> is told. And by performing breath control, the purification of impurities <term>mala-shuddhi</term> occurs. And when the impurities are purified, the perfection of Hatha <term>hatha-siddhi</term> is attained. And through breath control, the vital air <term>vayu</term> becomes steady. And by the steadiness of the vital air, the mind <term>chitta</term> becomes steady. And by the steadiness of both the vital air and the mind, the yogi becomes long-lived and even attains godlike powers <note>original: "ishata"</note>. And from the steadiness of the mind, all perfections <term>siddhi</term> are attained; therefore, the rules for breath control were stated.

And in the third chapter, the ten seals <term>mudras</term> such as the *Maha-mudra* are told. One should remain devoted to the words of the Gurus who teach the seals. And one should perform postures, breath-retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>, and the like, and remain diligent regarding food, recreation, and activities. And if one remains careful in the practice and repeated repetition of the *Maha-mudra* and others, then along with perfections such as the power to become tiny <term>anima-siddhi</term>, one escapes death.

And in the fourth chapter, the sequence of Samadhi in the form of withdrawal of the senses <term>pratyahara</term> and the like is told. What is that sequence of Samadhi like? It is very excellent, and consists of millions of types of Samadhi accomplished by Lord Adinatha <note>The First Lord, Shiva</note>...

<summary>This preface introduces the Hatha Pradipika, tracing its lineage from deities like Shiva and Brahma down to the author Swatmarama, and summarizes the contents of its four chapters.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Swatmarama, Shiva, Matsyendranath, Gorakhnath, Asana, Pranayama, Mudra, Samadhi, spiritual lineage</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of the Hatha Pradipika, moving from the historical lineage of yoga masters to the spiritual goals of the practice and the specific history of this printed edition.</meta>

# Preface.

Among these, the sequence of meditative union <term>samadhi-krama: the orderly progression toward the highest state of consciousness</term> is described as supreme. It is the conqueror of time <note>referring to the overcoming of death</note> and enables a yogi to leave the body at will. Through the dawning of essential knowledge <term>tattva-jnana</term>, it leads to the destruction of worldly cravings and serves as the means to experience the bliss of liberation while still living <term>jivanmukti</term>. By exhausting the fruits of past actions <term>prarabdha-karma</term> and realizing the non-duality of the individual soul and the Supreme Reality <note>original: "जीव और ब्रह्मको अभेदकरकै" — making the Jiva and Brahma one</note>, it brings about liberation in the form of attaining absolute, divine bliss.

This *Hatha Pradipika* is highly suited for those who wish to know the path of Yoga. The Sanskrit commentary written upon it, titled *Jyotsna* <note>meaning "Moonlight"</note> by Brahmananda, is an ancient companion to the *Hatha Pradipika*. We have edited this with great labor and published it for the benefit of the public. The reason is that although the description of Yoga is found in the *Shrimad Bhagavatam* and other such scriptures, and the practice of Yoga is required in all sacred sciences, people generally do not understand Yoga. This is because Yoga scriptures are not easily encountered, and thus the path of Yoga has not flourished. For this reason, so that it may be useful to the people, we have prepared and published the original *Hatha Pradipika* along with the Sanskrit commentary and a vernacular translation <term>bhashantar: a translation into the common language, in this case, Hindi</term>, having purified the text with immense effort. May all noble men show me kindness and accept this work.

---

### Preface to the Third Edition.

By the grace of God, as the two previous editions of this book have been exhausted, noble readers have validated the labor of my late husband. For this, I express my deep gratitude to them. As this popular book has recently become difficult to obtain, many gentlemen urged me to publish it again, insisting that my name also be made immortal alongside my late husband's name through this book. Therefore, I have published and made famous this third edition. I have full hope that just as you distinguished gentlemen encouraged the two previously published editions and made us feel fulfilled, you will likewise encourage this edition and make me feel fulfilled.

The effort to print this third edition was realized through the assistance of Sheth Ishwardas Tribhuvandas Sattawale and Pandit Madhavji Ramprasad. Furthermore, Sheth Govardhandas Lakshmidas of the "Ancient Text Publications" took the trouble of checking the printing proofs <note>original: "फारम" — printer's forms</note> and other details. For this, I am very grateful to them.

->Seeking your kind favor,<-
->The wife of the late Pandit Shridhar Jatashankar,<-
->**Tarabai.**<-

<summary>This page concludes the general preface by explaining the spiritual benefits of Samadhi and the necessity of this edition, followed by a personal note from Tarabai, the widow of the editor, regarding the publication of the third edition.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Hatha Pradipika, Jyotsna commentary, Jivanmukti, Brahmananda, Tarabai, Shridhar Jatashankar, yoga publication history</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual text of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, transitioning from the Table of Contents found on the previous pages to the introductory verse and the start of two commentaries: the Sanskrit "Jyotsna" (Moonlight) and a Braj Bhasha vernacular translation.</meta>

->॥ Glory to the Auspicious One ॥<-

# ॥ The Light on Hatha Yoga ॥
## ॥ Accompanied by Commentary and Vernacular Translation ॥

---

### First Instruction

**Root Text: Salutation to that First Lord <note>original: "Adinatha," a title for Lord Shiva as the first teacher of yoga</note>, who taught the science of Hatha Yoga; it shines like a ladder for those who desire to climb to the heights of Raja Yoga <term>Raja Yoga: the "Royal Path" of meditation and spiritual absorption, which Hatha Yoga is intended to prepare the body for</term>. ॥ 1 ॥**

### ॥ Commentary ॥

Salutations to Lord Ganesha <note>The elephant-headed deity invoked at the start of Hindu texts to remove obstacles</note>. Having bowed to the Guru and to Shiva himself, Brahmananda <note>The author of this specific Sanskrit commentary</note> spreads this *Moonlight* (*Jyotsna*) on the *Hatha Pradipika*, which illuminates the path of Yoga. ॥ 1 ॥ Now, for the easy understanding of people of the present time, having well understood the essence of the teachings of Goraksha <note>Gorakshanath, a semi-legendary 10th-11th century yogi and founding figure of the Nath tradition</note>, and at the urging of the chief scholar Meru Shastri, I shall clearly explain the most hidden meanings. ॥ 2 ॥ Wishing to compose this *Hatha Pradipika*, which leads to the fruit of absolute liberation through Raja Yoga for the benefit of seekers of liberation, the most compassionate master of yogis, Svatmarama, performs an auspicious salutation to the illustrious First Lord, the originator of Hatha Yoga, to remove any obstacles to his work. This is done with the words "To the First Lord..." and so on.

The phrasing "To that First Lord, let there be salutation" is the grammatical connection. "First" and "Lord" combined means the First Lord, the Supreme Sovereign, Shiva. The word "Shri" (Illustrious) is prefixed to his name. It could also refer to the Illustrious Lord Vishnu. As for the lack of a specific vowel-joining rule <term>sandhi: the phonetic blending of words in Sanskrit grammar</term> in the phrase "Shri-Adinathaya," this is understood to be following the tradition of experts in poetic meter, who say: "One may even turn a bean into a different bean rather than break the meter of the speech," choosing melodic flow over strict grammar. In fact, if one accepts the reading "Shri-Adinathaya" without merging the vowels, it avoids the application of certain eternal rules...

### ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥

Salutations to Lord Ganesha. Salutations to the lotus feet of the Holy Guru. For the benefit of those desiring liberation, the most compassionate master of yogis, Svatmarama, wishing to compose this *Hatha Pradipika*—in which the fruit of liberation is found through Raja Yoga—performs this auspicious opening by offering salutations to the illustrious First Lord Shiva, the promoter of Hatha Yoga. "To the First Lord": Let there be salutation to the First Lord who is Shiva. Or, let there be salutation to the Lord Vishnu, before whom the word "Shri" is placed. This Shiva taught the science of Hatha Yoga to the Goddess Parvati. In this science, "Ha" is said to be the Sun and "Tha" is said to be... <note>The text cuts off here, but usually continues to explain that "Tha" represents the Moon, symbolizing the balancing of solar and lunar energies in the body</note>

<summary>This opening page introduces the Hatha Yoga Pradipika with a dedication to the First Lord (Shiva), framing Hatha Yoga as a foundational "ladder" to reach the higher spiritual state of Raja Yoga. It includes a Sanskrit commentary by Brahmananda and a vernacular explanation to make the text accessible to a wider audience.</summary>
<keywords>Adinatha, Shiva, Hatha Yoga, Raja Yoga, Svatmarama, Brahmananda, Jyotsna commentary, Sanskrit, Braj Bhasha, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the opening invocation and introduces the second verse of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It provides the famous etymological definition of "Hatha" and establishes the relationship between Hatha Yoga as a physical practice and Raja Yoga as the ultimate spiritual goal.</meta>

<page-num>(2)</page-num>
->**In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika**<-

## **Root Text: Having saluted the glorious Guru and Lord, the yogi Svatmarama teaches the science of Hatha solely for the sake of Raja Yoga. ॥ 2 ॥**

### ॥ Commentary ॥

<note>This section begins with a technical grammatical discussion regarding the use of similes and the avoidance of linguistic errors.</note>
Wise scholars should consider this: since the characteristics of the subject are clearly defined, its perfection is possible. Even if literary theorists point out certain flaws in the structure of the predicates, these are sometimes accepted by experts, and the great masters of grammar have accepted these forms for the sake of extreme brevity.

**"Salutation"** <note>original: "namah"</note> means a bowing down or a state of humility. Here, it is expressed as a prayer. To the question "To whom is this salutation directed?", the text answers: **"By whom..."** <note>original: "yena"</note>. 

By that First Lord <note>Adinatha/Shiva</note>, the science of Hatha Yoga was taught to the Daughter of the Mountain <note>the goddess Parvati/Girija</note>. The word **"Hatha"** is composed of **"Ha"** and **"Tha"**, representing the **Sun** and the **Moon**. The union <note>Yoga</note> of these two is Hatha Yoga. By this, the definition of Hatha Yoga is established as the practice of breath control <term>Pranayama: the regulation of the vital life force</term> characterized by the union of the **Prana** <note>the vital energy associated with the sun and inhalation</note> and the **Apana** <note>the energy associated with the moon and exhalation</note>. 

As it has been stated by Gorakshanatha in the *Method of Siddha Doctrine* <note>original: "Siddhasiddhantapaddhati"</note>: 
> **"The letter 'Ha' is said to be the Sun, and the letter 'Tha' is called the Moon. Because of the union of the Sun and Moon, it is called Hatha Yoga."** 

The "science of Hatha Yoga" <note>Hatha-vidya</note> refers to the scripture or teaching that explains this practice. The fact that the First Lord <note>Shiva</note> taught this to Parvati is well-known in scriptures such as the *Yoga Treatise of Mahakala*. 

The text describes this path as **"highly elevated"** <note>original: "pronnata"</note>. Because preliminary practices like Mantra Yoga and Hatha Yoga serve as the "lower ground," Raja Yoga is considered the "higher ground" or the superior stage. **Raja Yoga** is the state of cognitive absorption <term>Asamprajnata Samadhi: the highest state of meditation where the mind is completely still and free from all fluctuations</term>. 

For one who desires this state—a seeker of liberation—this science acts like a **ladder** <note>original: "adhiruhyate anaya," literally "that by which one climbs"</note>. It shines with particular brilliance. Just as a ladder allows someone who wishes to reach the top of a high palace to do so without difficulty, the *Light on Hatha* <note>Hatha-dipika</note> allows someone who wishes to reach the heights of Raja Yoga to do so with ease. This is a poetic comparison <note>upama-alamkara</note>. The verse is written in the *Indravajra* meter. ॥ 1 ॥

Having thus performed the auspicious salutation to the Supreme Guru to remove many obstacles...

### ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥

Hatha Yoga is the name given to the practice of breath control <note>Pranayama</note> that unites the **Prana** and **Apana**, which are symbolized by the **Sun** and the **Moon**. The "Science of Hatha Yoga" is that which explains this practice. This science is the gateway to the highly elevated **Raja Yoga**. 

While there are eighteen forms of yoga, including Mantra Yoga and others which form the "lower ground," Raja Yoga is the "higher ground" or the peak. Raja Yoga refers to the state of meditative absorption <note>Samadhi</note>. It sits above all else. For the seeker of liberation who desires to climb to that state, this Hatha science illuminates the way. Just as a wooden ladder is used by someone wishing to climb to a high place, this *Light on Hatha* <note>Hatha-Pradipika</note> shines to guide the way. ॥ 1 ॥

Now, having performed the auspicious salutation to his own Guru, the author prepares to show the subject matter and purpose of the book...

<summary>This page defines Hatha Yoga as the union of the "Sun" (Ha) and "Moon" (Tha) energies (Prana and Apana) and establishes Hatha as a necessary "ladder" to reach the spiritual heights of Raja Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Raja Yoga, Adinatha, Prana, Apana, Gorakshanatha, Sun and Moon, Samadhi, spiritual ladder</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the second verse of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, establishing the philosophical framework (Anubandha Chatushtaya) of the text, and introduces the third verse, which explains why this manual was written.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>First Instruction (3)</header>

# **Root Text: For those who wander in the darkness of many conflicting opinions, unable to know Raja Yoga, [this lamp is lit]...**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Because of his devotion to his own teacher, the author performs an auspicious salutation and demonstrates the subject matter, purpose, and other essential elements of this book. He says, **"Having saluted"** <note>original: "pranamya"</note>. This means bowing down with intense devotion to the glorious Guru, the Lord, his own master. By whom? By the yogi **Svatmarama** <note>literally "One who rejoices in the Self"</note>. The construction is: the science of Hatha is taught **"solely for the sake of Raja Yoga."** 

The intent of the word **"solely"** <note>original: "kevalam"</note> is that the primary fruit of Hatha Vidya is Raja Yoga <term>Raja Yoga: the "Royal Path" of meditation leading to the highest state of consciousness</term>, not the attainment of supernatural powers <term>Siddhis: extraordinary powers like flying or clairvoyance</term>. Such powers are merely incidental or secondary results.

By this, the **Subject Matter** <note>original: "vishaya"</note> of this book is established as Hatha Yoga combined with the fruit of Raja Yoga. The **Purpose** <note>original: "phalam"</note> is the attainment of absolute liberation <term>Kaivalya: the state of final spiritual independence and enlightenment</term> through the path of Raja Yoga. The **Qualified Student** <note>original: "adhikari"</note> is the one who desires that liberation. 

The **Relationships** <note>original: "sambandha"</note> are defined as follows:
1. Between the book and its subject, the relationship is that of the **"describer and the described."** 
2. Between the book and liberation, the relationship is that of the **"cause and the effect."** 
3. Between the entirety of Yoga taught here and liberation, the relationship is that of the **"means and the goal."** ॥ 2 ॥

One might ask: "If Raja Yoga can be perfected through the yoga of sound <term>Mantra-yoga</term>, meditation on a form <term>Saguna-dhyana</term>, meditation without form <term>Nirguna-dhyana</term>, or physical seals <term>Mudras</term>, then what is the need for teaching the science of Hatha?" 

Anticipating this doubt, the author begins the next verse by stating that for those with distracted or restless minds, Raja Yoga cannot be perfected through Mantra-yoga and the like. Perfection in Raja Yoga is achieved specifically through Hatha Yoga. 

**"Through confusion"** <note>original: "bhrantya"</note>: This refers to the error or delusion caused by the **"darkness"** <note>original: "dhvante"</note> of the many conflicting opinions regarding Mantra-yoga and other paths. Because people seek Raja Yoga through various means but fail to find it there, they remain in the dark. As the author will state later: "Without Raja Yoga, [the practice is in vain]." 

Furthermore, for those who **"do not know"** <note>original: "ajanantam"</note> Raja Yoga—meaning those people who lack this knowledge—the author acts as a **"giver of compassion"** <note>original: "kripakara"</note>, or "a mine of compassion." This indicates that the sole reason for composing the *Hatha Pradipika* is compassion for the ignorant. 

The name **Svatmarama** refers to one who delights in the Self. He provides the **"Hatha Pradipika"** <note>The Light or Lamp of Hatha</note> because it illuminates this practice. Alternatively, Hatha itself is the "Lamp" because it illuminates the path to Raja Yoga. He "holds" or creates this lamp. By using the name Svatmarama, it is implied that he has reached the seventh stage of knowledge <note>The highest state of wisdom in the Yogic tradition, known as Turiyatita</note>.

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Having saluted his glorious Guru and Lord, the yogi Svatmarama speaks the science of Hatha solely for the purpose of Raja Yoga. The subject of this book is Hatha Yoga along with its fruit, Raja Yoga. Its result is liberation through Raja Yoga. He who desires this is the qualified student. The relationship between the book and the subject is that of the "describer and the described." ॥ 2 ॥

If someone asks what the purpose of teaching the science of Hatha is, given that Raja Yoga can be attained through Mantra-yoga, meditation on forms, meditation on the formless, and Mudras—the answer is that Raja Yoga is not perfected by Mantra-yoga and the like [for everyone]. Raja Yoga is perfected only through Hatha Yoga. **"Through confusion"**: in the darkness of the many opinions concerning Mantra-yoga and other methods...

<summary>This page defines the philosophical purpose of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, asserting that Hatha Yoga is a necessary physical foundation for the spiritual heights of Raja Yoga, written out of compassion for those confused by conflicting teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Hatha Yoga, Svatmarama, Kaivalya, Siddhi, Mantra-yoga, spiritual prerequisites, compassion for the ignorant</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the opening of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. Having established the lineage and the purpose of the work in the previous verses, the text now introduces the author, Svatmarama, and defines his spiritual status through the framework of the seven stages of knowledge (Jnana-bhumis).</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

# Svatmarama, full of compassion, holds [forth] the Hatha-Pradipika (The Lamp of Hatha). || 3 ||

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

It has been said that he is the **"best among the knowers of Brahman"** <note>original: "brahmavid-varishtha"; this refers to the highest level of a self-realized soul</note>. And so says the Scripture <note>original: "Shruti"</note>: "He whose play is the Self, whose pleasure is the Self, and who is active, is the best of the knowers of Brahman." The seven stages of knowledge are also described in the *Yoga Vashistha* <note>A famous philosophical text focusing on the teachings of Sage Vashistha to Rama</note>:

> "The first stage is called **Good Desire** (*Shubheccha*); 
> The second is **Inquiry** (*Vicharana*); the third is **Thinning of the Mind** (*Tanumanasa*). 
> The fourth is **Attainment of Purity** (*Sattvapatti*); then follows **Detachment** (*Asansakti*). 
> The sixth is **Absence of Perception** (*Padarthabhavini*); the seventh is known as **Entering the Fourth State** (*Turyaga*)." 

The meaning of this is as follows: 
1. **Good Desire** <note>original: "shubheccha"</note>: This is the first stage of knowledge, described by yogis as the intense desire for liberation accompanied by discrimination, renunciation, and the 'six virtues' such as inner calm. 
2. **Inquiry** <note>original: "vicharana"</note>: This second stage consists of listening to and reflecting upon the sacred teachings. 
3. **Thinning of the Mind** <note>original: "tanumanasa"</note>: When the mind, which usually grasps many different objects, leaves them aside and flows like a continuous stream toward a single reality, this is the third stage, which takes the form of deep meditation <term>Nididhyasana: profound and continuous meditation on the Self</term>. 

These first three are the stages of **Practice** <note>original: "sadhana"</note>. In these stages, the seeker is called a "Practitioner" (*Sadhaka*). 

4. **Attainment of Purity** <note>original: "sattvapatti"</note>: Through these three stages, the internal faculty becomes pure and takes the form of direct perception, realizing "I am the Absolute" (*Aham Brahmasmi*). This fourth stage is the first level of **Fruit** <note>the result of practice</note>. In this stage, the yogi is called a **Knower of Brahman** (*Brahmavid*). This corresponds to the stage of **Cognitive Union** <term>Samprajnata Yoga: absorption where the mind remains aware of an object of focus</term>.

The remaining three stages are the stages of **Supra-cognitive Union** <term>Asamprajnata Yoga: the highest state where the mind is fully absorbed and transcends all objects</term>. 

5. **Detachment** <note>original: "asansakti"</note>: After the attainment of purity, the yogi becomes unattached to the supernatural powers <term>Siddhis: extraordinary abilities like clairvoyance</term> that may appear. This is the fifth stage. In this state, the yogi returns to worldly consciousness on their own. One who has reached this stage is called **Excellent Knower of Brahman** (*Brahmavid-vara*).
6. **Absence of Perception** <note>original: "padarthabhavini"</note>: The sixth stage is that in which the yogi perceives no object other than the Supreme Brahman. In this state, the yogi returns to consciousness only when prompted by others. One who has reached this is called a **Superior Knower of Brahman** (*Brahmavid-variyan*).
7. **Entering the Fourth State** <note>original: "turyaga"</note>: The seventh stage is known as *Turyaga*. In this, the yogi does not return to consciousness, neither by their own will nor by the efforts of others. One who has reached this is called the **Best Knower of Brahman** (*Brahmavid-varishtha*). 

The scriptural evidence for this has already been stated. Previously, it was said that such a person is a **Liberated Soul** <term>Jivanmukta: one who is liberated while still in the body</term>. This is exactly what is meant by the title **Svatmarama** <note>literally "one who rejoices in his own Self"</note>. Enough has been said on this matter. || 3 ||

-> ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥ <-

For those men who do not know the knowledge of **Raja Yoga** because of the error and confusion residing in that darkness [of ignorance], the compassionate Svatmarama composes the *Hatha-Pradipika*, which illuminates the science of Hatha Yoga. 

The meaning of **Svatmarama** is this: one who delights in his own Soul/Self. He has attained the seven stages of knowledge and is the greatest among the knowers of the Absolute. In the *Yoga Vashistha*, the seven stages of knowledge are stated: 
1. The Stage of Knowledge (Good Desire), 2. Inquiry, 3. Thinning of the Mind, 4. Attainment of Purity, 5. The name Detachment, 6. Absence of Perception, 7. Entering the Fourth State. 
The meaning of these seven stages of knowledge—Discrimination, Renunciation—

<summary>This page introduces the author Svatmarama and justifies his authority by classifying him as a 'Brahmavid-varishtha' (the highest level of a liberated being). It provides a detailed breakdown of the seven stages of spiritual knowledge (Jnana-bhumis) according to the Yoga Vashistha.</summary>
<keywords>Svatmarama, Hatha Pradipika, Jnana-bhumi, Yoga Vashistha, Brahmavid, Samprajnata Yoga, Asamprajnata Yoga, Jivanmukta, Seven stages of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed explanation of the Seven Stages of Knowledge (Jnana-bhumis) from the previous page's vernacular commentary, then moves into Verse 4 of the root text. Verse 4 establishes the lineage of Hatha Yoga, connecting the author Svatmarama to the great masters Matsyendra and Goraksha.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>First Lesson. (5)</header>

**Root Text:**
# Indeed, Matsyendra, Goraksha, and others know the Hatha science. By their grace, the yogi Svatmarama also knows it. || 4 ||

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

By praising the Hatha science because it is practiced by the great ones, the author highlights his own dignity, having obtained this science from such great company. Regarding the phrase **"Indeed, the Hatha science"**: the word "indeed" <note>original: "hi"</note> indicates its renown. **"Matsyendra, Goraksha, and others"** refers to those of whom Matsyendra and Goraksha are the first. By the word **"others"** <note>original: "adya"</note>, we should include masters such as Jalandharanatha, Bhartrihari, Gopichandra, and their peers. They **"know"** the Hatha science—meaning the science of Hatha Yoga—specifically through its practices, characteristics, distinctions, and fruits. **Svatmarama** is the name of the author. The word **"or"** <note>original: "athava"</note> here acts as a conjunction. The **"yogi"** (the one possessing yoga) knows it through **"their grace"**—specifically through the grace of Goraksha. This is the grammatical connection.

Even the Supreme Great Being, Brahma, practiced this science. On this point, the *Yogi-Yajnavalkya Smriti* <note>An ancient text on yoga attributed to the sage Yajnavalkya</note> states: "The Golden Womb <note>original: "Hiranyagarbha," a name for the creator god Brahma</note> is the first speaker of Yoga; there is no other ancient one." This "speaking" is preceded by mental activity, and mental activity implies the traditional revelation <note>original: "Agama"</note>. As the Scripture <note>original: "Shruti"</note> says: "What one contemplates with the mind, that one speaks with the voice." This science was also taught by the Lord <note>Vishnu/Krishna</note> to his devotees like Uddhava. Shiva, of course, is famous as a yogi. Thus, this science is practiced by the highest of all: Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva.

One should not doubt that Vyasa, the author of the *Brahma Sutras*, rejected the yogi. He only rejected the parts concerning the absolute independence of Nature <note>original: "Prakriti"</note> held by those who focus on duality; he did not reject Yoga in the form of a specific meditative contemplation <note>original: "Bhavana"</note>.

-> ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥ <-

...accompanied by the "six virtues" such as inner calm and self-control mentioned before. Where there is intense desire for liberation, that is the **First stage** (1). The **Second stage** consists of listening to and reflecting on the teachings (2). The mind is usually something that grasps many different objects; when it abandons those many objects and becomes a steady flow of a single reality, that is the **Third stage** (3).

These three are the "stages of practice." Through these three, when the internal instrument of the mind becomes pure being <note>original: "shuddha-sattva"; a state of clarity and light</note>, the yogi says, "I am Brahman" <note>original: "Aham Brahmasmi"</note>. This is the **Fourth stage**, the **Attainment of Purity** (*Sattvapatti*). This is the "stage of the result." The yogi who has reached this is called a "Knower of Brahman" <note>term: Brahmavid - one who has realized the ultimate reality</term> (4).

Following this, if one is not attached to the supernatural powers <note>term: Siddhis - powers like levitation or clairvoyance</term> that arise near this stage of Purity, it is called the **Fifth stage**, **Detachment** (*Asansakti*). The yogi who reaches this is called a "Better Knower of Brahman" (5).

The **Sixth stage** is called **Absence of Perception** (*Padarthabhavini*), where one does not contemplate anything other than the Supreme Brahman. When a yogi in this stage is woken up by someone else, they rise. This yogi is called an "Excellent Knower of Brahman" (6).

The **Seventh stage** is named **Entering the Fourth State** (*Turyaga*). The yogi who reaches this is called the "Best Knower of Brahman." Such a person was previously described as "Liberated-while-living" <note>term: Jivanmukta - a soul that is spiritually free even while inhabiting a physical body</note>; this is what is meant by the term "Svatmarama" <note>literally "one who delights in the Self"</note> in this verse. || 3 ||

Because it is practiced by the Great Souls <note>original: "Mahatmas"</note>, he praises the Hatha science, showing that he himself is also a great...

<summary>This page lists the lineage of Hatha Yoga (Matsyendra and Goraksha) and justifies the practice of Yoga by noting its acceptance by Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. It also concludes the description of the seven stages of spiritual knowledge, defining the highest stage as being "Svatmarama" or a Jivanmukta.</summary>

<keywords>Matsyendra, Goraksha, Svatmarama, Sattvapatti, Jivanmukta, Jnana-bhumis, Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, Hatha Yoga lineage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Verse 4, emphasizing the spiritual necessity of Yoga as supported by the Bhagavad Gita and the Brahma Sutras. It then transitions into Verse 5, which lists the legendary lineage of the Natha Siddhas, the perfected masters of Hatha Yoga.</meta>

<page-num>६</page-num>
<header>The Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

# Lord Adinatha, Matsyendra, Shavara, Anandabhairava,
# Chaurangi, Meena, Goraksha, Virupaksha, and Bileshaya. || 5 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...because it is widely accepted, and without it, even happiness is impossible. Thus it is said in the *Bhagavad Gita*: "One who is not disciplined <note>original: "ayukta"</note> has no wisdom, nor has he the power of contemplation. For one who does not contemplate, there is no peace; and for one without peace, how can there be happiness?" <note>Bhagavad Gita 2.66</note>. 

Narayana Tirtha has also said: "In his own *Brahma Sutras*, the sage Vyasa did not reject the practice known as Yoga, though he used his verses to refute the ideas of independence and absolute reality in Primordial Nature <note>The Prakriti of the Samkhya school</note>, or the absolute reality of the distinction between consciousness and the world. Furthermore, the wise Vyasa himself explained the Yoga that grants self-realization in his commentaries and other works. Therefore, Yoga is highly regarded by the foremost teachers. The Lord Himself favored Yoga above other paths in the *Gita*. Sages like Shuka and others practiced it; therefore, the virtuous show great respect for it." 

Also, the Lord's own words state: "Whatever meritorious fruit is promised in the Vedas, in sacrifices, in austerities, and in gift-giving—the Yogi transcends all these by knowing this truth, and attains the supreme, original station." <note>Bhagavad Gita 8.28</note>. Why say more? By saying, "Even one who merely desires to know Yoga <note>original: "jijnasu"</note> transcends the Word-Brahman <note>The ritualistic portion of the Vedas</note>," the Lord describes the excellence of even a seeker of Yoga; what then need be said of a perfected Yogi? Since this science has been practiced by the greatest devotees like Narada and the chief among the wise like Yajnavalkya, it is concluded that it is not contradictory to the paths of devotion or knowledge. || 4 ||

To inspire people to engage in Hatha Yoga, the author mentions the Siddhas <note>Perfected masters</note> who attained supernatural powers <note>original: "aishvarya"</note> through the science of Hatha. **"Lord Adinatha"** and so on. **Adinatha** is Lord Shiva, the first Lord among all the Natha masters. From him, the Natha tradition <note>original: "Sampradaya"</note> began—so say the followers of the Natha lineage. The master named **Matsyendra** was the disciple of Adinatha. 

Regarding this, there is a legend: Once, Adinatha was on a certain island. Thinking it was a secluded place, he taught the science of Yoga to Girija <note>The goddess Parvati</note>. A certain fish <note>original: "matsya"</note> staying in the water near the shore heard that instruction on Yoga and remained still with a one-pointed mind. Seeing the fish in such a state, Adinatha realized that it had heard the Yoga teachings. Out of compassion, the Primal Lord...

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

The author describes the greatness achieved through the profit of Hatha science. Regarding **"The Hatha science indeed"**: Matsyendra and Goraksha are the foremost among those such as Jalandharanatha, Bhartrihari, and Gopichandra, who know the Hatha science particularly through its practices, characteristics, distinctions, and fruits. "I, Svatmarama, being a possessor of Yoga, know this by the grace of Goraksha." This science is very great; even Lord Brahma practices it, and the Supreme Lord <note>Vishnu/Krishna</note> taught it to Uddhava and others. Lord Shiva himself is famously known as a Yogi. In this way, this science is practiced by the highest—Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva. || 4 ||

To create an inclination toward Hatha Yoga, the author names the Siddhas who have attained supernatural powers through the Hatha science. **"Lord Adinatha"** and so on. Lord Shiva, known as **Adinatha**, is the first among all the Natha masters...

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of the great masters (Siddhas) of the Natha lineage, following the names mentioned in Verse 5. It provides both a Sanskrit commentary and a vernacular explanation of the miraculous origins of Matsyendranatha and Chaurangi.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>The First Instruction</header>

**Manthana, Bhairava, Yogi, Siddhi, Buddha, Kanthadi, Korantaka, Surananda, Siddhapada, and Charpati. || 6 ||**

**Kaneri, Pujyapada, Nityanatha, Niranjana, Kapali, Bindunatha, and he known as Kakachandishvara. || 7 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The Lord <note>Adinatha, or Shiva</note> sprinkled him with water. By the mere act of sprinkling, he became the perfected master Matsyendra, endowed with a divine body. People call him Matsyendranatha. One Siddha is named Shavara; another is named Anandabhairava. These names are grouped together here. In the language of Hindusthan <note>Northern India</note>, a man with severed hands and feet is called "Chaurangi." Once, while Matsyendranatha—who had obtained Yoga from Adinatha—was wandering the earth, he saw Chaurangi in a forest. By the mere grace of Matsyendra's glance, Chaurangi’s hands and feet sprouted anew. Realizing, "I have gained my limbs by his grace," Chaurangi bowed at his feet and prayed, "Please show me favor." Matsyendra favored him, and by that grace, he became the famous Siddha known as Chaurangi. "Meena" refers to Meenanatha; "Goraksha" is Gorakshanatha. There are also those named Virupaksha and Bileshaya. The verse forms a compound of these names starting with Chaurangi. || 5 ||

Regarding "Manthana": The title "Yogi" serves as an adjective for all these masters starting with Manthana, Bhairava, and so on. || 6 ||

Regarding "Kaneri": He who is called by the name <note>original: "ahvaya"</note> Kakachandishvara. The other names are self-explanatory. || 7 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

The Natha tradition <term>sampradaya: A lineage of teachers and disciples</term> began with these masters. Matsyendra is the disciple of Adinatha <note>Lord Shiva</note>. At one time, while Lord Mahadeva <note>Shiva</note> was staying on an island, he was teaching the science of Yoga to Goddess Parvati. Nearby, in the water at the shore, a certain fish <term>matsya</term> heard the instruction on Yoga. With a one-pointed mind, the fish became perfectly still. Seeing this, Shiva realized that the fish had heard the Yoga teaching. Out of compassion, the Lord sprinkled the fish with water, and by that sprinkling, the fish attained a divine body and became the Siddha Matsyendra. He is called Matsyendranatha. 

Shavaranatha, Anandabhairavanatha, and Chaurangi also obtained Yoga from Adinatha. While they were wandering the earth, they came across a forest where a man sat with his hands and feet cut off. By their graceful glance, his hands and feet were restored. Thinking in his mind, "My limbs have returned through their grace," he bowed at their feet and prayed, "Please be merciful to me." Then Matsyendra showed him favor, and through that grace, he became famous by the name Chaurangi. Other masters include Meenanatha, Gorakshanatha, Virupaksha, and Bileshaya. || 5 ||

Regarding "Manthana": Manthana, Bhairava, Yogi, Siddhi, Buddha, Kanthadi, Korantaka, Surananda, Siddhapada, and Charpati. || 6 ||

Regarding "Kaneri": Kaneri, Pujyapada, Nityanatha, Niranjana, Kapali, Bindunatha, and Kakachandishvara. || 7 ||

<summary>This page lists the remaining names in the lineage of the thirty-three Mahasiddhas and recounts the legendary origins of Matsyendranatha (born from a fish) and Chaurangi (healed of mutilation).</summary>
<keywords>Siddhas, Matsyendranatha, Chaurangi, Natha lineage, Adinatha, Hatha Yoga masters, miraculous healing, Shiva and Parvati</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Great Masters (Mahasiddhas) of the Natha lineage. It concludes the list of names and explains the existential benefits of Hatha Yoga as both a refuge from suffering and the foundational support for all other spiritual practices.</meta>

<page-num>( 8 )</page-num>
<header>The Lamp on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Allama, Prabhudeva, Ghoda, Choli, Tintini, Bhanuki, Naradeva, Khanda, and Kapalika. || 8 ||**

**These and other Great Masters <term>Mahasiddhas: Perfected beings who have attained physical and spiritual immortality</term>, through the power of Hatha Yoga, have broken the rod of Death <note>original: "kala-dandam," literally the staff or punishment of Time/Death</note> and now roam throughout the entire universe. || 9 ||**

**Hatha Yoga is a sheltering monastery for those scorched by every kind of suffering. For those engaged in any form of Yoga, Hatha is the supporting tortoise. || 10 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding "Allama": The word "and" <note>original: "tatha"</note> is used here as a conjunction to connect the names. || 8 ||

Regarding "These and others": This refers to the masters previously mentioned and those who follow. The word "others" <note>original: "adi"</note> includes masters such as Taranatha and his peers. They are called "Great Masters" <note>original: "Mahasiddhas"</note> because they possess unobstructed divine power. "Through the power of Hatha Yoga" means by its inherent strength. "Time" <note>original: "kala"</note> refers to death; its "rod" or "punishment" <note>original: "danda"</note> is the process of separating the life force from the body. Having "broken" or severed this rod, they have conquered death. They "roam the universe" <note>original: "brahmandam vicharanti"</note>, meaning they move with total freedom and without obstruction. As stated in the *Bhagavata Purana*: "The path of the lords of Yoga—who are the inner soul of the winds—is said to be both within and without the three worlds." || 9 ||

The author uses the metaphors of a "monastery" <note>original: "matha"</note> and a "tortoise" <note>original: "kamatha"</note> to describe how Hatha Yoga destroys all suffering and facilitates all other Yogas. 

Regarding "scorched by every kind": There are three types of suffering:
1. **Internal** <term>Adhyatmika: Suffering arising from one's own body or mind</term>. This is twofold: bodily (caused by diseases) and mental (caused by desires and passions).
2. **Environmental** <term>Adhibhautika: Suffering caused by other living beings</term>, such as tigers or snakes.
3. **Celestial** <term>Adhidaivika: Suffering caused by supernatural or planetary influences</term>, such as unfavorable astrological alignments.

For people tormented by these sufferings, Hatha Yoga is a "sheltering monastery"—a place of perfect refuge. Furthermore, for those practicing "all forms of Yoga"—such as Mantra Yoga or Karma Yoga—Hatha is the "supporting tortoise." Just as the cosmic tortoise supports the foundation of the entire world, Hatha Yoga is the foundation for all practitioners of Yoga. || 10 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Regarding "Allama": Allama, Prabhudeva, Ghoda, Choli, Tintini, Bhanuki, Naradeva, Khanda, and Kapalika. || 8 ||

Regarding "These and others": These masters, beginning with those named and including others like Taranatha, are Great Masters of unbroken power. Through the influence of Hatha Yoga, they have severed the rod of Death—which is to say, they have conquered death—and roam the universe with unobstructed movement. || 9 ||

Regarding "Every kind": There are three types of suffering: Internal, Environmental, and Celestial...

<summary>This page completes the lineage list of 33 Mahasiddhas and defines Hatha Yoga as the essential foundation (metaphorically the "cosmic tortoise") that supports all other spiritual paths and provides a refuge from the three types of human suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Allama, Mahasiddha, conquering death, three types of suffering, Adhyatmika, Adhibhautika, Adhidaivika, cosmic tortoise, foundation of yoga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the Mahasiddhas (Great Masters) who conquered death. It transitions into the specific requirements for secrecy in Hatha Yoga and the qualifications necessary for a student to begin the practice.</meta>

<page-num>९</page-num>
<header>First Lesson</header>

## Root: Hatha Yoga should be kept highly secret by the Yogi who desires success.
## It remains potent when kept secret, but becomes powerless when revealed. || 11 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Now he explains why Hatha Yoga is to be kept more secret than all other sciences.
**Hatha Yoga...** For the Yogi who desires success <term>Siddhi: supernatural powers like becoming tiny, etc.</term> or who desires the success of absolute liberation <term>Kaivalya-siddhi: the ultimate state of spiritual independence and enlightenment</term>, the science of Hatha Yoga is something that must be guarded and kept extremely secret. He provides the reason: because when Hatha Yoga is kept secret, it is "potent" <note>original: "viryavati," meaning full of virility or effective power</note>, capable of producing unobstructed divine powers. It is capable of producing liberation or the perfection of liberation. 

Next, regarding the qualified student <term>Yogadhikari: one who is fit or entitled to practice Yoga</term>:
> "One who has conquered the senses, is peaceful, devoted to liberation, free from faults, and detached from worldly results. Hatha should be given to such a person, and never to any other."

Yajnavalkya says:
> "One who is joined with duties prescribed by scripture, free from the desire for the fruits of his actions, endowed with self-restraints <term>Yama</term> and fixed rules <term>Niyama</term>, and abandoned all worldly attachments. One who is learned, has conquered anger, is devoted to truth and righteousness, intent on serving the Guru, and devoted to his father and mother. One who remains in his proper stage of life, follows good conduct, and is well-instructed by the wise."

In another place it is said: "It should not be given to one who is only interested in his stomach <note>original: "shishnodararataya," literally one devoted to the genitals and belly</note> or who merely wears the robes of a practitioner for show."
On this point, the author of the *Yoga Chintamani* <note>A famous 16th-century compendium on Yoga</note> states: although in the verses of the Puranas it is seen that all living beings—including Brahmins, warriors, merchants, women, and laborers—have the right to practice Yoga for the sake of purification, the fruit in the form of total liberation <note>original: "moksha"</note> only comes to one who is truly detached <note>original: "virakta," a person who has renounced worldly desires</note>. Therefore, only such a person is truly fit for Yoga.
As stated in the *Vayu Samhita*:
> "Only the one whose mind is detached from objects seen in this world or heard of in the scriptures has the right to this Yoga, and no one else."

Suresvaracharya <note>A famous disciple of the philosopher Shankara</note> says:
> "The qualification for this Yoga belongs only to that rare person who is detached from this world and the next, who wishes to leave behind the cycle of rebirth, and who is a seeker of truth."

The elders have also said: "This should not be given to the ill-mannered..."

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

Now, in this context, there are two types of "heat" or suffering <term>Tapa: the 'burning' distress of existence</term>. When pain occurs in the body due to diseases and such, it is physical suffering. When heat or distress occurs in the mind due to lust and other passions, it is called mental suffering; these two together are called internal sufferings <term>Adhyatmika</term>. Suffering caused by tigers, snakes, and other creatures is called external suffering <term>Adhibhautika</term>. The pain caused by the planets and celestial forces is called divine suffering <term>Adhidaivika</term>. 

For men who are being scorched by all these types of suffering, Hatha Yoga is like a sheltering monastery <note>original: "matha"</note>. "Matha" refers to a cave or a place of retreat. For those practitioners engaged in Mantra Yoga or Karma Yoga, Hatha Yoga is the foundational support, like the Great Tortoise <note>original: "kamatha"</note> or the "Tortoise-Circle" <term>Kurma-chakra</term>. Just as the cosmic tortoise is the support of the entire universe, so too is Hatha Yoga the support of all other Yogas. || 10 ||

Following this, he explains that compared to all other sciences, Hatha Yoga is to be kept extremely secret. **Regarding Hatha Yoga...** The eight supernatural powers <term>Ashta-siddhi</term> are: becoming tiny, becoming huge, becoming heavy, becoming light, reaching any place, irresistible will, lordship, and mastery. These eight...

<sig>2</sig>
<vocab>Hatha Yoga, Siddhi, Kaivalya, Yogadhikari, Yajnavalkya, Vayu Samhita, Suresvaracharya, Yoga Chintamani, Adhyatma, Adhibhautika, Adhidaivika, Kurma, Ashta Siddhi</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that Hatha Yoga must be kept secret to maintain its power and defines the high moral and spiritual qualifications required of a student. It also describes Hatha Yoga as the ultimate refuge from the three types of human suffering and the foundational support for all other spiritual practices.</summary>
<keywords>secrecy in yoga, qualifications for yoga, physical and mental suffering, three tapās, cosmic tortoise metaphor, supernatural powers, renunciation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the practitioner of Hatha Yoga. Having established the need for secrecy in the previous verses, it now concludes that thought and begins to describe the physical environment necessary for successful practice.</meta>

<page-num>( 10 )</page-num>
<header>On the Lamp of Hatha Yoga</header>

-> In a well-governed kingdom, a righteous country, where food is plentiful and free from disturbances, <-
-> within a distance of a bow's length, free from rocks, fire, and water, <-
-> in a solitary place, inside a small hut, the Hatha Yogi should remain. || 12 || <-

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

Knowledge that is kept secret leads to the proper result. As it is said: "If the Goddess of Speech <note>original: 'vacham devi', referring to Saraswati or sacred knowledge</note> is placed in an improper place, she may consume the practitioner with her anger before long." || 11 ||

Now, in one and a half verses, he describes the country suitable for Hatha practice. **In a well-governed kingdom...** "Rajya" refers to the actions or the nature of a king; a "good" one is a "Su-rajya." According to the great saying, "As is the king, so are the subjects," a good king implies that the subjects are also virtuous. **In a righteous country** <term>Dharmike: a place where people follow moral and spiritual laws</term>. This indicates that the practitioner of Hatha will easily obtain favorable food and support. **Where food is plentiful** <term>Subhiksha: literally 'good alms,' a place where one does not struggle to find nourishment</term>; this indicates that such things can be obtained without great effort. **Free from disturbances**, meaning free from the dangers of thieves, tigers, and such. This indicates the suitability of the place for a long-term stay. **A bow's length** <note>original: 'dhanu-pramanam', a measurement of approximately four cubits or six feet</note>; within this distance there should be **no rocks, fire, or water**. A rock refers to stone, fire to flame, and water to any body of water. The meaning is that where one places their seat, there should be no rocks, fire, or water within six feet. This indicates an absence of distress from extreme cold or heat. **In a solitary place**, meaning a secluded area. This indicates an absence of quarreling, which arises from the gathering of people.

-> ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥ <-

Whether the Yogi desires these eight supernatural powers <term>Siddhis: miraculous abilities</term> or desires the success of absolute liberation <term>Kaivalya-siddhi</term>, the science of Hatha must be kept extremely secret. Because when Hatha Yoga remains hidden, it is capable of manifesting unbroken power and absolute liberation. But the science that is made public becomes "powerless" <note>original: 'nirvirya', lacking virility or potency</note> || 11 ||

Now he describes the country suitable for Hatha practice. **In a well-governed kingdom...** A "Su-rajya" is a place where the king’s conduct is entirely virtuous, where the land is righteous, and where the Hatha practitioner can obtain favorable food and other necessities. It should be a place where there is a "good harvest" <note>original: 'sukala'</note> and where one is free from the disturbances of thieves and tigers. From the place where one sits, there should be no rocks, fire, or water within the distance of a "bow's length" (meaning four hands' distance). It should be a solitary place where there is no gathering of people, for crowds lead to conflict. In such a place, having built a small <insert>humble</insert> **hut** <term>Mathika: a small cell or hermitage for practice</term>, the Yogi who practices Hatha should remain inside. By sitting within this hut, one does not suffer the distress of cold, heat, or rain. || 12 ||

<summary>This page defines the ideal environmental conditions for practicing Hatha Yoga, emphasizing a stable, righteous, and secluded location. It also warns that the potency of the practice is lost if it is not kept secret from the uninitiated.</summary>
<keywords>sacred space, hermitage, solitude, secrecy, Hatha Yoga, Siddhis, Kaivalya, environment for yoga</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the practitioner's environment. While the previous verse established the general location (a righteous kingdom, a solitary place), this verse focuses on the architectural requirements and cleanliness of the yoga hut (matha) itself to ensure the practitioner remains focused and undisturbed by the elements or other people.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>First Instruction</header>

**A small door, without holes, pits, or crevices; neither too high, too low, nor too long.**
**Thoroughly smeared with thick cow dung, pure, and free from all insects.**
**Outside, beautiful with a pavilion, an altar, and a well, surrounded by a wall.**
**This is said to be the description of a yoga hut by the perfected masters of Hatha practice. || 13 ||**

## ॥ Commentary ॥

Indeed, if many people live together, quarrels will surely occur. As it is said in the *Bhagavata*: "Among many dwellers, there is conflict; even with two, there is chatter." <note>original: "vāse bahūnāṃ kalaho bhaved vārttā dvayorapi"</note>. In such a small hermitage—a "matha" refers to a small hut, with the suffix indicating its small size—the Hatha Yogi should remain. The phrase "inside a small hut" implies the avoidance of the suffering caused by cold, heat, and other weather conditions.

Here, the half-verse "With proper diet and movement for the success of Hatha Yoga" has not been explained because it was an interpolation <note>an addition made later by a different author</note> by someone else. Only the original verses are explained. The same applies hereafter: any verses found in other versions of the *Lamp of Hatha Yoga* <note>Hatha-Pradipika</note> that I do not explain should be understood as interpolations. || 13 ||

Now, he describes the characteristics of the hut: **A small door...** refers to a place that has a small entrance. **Without holes, pits, or crevices** means there are no windows or ventilation gaps <term>gavaksha: literally "cow-eyes," referring to small windows</term>, no low depressions in the floor, and no holes made by mice or other animals. **Neither too high, too low, nor too long** describes the dimensions. <note>The commentator here discusses a complex grammatical compound (karmadharaya) regarding these adjectives.</note> If the hut is too high, there is the fatigue of climbing; if it is too low, there is the fatigue of stooping or descending; if it is too long (spacious), the vision wanders too far, which is a distraction. To prevent these, it is said to be "neither too high, low, nor long."

**Thoroughly smeared with thick cow dung** <term>Gomaya: cow dung, traditionally used as a sacred and antiseptic plaster for floors</term>. It should be applied thickly so that the surface is smooth and **pure** (clean). **Free from all insects** means it is abandoned by or devoid of all creatures such as mosquitoes and bedbugs. **Outside**, meaning in the area surrounding the hut, there should be a **pavilion** <term>mandapa: an open hall or shaded area</term>, an **altar** <term>vedi: a raised, purified platform for ritual or sitting</term>, and a **well** <term>kupa: a water source</term>. These make the place **beautiful** (pleasant). It should be **surrounded by a wall** or a fence on all sides.

## ॥ Vernacular Summary ॥

Now, after this, the characteristics of the hut are described: **A small door...** There should be a small door. There should be no lattice-windows, skylights, or holes, nor any pits in the ground or mouse holes. The place should not be extremely low, extremely high, or extremely wide. Why? Because climbing up and going down causes fatigue.

<vocab>hermitage (matha), Hatha Yoga, characteristics (lakshana), commentary (tika), cow dung (gomaya), Lamp of Hatha Yoga (Hatha-Pradipika)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the architectural and hygienic requirements for a Hatha Yoga practitioner's hut, specifying that it should be small, clean, and secluded to prevent physical fatigue and mental distraction.</summary>
<keywords>yoga hut, matha, hermitage construction, cow dung plaster, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, solitude, distraction, sacred space</keywords>
