<meta>This page continues the specific architectural requirements for the yoga hut (matha) and transitions into the actual practice of yoga within that space. It draws from various Puranic and Yogic texts to explain how the practitioner’s environment—both physical and visual—should support their mental state.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>On the Lamp of Hatha Yoga</header>

## Staying in such a hut, freed from all anxieties,
## one should practice Yoga as taught by the Guru. || 14 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

This means the hut is enclosed by walls. These characteristics of the Yoga hut—the small door and so on—were stated by the <term>Siddhas</term><note>Perfected masters who have attained the goals of Yoga</note> who are dedicated to the practice of Hatha Yoga. In the *Nandikeshvara Purana*, the characteristics of a hut are described as follows:

"The temple should be beautifully laid out, pleasing, and scented with fragrances. It should be fragrant with incense and decorated with heaps of flowers. It should be beautified by the presence of sages, holy bathing places, rivers, trees, lotus ponds, and hills. The wise practitioner should create a highly beautiful Yoga-house with a clean path, decorated with various murals and paintings. By seeing the peaceful sages in the paintings, the mind attains tranquility. By seeing the <term>Siddhas</term> in the paintings, the resolve for effort is born. In the middle of the Yoga-house, one should have the 'cycle of worldly existence' <note>original: "saṃsāramaṇḍalaṃ"</note> painted. In some places, one should paint the terrifying cremation grounds and the various hells. Seeing those fearsome forms in a world devoid of essence, the Yogi, desiring perfection, becomes free from despondency. One should also see images of beings who are diseased, aging, intoxicated, or suffering from ailments." || 13 ||

Having stated the characteristics of the hut, he now says what should be done within the hut: **Staying in such a...** "Such" means of the kind described previously. **Staying** means taking up residence there. **Freed from all anxieties** means being entirely devoid of all worries and cares. **As taught by the Guru** means following the path or the specific method of Hatha practice instructed by the teacher. One should **practice Yoga alone**—daily and constantly. The word "alone" (*eva*) indicates that other types of practices may act as obstacles to Yoga.

This is stated in the *Seed of Yoga* <note>original: "Yoga-bija"</note>: "One should always serve the Guru through whom the mastery of the breath is attained. A wise person should conquer the <term>Prana</term><note>The vital life force or breath</note> through the grace of the Guru’s own words."

In the *Royal Yoga* <note>original: "Rajayoga"</note>: "The wish-fulfilling gem of Yoga cannot be reached through the logic of Vedanta, various scriptures, or collections of sciences, nor through meditation and rituals, if one leaves aside the one true Guru."

In the *Skanda Purana*: "Having obtained the absolute essence of Yoga from the teacher, one of steady mind attains the goal as it was described."

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

The sight should be clear and far-reaching. The floor should be thickly smeared with beautiful cow dung <note>Historically used in India as a purifying and cooling floor coating</note> and be pure. There should be no insects, mosquitoes, bedbugs, or anything of the sort. Outside the hut, there should be a pavilion, a raised altar, a well or water reservoir, and rows of trees and flowers, making the site delightful. It must be surrounded by walls on all four sides. These are the characteristics and form of the Yoga hut, as told by the <term>Siddhas</term> whose nature is dedicated to Hatha practice, where the entrance is a small door. || 13 ||

Having described the hut, he explains what is proper to do in the hut: **In such a...** In a hut of this kind, one should reside, becoming free from all anxieties. One should always practice Yoga through the path and method of Hatha instruction given by the Guru. || 14 ||

<summary>This page concludes the description of the ideal practitioner's hut—recommending murals of both peaceful sages and terrifying hells to foster focus and detachment—and introduces the necessity of practicing under a Guru's specific guidance.</summary>
<keywords>Matha, Guru, Hatha Yoga, Nandikeshvara Purana, detatchment, murals, environment, Yoga-bija, Rajayoga, Skanda Purana</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical environment of the Yoga hut to the internal and behavioral disciplines required for success. It follows the instruction to practice Yoga under a Guru’s guidance mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>The First Instruction</header>

**Over-eating, over-exertion, talkativeness, obsession with rules,
association with common people, and fickleness—by these six, Yoga is destroyed.** || 15 ||

**Enthusiasm, boldness, perseverance, knowledge of the truth, firm resolve,
and abandoning the company of common people—by these six, Yoga succeeds.** || 16 ||

->।। Commentary ।।<-

"By that practice, one also attains liberation <note>original: "nirvṛtiṃ"</note>." Thus says Suresvaracharya <note>A famous 8th-century philosopher and disciple of Shankara</note>. "Through the Guru's grace, one attains Yoga with its eight limbs. Through Shiva's grace, one attains eternal success in Yoga. For the high-souled person who has supreme devotion to God, and as much devotion to the Guru as to God, these truths shine forth." And the Scripture <note>referring to the Chandogya Upanishad 6.14.2</note> says: "The man who has a teacher knows the truth." || 14 ||

Now he states the obstacles to the practice of Yoga: **Over-eating...** and so on. **Over-eating** <note>original: "atyāhāra"</note> is excessive eating, consuming more food than one’s hunger requires. **Over-exertion** <note>original: "prayāsa"</note> refers to activities that produce physical fatigue. **Talkativeness** <note>original: "prajalpa"</note> is excessive speaking. **Obsession with rules** <note>original: "niyamagraha"</note> refers to the stubborn adherence to rituals such as bathing in cold water at dawn, eating only at night, or a diet consisting only of fruit. **Association with common people** <note>original: "janasaṅga"</note> refers to the company of worldly people, because it gives rise to desire and other distractions. **Fickleness** <note>original: "laulya"</note> is the state of being unsteady or restless. Because these six, starting with over-eating, obstruct the practice, Yoga is utterly destroyed. || 15 ||

Now he states the factors that lead to success in Yoga: **Enthusiasm...** and so on. **Enthusiasm** <note>original: "utsāha"</note> is the effort or zeal expressed as: "I will certainly restrain the mind which is prone to worldly objects." **Boldness** <note>original: "sāhasa"</note> is the act of proceeding quickly after considering what is achievable and what is not. **Perseverance** <note>original: "dhairya"</note> is the lack of distress or fatigue, the resolve that "I will surely succeed throughout my life." **Knowledge of the truth** <note>original: "tattvajñāna"</note> is the realization that worldly objects are as unreal as water in a mirage, and that the Absolute <note>original: "brahma"</note> alone is real...

->।। Vernacular Translation ।।<-

Now the obstacles to Yoga practice are described: **Over-eating...** Over-eating means eating more food so that one does not feel hunger again; that is over-eating. That which causes much fatigue is **over-exertion**. Speaking too much is **talkativeness**. Adhering to rules <note>original: "niyama grahana"</note> like bathing in cold water in the morning, eating only at night, or fruit-only diets, and so on, is **obsession with rules**. The company of worldly people and restlessness—by these six, Yoga is destroyed. || 15 ||

Now the factors that create success in Yoga are described: **Enthusiasm...** (1) Enthusiasm, (2) Boldness, (3) Perseverance, (4) Knowledge of the truth, (5) Firm Resolve, (6) Abandoning the company of common people. Their meaning: the effort to restrain the mind from worldly objects is **enthusiasm**. To engage suddenly in practice without over-analyzing whether these means are suitable or unsuitable is **boldness**. **Perseverance** is having patience. Realizing that worldly objects are like the water of a mirage...

<summary>This page identifies the six behavioral obstacles that destroy Yoga practice (such as over-eating and gossip) and the six virtues that ensure its success (such as enthusiasm and perseverance).</summary>
<keywords>Atyahara, Prayasa, Prajalpa, Niyamagraha, Janasanga, Laulya, Utsaha, Sahasa, Dhairya, Tattvajnana, Niscaya, Yoga obstacles, Yoga success</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the six factors that lead to success in Yoga (from Verse 16) and introduces the first physical practice of Hatha Yoga: Asana (posture). It bridges the moral restraints of general Yoga with the specific physical focus of the Hatha tradition.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>In the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

## ।। Now, the Restraints and Observances ।।

> “Non-violence, truth, non-stealing, continence, forgiveness, fortitude,
> compassion, sincerity, moderate diet, and purity—these ten are the Restraints <note>original: "yamas"</note>. || 1 ||
> Austerity, contentment, belief in the sacred, charity, worship of God,
> listening to the established doctrines, modesty, intellect, penance, and sacrifice—
> these ten are described as the Observances <note>original: "niyamas"</note> by those skilled in the science of Yoga.” || 2 ||

**Since posture is the first limb of Hatha Yoga, it is described first. One should practice posture, which brings stability, health, and lightness of limbs. || 17 ||**

### ।। Scholarly Commentary ।।

Knowledge <note>original: "vittvajnānam"</note> refers to the knowledge of reality <note>original: "tattvajñāna"</note>, the actual understanding of the principles of Yoga. Resolve <note>original: "niścaya"</note> means faith or conviction in the scriptures and the words of the Teacher. By "abandoning the company of people," he means avoiding the society of those who are unfavorable to the practice of Yoga. Through these six qualities <note>referring to verse 16</note>, Yoga is accomplished excellently and without delay. || 16 ||

Now, he explains the connection for describing posture first, and its general benefits. **Since posture is the first limb...** and so on. Regarding "Hatha," there are four limbs which will be described later: Posture <note>original: "āsana"</note>, Breath Retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note>, the practices called Seals <note>original: "mudrā"</note>, and the Exploration of the Inner Sound <note>original: "nādānusandhāna"</note>. The stages from Sense-Withdrawal to Meditative Absorption <note>original: "pratyāhāra" to "samādhi"</note> are included within the Exploration of the Inner Sound. Because Posture is the first limb among these, it is described first—this is the connection. **That posture** produces stability, meaning a steadiness of the body by destroying the quality of Passion <term>Rajas: the energetic or restless quality of nature</term> which manifests as the flickering of the mind. This is supported by the statement: "Posture destroys Passion." **Health** refers to the absence of diseases that distract the mind. The fact that disease is a distraction for the mind is stated in the Aphorisms of Patanjali <note>Yoga Sutra 1.30</note>: "Disease, dullness, doubt..."

### ।। Vernacular Explanation ।।

Just as a thing may be false, the Absolute <note>original: "Brahman"</note> alone is true; this actual knowledge is "Knowledge of Reality." Belief and faith in the scriptures and the Teacher’s words is "Resolve." By abandoning the company of people who create obstacles to Yoga practice, the Hatha practitioner attains success in Yoga very quickly through these six qualities. || 16 ||

Now he tells the fruit of the postures. **Since posture is the first limb...** Hatha Yoga has four limbs: 1. Posture, 2. Breath Retention, 3. The practice of Seals, and 4. Exploration of the Inner Sound. These will be spoken of later. Among these, Posture is the first limb, and therefore postures are described first. This posture makes the body and mind stable by removing the quality of Passion <note>original: "rajoguṇa"</note> which is characterized by restlessness. It also removes disease. It removes the quality of Darkness <term>Tamas: the quality of heaviness, lethargy, or ignorance</term> which manifests as heaviness in the limbs, and thus it brings lightness to the limbs. It also removes the increase of hunger and thirst. || 17 ||

<summary>This page defines the moral foundations of Yoga (Yamas and Niyamas) and introduces Posture (Asana) as the essential first physical step of Hatha Yoga, intended to stabilize the mind and body.</summary>
<keywords>Asana, Posture, Yama, Niyama, Rajas, Tamas, Tattvajnana, Hatha Yoga limbs, health, stability</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to physical postures (Asanas) following the general benefits mentioned on page 14. It establishes the authority of the teachings by citing legendary lineages and provides the first technical instruction for a specific posture, the Svastikasana.</meta>

( 15 )

# First Lesson

**I shall now describe some of the postures that have been accepted by sages such as Vasistha and by yogis such as Matsyendra. || 18 ||**

**Placing both soles of the feet properly between the knees and the thighs, one should sit with the body straight. This is called the Auspicious Posture <note>original: "Svastikasana"</note>. || 19 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The obstacles to progress are "disease, sloth, doubt, lack of enthusiasm, laziness, worldly-mindedness, delusion, failing to attain a state of concentration, and instability." <note>The commentator is citing the Yoga Sutras of Patanjali to explain what these practices overcome.</note> By this practice, the "lightness of the limbs" is achieved, which means the destruction of the quality of heaviness or inertia <note>original: "tamas"</note>. Through the word "and," we should also understand the increase of digestive fire and other benefits. || 17 ||

He states that he will describe the best among the postures approved by Vasistha and others. **By Vasistha and others...** refers to the meditative sages <note>original: "munis"</note> like Vasistha and Yajnavalkya. The word "and" implies those devoted to sacred chants <note>original: "mantras"</note>. **By Matsyendra and others...** refers to Hatha yogis like Jalandharanatha and his followers. The word "and" here implies those devoted to the physical Seals <note>original: "mudras"</note>. Among the eighty-four traditional postures accepted by them, I shall describe some of the best. Although both groups practice both contemplation and physical Hatha, they are mentioned separately because Vasistha and his lineage emphasize contemplation <note>original: "manana"</note>, while Matsyendra and his lineage emphasize physical Hatha practice. || 18 ||

Because it is easy to perform, he describes the Auspicious Posture first. **Between the knees and thighs...** In this context, the word "knee" <note>original: "janu"</note> should be understood as the region of the calf near the knee. The reading "between the calves and thighs" is actually better. When one sits with the soles of both feet placed between these two, keeping the body straight and balanced, the yogis call that posture the "Svastika" or Auspicious Posture. It is said by Shridhara: "Placing the feet between the thighs and calves..."

---

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

**By Vasistha and others...** Sages like Vasistha and Yajnavalkya, who are naturally inclined toward contemplation and the mastery of sacred chants, as well as Hatha yogis like Matsyendra and Jalandhara, who are devoted to physical practice and the Seals—these masters have accepted eighty-four postures. Among those, I will describe a few of the best. Sages like Vasistha are primary in contemplation, while those like Matsyendra are primary in Hatha practice; therefore, their names are mentioned separately. || 18 ||

Since it is the most accessible of all, the Auspicious Posture <note>original: "Svastikasana"</note> is described first. **Between the knees and thighs...** Having placed both soles between the knees and the thighs, one should sit with a straight body. This is called the Auspicious Posture. || 19 ||

<vocab>
<term>Svastikasana</term>: The "Auspicious Posture," a seated cross-legged position used for meditation.
<term>Vasistha</term>: A legendary Vedic sage representing the path of knowledge and contemplation.
<term>Matsyendra</term>: A founding figure of the Nath lineage of Hatha Yoga, representing physical mastery.
<term>Tamas</term>: The quality of darkness, heaviness, or lethargy in the body and mind.
<term>Hatha Yoga</term>: A system of yoga emphasizing physical techniques to balance the body's energies.
</vocab>

<summary>This page lists the lineages of Yoga (the meditative path of Vasistha and the physical path of Matsyendra) and provides the first specific technical instruction for Svastikasana (the Auspicious Posture).</summary>
<keywords>Svastikasana, Vasistha, Matsyendra, lineage, Hatha Yoga, posture, anatomy, eighty-four asanas</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the eighty-four classical postures (asanas). Following the description of Svastikasana (the Auspicious Posture) on the previous page, the text now provides the technical instructions for three additional poses: the Cow-Face, the Hero, and the Tortoise.</meta>

( 16 )

### In the Light on Hatha Yoga
<note>original: "Hathayogapradipika"</note>

**Place the right ankle on the left side of the back, and likewise place the left ankle on the right side. This is the Cow-Face Posture <note>original: "Gomukhasana"</note>, so called because it resembles the face of a cow. || 20 ||**

**Place one foot upon the opposite thigh, and place the other thigh over the other foot. This is proclaimed as the Hero Posture <note>original: "Virasana"</note>. || 21 ||**

**Pressing the anus firmly with the ankles in a crossed position, sitting well-composed: this is known as the Tortoise Posture <note>original: "Kurmasana"</note> by those who know Yoga. || 22 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...The wise know that to be the Auspicious Posture. || 19 || <note>This refers back to the end of the previous page's discussion on Svastikasana.</note>

He now describes the Cow-Face Posture. **On the left...** means on the left side of the back—according to the oral tradition <note>original: "sampradaya"</note>, in the lower part of the waist—one should firmly place the right ankle. That posture, the appearance of which is like the face of a cow, becomes the one known as Cow-Face. || 20 ||

He now describes the Hero Posture. **One...** refers to the right foot. **Thus...** is used to fill the meter of the verse. One should place it so it rests on the left thigh. On the other—the left foot—one should place the right thigh. This is called or proclaimed as the Hero Posture. || 21 ||

He now describes the Tortoise Posture. **The anus...** By restraining or pressing the anus with the two ankles in a reversed or crossed manner, where one becomes properly "composed" or steadily situated. This becomes the Tortoise Posture. "So the masters of Yoga understand it" is how the sentence concludes. || 22 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Now the Cow-Face Posture is described. **On the left...** Having placed the right ankle <note>original: "gulpha"</note> below the left side of the waist, and then placing the left ankle below the right side of the waist, one sits down. Its shape is like that of a cow's face, therefore it is the posture named Cow-Face. || 20 ||

The Hero Posture is described. **One...** Having placed the right foot on the left thigh, and then placing the left foot on the ground by the right thigh, one remains steady. This is called the Hero Posture. || 21 ||

Now the Tortoise Posture is described. **The anus...** Having blocked the anus with the heels of both feet, one should remain steady and attentive. This is the Tortoise Posture; its variations will be spoken of further ahead. || 22 ||

<summary>This page provides the technical instructions for three important physical postures: Gomukhasana (Cow-Face), Virasana (Hero), and Kurmasana (Tortoise), supplemented by both a Sanskrit commentary and a vernacular explanation of the specific leg placements.</summary>
<keywords>Gomukhasana, Cow-Face Posture, Virasana, Hero Posture, Kurmasana, Tortoise Posture, asana, ankles, yoga technique</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the eighty-four classical postures (asanas). Following the descriptions of the Cow-Face, Hero, and Tortoise poses on the previous page, the text now details the Cock Pose, the Extended Tortoise Pose, and the Bow Pose.</meta>

( 17 )

->### First Instruction<-

> **Original Verse:** Having firmly established the Lotus Posture <note>original: "Padmasana"</note>, one should insert the hands between the thighs and the knees. Placing the hands firmly on the ground, one remains suspended in the air; this is the Cock Posture <note>original: "Kukkutasana"</note>. || 23 ||
> 
> **Original Verse:** Remaining bound in the Cock Posture, one should clasp the neck with the two arms and lie on the back like a tortoise. This is the Extended Tortoise Posture <note>original: "Uttana-Kurmasaka"</note>. || 24 ||
> 
> **Original Verse:** Holding the two big toes with the hands, one should draw them as far as the ears, as if pulling a bow. This is called the Bow Posture <note>original: "Dhanurasana"</note>. || 25 ||

## ॥ Commentary ॥

He describes the Cock Posture in the verse beginning with **"Having firmly established the Lotus Posture..."** <note>original: "padmasanaṃ tu"</note>. The Lotus Posture consists of placing the upturned feet properly upon the thighs. By the word "knee" <note>original: "janu"</note>, the text refers to the region of the calves near the knees. Between these—the knees and the thighs—one should insert the hands and place them firmly on the ground. The word "hands" is also connected to the action of supporting the weight. That which resembles the Lotus Posture but is "situated in the sky" <note>original: "vyomasthaṃ"</note>—meaning suspended off the ground—is the Cock Posture. || 23 ||

He describes the Extended Tortoise Posture in the verse beginning with **"Remaining bound in the Cock Posture..."** <note>original: "kukkutasana-"</note>. While situated in the "binding" or lock of the Cock Posture described in the previous verse, one clasps the neck <note>original: "kandharaṃ"</note> with the two arms and lies on the back <note>original: "uttana"</note> like a tortoise. The posture in which this occurs is named the Extended Tortoise. || 24 ||

He describes the Bow Posture in the verse beginning with **"Holding the two big toes..."** <note>original: "padanguṣthau tu"</note>. Holding the big toes of the feet with the hands, one should draw them back as far as the ears <note>original: "shravanavadhi"</note>, just as a bow is drawn. The meaning is: while one hand remains extended holding a toe, the other hand holding the other toe should be drawn back, contracted toward the ear. This is called the Bow Posture. || 25 ||

---

## ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥

Now the Cock Posture is described. **"Having firmly established the Lotus Posture..."** Placing the feet high upon both thighs, and inserting both hands between the knees and the thighs, one should fix them to the earth. By the strength of the hands, one rises from the ground and remains suspended; this is the Cock Posture. || 23 ||

The Extended Tortoise Posture is described. **"Remaining bound in the Cock Posture..."** While situated in the same bind as the previously mentioned Cock Posture, one should grasp the neck <note>Old Hindi: "nāḍa," literally 'vein' or 'tendon,' here referring to the back of the neck</note> with those same arms and lie on the back like a tortoise. This is called the posture named Extended Tortoise. || 24 ||

Now the Bow Posture is described. **"Holding the two big toes..."** Grasping the big toes of both feet with both hands, one should pull them toward the ears like the drawing of a bow. While one big toe is held—

It emphasizes the physical mechanics of lifting the body off the ground and the stretching mimicry of animals and tools (cock, tortoise, and bow).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of yoga postures (asanas). The previous page concluded with the Bow Pose (Dhanurasana). This page provides the technical description and benefits of the Lord of the Fishes Pose (Matsyendrasana), followed by a commentary and a vernacular explanation.</meta>

( 18 )

->### In the Light on Hatha Yoga<-

**Root Text:**
Place the right foot at the base of the left thigh, and the left foot outside the [right] knee. Holding [the feet], remain with the body twisted. This is the posture told by the illustrious Matsyendranatha. || 26 ||

The Seat of Matsyendra <note>original: "Matsyendra-pitham"</note> kindles the digestive fire <note>original: "jathara-pradiptim"</note> and is a weapon that shatters the assembly of terrible diseases. Through practice, it grants men the awakening of the Kundalini and stability of the Moon. || 27 ||

->|| Commentary ||<-

He describes the Lord of the Fishes Pose <note>original: "Matsyendrasanam"</note> in the verse beginning with **"At the base of the left thigh..."** <note>original: "vamoru-mule"</note>. The right foot is placed at the base of the left thigh. According to tradition, one reaches behind the back with the left hand to grasp the ankle. The left foot is then placed outside the right knee. With the right hand reaching around the outside of the left knee, one should grasp the big toe. With the body twisted <note>original: "parivartitanga"</note>—meaning the face is turned toward the back over the left side—one remains in this position. This posture was taught by Matsyendranatha. Because it was taught by him, people call it by his name. In the same way, one should practice the opposite side: place the left foot at the base of the right thigh, grasp it with the right hand reaching behind the back, place the right foot outside the left knee, and grasp it with the left hand, keeping the body twisted toward the right. || 26 ||

He describes the results of the Lord of the Fishes Pose in the verse beginning with **"The Seat of Matsyendra..."** <note>original: "Matsyendra-pitham"</note>. It acts like a weapon <note>original: "astram"</note> for cutting down or shattering the circle or collection of fierce and unbearable diseases. Through practice—meaning through daily...

->|| Vernacular Language ||<-

...extending one hand and grasping the toe, one pulls the other hand back as far as the ear; this is called the Bow Pose <note>original: "Dhanurasana"</note>. <note>This sentence completes the description of the previous pose from page 17.</note> || 25 ||

Now he describes the Lord of the Fishes Pose. Place the right foot at the base of the left thigh. Reaching behind the back with the hand, grasp the upper part of the heel. Then, placing the left foot outside the right knee, reach around with the right hand and hold the big toe. The yogi who remains in this position with a twisted body performs the posture taught by Lord Matsyendranatha. Therefore, it is called by this name. In this same way, one should practice by placing the left foot at the base of the right thigh, reaching behind the back with the right hand to grasp it, and holding the right foot (which is outside the left knee) with the left hand. This is the Lord of the Fishes Pose. || 26 ||

Now he describes the benefits of the Lord of the Fishes Pose. It is like a weapon for destroying the collection of fierce and unbearable diseases. Furthermore, for those men who practice this regularly, the digestive fire <note>original: "jatharagni"</note> in the stomach is greatly increased...

<summary>This page describes Matsyendrasana (Lord of the Fishes Pose), a seated twist named after the sage Matsyendranatha. It details the physical alignment and attributes the pose with the power to cure diseases, awaken Kundalini, and increase digestive fire.</summary>
<keywords>Matsyendrasana, Matsyendranatha, Hatha Yoga, yoga postures, digestive fire, Kundalini, disease prevention, yoga commentary, Dhanurasana</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page concludes the commentary on verse 27 regarding the Lord of the Fishes Pose (Matsyendrasana) and introduces verse 28 and 29, which describe the Back-Stretching Pose (Paschimottanasana). The text transitions from the benefits of the previous posture to the technique and results of this new one.</meta>

( 19 )

### First Instruction ###

**Root Text: Stretching both legs on the ground like sticks and grasping the two toes with the hands, one should stay with the forehead resting upon the knees. This is called the Back-Stretching Pose <note>original: "Paschimottanasana"</note>. || 28 ||**

**This Back-Stretching Pose, the foremost of postures, makes the breath <note>original: "pavana"</note> flow through the back channel <note>referring to the Sushumna, the central energetic channel of the spine</note>. It kindles the digestive fire <note>original: "jatharanala"</note>, makes the belly lean, and brings health to men. || 29 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...through the practice of twisting the body <note>original: "bhramavartana-rupa"</note>, it grants men a powerful kindling and increase of the digestive fire. Similarly, it grants the awakening of the Kundalini <note>the fundamental evolutionary energy at the base of the spine</note>—meaning the absence of its dormant state—and grants stability to the "Moon" located in the upper part of the palate, which naturally tends to leak, meaning it prevents the loss of vital nectar. || 27 ||

He now describes the Back-Stretching Pose <note>original: "Paschimattanam-asanam"</note> in the verse beginning with **"Stretching..."**. On the ground, stretch out both legs until they are shaped like sticks, with the ankles touching. With the arms—specifically using the index fingers hooked—grasp the two big toes. Pulling with force so that the area behind the knees does not rise from the ground, one should stay there with the forehead placed upon the knees. This they call the posture named the Back-Stretching Pose <note>Paschima means "west" or "back," and Uttana means "intense stretch"</note>. || 28 ||

Now for its results: **"Thus..."**. This Back-Stretching Pose, which is the chief among all postures previously mentioned, makes the breath <note>the life-force or Prana</note> flow through the "back way"—the back way being the Sushumna channel. It brings about the "rising" or increase of the fire in the stomach. It creates leanness in the middle region of the abdomen. It brings about health, and through the word "and," it implies the balancing of the pulse and other vital functions. || 29 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

It grants [these benefits]: it awakens the Kundalini, which is the foundational power <note>original: "adhara-shakti"</note>, meaning it removes its state of sleep. Likewise, the Moon situated in the upper palate constantly drips [nectar]; this pose makes that dripping stop and become stable. || 27 ||

Now the Back-Stretching Pose is described: **"Stretching..."**. Straighten both hands on the ground like sticks and stretch both legs out long. With the arms, pull both big toes with force. Then, remain with the forehead placed upon the knees. This is the posture named the Back-Stretching Pose. || 28 ||

Regarding the fruits: **"Thus..."**. Among the postures mentioned earlier, this Back-Stretching Pose is the chief. It causes the breath, which flows through the central channel, to flow properly through that Sushumna. It increases the fire in the belly, creates slimness in the middle of the stomach, and brings about health. In other ways, it makes the movement of the pulses and other systems equal and balanced. || 29 ||

<vocab>Paschimottanasana, Asana, Digestive fire, Kundalini, Sushumna path, Pranayama</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the "First Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. The previous page detailed the Back-Stretching Pose (Paschimottanasana). This page introduces the technique and medical benefits of the Peacock Pose (Mayurasana).</meta>

( 20 )

### In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika ###

**Root Text: Supporting the body on the ground with the palms of both hands, with the elbows placed at the sides of the navel, one should stay elevated and raised up like a stick in the air. This they call the Peacock Pose <note>original: "Mayura-pitham"</note>. || 30 ||**

**The glorious Peacock Pose <note>original: "Shri-Mayuram"</note> quickly destroys all diseases, such as abdominal tumors and dropsy <note>original: "gulma-udara-adi"; "udara" often refers to enlargement of the abdomen or dropsy</note>, and overcomes the imbalances of the body <note>original: "doshas"</note>. It completely turns to ashes all unwholesome food that has been eaten, kindles the digestive fire <note>original: "jathara-agni"</note>, and even digests the deadliest poison <note>original: "kalakuta"</note>. || 31 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He now describes the Peacock Pose <note>original: "Mayurasana"</note> with the verse beginning **"Supporting the body..."**. With both hands—meaning the pair or the couple of hands—supporting or resting upon the "earth"—meaning the ground—one should stay with the fingers spread and the palms touching the ground. On the two elbows—the middle joints of the arms—of those two hands, the two sides of the navel are held or placed. One who is thus in a "high seat"—meaning an elevated or raised posture—is raised up in the "void" <note>original: "khe"; referring here to the air or space</note> like a "stick"—meaning straight like a staff. Where this position occurs, the yogis—this word is implied—call it the "Peacock" because it belongs to or is associated with the peacock. || 30 ||

He now describes the qualities of the Peacock Pose with the verse beginning **"Destroys..."**. "Tumors" <note>original: "gulma"</note> is a specific type of disease; "dropsy" <note>original: "udara"</note> refers to abdominal swelling. These "and others"—meaning diseases of the spleen <note>original: "pliha"</note> and so forth—all these diseases are "quickly"—meaning instantly—destroyed or removed. The phrase "Glorious Peacock Pose" is connected to all these benefits. It "overcomes"—meaning sets aside—the "imbalances" <note>original: "doshas"</note>, namely wind, bile, and phlegm <note>Vata, Pitta, and Kapha</note>, as well as those things that produce lethargy. Whatever large amount of "unwholesome food" <note>original: "kad-ashana" or "kad-anna"</note> has been eaten, it makes it all into "ashes"—meaning it causes it to be digested. It "produces"—meaning brings to light—the "digestive fire" <note>original: "jatharagni"</note>. It causes even "deadly poison" <note>original: "kalakuta"</note>—or food that is as harmful as that poison—to be entirely "broken down"—meaning it causes it to be digested or assimilated. || 31 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Now the Peacock Pose <note>original: "Mayurasana"</note> is described. Placing both arms on the earth, one holds the sides of the navel with the middle joints of the arms—the elbows. In this way, the posture is "high," being raised up from the earth. When one stays elevated and upright, it is called the Peacock Pose, as it relates to the peacock. || 30 ||

Now the qualities of the Peacock Pose are described. It quickly removes all diseases beginning with dropsy and spleen disorders. It overcomes wind, bile, and phlegm <note>the three bodily humors</note>, as well as that which causes lethargy. Even if a large amount of very bad food is eaten, it turns it to ashes. It awakens the digestive fire and digests food that acts as harmfully as poison. || 31 ||

<summary>This page describes the technique of Mayurasana (Peacock Pose), emphasizing the balance of the navel on the elbows, and lists its powerful digestive benefits, including the destruction of toxins and abdominal diseases.</summary>
<keywords>Mayurasana, Peacock Pose, digestive fire, jatharagni, doshas, toxins, Kalakuta poison, Hatha Yoga, abdominal health</keywords>

### First Instruction ( 21 ) ###

**Root Text: Lying on the back on the ground like a corpse is the Corpse Pose <note>original: "Shavasana"</note>. The Corpse Pose removes fatigue and brings rest to the mind. || 32 ||**

**Eighty-four asanas were taught by Lord Shiva. Taking from them the four that are the essence, I shall describe them. || 33 ||**

**These four are the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddha"</note>, the Lotus Pose <note>original: "Padma"</note>, the Lion Pose <note>original: "Simha"</note>, and the Auspicious Pose <note>original: "Bhadra"</note>. Among those, one should always sit comfortably in the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note>. || 34 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He explains the Corpse Pose <note>original: "Shavasana"</note> with half a verse beginning with **"Lying on the back..."**. Like a "corpse"—meaning a dead body—lying supine with the back touching the ground, arranged as if in sleep; that is the posture called "Shava" or the Corpse Pose. He states the purpose of the Corpse Pose in the second half of the verse. The Corpse Pose is "fatigue-removing"—it takes away the exhaustion or weariness caused by the practice of Hatha Yoga—and it is the "causer of rest for the mind." || 32 ||

Stating the superiority of the four postures to be described, he says **"Eighty-four..."**. Eighty-four asanas were spoken of by Shiva—that is, the Lord—and the word "and" <note>original: "ca"</note> implies eighty-four hundred thousand <note>8.4 million</note>. As was said by Gorakshanatha: "There are as many asanas as there are species of living beings. Maheshvara <note>a title for Shiva meaning 'Great Lord'</note> knows all their variations. Eighty-four hundred thousand have been mentioned, one for each. From those, Shiva selected eighty-four seats <note>original: "pithanam"</note> by taking one hundred and subtracting sixteen." From among those eighty-four excellent postures spoken by Shiva, "taking"—meaning selecting—the "quartet" <note>original: "chatushka"</note> that constitutes the "essence" or the best part, "I shall speak"—this is the connection. || 33 ||

He now specifies the names of that quartet: **"Siddha..."**. "Siddha" is the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note>. "Padma" is...

---

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

He describes the Corpse Pose: **"Lying on the back..."**. Like a corpse, placing the back against the earth and lying down, staying as if in sleep—this is the Corpse Pose. What is the purpose of doing this? This posture removes the weariness from the practice of Hatha Yoga and provides rest to the mind. || 32 ||

He now speaks of the superiority of the four coming asanas: **"Eighty-four..."**. There are 8.4 million asanas. There are as many asanas as there are species of living beings. Shiva knows their variations. Among them, eighty-four are famous. From those eighty-four, I select and speak of the four essential, superior postures. These are called the "Quartet" <note>original: "Chatushka"</note>. || 33 ||

**"Siddha..."**:
1. The Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note>
2. The Lotus Pose <note>original: "Padmasana"</note>
3. The Lion Pose <note>original: "Simhasana"</note>
4. The Auspicious Pose <note>original: "Bhadrasana"</note>
These four asanas are the best.

<summary>This page describes Shavasana (Corpse Pose) for relaxation and mental rest, then notes that while Shiva taught 8.4 million poses, four—Siddha, Padma, Simha, and Bhadra—are the most essential.</summary>
<keywords>Shavasana, Corpse Pose, 84 asanas, Lord Shiva, Gorakshanatha, Siddhasana, Padmasana, Simhasana, Bhadrasana, Hatha Yoga, fatigue removal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "First Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. The previous page introduced the four primary seated postures (Adept, Lotus, Lion, and Auspicious) and established that the Adept Pose (Siddhasana) is the most important of them all.</meta>

### Light on Hatha Yoga <note>original: "Hathayogapradipika"</note> ###

( 22 )

**Root Text: Press the heel firmly against the perineum <note>original: "yonisthanakam"</note>. Place the other foot above the genitals. Fix the chin firmly against the heart <note>referring to the chest/sternum</note>. Remaining steady with senses controlled and a steady gaze, look at the space between the eyebrows. This is called the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note>; it forces open the door to liberation <note>original: "moksha"</note>. || 35 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The Lotus Pose, the Lion Pose, and the Auspicious Pose—these four are considered the most excellent and highly praised. Even among these four, one should always remain in the comfortable and ease-giving **Adept Pose**. This indicates that the **Adept Pose** is the most superior even among the primary four. || 34 ||

Because it is the best of the four postures, he describes the **Adept Pose** first with the verse starting **"The perineum..."**. The "perineum" <note>original: "yonisthanakam"</note> refers to the region between the anus and the genitals. <gloss>The suffix "-ka" is added here in a self-referential sense.</gloss> Having placed the "base"—meaning the heel—of the left foot firmly against that perineum, one should then place the other foot—the right foot—firmly upon the upper part of the genitals. Fix the chin <note>original: "hanu"</note> very steadily near the heart. The "secret" <note>the traditional oral instruction</note> is to maintain a distance of four finger-widths between the chin and the heart. One who has "senses controlled" is one who has withdrawn the senses from worldly objects. With a steady gaze, one should look at the "space between the eyebrows"—the middle point. It is well-known that this pose "generates the breaking"—meaning the destruction—of the "door" which acts as an obstacle to liberation. This is the posture named the **Adept Pose**; it is called this because it is used by the Adepts <note>perfected yogis</note>, or because one "sits" <note>original: "aste"</note> in this manner. || 35 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

These four poses are famous for bringing ease. Among these four, the most ease-giving is the **Adept Pose**. It is the best of the four, so one should always practice it. || 34 ||

Since it is the most excellent of the four poses, the **Adept Pose** is described first with the verse starting **"The perineum..."**. The region between the anus and the genitals is the perineum. Fix the heel of the left foot against the perineum; similarly, fix the heel of the right foot on the upper part of the genitals. Fix the chin <note>the jaw</note> four finger-widths above the heart. Withdrawing the senses from their objects and with a single steady gaze, look at the middle of the eyebrows. This certainly removes the "door" <note>barrier</note> to liberation. This posture is called the name **Adept Pose**. || 35 ||

<summary>This page provides the technical instructions for Siddhasana (the Adept Pose), detailing the placement of the heels and the chin, the control of the senses, and the focus of the gaze. It emphasizes that this specific posture is the most effective for achieving spiritual liberation.</summary>

<keywords>Siddhasana, Adept Pose, perineum, meditation posture, liberation, moksha, Hatha Yoga, gaze, eyebrow center</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "First Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. ### First Instruction ###

( 23 )

->In Another Tradition ||<-

**Place the left heel <note>original: "gulpha," literally ankle, but referring to the heel in this context</note> above the genitals <note>original: "mendra"</note>, and then place the other heel upon that. This is the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note>. || 36 ||**

**The Great Ones call this the Adept Pose; others know it as the Thunderbolt Pose <note>original: "Vajrasana"</note>. Some call it the Liberated Pose <note>original: "Muktasana"</note>, and others call it the Hidden Pose <note>original: "Guptasana"</note>. || 37 ||**

**Just as moderate diet <note>original: "mitahara"</note> is the most important of the restraints <note>original: "yama"</note>, and non-violence <note>original: "ahimsa"</note> is the most important of the observances <note>original: "niyama"</note>, the Adepts know the Adept Pose to be the primary among all postures. || 38 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

Having described the Adept Pose according to the view of Matsyendranath, the author now speaks according to the view of others with the phrase: **"In another tradition."** He demonstrates this with the verse beginning **"Above the genitals."** Above the genitals—meaning the male organ—one should place the left heel. Similarly, place the "other heel"—the right heel—upon the first, rather than on the genitals themselves. One should remain in this position. The meaning is that this version of the Adept Pose is favored in other traditions and is essentially the same practice. || 36 ||

To clarify the naming conventions of the Great Adepts, he explains the different names for the same pose according to various opinions with the verse beginning **"This..."**. This previously mentioned pose is called the **Adept Pose** by some. Others "know it"—recognize it—by the name **Thunderbolt Pose**. Some call it the **Liberated Pose**, while others call it the **Hidden Pose**. Those who are experts in these postures explain the distinctions thus:
1. When the left heel is pressed against the perineum <note>original: "yoni"</note> and the right heel is placed above the genitals, it is **Siddhasana**.
2. When the left heel is pressed against the perineum and the right heel is placed above the genitals <note>The commentator notes that some traditions consider this identical to the first</note>, it is **Vajrasana**.
3. When both the right and left heels are joined together one above the other and pressed against the perineum, it is **Muktasana**.
4. When both heels are joined as before but placed above the genitals, it is **Guptasana**. || 37 ||

Now, with the seven verses beginning **"Just as among the restraints,"** he praises the Adept Pose. Among the restraints, moderate diet—

->|| Vernacular Translation <note>Early modern Hindi/Braj Bhasha</note> ||<-

Having stated the Adept Pose according to Matsyendra, the author now states the view of other traditions. **Above the genitals:** Having placed the left heel on the upper part of the genitals, and then placing the right heel over the left foot, one remains still. This is the Adept Pose according to other traditions. || 36 ||

**This:** Regarding the previously mentioned Adept Pose, some call it "Siddhasana," others know it by the name "Vajrasana," some call it the "Muktasana" name, and others call it "Guptasana." || 37 ||

Now he praises the Adept Pose through seven verses. **Just as among the restraints:** Among the restraints...

<meta>catchword: Among the restraints</meta>

<summary>This page describes an alternative method for performing Siddhasana (the Adept Pose) and explains that terms like Vajrasana, Muktasana, and Guptasana are often used for variations of this same posture. It concludes by asserting that Siddhasana is the most vital of all 84 yoga postures.</summary>

### First Instruction ###

( 24 )

### On the Light on Hatha Yoga ###

**Among the eighty-four postures, one should always practice the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note> alone. It purifies the impurities of the seventy-two thousand energy channels <note>original: "nadis"</note>. || 39 ||**

**One who meditates on the Self and observes a moderate diet <note>original: "mitahara"</note> for a period of twelve years, attains the state of completion <note>original: "nishpatti"</note> through the constant practice of the Adept Pose. || 40 ||**

**What use are many other postures when the Adept Pose is mastered, and the breath <note>original: "prananila"</note> is carefully restrained in Absolute Retention <note>original: "kevala kumbhaka," a state of spontaneous breath suspension</note>? || 41 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

Regarding the previous comparison to a necklace: "Moderate diet" will be described later in the text with the verse beginning "Unctuous and sweet food..." [Verse 1.58]. Regarding the observances <note>original: "niyamas"</note>, it is compared to non-violence <note>original: "ahimsa"</note>. The connection is that the Adepts <note>original: "Siddhas"</note> consider this one Adept Pose to be the primary one among all existing postures. || 38 ||

Regarding the verse **"Among the eighty-four"**: Among those postures numbering eighty-four, one should always—at all times—practice the Adept Pose. The reason for this constant practice is embedded in the description: it is the "purifier of the impurities" of the seventy-two thousand energy channels <note>original: "nadis," the pathways through which vital life force flows</note>. || 39 ||

Regarding the verse **"Meditating on the Self"**: One who meditates on the Self is a "Self-meditator." One who possesses a measured or limited diet is a "moderate eater." For as long as twelve years—this is an "uninterrupted duration" <note>original: an "avyayibhava" compound indicating the full extent of the time</note>. Through the constant practice of the Adept Pose for twelve years, the yogi—the practitioner of yoga—attains "completion," meaning the perfection of yoga <note>original: "yoga-siddhi"</note>. The meaning is that one can attain perfection through the practice of the Adept Pose alone, without the need for practicing other forms of yoga. || 40 ||

Regarding the verse **"What use are others"**: If the Adept Pose is mastered <note>original: "siddha," meaning perfected</note>, what is the use of many other postures? There is no need for them—

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

Just as moderate diet is key among the restraints, and non-violence is key among the observances, the yogi says the Adept Pose is the primary among all postures. || 38 ||

Regarding the verse **"Among the eighty-four"**: Out of eighty-four postures, this Adept Pose is "perfected." One should always practice it because it cleanses the impurities of the seventy-two thousand energy channels. || 39 ||

Regarding the verse **"Meditating on the Self"**: One who meditates on the soul is a "Self-meditator," and one who eats a measured amount of food is a "moderate eater." Being such a person, if one practices the Adept Pose constantly for twelve years, then the practitioner attains the perfection of yoga. Perfection is attained through the practice of this Adept Pose alone, even without practicing other yogic methods. || 40 ||

Regarding the verse **"What use are others"**: If the Adept Pose becomes mastered, then what is the use of many other postures?

### First Instruction ###

**The state of mind-beyond-thought <note>original: "unmani kala"</note> arises spontaneously and without effort. Similarly, when the Adept Pose <note>original: "Siddhasana"</note> is firmly mastered, the three locks <note>original: "bandhatraya"</note> occur naturally without exertion. || 42 ||**

**There is no posture like the Adept Pose; no breath retention like the Absolute Retention <note>original: "kevala kumbhaka"</note>; no seal like the Sky-Moving Seal <note>original: "khechari"</note>; and no mental dissolution <note>original: "laya"</note> like that produced by the Inner Sound <note>original: "nada"</note>. || 43 ||**

-> अथ पद्मासनं ॥ <-
-> Now, the Lotus Pose <note>original: "Padmasana"</note> <-

**Place the right foot upon the left thigh and the left foot upon the right thigh likewise. Crossing the arms behind the back, hold the toes firmly with the hands...**

### || Commentary ||

...meaning: when the vital breath <note>original: "prananila"</note> is carefully restrained in Absolute Retention <note>original: "kevala kumbhaka"</note>. || 41 ||

Regarding the verse **"arises"**: The state of mind-beyond-thought <note>original: "unmani"</note>—which is like a digit of the moon <note>original: "kala"</note> because of its delightful and illuminating nature—arises and manifests of its own accord without any strain. Regarding **"similarly"**: In the same manner described, once the Adept Pose is mastered and held firmly, the three locks—namely the Root Lock <note>original: "mula bandha"</note>, the Upward-Flying Lock <note>original: "uddiyana bandha"</note>, and the Throat Lock <note>original: "jalandhara bandha"</note>—arise spontaneously. This happens without the physical labor usually required, such as "pressing the perineum with the heel and firmly contracting the seat," which will be explained later in the section on the Root Lock. || 42 ||

Regarding the verse **"no posture"**: There is no posture equal to the Adept Pose. (This is the implied conclusion). There is no retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> that can be compared to Absolute Retention. There is no seal <note>original: "mudra"</note> equal to the Sky-Moving Seal <note>original: "khechari"</note>. There is no cause for mental dissolution <note>original: "laya"</note> equal to the Inner Sound <note>original: "nada"</note>. || 43 ||

He now begins to describe the Lotus Pose. Regarding **"now"**: He introduces the Lotus Pose. Regarding **"upon the left thigh"**: Place the right foot upon the left thigh. The "and" in the verse is a poetic filler. "Having placed" means positioning the foot so it faces upward...

### || Vernacular Translation ||

...If one becomes attentive and the vital breath is held in Absolute Retention without the need for forced inhalation or exhalation, then— || 41 ||

The fourth state of consciousness <note>original: "turya avastha"</note>, which grants bliss like a sliver of the moon, manifests itself without effort. Furthermore, as stated, if the Adept Pose is perfected, then the three locks—the Root Lock, the Upward-Flying Lock, and the Throat Lock—which will be described further on, will all manifest spontaneously without the practitioner having to labor over them. || 42 ||

Regarding **"no posture"**: There is no posture equal to the Adept Pose. There is no breath control <note>original: "pranayama"</note> equal to Absolute Retention. There is no seal equal to the Sky-Moving Seal. And there is no dissolution equal to the Inner Sound. That is to say, there is no better way to achieve mental absorption. || 43 ||

Now the Lotus Pose is described. Regarding **"upon the left thigh"**: Place the right foot upon the left thigh...

<meta>This page continues the description of the Lotus Pose (Padmasana), which began on the previous page. While the previous page described the crossing of the arms behind the back to grasp the toes, this page completes that instruction and introduces a second variation of the pose favored by the founding master Matsyendranath.</meta>

( 26 )

### In the Light on Hatha Yoga <note>original: "Hathayogapradipika"</note> ###

**...placing the [thumbs] at the heart, one should gaze at the tip of the nose. This is called the Lotus Pose <note>original: "Padmasana"</note>, which destroys the diseases of the self-restrained yogis. || 44 ||**

**Carefully placing the feet, turned upward, upon the thighs, one should then place the hands, also turned upward, in the space between the thighs. || 45 ||**

**One should fix the gaze on the tip of the nose and place the tongue at the root of the "royal teeth" <note>original: "rajadanta-mule"; referring to the base of the upper front teeth or the palate</note>.**

-> || Commentary || <-

Regarding the first variation: Having placed the left foot on the right thigh and the right foot on the left thigh, from the back—meaning from behind the torso—one follows the rule for the hands. This means one should firmly grasp the big toes of the feet with the hands. Specifically, by putting the right hand behind the back, one grasps the big toe of the right foot which is resting on the left thigh; by putting the left hand behind the back, one grasps the big toe of the left foot resting on the right thigh. One should place the chin <note>original: "chibuka"</note> at the heart—meaning near the heart. In this context, "at the heart" is a grammatical locative indicating proximity. The secret instruction is to place the chin four finger-widths away from the chest. One should look at the tip of the nose <note>original: "nasagram"</note>. This is called the **Lotus Pose**, a name given by the Perfected Ones <note>original: "Siddhas"</note>, because it brings about the destruction of diseases for practitioners. || 44 ||

Now the author describes the Lotus Pose favored by the great master Matsyendranath <note>original: "Matsyendranatha"</note> in the verse starting with **"turned upward"**. One should carefully place the feet upon the thighs so that the soles face upward and the backs of the feet are pressed against the thighs. Then, in the lap—the space between the thighs—one should place the hands facing upward. This means one should place the left hand with its back resting on the heels of the upturned feet, and then place the right hand on top of it, also facing upward. Then, the gaze— || 45 ||

Regarding **"at the tip of the nose"**: One should fix the gaze specifically on the tip of the nose with complete steadiness.

-> || Vernacular Translation || <-

Having placed the left foot on the right thigh, one should take the right hand behind the back and grasp the toe of the foot resting on the left thigh. Similarly, taking the left hand behind the back, one should grasp the toe of the foot resting on the right thigh. Then, placing the chin near the heart, one should look at the tip of the nose. The Perfected Ones have said that this Lotus Pose removes the diseases of yogis. || 44 ||

Now, the Lotus Pose approved by Matsyendranath is described. One should place the feet on the thighs so that the backs of the feet are pressed against them. Then, place both hands straight upon the heels: first the left hand, and over that, the right hand. After that, the gaze— || 45 ||

—should be kept steady on the tip of the nose. Then, the root of the "royal teeth" located in the right and left parts of the...

<meta>catchword: ...of the [palate/teeth]</meta>

### First Instruction ( 27 ) ###

**Main Text**

**Pressing the chin against the chest and slowly raising the breath... || 46 ||**

**This is called the Lotus Pose <note>original: "Padmasana"</note>, the destroyer of all diseases. It is difficult for just anyone to attain; it is achieved on earth only by the wise. || 47 ||**

**Having joined the hands together in the lap, firmly assuming the Lotus Pose, and pressing the chin tightly to the chest; meditating on *That* <note>the Supreme Reality or the Self</note> in the mind; repeatedly drawing the downward-moving breath <term>Apana: the energy responsible for elimination and downward movement</term> upward and exhaling the inhaled breath <term>Prana: the vital life-force</term>—a person attains incomparable awakening through the power of the Goddess Energy <note>original: "Shakti"; referring to Kundalini</note>. || 48 ||**

-> || Commentary || <-

Regarding the roots of the "royal teeth" <note>the upper front incisors</note> and the canines located on the left and right sides: one should support or steady the roots of these areas with the tongue. This **Tongue-Lock** <note>original: "jihva-bandha"</note> must be understood directly from the mouth of a Guru. The phrase "placing the chin on the chest" is understood as the remaining part of the instruction.

Slowly, very slowly, one should raise the breath. By this, the **Root Lock** <note>original: "mula-bandha"; a contraction of the pelvic floor</note> is described. The Root Lock must also be understood only from the mouth of a Guru. However, those who know the secrets of **Hatha Yoga** say that this practice is effectively accomplished by the Tongue-Lock alone. || 46 ||

Regarding the verse "This is...": The manner in which one sits in this way is called the **Lotus Pose**. This is the name given by the masters of asana. What is its nature? It specifically destroys all diseases. It is "difficult to attain," meaning it cannot be mastered by those lacking in spiritual fortune. It is "achieved by the wise," meaning those with steady minds, here on this earth. || 47 ||

To clarify that this is also the consensus of the great yogis, the author describes another specific action within the Lotus Pose.

Regarding the verse "Having joined...": "Joined hands" means the hands are placed together in the lap. "Firmly" means the Lotus Pose is made exceptionally steady and stable. One should place the chin near the chest as "tightly" as possible—though it should be understood from the yogic tradition that a distance of four finger-widths should be maintained. This means one should perform the **Chin Lock** <note>original: "Jalandhara-bandha"</note>. Then, one should meditate on the form of one's chosen deity or on the Absolute <note>Brahman</note>. As it is said: "Om Tat Sat <note>Om, That is the Truth</note> is the designation..."

-> || Vernacular Commentary || <-

At the root of the teeth, one should steady the tongue upward; this **Tongue-Lock** is worthy of being learned from the mouth of a Guru. Then, holding the chin at a distance of four finger-widths from the chest, one should slowly, slowly raise the breath. This is the **Root Lock**, which is also to be understood from the mouth of a Guru. || 46 ||

Regarding the verse "This is...": What is this Lotus Pose like? It destroys all diseases. It is difficult for the unfortunate to attain. On this earth, it is obtained by meritorious and wise men. || 47 ||

This is the consensus of the great yogis; therefore, further specific actions in the Lotus Pose are described. "Having joined": Having placed both hands together in the lap and having assumed an exceptionally stable Lotus Pose, the chin...

**Header: On the Light of Hatha Yoga**

# **Main Text: The Yogi established in the Lotus Pose, who holds the breath—which has been drawn in through the channels of the nostrils—is undoubtedly liberated. There is no doubt about this. || 49 ||**

# **Now, the Lion Pose**
### **One should place the two ankles under the scrotum, on either side of the perineum. ||**
### **Place the left ankle on the right side and the right ankle on the left side. || 50 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding the statement, "Threefold is the memory of the Absolute" <note>original: "brahmanas trividhah smrtah"</note>, as spoken by the Lord: One should meditate and reflect within the heart or mind. By performing the **Root Lock** <term>Mula-bandha: a contraction of the pelvic floor used to redirect energy</term> and driving the downward-moving breath <term>Apana-vayu</term> upward, one leads the vital life-force <term>Prana</term> through the central channel <term>Sushumna-marga</term>. 

The breath that has been held inside through inhalation <term>Puraka</term> is then directed downward. This means one should create a union between the upward-moving life-force and the downward-moving energy. Through the power of the Goddess <note>original: "Shakti"; referring to the foundational energy known as Kundalini</note>, a person attains incomparable awakening and peerless knowledge. 

When the life-force and the downward energy unite, the awakening of **Kundalini** <note>the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note> occurs. Upon this awakening, the life-force travels through the central channel to the **Opening of Brahman** <term>Brahmarandhra: the energy center at the crown of the head</term>. Once it reaches there, the mind becomes steady. Through this mental steadiness and disciplined focus <term>Samyama</term>, the direct realization of the Self occurs. This is the intended meaning. || 48 ||

Regarding the verse on the **Lotus Pose** <note>original: "Padmasana"</note>: The practitioner of yoga, established in this pose, takes the air inside through inhalation and leads it through the central channel to the head. The instruction implies that by steadying it there, the practitioner is liberated; there is no doubt regarding this. || 49 ||

Now the **Lion Pose** <note>original: "Simhasana"</note> is described: "The two ankles..." One should place or press the ankles into the lower region below the scrotum, on both sides of the **perineum** <note>original: "sivani"; the "seam" or midline of the pelvic floor</note>. The author now explains the specific method for placing the ankles.

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

One should place the hands near the heart and perform the **Chin Lock** <term>Jalandhara-bandha</term>. Then, meditating in the mind upon one's chosen deity or the Absolute, one draws the downward energy upward by performing the **Root Lock**. Through the central channel, the life-force is raised. By holding the inhaled breath inside and then directing it downward, one unites the upward and downward energies. 

By this union, a man attains incomparable awakening—knowledge that has no equal—through the influence of the Goddess Energy, or Kundalini. When these energies unite, the awakening of Kundalini occurs. || 48 ||

As soon as Kundalini is awakened, the life-force travels through the central channel to the **Opening of Brahman**. When the life-force reaches that crown center, the mind becomes still. Then, through disciplined meditation, the direct perception of the Soul occurs. This is the meaning. The yogi in the Lotus Pose takes the air drawn inside via inhalation, leads it through the central channel into the head, and steadies it there; such a person becomes liberated, of this there is no doubt. || 49 ||

Now the **Lion Pose** is described: "The two ankles..." Below the scrotum, on the right side of the perineum, the left [ankle]...

# **First Instruction**

**Main Text: Place the hands upon the knees, spreading the fingers wide. With an open mouth, one should gaze at the tip of the nose with a well-composed mind. || 51 ||**

**This is the Lion Pose, revered by the best of yogis. This excellent posture facilitates the union of the three locks <term>Bandha-tritaya: the three energetic seals—the Root Lock, Abdominal Lock, and Chin Lock</term>. || 52 ||**

->**Now, the Auspicious Pose**<-

**Place the two ankles under the scrotum on either side of the perineum <note>original: "sivani"; the anatomical midline or "seam" of the pelvic floor</note>. Place the left ankle on the left side and the right ankle on the right side. || 53 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding the "Right side": On the right side of the perineum, one should place the left ankle; on the left side of the perineum, one should place the right ankle. <note>The commentary here provides the specific "crossing" instruction for the previous verse 50, which completes the setup for the following movements.</note> || 50 ||

Regarding the "Hands": Having placed the hands upon the knees so that the palms are firmly placed against the knees; "spreading the fingers," meaning having fully extended the fingers of the hands. "With an open mouth," meaning the mouth has the tongue extended and protruding. "Well-composed," meaning with a one-pointed mind. "Gaze at the tip of the nose," where one looks at the end of the nostrils. || 51 ||

Regarding the "Lion Pose": This is the Lion Pose. What is it like? It is "revered by the best of yogis," meaning the greatest of practitioners. It is the "excellent" one among the postures presented. It "facilitates the union," meaning the close engagement, of the "three locks," starting with the **Root Lock** <term>Mula-bandha</term> and the others. || 52 ||

He now describes the **Auspicious Pose** <term>Bhadrasana</term>: "The two ankles..." Beneath the scrotum, on the "sides of the perineum," meaning on both sides of the perineal seam. Place the "ankles," the joints of the feet. He explains the method of placement: "The left ankle..." Place the left ankle on the left side of the perineum. "And so," this completes the verse. Place the "right ankle" on the right side of the perineum. || 53 ||

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

Place the [left] heel, and in the left side of the perineum, place the right heel. || 50 ||

Regarding the "Hands": Then, having placed both hands face down upon the knees and spreading the fingers, open the mouth wide and extend the tongue. Being of one-pointed mind, look at the tip of the nose. || 51 ||

Regarding the "Lion Pose": This Lion Pose—how is it? It is worshipped by those who are best among yogis. Among the best of postures, this Lion Pose makes the three [locks], such as the Root Lock and others, manifest. || 52 ||

Now, the Auspicious Pose is described: "The two ankles..." Below the scrotum, in the left side of the perineum, place the left heel, and in the right side of the perineum, place the right heel. || 53 ||

# **Hatha Yoga Pradipika**

**Main Text: Firmly grasping the sides of the feet with both hands, one should remain perfectly steady. This is the Auspicious Pose <term>Bhadrasana: "Bhadra" means auspicious or fortunate; this pose is used to steady the body for higher practices</term>, which destroys all diseases. || 54 ||**

**The accomplished yogis call this the Pose of Goraksha <note>original: "Gorakshasana"; named after the legendary sage Gorakshanatha</note>. Thus, when the master yogi <note>original: "yogindra"; king of yogis</note> is free from fatigue while maintained in these various postures... || 55 ||**

**...he should practice the purification of the energy channels <term>Nadika-shuddhi: the clearing of the subtle pathways, such as the Ida and Pingala, to allow life-force to flow</term> and the breath-control activities <note>original: "pavana-kriya"</note> such as the seals <term>Mudras: physical gestures or internal locks used to redirect energy</term>. Postures, diverse breath retentions <term>Kumbhaka: the intentional holding of the breath to still the mind</term>, and the techniques known as Mudras [should then be practiced]. || 56 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding the "Sides of the feet": One should firmly grasp the two feet that have been brought near the sides with both hands or arms. This means grasping them with the fingers interlocked and the soles of the feet pressed toward the abdomen. This becomes the Auspicious Pose. What is its quality? It is specifically the "destroyer of all diseases." || 54 ||

Regarding "Goraksha": The "accomplished yogis"—those who are both perfected <note>Siddhas</note> and practitioners—call this Auspicious Pose the "Goraksha Pose." They call it the Goraksha Pose because it was frequently practiced by the sage Goraksha. The postures have now been described. Now the author explains what must be done within those practices. "Thus": In the various types of "posture-bindings" <note>asana-bandha</note> already mentioned, the practitioner is "free from fatigue"—meaning one who no longer feels exhaustion while holding these postures. A "master yogi" is a lord among practitioners. || 55 ||

Regarding "Should practice": One should practice the "purification of the channels"—the cleansing of the *nadis*. This is performed in the manner to be described later: "If one should regularly drink the breath through the left nostril <note>original: "ida"</note>..." One should practice the "breath-control activities" <note>pranayama</note> which consist of the "seals" <note>mudras</note> and the like, as well as techniques such as the Sun-Piercing breath <note>original: "suryabheda"</note>. Now, the author describes the sequence of Hatha practice: "Postures" (as previously defined), and "diverse breath retentions" such as the Sun-Piercing and Ujjayi breaths which will be described later. The techniques known as "Mudras"—such as the Great Seal <note>original: "mahamudra"</note>—are exceptionally helpful in achieving success in Hatha Yoga. And the word "likewise" <note>original: "tatha"</note> is used here in the sense of "and." || 56 ||

->॥ Vernacular Paraphrase ॥<-

Regarding the "Sides of the feet": Furthermore, having brought the feet near the sides, one should bind them with both arms. This is the Auspicious Pose. How is it? it specifically destroys all diseases. The binding of this Auspicious Pose is singular; there is certainly no difference between this and the other [version]. || 54 ||

Regarding "Goraksha": Accomplished yogis call this Auspicious Pose the Goraksha Pose. Sage Gorakshanatha practiced the Auspicious Pose extensively, and therefore it is famously known by his name. Those who are master yogis—being free from fatigue in these mentioned types of posture-bindings... || 55 ||

Regarding "Should practice": One should practice that activity which consists of breath control <note>pranayama</note>, which begins with the seals and includes the Piercing of the Sun and Moon, through which the purification of the channels <note>nadis</note> occurs. Furthermore, diverse and wonderful postures, breath retentions, and the performance of seals—these three are the facilitators for achieving success in Hatha Yoga. || 56 ||

<vocab>Bhadrasana, Gorakshasana, Nadi Shuddhi, Kumbhaka, Mudra, Hatha Yoga, Yogindra, Pranayama</vocab>

It emphasizes that mastery of Asana is the prerequisite for the more advanced stages of Hatha Yoga.</summary>

### **First Lesson**

**Main Text: Now follows the sequential practice of internal sound meditation <term>Nadanusandhana: "Nada" is the internal mystic sound; "anusandhana" is the concentrated exploration or meditation upon it</term> in Hatha Yoga. A practitioner who is celibate <note>original: "brahmachari"</note>, maintains a moderate diet <term>Mitahara: from "mita" (measured) and "ahara" (food); a regulated diet</term>, is a renunciant, and is devoted to yoga, will become an adept <note>original: "siddha"</note> after one year; regarding this, there should be no doubt. || 57 ||**

**A moderate diet is defined as that which is pleasant, sweet, leaves one-fourth of the stomach empty, and is eaten for the pleasure of Shiva <note>In this context, Shiva represents the supreme consciousness or the internal digestive fire</note>. || 58 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding "Now" <note>original: "Atha"</note>: This signifies that after the performance of the three previously mentioned practices [Postures, Breath-retention, and Seals], comes the sequential order of practicing internal sound meditation—the contemplation of the unstruck sound <note>original: "Anahata-dhvani"</note>—within Hatha Yoga. 

The text then describes the limit for achieving success in Hatha: "A practitioner who is celibate," meaning one who practices sexual restraint; "maintains a moderate diet," as will be described hereafter; "is a renunciant," meaning one who is either charitable by nature or has abandoned worldly objects; and "is devoted to yoga," meaning one who is intent on the practice of yoga. Such a person becomes "an adept"—one who has perfected Hatha—after the duration of one year. Regarding the meaning stated here, no "doubt" born of uncertainty should be entertained. This means the result is absolutely certain. || 57 ||

Because the term "moderate diet" <note>original: "mitahari"</note> was used in the previous verse, the text now addresses the question of what kind of moderate diet is required for yogis: "Pleasant," meaning very rich or smooth <note>original: "susnigdha"; usually implying food cooked with ghee</note>, and also "sweet." Such food should be "devoid of a fourth part," meaning one-fourth of the stomach remains empty. 

As stated by the experts: "Two parts should be filled with food, one part with water, and the fourth part should be left empty for the movement of air." 

"Shiva" refers to either the individual soul <note>Jiva</note> or the Supreme Lord <note>Ishvara</note>, according to the saying: "The eater is the Great Lord <note>original: Mahesvara</note>." That which is eaten for His "pleasure"—for the sake of complete satisfaction—is called a moderate diet. || 58 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

"Now": After performing these three—Postures <note>Asana</note>, Breath-retention <note>Kumbhaka</note>, and the performance of Seals <note>Mudrakarana</note>—one should practice the meditation on internal sound. This is the sequential order of Hatha Yoga practice. 

Now the duration for the perfection of Hatha is stated: if one remains in celibacy, eats a measured amount of food, is a renunciant—being of a charitable nature and abandoning worldly desires—and remains devoted to the practice of the limbs of yoga, then after one year, perfection in Hatha occurs. One should not have any doubt or uncertainty regarding this stated meaning; it is a certainty. || 57 ||

"Pleasant": What should the moderate diet of yogis be like? The food should be very rich and sweet. It should be "devoid of a fourth part," meaning one should fill the stomach with two parts food, one part water, and leave one part empty for the movement of air. For the sake of the pleasure of Shiva—meaning either the individual soul or the Lord—the food taken in this manner is called a "moderate diet" <note>original: "mitahari"</note>. || 58 ||

It introduces the practice of Nadanusandhana (meditation on internal sound) as the next stage of training.</summary>
