<meta>This page continues the specific dietary regulations for a practitioner of Hatha Yoga. While the previous page defined "moderate diet" (Mitahara) in positive terms, this page lists the specific foods and habits that are considered "unsuitable" (apathya) and must be avoided to ensure success in yoga practice.</meta>

Page 32
### **Header: In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika**

### **Root Text: Bitter, sour, pungent, salty, hot, green leafy vegetables,**
### **fermented gruel, oil, sesame, mustard, wine, and fish;**
### **The flesh of animals such as goats, yogurt, buttermilk, horse-gram, jujube,**
### **oil-cake, asafoetida, and garlic are said to be unsuitable. || 59 ||**
### **One should know that food which is reheated, dry,**
### **excessively salty, acidic, or contains an abundance of stale vegetables must be avoided. || 60 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

Now, the author describes what is unsuitable <term>Apathya: literally "not of the path"; refers to foods or habits that deviate from the yogic lifestyle</term> for yogis in two verses. Regarding the word "Bitter" <note>original: "katu"</note>: this refers to items like bitter gourd and the like. "Sour" <note>original: "amla"</note> refers to tamarind fruit and so forth. "Pungent" <note>original: "tikshna"</note> refers to black pepper and similar spices. "Salty" <note>original: "lavana"</note> is well-known. "Hot" <note>original: "ushna"</note> refers to substances like jaggery. "Green leafy vegetables" <note>original: "harita-shakam"</note> refers specifically to leafy greens. "Fermented gruel" <note>original: "sauvira"</note> is a type of sour rice water <note>original: "kanjika"</note>. "Oil" refers to fats derived from sesame, mustard, and the like. "Sesame" and "Mustard" are well-known. "Wine" <note>original: "madya"</note> is liquor. "Fish" is also mentioned. These items are listed together as a group to be avoided. 

"Flesh of animals such as goats" includes the meat of goats, wild boars, and similar creatures. "Yogurt" <note>original: "dadhi"</note> is a specific transformation of milk. "Buttermilk" <note>original: "takra"</note> is yogurt with the fat removed. "Horse-gram" <note>original: "kulattha"</note> is a specific type of legume. "Jujube" <note>original: "kola"</note> is the fruit of the jujube tree; as the *Amarakosha* dictionary states: "Karkandhu, Badari, and Koli" are synonyms for the jujube. "Oil-cake" <note>original: "pinyaka"</note> is the residue left after pressing sesame seeds. "Asafoetida" <note>original: "hingu"</note> is also known as *ramatha*. Then there is "Garlic." 

By the word "etcetera" <note>original: "adya"</note>, one should also include onions, carrots, intoxicating substances, and food made from black gram <note>original: "masha"</note>. These are "unsuitable" or harmful—specifically for "yogis," as that word is implied here. The verse concludes that the great teachers "say" this is the rule. || 59 ||

Regarding "Food" <note>original: "bhojanam"</note>: This refers to food such as soup, rice, or bread that has been "reheated"—meaning it was cooked once, allowed to cool, and then put back over the fire. "Dry" <note>original: "ruksham"</note> means food lacking in clarified butter <note>original: "ghee"</note> or other fats. "Excessively salty" refers to a dish where salt is the predominant flavor, or perhaps food where the saltiness is overpowering. In common language, this also refers to food cooked with alkaline salts <note>original: "yavakshara"</note>. Because salt is to be avoided in all forms, this is the final conclusion.

->|| Vernacular Commentary ||<-

Now, the items unsuitable for a yogi are described in two verses. "Bitter" includes things like neem and other bitter substances. "Sour" includes tamarind and the like. "Pungent" includes black pepper. "Salty," "Hot," "Extremely Hot," and "Jaggery" are mentioned. "Green leafy vegetables," "fermented gruel," "oil," "sesame," "mustard," "wine," "liquor," and "fish" are called unsuitable. 

The meat of animals starting with goats, yogurt, milk, buttermilk, horse-gram, jujube fruit, oil-cake, and garlic—and by extension, onions, carrots, and black gram—all of these are harmful and unsuitable for yogis. || 59 ||

Regarding food: food that was cooked earlier but became cold and was then reheated by fire should be known as harmful. Also, "dry" food which lacks clarified butter, and food with too much salt should be avoided. Furthermore, excessive eating, excessive sleep, excessive talking, stale food, and food that has become tainted or foul-smelling—all these should be known as harmful to the yogi. || 60 ||

<summary>This page details the dietary prohibitions for Hatha Yoga practitioners, listing specific foods like garlic, meat, and fermented items, as well as habits like reheating food or eating without healthy fats (ghee).</summary>
<keywords>prohibited foods, Apathya, diet, garlic, meat, reheated food, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, yogic lifestyle</keywords>

<page-num>33</page-num>
# First Chapter

**Root Text: In the beginning, one should avoid fire, women, and traveling long distances. || 61 ||**

**As it is stated in the words of Goraksha <note>Goraksha or Gorakhnath was a legendary 11th-century yogi and a founding figure of the Hatha Yoga tradition.</note>:**
> **"One should avoid the company of wicked people, fire, women, and traveling;**
> **likewise, morning baths, fasting, and other rituals that cause bodily distress."**

->|| Commentary ||<-

This is correct. Likewise, Dattatreya <note>A foundational figure in Yoga, often considered an incarnation of the Trinity (Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva).</note> states: "Now I shall describe the things to be avoided, which cause obstacles to Yoga: salt, mustard, sour, fierce, pungent, and dry foods. Excessive eating, excessive sleep, and excessive talking must be abandoned." Also in the *Skanda Purana*: "One should abandon pungent, sour, and salty foods, and should always subsist on milk." The phrase "mixed with sour" <note>original: "amlyuktam"</note> means combined with acidic substances. If food mixed with sour substances is to be avoided, how much more so is pure sour liquid? 

Regarding the third line, some read "meat" <note>original: "palalam"</note> or "sesame-cake" <note>original: "tilapindam"</note>. In that version, the meaning is: meat, sesame-cake, dregs of oil <note>original: "pinyākam"</note>, bad food <note>original: "kadashanam"</note> such as coarse grains like millet or wild grass-seeds, and any vegetables other than those specifically permitted. "Inflammatory" <note>original: "vidāhi"</note> refers to what is commonly known as *mirichi* <note>original: "mirici"</note> or *mircha* in the Hindustani language <note>The chili pepper, which was a later addition to the Indian diet; the commentator is identifying "vidahi" (burning/inflammatory) with the chili.</note>. These items, beginning with bad food, are listed as a compound of things to be abandoned. If the reading is "tainted" <note>original: "dushtam"</note>, it refers to food that is putrid or stale. This should be understood as harmful. || 60 ||

Having thus stated what is always forbidden for yogis, the author now states what is specifically forbidden during the period of initial practice in half a verse: **"Fire..."** etc.

**Fire** <note>original: "vanni," referring to sitting near a fire for warmth</note>, **women** <note>referring to sexual intercourse</note>, and **paths** <note>referring to long-distance travel or pilgrimages</note>—the indulgence in these should be avoided in the beginning, during the period of practice. However, once the practice is perfected <note>original: "siddhe'bhyāse"</note>, occasionally warming oneself by the fire in winter, a householder visiting his wife during her fertile period, or traveling for pilgrimage is not forbidden; this is suggested by the word "etc." 

To provide authority for this, the author introduces the words of Goraksha: **"One should avoid..."** "The vicinity of wicked people" means living near bad company. Some versions read "affection for wicked people." "Fire, women, and traveling" have already been explained. "Morning baths, fasting, etc." refers to habits like eating only fruit or skipping meals. These are avoided because for a beginner, morning baths can cause "cold-disorders" <note>imbalances in the Kapha or Vata humors</note> and fasting causes an increase in "bile" <note>original: "pitta"</note>. 

"Rituals that cause bodily distress" <term>Kaya-klesha-vidhi: practices that involve physical suffering or extreme asceticism</term> refers to activities like performing an excessive number of Sun Salutations <note>original: "surya-namaskara"</note> or carrying heavy loads. The word "likewise" functions as a conjunction. Here, the verb "should avoid" applies to each item listed. || 61 ||

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

**"Fire..."** A yogi, in the initial period of practice, should first avoid warming himself by the fire, sexual contact, and traveling on paths such as pilgrimages. When the practice is perfected, then occasionally in the winter, the use of fire—or if he is a householder, approaching his own wife during her fertile season, or walking on a path for pilgrimage—is not forbidden. The proof for this is in the words of Goraksha: **"One should avoid..."** Sitting near a wicked person or having affection for a wicked person, as well as the company of fire, sexual contact, and traveling on paths, and morning bathing...

<sig>5</sig>

<vocab>First Instruction, Goraksha's words, fire-women-travel-service, Commentary, Vernacular, Dattatreya, Skanda Purana, bodily distress, practice period</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines specific lifestyle prohibitions for novice yogis, including avoiding heat, sexual activity, and long travel, while explaining that certain religious austerities like fasting and excessive Sun Salutations can actually hinder physical progress.</summary>

# In the Light of Hatha Yoga

**Root Text: Wheat, rice, barley, and sixty-day rice <note>original: "shashtika" - a variety of rice that ripens in exactly sixty days, considered highly pure</note> are excellent grains. Milk, ghee, brown sugar, butter, sugar candy, and honey; dried ginger, pointed gourd <note>original: "patola"</note>, and the "five greens," mung beans, and pure water are wholesome for the king of yogis. || 62 ||**

**The yogi should consume food that is nourishing, sweet, oily <note>original: "snigdha" - usually meaning cooked with ghee or fats to prevent dryness</note>, derived from the cow, supportive of the bodily tissues <note>the "dhatus" or the seven fundamental elements of the body in Ayurveda</note>, pleasing to the mind, and suitable. || 63 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

Now the wholesome diet for a yogi is described. Regarding the grains: wheat, rice, barley, and the sixty-day rice (which, as the name suggests, is a special rice harvested in sixty days) are considered "excellent" or "pure" grains, unlike inferior grains like millet or wild rice. 

Regarding the dairy and sweets: "Milk" refers to cow's milk; "ghee" is clarified butter; "brown sugar" <note>original: "khanda"</note> is unrefined sugar; "butter" is the essence of churned curd; and "sugar candy" <note>original: "sita"</note> refers to the white refined sugar known as *mishri* in the Hindustani language. "Honey" refers to the product of bees.

Regarding the vegetables: "Dried ginger" <note>original: "shunthi"</note> is well-known. "Pointed gourd" is called *parvar* in the common tongue. The "five greens" <note>original: "pancha-shaka"</note> refers to a specific group of five vegetables. As stated in the medical texts: "While most greens are harmful to the eyes, these five are beneficial: *Jivanti*, *Vastumulya* <note>lamb's quarters</note>, *Akshi*, *Meghanada*, and *Punarnava* <note>hogweed</note>." 

"Mung beans" <note>original: "mudga"</note> are a type of pulse; the word "and others" implies the inclusion of pigeon peas <note>original: "adhaki"</note>. "Pure water" <note>original: "divyam udakam"</note> means water that is free of defects. These are "wholesome" or beneficial for the "king of those who practice restraint" <note>the Yogi</note>. || 62 ||

Next, the rules for the yogi's meal are described. "Nourishing" means food like cooked rice that provides strength to the body...

->|| Vernacular Paraphrase ||<-

Additionally, both fasting and surviving solely on fruit are discouraged for the beginner. For one just starting the practice, bathing in the early morning causes "cold disorders" <note>excess phlegm or chills</note>, and fasting causes "bile disorders" <note>excess heat or acidity</note>. One should also avoid rituals that cause bodily distress, such as excessive Sun Salutations or lifting heavy weights. || 61 ||

Now the wholesome items for a yogi are stated: wheat, rice, barley, sixty-day rice, pure grains, millet, wild rice, milk, curd, ghee, brown sugar, butter, sugar candy, honey, dried ginger, pointed gourd, jackfruit, elephant-foot yam, various tubers, sweet potato, leafy greens like amaranth, mung beans, and pure fruits should be taken. Pure, flawless water is also wholesome and beneficial for the king of yogis. || 62 ||

Now the rule for the yogi’s meal is stated. "Nourishing" <note>original: "pushtam"</note> means food like cooked rice prepared with sugar. Ghee and milk should ideally be from a cow; if unavailable, buffalo milk may be taken. The food must support the bodily tissues...

<summary>This page defines the ideal "Yogic Diet," emphasizing pure grains like wheat and rice, dairy products, specific "eye-friendly" greens, and nourishing, sweet foods that support the body's tissues during practice.</summary>
<keywords>Yogic diet, Pathya, Sattvic food, Ayurveda, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Ghee, Cow's milk, Five Greens, Grains</keywords>

It asserts that neither age, physical weakness, nor mere scholarly study are barriers or substitutes for dedicated practice.</meta>

# First Chapter

**Root Text: Whether young, old, or extremely old; even if diseased or weak—one who practices tirelessly attains success in all forms of yoga. || 64 ||**

**Success comes to the one who is engaged in practice <note>original: "kriya-yuktasya" — literally, 'one joined to action'</note>. How could it come to the inactive? Success in yoga is not achieved by the mere reading of scriptures. || 65 ||**

---

->|| Commentary ||<-

Food should be very sweet, containing sugar and similar ingredients, and "oily" <note>original: "snigdham" — prepared with fats or ghee to balance the body's internal heat</note>, meaning it is served with ghee <note>clarified butter</note> and derived from the cow. If cow's milk and ghee are unavailable, buffalo products may be taken. The yogi should eat foods that nourish the bodily tissues <note>the seven "dhatus" or structural elements of the body in Ayurvedic medicine</note>, such as *laddus* <note>sweet flour balls</note> and *pupa* <note>fried cakes</note>, or whatever is pleasing to their own mind. 

Does "pleasing to the mind" mean one can eat forbidden things? The text says no; it must be "suitable," meaning only those foods prescribed by the scriptures. The meaning is that a yogi should eat food characterized by the qualities mentioned before, and should not try to survive solely on parched grain flour <note>original: "saktu"</note> or roasted rice. || 63 ||

The author now states that the yoga practitioner is not restricted by age or health: **"Whether young..."** This refers to a youth, one who has reached old age, or even one who has reached extreme old age. Through "practice"—the repeated performance of *asanas* <note>physical postures</note> and *kumbhakas* <note>breath-retention techniques</note>—one attains "success," which is the state of meditative absorption <note>original: "samadhi"</note> and its resulting fruits. 

The author further specifies the method of practice: **"In all forms of yoga..."** This means being "tireless" or without laziness in all the limbs <note>original: "angas"</note> of yoga. Success is attained through this diligent practice. Just as the word "life" is often used to describe the means of living (like agriculture or trade), here the word "Yoga" is used to describe the "limbs of yoga" which are the direct or indirect means of achieving that state. || 64 ||

To emphasize that success comes only from practice, the author provides two points: **"Success comes to the one engaged in practice..."** "Action" here refers to the actual performance of the limbs of yoga. Success—the perfection of yoga—belongs to the person who does this. How could it belong to the inactive, the one who does not perform these limbs? It is impossible. Could one attain yoga-success simply by studying the yoga scriptures? No. || 65 ||

---

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

A yogi should eat foods like *laddu* and *pua* <note>traditional sweets</note> that are pleasing to the mind. If a thing is unsuitable, even if the mind desires it, one should not eat it. A yogi should never live solely on parched flour or survive only on grain. || 63 ||

**"Whether young..."** Whether one is a youth, old, or very old, by practicing postures and breath-retention, one attains success and meditative absorption. If one is free from laziness in all aspects of yoga, success comes through practice alone. || 64 ||

**"Success comes to the one engaged in practice..."** One who performs the actions and the limbs of yoga attains success. But for one who is devoid of action, how can there be success? It cannot be. Furthermore, success in yoga cannot be achieved by the mere reading of yoga scriptures. This is the meaning. || 65 ||

<summary>These verses emphasize that Hatha Yoga is a practical discipline accessible to everyone regardless of age or health, provided they are diligent. It clarifies that spiritual success comes from active practice (kriya) and a nourishing diet, rather than through theoretical study or extreme asceticism.</summary>

<keywords>accessibility of yoga, success in yoga, importance of practice, yogic diet, age and yoga, samadhi, asana, kumbhaka, scriptures vs practice</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the end of the previous page, the text concludes the discussion on verse 65, emphasizing that theoretical knowledge of the scriptures is insufficient for the attainment of yoga without direct experience.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

<header>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

<term>Root Text</term> Wearing the [yogi's] dress is not the cause of success, nor is talking about it.
Practice <note>original: "kriyā" — refers specifically to the active performance of yogic techniques</note> alone is the cause of success; this is the truth, without a doubt. || 66 ||
Postures, various breath-retentions <note>original: "kumbhaka" — the holding of the breath which "fills" the body like a pot</note>, and divine mudras <note>original: "karaṇāni" — refers here to the "seals" or physical locks used to direct energy</note>;
All these in the practice of Hatha Yoga serve as a means until the fruit of Raja Yoga <note>the "Royal Yoga," referring to the state of deep meditation or Samadhi</note> is achieved. || 67 ||

This concludes the first instruction, titled "Description of the Method of Postures," in the *Hatha Yoga Pradipika* composed by the great yogi Svatmarama, of the lineage of the blessed Sahajananda Chintamani. || 1 ||

->|| Commentary ||<-

The author asks whether success can come without practice: No. **"Not,"** he says. By the mere reading of the science—the science of Yoga—success in yoga is not produced; it simply does not happen. || 65 ||

**"Not"**: The wearing of dress, such as saffron-colored robes <note>original: "kāṣāya-vastra" — the traditional orange garments of a renunciant</note>, is not the cause of success in yoga. Nor is talking about that yoga a cause. What then is the cause of success? He says: **"Practice alone."** || 66 ||

He now states the limit for performing the limbs of yoga: **"Postures."** Postures <note>asanas</note>, various types of breath-retentions such as the "sun-piercing" breath <note>Suryabhedana</note> and others, and divine or excellent practices like the Great Seal <note>Mahamudra</note> are the primary causes and aids for attaining success in Hatha. In the practice of Hatha, all these—postures, breath-retentions, and seals—are to be performed until the fruit of Raja Yoga is reached. Raja Yoga is the ultimate result; until that point is reached, these practices should be performed—this is the implied meaning. || 67 ||

This concludes the first instruction in the commentary titled *Jyotsna* <note>meaning "The Moonlight"</note> on the *Hatha Pradipika*, composed by Brahmananda. || 1 ||

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

**"Not"**: Putting on specific clothes to create a "look" or outward appearance is not the cause of success in yoga. Nor is speaking the stories of yoga the cause of success. The only cause of success is the performance of the practices <note>original: "kriyā"</note>. This is the truth; there is no doubt in this. || 66 ||

**"Postures"**: The various and diverse postures, the breath-retentions, and the excellent Great Seals and similar techniques are the causes for progress in Hatha-success. In the practice of Hatha, these postures, breath-retentions, and seals should be performed until the fruit of Raja Yoga is obtained. || 67 ||

This concludes the first instruction in the vernacular commentary on the *Hatha Deepika* composed by the illustrious astrologer Shridhara. || 1 ||

<summary>This page concludes the first chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika by asserting that spiritual success depends entirely on dedicated practice rather than outward appearance or academic study. It defines the goal of Hatha Yoga's physical techniques as the attainment of the meditative state known as Raja Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Svatmarama, Practice (Kriya), Raja Yoga, Asana (Postures), Kumbhaka (Breath retention), Mudra (Seals), Brahmananda, Shridhara, Success (Siddhi)</keywords>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>Second Instruction</header>

**Root Text:** Now, when the posture is firm, the yogi—having mastered the senses and consuming wholesome, moderate food—should practice breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> according to the path instructed by the guru. || 1 ||
When the breath <note>original: "vayu" — literally "wind," referring here to the vital air or life-force</note> moves, the mind <note>original: "citta" — the field of consciousness and thought</note> moves; when it is still, the mind becomes still. The yogi attains steadiness; therefore, one should restrain the breath. || 2 ||
As long as the breath remains in the body, it is called life. Its departure is death; therefore, one should restrain the breath. || 3 ||

->**|| Commentary ||**<-

**Now,** following the instruction on postures, the author begins to describe breath control. The word **"Now"** <note>original: "atha"</note> is used to signal an auspicious beginning. When the posture is firm, the yogi—who has conquered the senses and whose food is both wholesome (suitable for health) and moderate (measured), as characterized in the previous instruction—is a "wholesome and moderate eater." Such a person should thoroughly practice the breath controls that are about to be described, according to the path or method of practice taught by the teacher, utilizing enthusiasm, courage, and perseverance. The term "firm" means steady and without tremors, or it may refer to specific poses like the Adept's Pose <note>Siddhasana</note> and others. || 1 ||

As the great saying goes: "Even a fool does not act without a purpose." Because no activity begins without a goal, he states the purpose of breath control: **"When the breath moves..."** When the breath is in motion, the mind is also in motion. "When the breath is still, the mind becomes still"—this connection is also applied here. When the breath and the mind are both still, the yogi attains "steadiness," which is to say, a steady and long life. Or, he attains divine lordship. Therefore, because of this result, one should restrain or "pot" <note>original: "kumbhayet" — to hold the breath like water in a jar</note> the breath. || 2 ||

**"As long as..."**: For as long a time as the breath remains in the body, for that long a time...

->**|| Vernacular Translation ||**<-

Now, after the instruction on posture, the author begins to speak of breath control. **"Now..."** For that yogi whose posture has become firm, who has conquered his senses, and who—as previously described—is one who eats food that is wholesome, beneficial, and in the proper measure, that yogi should practice the exercise of breath control according to the path taught by the teacher. || 1 ||

Now he states the purpose of breath control: **"When the breath moves..."** If the breath is restless, the mind is also restless; if the breath is still, the mind also becomes still. If both the breath and the mind are still, the yogi becomes steady and long-lived, or attains a divine state. Therefore, one should restrain the breath, which is the life-force. || 2 ||

**"As long as..."**: Life exists in the body only as long as the breath remains. The de- <meta>Catchword: de- (departure)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the second chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, establishing the vital link between breath and mind and explaining that breath control leads to longevity and mental stillness.</summary>
<keywords>pranayama, breath control, mind and breath, moderate diet, longevity, Vayu, Citta, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

**Root Text:**
When the channels <term>nādīs</term> are filled with impurities, the wind <note>original: "māruta" — the vital life force or breath</note> does not enter the middle path. How then can the state of mindlessness <term>unmanī-bhāva</term> occur? How can the goal be accomplished? || 4 ||
Only when the entire network of channels <note>original: "nāḍī-cakra"</note>—previously filled with impurities—attains purity, does the yogi become capable of restraining the breath <note>original: "prāṇa-saṁgrahaṇa"</note>. || 5 ||
Therefore, one should practice breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> daily with a pure <note>original: "sāttvika" — clear, balanced, and harmonious</note> intellect.

---

->**|| Commentary ||**<-

...is called "life" by the people. Because the union of the body and the breath is the very essence of life. The departure of that breath—its separation from the body—is called "death." Therefore, for that reason, one should restrain the wind <note>the breath</note>. || 3 ||

He explains how the purification of impurities is the cause of success in Hatha Yoga by showing the negative consequences of its absence: **"When [the channels] are filled with impurities..."** When the channels <term>nādīs</term> are filled with and pervaded by impurities <note>original: "mala" — metabolic or energetic waste</note>, the wind (the life force) cannot travel through the middle path—that is, it cannot flow through the central channel <note>original: "suṣumnā"</note>. Rather, it only flows through the middle when the impurities have been cleansed. How could the "state of mindlessness" <note>original: "unmanī-bhāva" — a high meditative state where the mind dissolves into pure consciousness</note> then occur? The meaning is: it cannot happen at all. How could the accomplishment or perfection of the "work"—which is absolute liberation <term>kaivalya</term>—be achieved? It cannot be achieved by any means. || 4 ||

He states the cause of success in Hatha through the positive connection of purification: **"It attains purity..."** When—at which time—the entire collection or network of channels <term>nāḍī-cakra</term>, which was pervaded by impurities, reaches the state of being free from waste, only then—at that very moment—does the yogi (the practitioner of yoga) become capable and empowered to hold the breath. || 5 ||

In response to the expectation or question "How does purification of the channels occur?", he introduces the breath control that performs this cleansing. **"Breath control..."** Because one is not capable of restraining the life force without the purification of the channels, for that reason—[driven by] devotion to the Lord, enthusiasm, and...

---

->**|| Vernacular Version ||**<-

...Yoga happens. When the breath leaves, it is called death; therefore, one should practice the retention of the wind <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note>. || 3 ||
**"When the channels are filled..."** When the channels are pervaded by impurities, the breath cannot move through the central channel <note>original: "suṣumnā"</note>. Then how can one attain the "state of mindlessness," meaning the "fourth state" <term>turīya</term>? And how can the perfection of liberation <note>original: "mokṣa"</note> be accomplished? It cannot happen without the purification of impurities. || 4 ||
**"It attains purity..."** At the time when the entire network of channels becomes free from impurities, the yoga practitioner becomes capable of holding the life-breath. || 5 ||
When the question "How is purification achieved?" arises, he describes the breath control that cleanses impurities. **"Breath control..."** Without the purification of impurities, one is not capable of holding the breath; therefore, in meditation upon God...

<summary>This page explains that the life force (prana) cannot enter the central channel (Sushumna) if the energetic channels (nadis) are blocked by impurities. It establishes that purification is a mandatory prerequisite for successful breath control and the higher states of meditative absorption.</summary>
<keywords>Nadis, Sushumna, Unmani-bhava, Prana, Pranayama, impurities, Kaivalya, breath retention</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>Continuing from the Second Instruction, where the text established that the central channel (suṣumnā) must be cleared of impurities (mala) for the breath to enter the middle path and for the mind to reach a state of stillness.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>The Second Instruction</header>

**Root: So that the impurities residing in the central channel <term>suṣumnā-nāḍī</term> may attain purification. || 6 ||**
**Seated in the bound lotus posture <term>baddha-padmāsana</term>, the yogi should fill the breath through the moon <note>the left nostril, or iḍā</note>. ||**
**Having held it according to his capacity, he should exhale again through the sun <note>the right nostril, or piṅgalā</note>. || 7 ||**
**Drawing the breath through the sun, he should slowly fill the belly. ||**
**Having performed retention <term>kumbhaka</term> according to the rules, he should exhale again through the moon. || 8 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

One should practice breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> daily with a pure <term>sāttvika</term> intellect <note>original: "dhiyā buddhyā"</note>—an intellect characterized by clarity and grace—which overcomes the qualities of passion <term>rajas</term> and dullness <term>tamas</term>, such as distraction and laziness, through efforts like enthusiasm. This should be done in such a way <note>original: "yathā"</note> that the impurities <term>mala</term> located in the central channel <term>suṣumnā-nāḍī</term> attain purification, meaning they are destroyed. || 6 ||

He describes the method of impurity-cleansing <term>mala-śodhaka</term> breath control in two verses. **"Seated in the lotus posture..."** The yogi, having assumed the bound lotus posture <term>padmāsana</term>, should fill the breath—the vital air—through the "moon," which is the moon-channel known as the <term>iḍā</term>. Without exceeding his strength, and having held it <note>original: "dhārayitvā"</note> or retained it <term>kumbhayitvā</term> according to his capacity, he should exhale again through the "sun," the sun-channel known as the <term>piṅgalā</term>.

The definitions of the three parts of breath control are as follows:
1. **Inhalation <term>pūraka</term>:** The taking in of external air through a specific effort.
2. **Retention <term>kumbhaka</term>:** The restraint of the breath after first applying the "locks," such as the throat lock <term>jālandhara-bandha</term>.
3. **Exhalation <term>recaka</term>:** The movement of the retained air outward through a specific effort.

These are the definitions of the components of breath control. The statement "like the bellows of a blacksmith, exhalation and inhalation are done rapidly" <note>original: "bhastrāval-lohakārasya reca-pūrau sasaṁbhramau"</note> refers to "secondary" exhalation and inhalation <note>referring to the Bhastrika or 'bellows' technique</note>, as they do not fit the primary definitions mentioned here. || 7 ||

**"The breath..."** Having drawn in the breath through the sun <note>the right nostril, piṅgalā</note>, one should slowly fill the belly <term>udara</term>. After performing retention <term>kumbhaka</term> accompanied by the proper "locks" <term>bandhas</term> according to the rules, one should exhale again through the moon <term>iḍā</term>. || 8 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

By putting forth effort such as enthusiasm and courage, those qualities that cause distraction, like laziness <term>ālasya</term>, are removed. With a pure <term>sāttvika</term> intellect, whose nature is to create clarity and joy, one should practice breath control daily so that the filth <note>original: "mail" — impurities</note> situated in the central channel <term>suṣumnā</term> is destroyed. || 6 ||

**"Seated in the lotus posture..."** The yogi who has assumed the lotus posture should fill the breath through the moon-channel, the <term>iḍā</term>. Then, having held it according to his strength, he should exhale through the sun-channel, the <term>piṅgalā</term>. Taking in the outside air with great effort is called <term>pūraka</term> (filling). Stopping the breath after first applying the throat lock <term>jālandhara-bandha</term> is called <term>kumbhaka</term> (pot-like retention). Then, releasing that held air with a specific effort—that is, letting it out very slowly—is called <term>recaka</term> (emptying). || 7 ||

**"The breath..."** Having pulled the breath through the sun-channel <term>piṅgalā</term>, one should perform the filling <term>pūraka</term> into the belly very slowly.

<summary>This page details the specific technique for "Alternate Nostril Breathing" (Nadi Shodhana) to purify the central energy channel, defining the three core stages of pranayama: inhalation (puraka), retention (kumbhaka), and exhalation (rechaka).</summary>
<keywords>Nadi Shodhana, Susumna, Pranayama, Padmasana, Puraka, Kumbhaka, Rechaka, Ida, Pingala, purification</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 39, which described the initial steps of the channel-purification process and the seated posture for breath control.</meta>

# Light on Hatha Yoga

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

**Root: Through whichever channel one exhales, through that same channel one should drink the breath and hold it with great restraint. Then, one should exhale through the other channel slowly, and never with force. || 9 ||**

**If one drinks the breath through the <term>iḍā</term> <note>the left nostril</note>, one should exhale through the other after restraining it. After drinking the air through the <term>piṅgalā</term> <note>the right nostril</note> and holding it, one should release it through the left. For those self-controlled yogis who constantly perform this practice using the Sun and the Moon <note>the right and left nostrils</note> according to this method, the entire collection of channels becomes pure after three months. || 10 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding the previously mentioned breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>, the author explains a specific detail. **"Through whichever..."** Through whichever channel—the Moon or the Sun—one should exhale <note>original: "tyajet-recayet"</note>, through that very same channel one should "drink" or fill the breath <note>original: "pūrayitvā"</note>. This should be done with "great restraint" <note>original: "ati-rodhataḥ"</note>, meaning holding the breath until the point where sweat and trembling are produced. The suffix "-tas" here is used in a general grammatical sense. Through whichever side the inhalation <term>pūraka</term> was performed, one should exhale slowly through the other side, and not with speed. Rapid exhalation causes a loss of strength <note>original: "bala-hāniḥ"</note>. The internal logic is this: the side through which you just exhaled is the side through which you must next inhale; the side through which you just inhaled must not be the side through which you immediately exhale. || 9 ||

While summarizing the meaning of the previous instructions regarding the bound lotus posture and other details, the author describes the secondary result of this breath control. **"The breath..."** If one drinks or fills the breath through the <term>iḍā</term> or the left channel, then that breath, having been restrained or held <term>kumbhitam</term>, should be exhaled again through the other, the <term>piṅgalā</term>. Having drunk or filled the air through the <term>piṅgalā</term> or right channel, then after that filling, having bound or held it in <term>kumbhaka</term>, one should release or exhale it through the <term>iḍā</term> or left channel. "Of the Sun and Moon" <note>original: "sūryācandramasau"</note> refers to the two nostrils; the grammatical form uses a specific Vedic dual compound. By this stated method or manner, for those self-controlled practitioners <term>yaminām</term> who expand their practice daily—inhaling through the Moon, holding, exhaling through the Sun, then inhaling through the Sun, holding, and exhaling through the Moon—the entire system of channels <note>original: "nāḍi-gaṇā"</note> becomes pure and free of impurities <note>original: "mala-rahitā"</note> after a period of three months. || 10 ||

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

Then, after performing the breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> according to the rules and with the locks <term>bandhas</term>, one should exhale through the Moon, which is the <term>iḍā</term>. || 8 ||
**"Through whichever..."** Through whichever channel, be it the Moon or the Sun, that one exhales, through that same channel one should perform the inhalation <term>pūraka</term>. One should hold the breath with great restraint <note>original: "ati-rodha"</note> until sweat and trembling occur. Then, having inhaled, one should exhale very slowly through the other channel. One should not exhale with force, as exhaling with force results in the loss of strength. It is not proper to exhale through the same channel used for inhalation; rather, it is one's duty to inhale through the same channel that was just used for exhalation. || 9 ||
**"The breath..."** One should inhale the breath through the <term>iḍā</term>, the left channel. Then, that breath which was held in <term>kumbhaka</term> should be exhaled through the other, the <term>piṅgalā</term> or right channel. Then, having inhaled through the right...

<vocab>prāṇāyāma, iḍā, piṅgalā, kumbhaka, nāḍī, hatha yoga, recaka, pūraka, sūrya, candra</vocab>

<summary>This page details the specific technique of alternate nostril breathing, emphasizing that the practitioner must inhale through the same nostril they just exhaled from. It notes that three months of consistent practice will completely purify the body's energy channels (nadis).</summary>

# Second Lesson
<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>Second Lesson</header>
<sig>6</sig>

**Root: One should practice breath-retentions four times a day—in the morning, at midday, in the evening, and at midnight—gradually increasing the count up to eighty repetitions [per session]. || 11 ||**

**In the lowest stage, sweat is produced; in the middle stage, there is trembling.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Now, the author explains the specific times for practice and the duration of breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>. **"In the morning..."** This means beginning from dawn <note>original: "aruṇodaya," the period just before sunrise</note> until three <term>ghaṭikā</term>s <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes; thus 72 minutes total</note> after sunrise. "At midday" <note>original: "madhyandine"</note> refers to the middle portion of the day when it is divided into five parts. "At the evening twilight" refers to the period defined as three <term>nāḍī</term>s <note>another term for a 24-minute unit</note> before and after sunset. "At midnight" refers to the middle of the night, specifically a period of two <term>muhūrta</term>s <note>roughly 96 minutes</note>. During these four times, one should practice "slowly up to eighty," meaning reaching a limit of eighty repetitions per session. According to the grammatical rule of "continuous connection of time and space," this means eighty breath-retentions <term>prāṇāyāma</term> are to be performed in each of the four periods. The tradition <note>original: "sampradāya"</note> holds that if one is unable to practice at midnight, it must be performed at the three junctions of the day <note>dawn, noon, and dusk</note>. If performed four times daily, there are 320 breath-retentions in total. If performed three times, there are 240. || 11 ||

He now explains the specific physical effects that characterize the lowest, middle, and highest stages of breath control. **"In the lowest..."** In the lowest <note>original: "kanīyasi"</note> stage of breath control, sweat <note>original: "sveda"</note> is produced. This stage is recognized by the appearance of perspiration. In the middle stage of breath control, trembling <note>original: "kampa"</note> occurs. The middle stage is recognized by this trembling. In the highest stage, the [breath reaches the] place... <note>Text continues on the next page</note>.

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

By filling the breath through the <term>piṅgalā</term> <note>the right nostril</note>, holding it <term>kumbhaka</term>, and then exhaling through the left-side <term>iḍā</term>; and by the previously mentioned method of the Sun and Moon—daily practicing inhalation through the Moon <note>left nostril</note>, holding, and exhaling through the Sun <note>right nostril</note>, then inhaling through the Sun and exhaling through the Moon—the yogis who practice in this way will find their entire system of energy channels <term>nāḍī</term>s becomes pure after three months. || 10 ||

Now the time of practice for breath control and the duration are described. **"In the morning..."** From dawn until three <term>ghaḍī</term>s <note>24-minute units</note> after sunrise is the morning time; one should practice then, and at midday—the middle part when the day is divided into five parts—and at the evening twilight—three <term>ghaḍī</term>s before and three <term>ghaḍī</term>s after sunset—and at midnight for a period of two <term>muhūrta</term>s. In these four times, it is proper to do 80 breath-retentions in each period. If one is unable to do it at midnight, it should be done during the three twilights. Daily, for the four periods, there are 320 retentions, and if done only three times, there are 240. || 11 ||

**"In the lowest..."** In the lowest stage of breath control, sweat comes, and in the middle stage, trembling...

<vocab>prāṇāyāma (breath control), kumbhaka (breath retention), prātaḥkāla (morning time), madhyandina (midday), sāyaṃsandhyā (evening twilight), ardharātra (midnight), nāḍī-śuddhi (purification of the channels), kaniṣṭha (lowest/beginner), madhyama (middle/intermediate)</vocab>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>In the Lamp on Hatha Yoga</header>

# Root: In the highest stage, one attains the place; thereafter, one should restrain the breath. || 12 ||

## ॥ Commentary ॥

"One attains the place" means one reaches the <term>Brahma-randhra</term> <note>the "Aperture of Brahma," a spiritual center at the crown of the head</note>. The "highest" stage of practice is inferred when this place is reached. "Thereafter"—from that point on—one should "restrain the breath" <note>original: "niban-dhayet," meaning to bind or hold firmly</note>, meaning one should thoroughly control the <term>prāṇa</term> (vital breath). The characteristics of the "lowest" and other stages are described in the *Linga Purāṇa*:

> "The measure of breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> is known to be twelve units <term>mātrā</term>s. The lowest stage is twelve units, and it is said to cause a single 'rising' <term>udghāta</term> <note>a physical surge of energy or a jolt felt in the body</note>. The middle stage is twenty-four units and causes a double rising. The highest stage is said to be thirty-six units and causes a triple rising. These stages respectively produce sweat, trembling, and a rising of the body from the ground. In this state, bliss arises, as well as [states resembling] sleep and smoke. One experiences hair standing on end, the perception of internal sounds, the twitching of limbs, and trembling. When one conquers fatigue, sweat, talkativeness, and the potential for fainting, then the breath control is called 'highest' and is truly auspicious."

"Smoke" <note>original: "dhūma"</note> refers to the vacillation or wandering of the mind. The sage Goraksha also says:

> "The lowest stage is declared by the best of the twice-born to be twelve units; the middle is known to be double that (24); and the highest is three times that (36) in breath control."

The definition of "rising" <term>udghāta</term> is as follows:

> "When the downward-moving breath <term>apāna</term> is pressed by the upward-moving breath <term>prāṇa</term> and, after moving upward, it returns—this is the characteristic of 'rising'."

The sage Yajnavalkya defines the "unit of time" <term>mātrā</term>:

> "The wise declare the measure of a <term>mātrā</term> to be the time it takes to snap the thumb and finger three times, or to circle one's hand around the knee three times."

In the *Skanda Purāṇa*:

> "A <term>mātrā</term> is defined as the duration of a single natural breath."

This is further explained in the *Yoga-cintāmaṇi*: "The time it takes for a single breath to go out and come in for a person who is in deep sleep is called one <term>mātrā</term> of breath control." The duration of a breath control session is thus defined by twelve breaths plus a half-breath. Six breaths make one <term>pala</term> <note>a unit of time approx. 24 seconds</note>. Thus, the duration of a breath control session is established as two and a half <term>pala</term>s. A breath control consisting of twelve and a half <term>mātrā</term>s is what is called the "highest" stage. One should not worry about contradictions with the previously cited verses from the *Linga Purāṇa* or Goraksha, where the lowest stage was said to be twelve units.

> "One should circle the knee neither too fast nor too slow; the time it takes to snap the fingers is called a <term>mātrā</term>."

This is also from the *Skanda Purāṇa*:

> "The release of the thumb and finger and the stroking of the knee; the time it takes to snap the fingers is called a <term>mātrā</term>."

And from the words of Dattatreya:

> "The snap of the thumb is counted as a <term>mātrā</term>."

In the verses from the *Linga Purāṇa* and Goraksha, the "unit" <term>mātrā</term> is intended to be the duration of a single finger-snap. In the verses of Yajnavalkya and others, the snap—

## ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥

In this highest stage of breath control, the <term>Brahma-randhra</term> is attained. Therefore, the Yogi should constantly restrain the breath. When the breath-retention <term>kumbhaka</term> lasts for approximately forty-two <term>vipala</term>s <note>a small unit of time, 1/60th of a *pala*</note>, it is the "lowest" stage. When it lasts for about eighty-four <term>vipala</term>s, it is the "middle" stage. And when, through firm restraint, the retention lasts for one hundred and twenty-five <term>vipala</term>s, it is called the "highest" stage. When the breath control becomes steady...

<summary>This page defines the three stages of breath control (lowest, middle, highest) based on time units and physical symptoms, reconciling different scriptural definitions of a "unit of time" (matra).</summary>
<keywords>stages of pranayama, Brahma-randhra, matra (time unit), udghata (energy rising), Linga Purana, Goraksha, Skanda Purana, breath-retention duration</keywords>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>The Second Lesson</header>

**Root: One should rub the body with the water [sweat] produced from the exertion [of practice]. By this, firmness and lightness of the body are produced. || 13 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Because a duration of three "units" <note>original: "mātrā"</note> is intended to be the measure here, the highest stage is described as being three times the lowest stage; thus, there is no contradiction. In all means of Yoga practice, breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> <note>the regulation of vital energy through the breath</note> is the primary tool, for when it is mastered, the stages of withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term> and the rest are also mastered. If it is not mastered, withdrawal and the subsequent stages cannot be attained. In reality, it is breath control itself that is referred to by terms like "withdrawal." This is stated in the *Yoga Chintamani*: "Breath control itself, through the sequence of practice, increases and is then called withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term>, concentration <term>dhāraṇā</term>, meditation <term>dhyāna</term>, and absorption <term>samādhi</term>."

This is also stated in the *Skanda Purāṇa*:
"Twelve breath controls are called one withdrawal. Twelve withdrawals are designated as one concentration. Twelve concentrations constitute meditation for the sake of union with the Lord. By twelve meditations, absorption is declared. In that absorption, the supreme, immediate, self-luminous light appears. When that is seen, one turns away from the bondage of ritual actions."

Furthermore:
"Concentration is achieved in five <term>nāḍī</term>s <note>here referring to units of time, specifically 'ghatikās' of 24 minutes each</note>, meditation in sixty units, and absorption occurs from the restraint of breath for twelve days." 

The sage Goraksha and others have also spoken thus. The system is as follows: The duration of the "lowest" breath control is slightly less than forty-two <term>vipala</term>s <note>a vipala is approximately 0.4 seconds</note>. This is considered a duration of twelve units <term>mātrā</term>s, if we define one unit as the time taken for one finger-snap <term>choṭikā</term>. The "middle" breath control is slightly less than eighty-four vipalas. This is a duration of twenty-four units. The "highest" breath control consists of one hundred and twenty-five vipalas. This is a duration of thirty-six units. However, if one defines a unit as the time of three finger-snaps, then even the highest stage is considered to be only twelve units. 

When the breath control is held firmly with the locks <term>bandha</term>s <note>internal physical seals used to direct energy</note> for one hundred and twenty-five vipalas, then the vital breath <term>prāṇa</term> enters the aperture of Brahma <term>Brahmarandhra</term> <note>the spiritual center at the crown of the head</note>. When the breath reaches the aperture of Brahma and remains there for twenty-five <term>pala</term>s <note>a pala is about 24 seconds</note>, that is withdrawal. When it remains for five <term>ghaṭikā</term>s <note>a ghaṭikā is 24 minutes; 5 ghaṭikās is 2 hours</note>, that is concentration. When it remains for sixty ghaṭikās <note>24 hours</note>, that is meditation. When it remains for twelve days, that is absorption. Thus, the entire system is beautiful. || 12 || 

Regarding the sweat produced while practicing breath control, the author states a specific instruction: "With the water..." From the exertion of breath control—

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

—When that occurs, the vital breath attains the aperture of Brahma. When the breath that has gone into the aperture of Brahma remains for twenty-five <term>pala</term>s, it is called withdrawal. When it remains for five <term>ghaḍī</term>s <note>same as ghaṭikā, 24 minutes</note>, concentration occurs. When it remains stable for six <note>Note: the Sanskrit says 60, the vernacular here says 6</note> ghaḍīs, it is meditation. When it remains for twelve days, it becomes absorption. || 12 || 

"With the water": By the sweat that is produced from the practice of breath control, one should rub the body [instead of] oil—

<summary>This page explains the benefits of rubbing sweat back into the skin and provides a detailed chronological hierarchy of Yoga, showing how breath control (Prāṇāyāma) evolves into deeper states like Samādhi based on the duration of the breath-hold.</summary>

<header>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

**Root: In the first stage of practice, a diet of milk and clarified butter <note>original: "ghṛta" or ghee</note> is recommended. Once the practice has become firm, such strict rules need not be maintained. || 14 ||**

**Just as a lion, an elephant, or a tiger is tamed gradually, so too should the breath be controlled; otherwise, it kills the practitioner. || 15 ||**

**Through the proper practice of breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>, all diseases are destroyed. Through improper practice, however, every kind of disease may arise. || 16 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

One should perform a massage <note>original: "mardanam"</note> of the limbs and body with the sweat produced by the exertion of practice, treating it like an oil massage. By this massage, firmness <note>original: "dāḍhyam"</note> and lightness—which is the absence of lethargy—are produced in the body. || 13 || <note>This commentary refers to the final part of verse 13 from the previous page.</note>

Now, the author speaks regarding the rules for milk and other foods during the initial and advanced stages of practice: **In the first stage of practice.** Food mixed with milk and clarified butter is "milk-and-butter food." This is a compound word formed in the manner of "the vegetable-loving king." <note>A reference to a Sanskrit grammatical rule (śākaparthivādi) where a middle term is dropped.</note> Once absolute breath retention <term>kevala kumbhaka</term> <note>a state of spontaneous suspension of breath without inhalation or exhalation</note> is mastered, the practice is considered firm. The rest is clear. || 14 ||

The author explains that one should control the breath slowly, like a lion, and not with haste: **Just as.** In the same manner that a lion, the king of beasts, or a wild elephant, or a tiger <note>original: "śārdūla"</note> is brought under one's own power very slowly and not all at once, so too is the breath <term>prāṇa</term> mastered through gradual practice. Otherwise, if the breath is grasped with haste, it kills the practitioner <note>original: "sādhaka"</note>, just as a lion or tiger would. || 15 ||

The author describes the results of both proper and improper practice: **Through breath control.** Breath control practiced with the proper methods of diet and the specific locks <term>bandha</term> such as the throat-lock <term>jālandhara bandha</term> is called "proper." Through this, all diseases are destroyed...

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

...just as with an oil massage, by massaging [the sweat], firmness and lightness—meaning the absence of heaviness—arise in the body. || 13 ||

**In the first stage of practice:** In the beginning of their training, the Yogi should eat food combined with milk and clarified butter. Once the practice of absolute breath retention is mastered and firm, there is no longer any need to insist upon this rule. || 14 ||

**Just as:** In the same way that a lion, a wild elephant, or a tiger are brought under control slowly and never by force, the breath must be tamed through gradual practice. Otherwise, if seized with haste, it destroys the practitioner just as a lion would. || 15 ||

**Through breath control:** By practicing breath control with the proper diet and the application of the locks like the throat-lock, all diseases...

**Root: Hiccups, labored breathing <note>original: "shvasa," often referring to asthma or gasping</note>, cough, and pains in the head, ears, and eyes—various diseases arise from the provocation of the breath <term>pavana</term> <note>literally "wind" or the vital air</note>. || 17 ||**
**One should exhale the breath <term>vāyu</term> properly, fill it properly, and restrain it properly; thus one attains success <term>siddhi</term>. || 18 ||**
**When the purification of the channels <term>nāḍī-śuddhi</term> occurs, then outward signs appear; thinness and luster of the body certainly arise then. || 19 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...destruction or loss of the [diseases] of the limbs occurs. Practice that is excessive and devoid of the aforementioned methods leads to the "arising of all diseases"—that is, the complete manifestation or birth of all illnesses. || 16 || <note>This continues the explanation of verse 16 from the previous page.</note>

In response to the question "What diseases occur through improper breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>?" the author says: **Hiccups**, etc. Hiccups, labored breathing, and coughs are specific diseases. Pain in the head, ears, and eyes refers to "head-ear-eye pain." Various diseases such as fever and others arise from the provocation <note>vitiation or imbalance</note> of the wind or breath <term>vāyu</term>. || 17 ||

Since various diseases arise from the provocation of the breath, what should be done? The author says: **Properly, properly.** One should release the breath <term>prāṇa</term> properly. The meaning is that at the time of exhalation <term>rechaka</term>, one should exhale only very slowly, not with force. One should inhale <term>pūrayet</term> properly—neither too little nor too much. One should restrain the breath <term>kumbhayet</term> properly, employing techniques such as the Throat Lock <term>jālandhara-bandha</term> <note>a chin lock that redirects the flow of energy</note>. One who practices in this way attains that success—the success of Hatha Yoga. || 18 ||

Now, in two verses, the author describes the signs of the purification of the channels <term>nāḍī-śuddhi</term> that arises for one practicing breath control properly. **When, however...** At whatever time the purification of the channels—the removal of impurities—occurs, then outwardly...

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

...destruction occurs. And through breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> accompanied by yoga practice that is devoid of the methods mentioned, the birth of all diseases occurs. || 16 ||

**Hiccups**, etc. Hiccups, labored breathing, cough, and pain in the head, ears, and eyes, as well as various types of diseases like fever, etc., occur through the aggravation of the wind <term>vāyu</term>. || 17 ||

**Properly, properly.** At the time of exhalation, one should exhale the breath slowly and not use force. And one should not perform inhalation <term>pūraka</term> too little, nor too much; it should be done appropriately. And one should perform retention <term>kumbhaka</term> appropriately, joined with the Throat Lock <term>jālandhara-bandha</term> and others. By acting in this manner, the success of Hatha Yoga is obtained. || 18 ||

The signs of channel purification <term>nāḍī-śuddhi</term> are told in two verses. **When, however...** When the purification of the channels occurs...

<meta>This page continues the second chapter (Upadesha) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, following the discussion of signs that appear when the energy channels are purified. It introduces the "Six Actions" (Shatkarma), which are preparatory cleansing techniques used to balance the body's humors before beginning breath control exercises.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

### Root
**The ability to hold the breath as long as desired, the kindling of the digestive fire <note>original: "analasya," the internal fire of digestion</note>, the manifestation of internal sound <term>nāda</term>, and perfect health arise from the purification of the channels <term>nāḍī-śodhana</term>. || 20 ||**

**One who has an excess of fat or phlegm should first practice the six actions <term>ṣaṭkarma</term>. Others, however, should not perform them if their humors <term>doṣa</term> <note>the three biological humors: Vata (air), Pitta (fire/bile), and Kapha (water/phlegm)</note> are already in a state of balance. || 21 ||**

**Dhauti, Basti, Neti, Trataka, Nauli, and Kapalabhati—these are known as the six actions. || 22 ||**

**These six actions are to be kept secret; they are the causes of the purification of the "pot" <note>the "ghata," a yogic term for the physical body</note>.**

### ॥ Commentary ॥

...one who knows. The suffix "tasi" is used here to denote various grammatical cases. The "signs" refer to characteristics, and the word "and" implies that other signs also occur. He lists them: **Of the body.** Of the body <note>original: "kayasya"</note>, thinness or leanness, and a certain luster or beautiful glow <note>original: "kanti"</note> will surely arise. || 19 || <note>This commentary refers back to the final verse of the previous page.</note>

**As desired.** Holding the breath <term>prāṇa</term> as much as one wishes for many repetitions during retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>. The kindling of the fire—meaning the excellent illumination of the gastric fire. The manifestation of sound—meaning its clear appearance. Health—meaning the state of being free from disease. These arise from the purification of the channels, which is the removal of impurities <note>original: "mala"</note> from the energy channels <term>nāḍīs</term>. || 20 ||

He now describes another method for those with an excess of fat and so on: **Excess fat and phlegm.** A person who has an abundance of both fat <note>original: "medas"</note> and phlegm <note>original: "shleshman"</note>. **First**—meaning before the practice of breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>. He should thoroughly perform the six actions which will be described. **Others**—meaning those free from an excess of fat and phlegm—should not perform those six actions. He gives the reason: because their humors—the wind, bile, and phlegm <term>vāta-pitta-kapha</term>—are in a state of balance. Therefore, it is because of the equilibrium of the humors. || 21 ||

He teaches the six actions: **Dhauti.** [The meaning is] clear. || 22 ||

He states that this is a secret: **The six actions.** The purification of the "pot" or the body—meaning the removal of impurities...

### ॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥

Then these external signs appear: the body definitely becomes thin and takes on a luster. || 19 ||

**As desired.** If one holds the breath for a long time in retention <term>kumbhaka</term>, the gastric fire is kindled. The manifestation of internal sound and health—all these arise from the purification of the channels. || 20 ||

**Excess fat and phlegm.** A man who has an excess of both fat and phlegm should perform the six actions (which will be explained ahead) before the practice of breath control. And he whose humors—wind, bile, and phlegm—are in a state of balance, and who does not have an excess of fat and phlegm, should not do them. || 21 ||

Now the six actions are named: **Dhauti.** 1. Dhauti, 2. Basti, 3. Neti, 4. Trataka, 5. Nauli, 6. Kapala[bhati]...

<summary>This page lists the benefits of purifying the energy channels, such as health and internal sound, and introduces the Shatkarmas (six cleansing acts) as preparatory practices for students with imbalanced humors.</summary>
<keywords>Nadi Shodhana, Digestive Fire, Nada, Shatkarma, Dosha, Dhauti, Basti, Neti, Trataka, Nauli, Kapalabhati, Body Purification</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the second chapter (Upadesha) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. Following the introduction of the six purification actions (Shatkarma), the text now explains why these practices are revered and begins a detailed technical description of the first action, Dhauti (internal cleansing with cloth).</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>Second Instruction</header>

**Root:**
**This practice, which brings about various extraordinary qualities, is worshipped by the foremost of yogis. || 23 ||**

### Regarding Dhauti

**One should slowly swallow a moistened cloth, four fingers wide and fifteen cubits <note>original: "hasta," a measurement from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, approximately 18 inches</note> long, according to the method instructed by the Guru. || 24 ||**
**Then, one should pull it out again. This is known as the practice of Dhauti.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The text states that these six actions, beginning with **Dhauti**, which perform the **purification of the pot** <note>original: "ghata-shodhana," meaning the purification of the physical body</note>, must be kept secret. Why? Because it **brings about various qualities**. This means it is the nature of these six actions to bring about extraordinary and unique virtues. For this reason, it is **worshipped**—meaning honored—by the **foremost of yogis** (the greatest among practitioners).

The implication is that if these were not kept secret, the six actions would be performed by everyone, and the special status of the yogi would be lost. Furthermore, it is suggested that this purification is the primary fruit of the six actions. While the destruction of excess fat and phlegm can also be achieved through breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term>, these actions are more direct. As it is said: "One dedicated to the practice of breath attains the yoga of the six actions." This aligns with the flow of the preceding and following texts. || 23 ||

He now describes the practice of **Dhauti**. **Four fingers wide.** This refers to a width equal to the sum of four fingers. It should be **fifteen cubits long**, moistened with water—specifically, slightly warm water. The cloth should be fine, such as a strip from a new turban. Following the **path taught by the Guru**—referring to the specific method of swallowing the cloth—one should swallow it **slowly**, little by little. On the second day, two cubits; on the third day, three cubits. In this way, by daily increase, one should swallow one additional cubit each day. || 24 ||

**Then.** Having kept the end of the cloth between the front teeth <note>original: "rajadanta," literally 'king teeth' or the incisors</note>, and having moved the cloth around well within the stomach using the **Nauli** <note>the abdominal churning technique</note> action, one should **slowly pull it out** or extract it. This is called the practice of Dhauti—

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

These are the names of the six actions <note>referring to verse 22 from the previous page</note>.

**Regarding the Six Actions:** These six actions should be kept secret. They remove the impurities of the body and, because their nature is to produce various extraordinary qualities, they are honored by the highest yogis. || 23 ||

Now the practice of **Dhauti** is described. **Four fingers wide.** One should take a cloth that is four fingers wide and fifteen cubits <note>the translation here uses "angula" (fingers) for length in error, but the Sanskrit root and the commentary correctly imply cubits</note> long, moistened with slightly warm water. It should be a fine, thin piece of a new turban. Then, using the method of swallowing cloth shown by the Guru, one should swallow it very slowly, bit by bit. On the second day, swallow two cubits; on the third day, three cubits. Thus, every day, swallow one or two cubits more. || 24 ||

**Then.** Leave about one span <note>a hand-breadth</note> of the end of that cloth hanging out and hold it between the teeth...

<vocab>Dhauti (cleansing), Shatkarma (six actions), Ghata-shodhana (body purification), Yogi-pungava (eminent yogi), Nauli (abdominal churning), Hasta (cubit measurement)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the importance of keeping the six purification actions secret and provides specific technical instructions for Dhauti, a practice involving swallowing a long strip of cloth to cleanse the stomach.</summary>
<keywords>Dhauti, Shatkarma, Hatha Yoga, internal cleansing, yogic hygiene, Guru's instruction, body purification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Shatkarma (six purification acts). It concludes the section on Dhauti (cloth cleansing) by listing its medicinal benefits and introduces the second purification act, Basti (yogic enema), detailing the specific measurements for the equipment and the posture required.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

# **Root: Cough, asthma, enlarged spleen, skin diseases, and the twenty varieties of diseases born of phlegm— || 25 ||**
# **By the power of the practice of Dhauti, these certainly depart; of this, there is no doubt. ||**
# **Standing in navel-deep water, with a tube inserted into the anus, one should assume the Utkatasana posture. || 26 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

The author explains the fruits of the practice of **Dhauti**. "Cough, asthma..." refers to cough, respiratory distress, enlargement of the spleen, and skin conditions <note>original: *kushtha*, a term covering various skin ailments including leprosy</note>. This is a collective compound of terms. These are specific types of illnesses, along with the twenty numbered varieties of diseases caused by excess phlegm <term>kapha</term>. || 25 ||

"Through the power of Dhauti..." means that by the influence of the cleansing practice, these diseases surely vanish; there is no doubt. This means this result is certain. Now, he describes the practice of **Basti** <term>a yogic internal wash or enema</term>. "Navel-deep" refers to a depth of water reaching the level of the navel. The suffix *daghnach* is used here to indicate a specific measurement. In that navel-deep water—taken from a river or similar body of water—one should insert a bamboo tube <note>original: *vamsanala*</note> into the anus <note>original: *payu*</note>. Having taken a bamboo tube six fingers long with a hollow center large enough for the little finger to enter, one should insert four fingers of its length into the anus and keep two fingers' length outside.

One who assumes the **Utkatasana** posture is called *Utkatasanah*. This posture is performed by placing the buttocks upon the two heels and balancing on the toes. One should then perform the contraction of the base <note>the anal sphincter</note> so that water enters inside. After moving the water that has entered by using the **Nauli** action <term>abdominal churning</term>, one should expel it. This internal washing is called the practice of **Basti**. Both the **Dhauti** and **Basti** practices should be performed before eating. Furthermore, one should not delay in eating a meal after these practices are finished.

However, some practitioners attempt the **Basti** practice while standing in water by first mastering the drawing in of air through the root center <term>muladhara</term> without the use of a tube. When performed that way, all of the water may not come back out. Therefore, because of the risk of various diseases and the depletion of the bodily humors <term>dhatus</term>, the practice should never be performed in that manner. Otherwise, why would the author **Svatmarama** explicitly state "placing a tube in the anus"? || 26 ||

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

Apply the cloth to the lips, then perform the **Nauli** action. By performing Nauli, the cloth that has gathered in the chest moves down into the stomach. Then, swirl the cloth within the stomach; the Nauli action will cause it to rotate. Then, slowly, slowly, extract the cloth. This is called the practice of **Dhauti**. By doing this, cough, asthma, enlarged spleen, and skin diseases which are poisonous ailments, as well as diseases of the phlegm— || 25 ||

"Through Dhauti..." All these diseases will certainly be removed by the power of the practice of Dhauti. Now, the practice of **Basti** is described. "Navel-deep..." Stand in water up to the navel. Take a bamboo tube six fingers long, with a hole large enough for the little finger to fit. Insert four fingers of it into the anus and keep two fingers outside. Then, assuming the **Utkatasana** posture, contract the base so that water enters within. Then, rotate that water using the **Nauli** action and expel it. This is the practice of Basti. Dha—

<meta>catchword: Dha-</meta>

<summary>This page lists the curative powers of Dhauti, specifically for respiratory and phlegm-related illnesses, and introduces the technical requirements for Basti (yogic enema), including the use of a bamboo tube and the Utkatasana posture.</summary>
<keywords>Dhauti, Basti, Kapha, Phlegm, Spleen, Skin diseases, Utkatasana, Nauli, Svatmarama, Yoga therapy</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical instructions for Basti (yogic enema) to its physiological and psychological benefits. It continues the numbered sequence of the Shatkarma (purification acts) section of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, concluding the section on Basti.</meta>

<page-num>( 49 )</page-num>
<header>Second Instruction</header>

**Root: One should contract the anus and perform the washing; that is the act of Basti. ||**
**Abdominal masses, enlargement of the spleen, and dropsy—as well as all illnesses arising from wind, bile, and phlegm <note>the three humors or *doshas*: vata, pitta, and kapha</note>— ||**
**Are diminished by the power of the Basti practice. || 27 ||**
**It bestows clarity upon the tissues, the senses, and the inner mind; it gives a healthy glow and kindles the digestive fire. ||**
**The regular practice of water-Basti <note>original: *jalabastikarma*</note> destroys the accumulation of all defects. || 28 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The author describes the benefits of the Basti act in two verses. "Abdominal masses, enlargement of the spleen..." refers to specific diseases: *Gulma* <term>a phantom tumor or mass in the abdomen</term>, *Pliha* <term>the spleen</term>, and *Udara* <term>dropsy or abdominal swelling</term>. These are joined here as a compound. Whether they are born from one, two, or all three of the humors—wind <term>vata</term>, bile <term>pitta</term>, and phlegm <term>kapha</term>—all these illnesses are destroyed by the strength and capacity of the Basti practice. || 27 ||

"The tissues..." refers to the practice of Basti performed in water. It provides the following to the practitioner:
The "tissues" <term>dhatus</term> are the seven constituents of the body: plasma, blood, muscle, fat, bone, marrow, and reproductive fluid.
The "senses" <term>indriyas</term> include the five organs of action—speech, hands, feet, the anus, and the genitals—and the five organs of knowledge—hearing, touch, sight, taste, and smell.
The "inner mind" <term>antahkarana</term> consists of the mind, intellect, consciousness, and ego.
"Clarity" <term>prasada</term> refers to the emergence of pure, balanced qualities <note>original: *sattva*, the quality of light and harmony</note>—such as happiness and lightness—by removing the agitated and dull qualities <note>original: *rajas* and *tamas*</note> which cause suffering, distraction, grief, delusion, heaviness, obstruction, and misery.
"Luster" <term>kanti</term> means brilliance.
"Digestive fire" <term>dahana</term> refers to the fire of the stomach <note>original: *jathara-agni*</note>, and "kindling" means its excellent activation.
Furthermore, "the accumulation of all defects" <term>doshas</term> refers to wind, bile, and phlegm. This also implies the correction of their decrease. It destroys both their excess and their deficiency; in other words, it establishes health by balancing the humors. || 28 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

This Basti practice should be performed before eating. After doing this, one should not delay their meal for too long. || 26 <note>The verse numbering in the vernacular commentary slightly differs from the root text</note> ||

"The base..." Now, the benefits of Basti are explained in two verses. Through the power of Basti, diseases like abdominal masses, spleen enlargement, dropsy, and all ailments born of wind, bile, and phlegm are destroyed. || 27 ||

"The tissues..." For one who practices Basti in water, the seven tissues—plasma, blood, muscle, fat, bone, marrow, and reproductive fluid—along with the five organs of knowledge, the five organs of action, and the inner mind (comprising the mind, intellect, consciousness, and ego) are purified. Heat, distraction, grief, delusion, heaviness, obstruction, and misery—the qualities of passion and dullness—are all removed. It grants clarity, luster, and kindles the digestive fire. It removes the excess of wind, bile, and phlegm, and brings about health. || 28 ||

<sig>7</sig>
<vocab>Basti-karma, Gulma, Pliha, Udara, Vata, Pitta, Kapha, Dhatu, Indriya, Antahkarana, Jathara-agni</vocab>

<summary>This page details the medicinal and psychological benefits of Basti (yogic enema), including the cure of abdominal and splenic diseases and the purification of the body's seven tissues and the mind.</summary>
<keywords>Basti, Shatkarma, humors, doshas, digestion, tissues, mental clarity, Ayurvedic medicine</keywords>
