<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

## Now, Neti (Nasal Cleansing) ##

> **Verse: One should insert a smooth thread, one span <term>vitasti; about 9 inches</term> long, into the nasal passage. ||**
> **One should pull it out through the mouth; this is called Neti by the accomplished masters <term>Siddhas</term>. || 29 ||**
> **Neti cleanses the skull and bestows divine vision. ||**
> **It also quickly destroys the multitude of diseases that arise above the collarbones. || 30 ||**

### ॥ Commentary ॥ ###

Now the act of *Neti* is described. Regarding the verse: "one span" refers to a measurement of one *vitasti* <note>original: *vitastimitam*</note>, but this is merely an indication that more may be used. One should take as much thread as is necessary to perform the *Neti* act properly. "Well-lubricated" <note>original: *susnigdham*</note> means the thread should be very smooth and free of knots or irregularities. It should be made strong by twisting together nine, ten, or fifteen strands. The nose is called a "tube" <note>original: *nala*</note> because it is hollow; one should insert the thread into it. "Pull it out through the mouth" means to extract it.

The method is as follows: After inserting the tip of the thread into the nasal passage, one should block the other nostril with a finger and perform an inhalation <term>puraka</term>. Then, one should exhale <term>rechaka</term> through the mouth. By doing this repeatedly, the tip of the thread reaches the mouth. Taking hold of that end and the end remaining outside the nose, one should move it slowly back and forth. 

Because of the word "and" <note>original: *chakara*</note> in the text, it is also implied that one may insert it in one nostril and bring it out through the other. The method for that is: insert the tip of the thread in one nostril, block the other nostril with a finger, perform an inhalation, and then exhale through the other nostril. By doing this repeatedly, the tip of the thread reaches the other nasal passage; then one should move it back and forth as before. This second method occurs occasionally for those who practice many times. This is described by the "Siddhas"—those endowed with supernatural qualities like *anima* <term>the power to become minute</term>. As it is said: "The Siddhas are described by the virtuous as those who have attained the eightfold lordly powers." This is what is called *Neti*. || 29 ||

The benefits of *Neti* are described: "Cleanser of the skull" means it purifies the skull, making it clean and free of impurities. Because of the word "and," it also cleanses the nasal passages and related areas. The word "ever" <note>original: *eva*</note> is used for emphasis. "Divine [vision]"...

### ॥ Vernacular Language ॥ ###

Now the act of *Neti* is spoken of. Regarding the verse: one should take a thread that is exactly one span long, very smooth, and free of knots. It should be made strong with nine, ten, or fifteen strands. Then, insert it into the nose and pull it out through the mouth. The method is this: insert the end of the thread into the nose, block the other nostril with a finger and inhale, then exhale through the mouth. By doing this repeatedly, the end of the thread will come into the mouth. Grasping both the end in the mouth and the end outside the nose, move it slowly. This *Neti* was taught by the Siddhas. || 29 ||

Now the benefits of *Neti* are spoken of. Regarding the verse: this *Neti* practice purifies the filth of the skull and also removes the filth of the nose and related parts. Through it, one sees subtle objects...

<summary>This page describes the technique and benefits of Neti, a purification practice involving passing a thread through the nose and out the mouth or other nostril. It is credited with clearing the head and improving vision.</summary>
<keywords>Neti, nasal cleansing, Hatha Yoga, purification, Shatkarma, skull cleansing, divine vision, Siddhas</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the *Shatkarma* (six purification acts). It begins by concluding the commentary on the benefits of *Neti* (nasal cleansing) and then introduces the fourth purification act: *Trataka* (steady gazing).</meta>

द्वितीयोपदेशः । <note>Second Lesson</note>
( ५१ )

# Now, Trataka (Steady Gazing) #

**Verse: One should look with a steady gaze at a small target with a concentrated mind until tears fall; this is called Trataka by the teachers. || 31 ||**

**It is the liberator from eye diseases and a shutter <note>closing the door</note> against lethargy and the like. Trataka should be guarded with great effort, just like a casket of gold. || 32 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Because it provides a vision capable of perceiving subtle objects, it is called the "bestower of divine sight." Regarding the practice of *Neti* mentioned previously, it quickly destroys the multitude of diseases arising above the collarbones—the joints of the shoulders and neck. The word "and" is used to complete the verse. The *Amarakosha* <note>A famous ancient Sanskrit dictionary</note> defines these terms: "The shoulder is the head of the arm; its joints are the collarbones." || 30 ||

Now he speaks of *Trataka*. **One should look...** "Concentrated" means having a one-pointed mind. A "steady gaze" is a vision that does not flicker. A "small target" <note>original: *sukshmalakshyam*</note> is a minute object of focus. "Until tears fall" refers to the proper discharge of tears; this indicates the time limit for the practice. One should look or gaze. This act of *Trataka* was taught or remembered by the great masters <term>Acharyas</term> such as Matsyendra <note>A legendary founding master of Hatha Yoga</note> and others. || 31 ||

He now describes the qualities of *Trataka*. **Liberator...** It is the "liberator," meaning the destroyer, of eye diseases. It is a "shutter" for lethargy and similar states; it acts like a door to keep them out or subdue them. "Lethargy" <term>tandra</term> is a specific state of mind dominated by dullness or inertia <term>tamas</term>. The practice known as *Trataka* should be kept secret with great effort. He provides an analogy for this secrecy: **Just as...** just as a casket <note>original: *petakam*</note> of gold <note>original: *hataka*</note> is well-known in the world to be hidden away, so should this practice be guarded. || 32 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

It <note>Neti</note> gives such divine vision and removes the group of diseases that arise in the upper part of the shoulder joints. || 30 ||

Now *Trataka* is described. **One should look...** Being of one-pointed mind, one should look at a small target or any object with a steady gaze. One should keep looking until water <note>tears</note> comes into the eyes. When water comes into the eyes, one should stop. Matsyendra and others have taught this act of *Trataka*. || 31 ||

Now the qualities of *Trataka* are described. **Liberator...** It is the destroyer of eye diseases and is like a shutter against laziness and excessive sleep. It removes lethargy—meaning the tendencies of the dull <term>tamasic</term> mind, such as anger and the like. And just as one keeps a box of gold hidden, in the same way, one should keep this *Trataka* practice secret with great effort. || 32 ||

<summary>This page concludes the benefits of Neti and introduces Trataka, a purification technique involving focused gazing at a small point until tears flow. It is praised for curing eye diseases and overcoming mental lethargy, and the text emphasizes that it should be kept highly secret.</summary>
<keywords>Trataka, steady gazing, eye diseases, Neti, Matsyendra, tamas, mental focus, Hatha Yoga, purification acts</keywords>

Having finished the explanation of *Trataka* (steady gazing) on page 51, the text now introduces the fifth purification act, *Nauli* (abdominal churning), and begins the sixth, *Kapalabhati* (skull-shining breath).</meta>

<page-num>५२</page-num>
हठयोगप्रदीपिकायां <note>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</note>

# Now, Nauli (Abdominal Churning) #

**Root Verse: One should rotate the abdomen toward the left and right with the speed of a rapid whirlpool, while leaning the shoulders forward. This is called *Nauli* by the perfected masters. || 33 ||**

**It always kindles the sluggish digestive fire, aids digestion, and brings joy. It dries up all imbalances <note>the three humors: vata, pitta, and kapha</note> and diseases; this *Nauli* is the crown jewel of Hatha Yoga practices. || 34 ||**

**Like the bellows of a blacksmith, rapid exhalation and inhalation—**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Now he describes the act of *Nauli*. **With the speed...** "Leaning forward" <term>natamsah</term> refers to a person whose shoulders <term>skandhau</term> are bent low. "Rapid" <term>amanda</term> means intense. "Whirlpool" <term>avarta</term> refers to a vortex in water. "With the speed" <term>vegena</term> means with momentum. "Abdomen" <term>tundam</term> refers to the belly. The *Amarakosha* <note>An ancient Sanskrit thesaurus</note> says: "*Pichanda*, *kukshi*, *jathara*, *udara*, and *tunda* are synonyms for the belly; *stana* and *kucha* refer to the breasts." "Left and right" <term>savyapasavyatah</term> refers to the right and left sides. The suffix *-tah* is used here in the sense of the seventh <note>locative</note> case. "Should rotate" <term>bhramayet</term> means to impel the movement. This is called *Nauli* by the perfected masters <term>Siddhas</term>. || 33 ||

He states the benefits of *Nauli*. **Digestive fire...** "Sluggish fire" <term>mandagni</term> is the digestive fire <term>jatharagni</term>. "Kindling" <term>dipanam</term> means properly igniting it. "Digestion" <term>pachanam</term> means the cooking or processing of food that has been eaten. The phrase "and so forth" <note>original: *adi*</note> includes the purification of waste matter. It is "always" a "bringer of joy" or happiness. It "dries up" or removes "all imbalances" <term>doshas</term> such as wind <term>vata</term> and others, and "ailments" <term>amayah</term> or diseases. It is the "crown" <term>mauli</term> or the highest of the Hatha Yoga practices like *Dhauti* and others, because practices like *Dhauti* <note>cleansing the digestive tract with cloth</note> and *Basti* <note>yogic enema</note> depend upon the success of *Nauli*. This is the *Nauli* just described. || 34 ||

Now he describes *Kapalabhati* <note>The skull-shining breath</note> and its benefits. **Like the bellows...** Like the bellows used by a blacksmith for blowing air into a fire, performed with rapid exhalation and inhalation—

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Now *Nauli* is described. **With the speed...** A person who has lowered both shoulders should rotate the belly toward the left and right sides with a speed like that of a great whirlpool in water. This is called *Nauli* by the perfected masters. || 33 ||

The benefits of *Nauli* are described. **Digestive fire...** It increases the sluggish digestive fire, aids in the digestion of consumed food, and brings joy. It dries up all imbalances and diseases like wind <note>original: *vata*</note> and others. It is like a crown among Hatha Yoga practices like *Dhauti* and others, because both *Dhauti* and *Basti* require the performance of *Nauli*. Thus, this is called *Nauli*. || 34 ||

Now *Kapalabhati* and its benefits are described. **Like the bellows...** Like the bellows of a blacksmith—

<summary>This page defines Nauli as the rhythmic rotation of the abdominal muscles and praises it as the most important of the six purification acts for its ability to stimulate digestion and balance the body's humors. It concludes by introducing Kapalabhati, comparing its breathing technique to a blacksmith's bellows.</summary>

<keywords>Nauli, abdominal churning, digestive fire, jatharagni, Shatkarma, Hatha Yoga, Kapalabhati, purification, doshas</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Six Acts" (Shatkarma) of Hatha Yoga. Specifically, it concludes the description of Kapalabhati (the sixth act) and explains the differing opinions among ancient masters regarding whether these cleansing acts are mandatory before practicing breath control.</meta>

<page-num>५३</page-num>
<header>Second Instruction</header>

**Root Verse: The practice known as *Kapalabhati* <note>literally "Skull-Shining"</note> is famous for drying up imbalances of the phlegm <term>kapha-dosha</term>. || 35 ||**

**One whose obesity, phlegm imbalances, and impurities have been removed through the Six Acts should then perform breath control <term>pranayama</term>; through this, success is achieved without strain. || 36 ||**

**Because all impurities are dried up by breath control alone, some teachers do not approve of any other practice <note>referring to the Six Acts</note>. || 37 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The text continues from the previous page's description of a blacksmith's bellows made of skin. **Exhalation and inhalation** <term>rechaka-purakau</term> performed with the same rapid momentum and speed as those bellows are what is famously known as **Kapalabhati**. What is its nature? It is a **drier of phlegm imbalances**. The imbalances of phlegm <note>original: "kapha"</note> are divided into twenty different types. As stated in the *Nidana* <note>a classical medical text on diagnosis</note>: "Phlegm diseases are twenty-fold." <note>original: "kapha-rogash-cha vimshatih"</note> This practice is the **drier**, meaning the destroyer, of those diseases. || 35 ||

He now describes how the Six Acts <term>shatkarma</term> assist in the practice of breath control. **Through the Six Acts...** By the Six Acts, such as the "cleansing" <term>dhauti</term> and "water-enema" <term>vasti</term>, impurities are removed. **Obesity** <term>sthaulya</term> refers to the state of being heavy or thick. One who has removed the twenty types of phlegm imbalances and other impurities is described as "one whose impurities are gone." The word **and so on** <term>adi</term> refers to imbalances of the bile <term>pitta</term> and other humors. Such a person should perform **breath control**. Then, because of the preliminary Six Acts, one achieves success in **Yoga** through breath control **without strain**, meaning without excessive effort. The implication is that if one performs breath control without first doing the Six Acts, there would be too much physical labor and difficulty. || 36 ||

He mentions the lack of utility of the Six Acts according to a different school of thought. **By breath control alone...** The word **alone** <term>eva</term> is used to exclude the necessity of the Six Acts. **All impurities are dried up.** Here, "impurities" is a general term representing obesity, phlegm, bile, and so on. For this reason, for **some teachers** <term>acharyanam</term>—such as the sage Yajnavalkya and others—the **other practice**, namely the Six Acts, is **not approved** or favored. The definition of a "Teacher" <term>Acharya</term> is given in the *Vayu Purana*: "One who gathers the meaning of the scriptures, establishes others in right conduct, and practices it themselves—for these reasons, they are called an Acharya." || 37 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Performing rapid exhalation and inhalation like a bellows is called Kapalabhati. This Kapalabhati is the drier of the twenty types of phlegm imbalances. || 35 ||

Regarding the Six Acts: Through the six acts beginning with *Dhauti*, the state of obesity, phlegm imbalances, and other impurities like bile are removed. Afterward, one should perform breath control. By doing these first, Yoga is perfected without any bodily fatigue. || 36 ||

Through breath control alone, all impurities are removed. Therefore, in the view of Yajnavalkya and other masters, these Six Acts of purification are not considered mandatory. || 37 ||

<summary>This page describes Kapalabhati as a cure for twenty types of phlegm-related illnesses and explains that while some masters require the Six Acts to make breath control (Pranayama) easier, others like Yajnavalkya believe Pranayama alone is sufficient to purify the body.</summary>

<keywords>Kapalabhati, Shatkarma, Pranayama, Kapha, Phlegm, Yajnavalkya, Vayu Purana, purification, obesity, Ayurvedic humors</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, moving from the Six Acts (Shatkarma) to the fundamental motivation for practicing breath control (Pranayama).</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>In the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Verse: By raising the downward-moving breath <term>apana</term> into the throat, they vomit up the contents of the stomach. Because the network of energy channels is brought under control through gradual practice, this is called the "Elephant Act" <term>Gajakarani</term> by those skilled in Hatha Yoga. || 38 ||**

**Even the gods, beginning with Brahma, were devoted to the practice of breath out of fear of death. Therefore, one should practice the breath. || 39 ||**

**As long as the breath is bound within the body, as long as the mind is undisturbed, and as long as the gaze is between the eyebrows, why should there be fear of death? || 40 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He describes the "Elephant Act" <term>Gajakarani</term> in the verse beginning with: **The contents of the stomach...** By raising the **downward-moving breath** <note>original: "apanam pavanatam"</note> into the **throat-pipe** <note>original: "kanthanala"</note>—the throat being like a pipe—the yogis **vomit up** <note>original: "udvamanti"</note> or expel the **substances** that have reached the stomach, such as food and water that have been eaten or drunk. This is the intended meaning. This action, in which the **network of energy channels** <term>nadi-chakra</term> is made **submissive** or under one’s own control through **gradual practice** or habituation, is called **Gajakarani** by those **skilled in Hatha Yoga**. In some versions, the text reads "the path of the channels is mastered through gradual practice," which means that the path of the *Shankhini* channel <note>a specific energy channel in yogic anatomy ending in the throat</note> up to the throat becomes controlled through regular practice. || 38 ||

He now explains, over the next four verses, that breath control <term>pranayama</term> must be practiced because it was practiced by the highest beings and because it yields great results. **Even the gods, beginning with Brahma...** Those such as Brahma and others. The implication is: if even they did so, what need is there to mention others? The **gods** <note>original: "tridashah"</note> became **devoted** or attentive to the **practice of breath**—which consists of performing breath control divided into exhalation <term>rechaka</term>, inhalation <term>puraka</term>, and retention <term>kumbhaka</term>—out of **fear of death** <note>original: "antaka-bhaya"</note>, meaning fear of time, which finishes all things. **Therefore, one should practice the breath** <term>pranam</term>. || 39 ||

**As long as...** For as long as the **breath** <term>marut</term> or vital air is **bound** or held in the **body**, and the inhalation and exhalation...

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Here, the "Elephant Act" <term>Gajakarani</term> is described. **The contents of the stomach...** By raising the **apana** wind into the throat and expelling the food and water that was eaten or drunk, and by doing this practice in sequence, the group of energy channels is brought under control. Such an action is called **Gajakarani** by yogis who know Hatha Yoga. || 38 ||

**The gods, beginning with Brahma...** Even the gods, starting with Brahma, became devoted to the practice of breath because of the fear of death. Therefore, one should practice the breath. || 39 ||

**As long as...** As long as the air remains stopped in the body, and as long as the inner self <note>original: "antahkarana"</note> is not agitated...

<summary>This page describes the "Elephant Act" (Gajakarani) for internal cleansing and begins a section praising breath control (Pranayama) as a means to transcend the fear of death, noting that even the creator god Brahma practiced it.</summary>
<keywords>Gajakarani, Elephant Act, Pranayama, Breath Control, Apana, Nadi, Energy Channels, Brahma, Death, Yoga anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, concluding the commentary on Verse 40 and detailing the results of breath control leading to the state of Manonmani.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>The Second Instruction</header>

**Root Verse: When the network of energy channels <term>nadi-chakra</term> is purified by breath controls <term>pranasamyama</term> performed according to the rules, the breath <term>maruta</term> pierces the opening of the central channel <term>sushumna</term> and enters it with ease. || 41 ||**

**Root Verse: When the breath moves through the middle path, mental stability arises. That state of perfect mental steadiness is called the "mind-transcending" state <term>manonmani</term>. || 42 ||**

**To achieve that, those who know the procedures perform various breath retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...devoid of action. As long as the mind <note>original: "chitta" or the inner faculty</note> is undisturbed, not distracted, and composed. As long as the gaze <note>the focus of the inner faculty</note> is between the eyebrows—the word "gaze" <note>original: "drishi"</note> here refers to general awareness—then for that long, Time <note>the power of death/change</note> counts or tracks the person; so where can fear come from? It means it comes from nowhere at all. As the author will state later: "The yogi who is joined in meditative absorption <term>samadhi</term> is not eaten by Time, not bound by his actions <term>karma</term>, and cannot be overcome by anyone." This means he becomes self-mastered. || 40 ||

Regarding **"According to the rules"**: When the **network of energy channels**—meaning the collection of channels—is made **pure** or stainless by **breath controls** <term>pranayama</term> that are joined with the rules of postures <term>asana</term> and the throat-lock <term>jalandhara-bandha</term>, then the **breath** <term>vayu</term> pierces the **mouth** or opening of the **Sushumna**—the channel situated between the *Ida* <note>the left channel</note> and *Pingala* <note>the right channel</note>—and **enters with ease** or without effort. "Into the Sushumna" is the implied remainder of the sentence. || 41 ||

Regarding **"When the breath"**: When the **breath** <note>original: "prana-vayu"</note> has its **movement** or proper travel through the **middle**—meaning through the center of the Sushumna—up to the head, then **mental stability** arises; a continuous flow of mental states taking the form of the object of meditation is produced. That **perfectly steady state** of the mind is the **Manonmani state**. The word *Manonmani* is a synonym for *Unmani* <note>the state beyond mind or the Fourth state of consciousness</note>. He will say as much later with the words: "Raja Yoga and Samadhi," and so on. || 42 ||

To create a desire in the student to engage in the **various retentions** <term>kumbhaka</term>, he describes their primary and secondary results with the verse: **"To achieve that..."**

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

Indeed. As long as the gaze remains between the eyebrows, how can there be fear of Time? There is none at all. || 40 ||

Regarding **"According to the rules"**: When the group of energy channels becomes pure through the practice of breath control <term>pranayama</term> performed with postures and the throat-lock, then the breath pierces the opening of the **Sushumna** channel, which lies between the *Ida* and *Pingala*, and enters the Sushumna comfortably. || 41 ||

Regarding **"When the breath"**: When the breath travels inside the Sushumna, then the mind attains stability. This means the flow of the mind's activity stays in a form suitable for meditation. This stable state of mind is called the **Manonmani** state. The "Fourth state" <term>turiya</term> is also called *Unmani* and *Manonmani*. || 42 ||

Regarding **"To achieve that"**: And those who know the methods for practicing retention <term>kumbhaka</term> perform them to reach the state of Unmani...

<summary>This page explains that purification of the energy channels allows breath to enter the central Sushumna channel, which naturally leads the mind into the high meditative state of Manonmani, where the yogi transcends the fear of death.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Sushumna, Manonmani, Nadi purification, Unmani, Samadhi, breath retention, yoga anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It follows the explanation of how breath enters the central channel (sushumna) to create the mind-transcending state (manonmani) and begins the specific catalog of the eight types of breath retention.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>In the Illumination of Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Verse: Through the practice of various breath retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>, one attains various supernatural perfections <term>siddhi</term>. || 43 ||**

->**The Varieties of Breath Retention**<-

**Sun-piercing <term>surya-bhedana</term>, the Victorious <term>ujjayi</term>, the Hissing <term>sitkari</term>, the Cooling <term>shitali</term>, as well as the Bellows <term>bhastrika</term>, the Bee-like <term>bhramari</term>, the Swooning <term>murcha</term>, and the Floating <term>plavini</term>—these are the eight retentions. || 44 ||**

**At the end of the inhalation <term>puraka</term>, the lock called Jalandhara <term>jalandhara-bandha</term> should be performed. At the end of the retention <term>kumbhaka</term> and the beginning of the exhalation <term>rechaka</term>, the Uddiyana lock <term>uddiyana-bandha</term> should be performed. || 45 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding **"Through the practice..."**: Those who know the procedures <note>meaning the specific methods of holding the breath</note> perform various kinds of retentions—such as the Sun-piercing and others—in order to achieve that goal, which is the attainment of the mind-transcending state <term>unmani-avastha</term>. By the practice or performance of these "various retentions," one attains "various" perfections. These perfections include powers like becoming infinitesimally small <term>anima</term>, or those born of birth, herbs, mantras, or austerities. As stated in the Bhagavata <note>a sacred devotional text</note>: <note>original: "janmauṣadhitapomantraiiryathāvatīriha siddhayaḥ | yoghenāpnoti tāḥ sarvā nānyairyoghaghatiṃ vrajet ||"</note> "Just as perfections are attained in this world through birth, herbs, austerities, and mantras, the yogi attains them all through yoga; no other can reach the state of yoga." The meaning is that one attains these through the progression of the limbs of yoga, such as sensory withdrawal <term>pratyahara</term>. || 43 ||

Now the names of the eight retentions are specified: **"Sun-piercing..."** This is clear. || 44 ||

Now, he describes the technique for the three locks, which is a unique method for success in Hatha Yoga used by the highest order of renunciates <term>paramahamsa</term> and common to all retentions: **"At the end of the inhalation..."** The lock named **Jalandhara** is so-called because it "binds" <note>original: "bandha"</note> the vital breath <term>prana-vayu</term>. The Jalandhara lock consists of contracting the throat and placing the chin firmly on the heart; it should be performed quickly at the end of the inhalation <term>puraka</term>, meaning immediately after inhaling. The word "but" <note>original: "tu"</note> implies it is done at the beginning of the retention. The **Uddiyana** lock, however, is performed at the end of the retention—when a small amount of the retention remains—and at the beginning of the exhalation <term>rechaka</term>, meaning just before exhaling. It is done with effort...

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

To achieve success, those who know the procedures describe various types of retentions <term>kumbhaka</term> such as Sun-piercing and others. Through the practice of these various retentions, various perfections <term>siddhi</term> are obtained. || 43 ||

Now the varieties of retention are stated: **"Sun-piercing..."** 1. Sun-piercing, 2. the Victorious, 3. the Hissing, 4. the Cooling, 5. the Bellows, 6. the Bee-like, 7. the Swooning, 8. the Floating—these are the eight retentions. || 44 ||

**"At the end of the inhalation..."** First, perform the root lock <term>mula-bandha</term> <note>the contraction of the perineum, though not explicitly in this verse, it is often implied in the sequence</note>. Then, at the end of the inhalation, quickly perform the Jalandhara lock. Lower the neck and place the chin slightly above the heart; this is the Jalandhara lock. And at the end of the retention, when a little bit of the breath remains, perform the Uddiyana lock at the start of the exhalation. Pulling the navel back with effort is the Uddiyana lock. || 45 ||

<summary>This page lists the eight primary types of breath retention (kumbhaka) in Hatha Yoga and introduces the essential internal "locks" (bandhas)—Jalandhara and Uddiyana—that must accompany them to direct the vital energy.</summary>
<keywords>eight kumbhakas, Surya-bhedana, Ujjayi, Sitkari, Shitali, Bhastrika, Bhramari, Murcha, Plavini, Jalandhara-bandha, Uddiyana-bandha, siddhi, Bhagavata Purana</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, moving from the list of eight breath retentions to the specific internal "locks" or bandhas required to direct the breath into the central channel.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>The Second Instruction</header>
<sig>8</sig>

**Root Verse: By contraction from below, by a quick contraction of the throat, and by drawing [the navel] back in the middle, the life-breath <term>prana</term> enters the Brahma-channel <term>brahma-nadi</term>. || 46 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Specifically, the pulling back of the navel region toward the spine is the "Flying-upward lock" <term>uddiyana-bandha</term>. || 45 ||

Regarding **"By contraction from below..."**: A "contraction of the throat" <note>original: kaṇṭha-saṃkocanam</note> refers to the performance of the Net-bearer lock <term>jalandhara-bandha</term>. Immediately following that, through "contraction from below"—meaning the contraction of the base or perineum—one performs the Root lock <term>mula-bandha</term>. Then, "in the middle" at the navel region, one performs a "pulling back" <note>original: paścimatānaṃ</note> toward the spine, which is the Flying-upward lock <term>uddiyana-bandha</term>. When these three locks are performed in the manner described, the breath <note>the internal air or vayu</note> goes to the Brahma-channel <term>brahma-nadi</term> <note>also known as the sushumna</note>; thus, it is said one "enters the Brahma-channel."

There is a secret here: If the Tongue lock <term>jihva-bandha</term> is properly understood from the mouth of a respected Guru, then breath control <term>pranayama</term> is perfected through the Tongue lock combined with the Throat lock alone. In that case, the signs of success appear—such as the absence of digestive wind disorders, a slender body <note>original: vapuḥ-kṛśatvam</note>, and a bright, pleasant face <note>original: vadane prasannatā</note>. Consequently, the Root lock and Flying-upward lock are not strictly necessary, as their purpose is fulfilled by the Throat lock when preceded by the Tongue lock. 

However, if the Tongue lock is not known, one must perform breath control according to the method in this verse: "by contraction from below." All three locks must be learned directly from a Guru's mouth. The Root lock <term>mula-bandha</term>, if not properly understood, can produce various diseases. For instance, if one performs the Root lock and experiences a wasting of bodily tissues <term>dhatu-kshaya</term>, constipation, sluggish digestion, or stool that is hard and shaped like goat droppings <note>original: ajasya-iva purīṣaṃ</note>, it should be understood that the Root lock is not being practiced correctly. If, on the other hand, there is nourishment of the tissues, proper elimination, a strong digestive fire, and the manifestation of inner sounds <term>nada</term>, then one knows the Root lock has been mastered. || 46 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Regarding **"By contraction from below..."**: One should contract the base in the lower region to perform the Root lock, then perform the Throat lock, and then the Flying-upward lock. Through these three locks, the breath reaches the Brahma-channel, which is the <term>sushumna</term>. 

The secret mentioned is this: if one learns the Tongue lock from a Guru, then breath control is perfected simply by the Throat lock preceded by the Tongue lock. In that state, no wind-related disorders occur, the body remains disciplined and slender, and the face remains bright. In this case, the Root and Flying-upward locks are not useful <note>redundant</note>, as their benefits are achieved through the Tongue and Throat locks. 

If the Tongue lock is not known, then it is proper to practice breath control according to the method stated in the verse "By contraction from below." All three locks must be known from a Guru. If the Root lock is not known well, it manifests various diseases. If performed without proper mastery, it leads to the wasting of bodily fluids, constipation, a dulling of the digestive fire, a weakening of the inner sound, and waste shaped like small pellets.

<summary>This page explains the simultaneous application of three physical locks (Mula, Jalandhara, and Uddiyana Bandha) to force the breath into the central channel, while introducing a "secret" alternative method involving a tongue lock.</summary>
<keywords>Mula Bandha, Jalandhara Bandha, Uddiyana Bandha, Jihva Bandha, Sushumna, Brahma-nadi, Pranayama, digestive fire, Guru, nada</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the explanation of the three internal "locks" (bandhas) and their effects on the body’s vitality, specifically the reversal of aging. It then transitions into the specific techniques of the eight types of breath retention, beginning with the Sun-Piercing Breath.</meta>

<page-num>( 58 )</page-num>
<header>In the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Verse: Raising the downward-moving breath <term>apana</term> upward and bringing the life-breath <term>prana</term> down from the throat, the yogi becomes free from old age and attains the vitality of a sixteen-year-old. || 47 ||**

### Now, the Sun-Piercing Breath <term>surya-bhedana</term>

**The yogi, having taken a comfortable seat and assumed the posture...**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding **"the downward-moving breath..."**: By contracting the foundation <note>the perineum or muladhara</note>, one raises the downward-moving air <term>apana-vayu</term> upward. Simultaneously, one should bring the life-breath <term>prana-vayu</term> down from the throat. The one who achieves this is a "Yogi"—one who possesses yoga through constant practice. Such a person becomes "free from old age," meaning specifically liberated from the infirmities of advanced years. They become "sixteen years of age" <note>original: ṣoḍaśābda-vayo</note>, a term referring to the peak of youthful vigor. 

Although verses 45 through 47 seem to cover the same ground, they have distinct purposes: Verse 45 explained the timing of the locks <note>to be done at the end of inhalation</note>; Verse 46 explained the physical form or technique of the locks; and this verse (47) explains the specific result or fruit of the locks. While the "Flying-upward lock" <term>uddiyana-bandha</term> (the drawing in of the navel) is not explicitly named in this specific verse, it occurs naturally when the Throat lock and Root lock are performed together. This is confirmed by the saint Jnaneshvara in his commentary on the sixth chapter of the Bhagavad Gita: "When the Root lock and Throat lock are performed, the contraction of the region below the navel happens of its own accord." || 47 ||

> "I shall now declare the sequence of yoga practice for the success of yogis. A wise person should rise at dawn <note>original: uṣaḥ-kāle</note> or early morning. || 1 || Mentally remembering the Guru at the crown of the head and their Chosen Deity <term>ishta-devata</term> in the heart, one should perform purification, clean the teeth, and apply sacred ash <term>bhasma</term>. || 2 || In a clean, pleasant monastery <term>matha</term>, having established a soft seat, one sits there and remembers the Guru and the Lord with the mind. || 3 || After announcing the time and place and making a formal vow <term>sankalpa</term> according to tradition, saying: 'Today, by the grace of the Supreme Lord, I shall perform the postures, breath control, and other practices to attain the goal of deep meditation <term>samadhi</term>.' One should bow to the infinite God, the Lord of Serpents, to attain mastery of the seat. || 4 || Salutations to the infinite King of Serpents, who supports the circle of the earth with a thousand shimmering hoods. Then, one should practice the postures. If fatigue arises, one should perform the Corpse Pose <term>shavasana</term> at the end. However, if there is no fatigue, one should not—"

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

When the stool is loose like a goat’s droppings, one should know that the Root lock is not being performed correctly. Know that the Root lock is mastered when the physical humors <term>dhatus</term> become firm, the body is purified of waste, the digestive fire <term>jatharagni</term> blazes brightly, and the internal divine sound <term>nada</term> begins to manifest. || 46 ||

Regarding **"the downward-moving breath..."**: By raising the *apana* air upward and contracting the foundation, and bringing the air down from the throat, that yogi is released from the state of old age and becomes like a sixteen-year-old. || 47 ||

Among the eight types of breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term>, the first, "Sun-Piercing" <term>surya-bhedana</term>, and its qualities are now described. Posture...

<meta>catchword: Posture</meta>

<summary>This page explains how balancing the internal winds (Prana and Apana) through physical locks grants a yogi youthful vitality. It also outlines the daily ritual preparations for a yoga session and introduces Surya Bhedana, the first of the eight major breathing techniques.</summary>

<keywords>Apana, Prana, youthfulness, Jnaneshvara, yoga sequence, daily ritual, Surya Bhedana, Root lock, Throat lock, vital winds</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the Sun-Piercing Breath (Surya-bhedana), detailing the daily routine, dietary habits, and the sequence of preparatory practices a yogi must follow before engaging in advanced breath retention.</meta>

( 59 )
# Chapter Two

**Root Verse: Slowly drawing in the external air through the right nostril <term>dakshina-nadi</term>... || 48 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

...one should practice. || 5 || One should practice the technique called the "Inverted Action" <term>viparita-karani</term> <note>an inversion where the positions of the sun (belly) and moon (head) are reversed, often identified with a headstand or shoulder-stand</note> before breath retention. This is done to gain the grace of the Throat Lock <term>jalandhara-bandha</term> through prior yoga practice. || 6 || 

After performing the ritual sipping of water <term>achamana</term> and breath control <term>pranayama</term> as part of the ritual duties, and having bowed to the Lord of Yogis and others, as stated in the *Kurma Purana* in the words of Shiva: || 7 || 

The word of Shiva in the *Kurma Purana*: "Having bowed to the Lord of Yogis, the disciples, Ganesha <note>the remover of obstacles, here called Vinayaka</note>, the Guru, and then to Me, the yogi should practice with a perfectly concentrated mind." || 8 || 

Having assumed the "Perfect Posture" <term>siddhasana</term> for practice, beginning with the locks during breath retention, ten retentions should be performed on the first day. This should be increased by five every day. || 9 || 

Those with a concentrated mind should perform up to eighty breath retentions. The Lord of Yogis should first practice the exercise of the "Moon" and the "Sun" <note>the left and right nostrils</note>. || 10 || 

The wise call this practice "Alternate Nostril Breathing" <term>anuloma-viloma</term>. Then, with a single-pointed mind, one should practice the "Sun-Piercing Breath" <term>surya-bhedana</term> along with the locks. || 11 || 

Then one should perform the "Victorious Breath" <term>ujjayi</term>, then the "Hissing Breath" <term>sitkari</term>, and the "Cooling Breath" <term>shitali</term>. Having thoroughly practiced the "Bellows Breath" <term>bhastrika</term>, one should then perform the others. || 12 || 

One should practice the Seals <term>mudras</term> in the proper order as learned from the mouth of the Guru. Then, having assumed the "Lotus Posture" <term>padmasana</term>, one should contemplate the internal sound <term>nada</term>. || 13 || 

One should perform the entire practice with devotion, offering it to the Lord <term>ishvara</term>. After rising from practice, one should bathe with warm water. || 14 || 

Having bathed, the wise one should briefly complete their daily duties. At midday, they should practice again, and after resting a little, they should take their meal. || 15 || 

One should eat only what is wholesome <term>pathya</term> for yogis and never what is unwholesome. One may eat cardamom or cloves after the meal. || 16 || 

Some desire camphor or a fine betel leaf <term>tambula</term>. For those practicing the yoga of breath, it is best if the betel is prepared without lime <note>original: churna</note>. || 17 || 

This statement from the *Chintamani* text is not entirely satisfactory, as some say those two <note>camphor and cloves</note> are used for their cooling or heating properties. || 18 || 

After the meal, one should look into the scriptures of liberation <term>moksha-shastra</term>, listen to the ancient histories <term>puranas</term>, or sing the names of the Lord. || 19 || 

Having performed the evening twilight rituals, one should practice yoga as before. Practice when there are three *ghatikas* <note>about 72 minutes</note> of daylight remaining. || 20 || 

After the practice, the wise should always perform the evening twilight rituals. At midnight, the self-controlled one should perform the Hatha Yoga practice as before. || 21 || 

However, the "Inverted Action" <term>viparita-karani</term> should not be practiced during the evening or at midnight after eating, as it is not recommended then. || 22 || 

Now, wishing to explain the sequence of breath retentions <term>kumbhakas</term>, he describes the first one mentioned:

->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

As stated: The yogi, as described by the sage Sukadeva, should reside in a pure place. One's seat should be stable—neither too high nor too low. In solitude, one should sit comfortably. While there are many postures like the "Auspicious Posture" <term>svastikasana</term>, the "Hero Posture" <term>virasana</term>, the "Perfect Posture" <term>siddhasana</term>, and the "Lotus Posture" <term>padmasana</term>, the "Perfect Posture" is the most important. Having assumed that seat, one should slowly draw in the external air through the <term>pingala</term> nerve <note>the right nostril</note> located on the right side and perform the inhalation <term>puraka</term>. || 48 ||

<summary>This page outlines the rigorous daily schedule and ethical preparations for a yogi, including a progression of breathing exercises (Kumbhakas) and dietary recommendations like avoiding lime in betel leaves. It emphasizes the Sun-Piercing Breath as a primary practice to be integrated with specific postures and mental devotion.</summary>

<keywords>Surya-bhedana, Siddhasana, Kumbhaka, Pingala, dietary rules for yogis, Kurma Purana, Viparita-karani, pranayama sequence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the "Sun-Piercing Breath" (Surya-bhedana), specifically focusing on the intensity of breath retention and the correct method for exhalation through the left nostril, following the inhalation through the right described on the previous page.</meta>

( 60 )
# Hatha Yoga Pradipika

**Root Verse: One should retain the breath until the limit of retention, from the hair of the head to the tips of the toenails. Then, one should slowly exhale the air through the left nostril <note>original: "savya-nadya"</note>. || 49 ||**

->|| Commentary ||<-

The author describes the technique of Sun-Piercing and its qualities in three verses. Regarding the posture: "that which gives comfort is comfortable" <note>a play on the word "Sukhada"</note>. As it is said: "Having established a steady seat for oneself in a clean place, neither too high nor too low, covered with a cloth, an antelope skin, and kusha grass <note>original: "chailajinakushottaram"</note>." In a solitary place with the characteristics described, the yogi remains pure and sits in a comfortable posture. The scripture <note>original: "shruti"</note> also states, "with the neck, head, and body held straight." An "asana" is that upon which one sits, or the act of sitting itself. The yogi is the practitioner of yoga. One should assume a posture such as the Auspicious <term>svastikasana</term>, Hero <term>virasana</term>, Perfect <term>siddhasana</term>, or Lotus <term>padmasana</term>. Because the Perfect Posture is the most important, one should firmly assume it. Then, after assuming the posture, one should slowly draw in the external air through the right-side channel known as the Sun Channel <term>pingala</term>. This means one performs the inhalation <term>puraka</term> slowly through the right nostril. || 48 ||

"From the hair": This refers to the boundary starting from the hair of the head down to the boundary of the tips of the nails. One should perform breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> until the absolute limit of the breath's restraint. This means one should exert great effort to ensure the breath is held throughout the entire body, reaching even the hair and nails. Now, a doubt arises: "If the breath is forcefully restrained by the Hatha method, it may escape through the pores of the skin. This tears the body and even produces diseases like leprosy <note>original: "kushthadi"</note>." Therefore, it must be tamed gradually, like a wild elephant in the forest. A wild elephant or a lion is brought to gentleness slowly. One must follow the instructions of the scriptures and not transgress them. In this way, the breath residing in the heart is brought under control by the yogi through gradual practice...

->|| Vernacular Explanation ||<-

"From the hair": One should retain the air from the hair to the tips of the nails; that is, one should perform breath retention with great effort. However, if the breath is forcefully stopped and then escapes through the pores, it tears the body and causes diseases like leprosy to appear. Just as one might capture a forest elephant or a lion slowly and methodically with ease—but if one acts with haste, it causes distress and the animal cannot be captured—likewise, one should perform breath retention with careful effort.

If a practitioner thinks, "I will conquer the life-force <term>prana</term> immediately," and becomes obsessed with excessive practice, it will be harmful. Therefore, it is said that one should proceed gradually, like taming a forest elephant. Some also say one should practice retention with extreme effort: the more effort one applies, the greater the benefit; the more sluggish the retention, the lesser the benefit. The experience of the great yogis is the proof of this. Inhalation <term>puraka</term> should be done slowly, though if done quickly, there is no fault. However, exhalation <term>rechaka</term> must be done very slowly. If one exhales too quickly, there is a loss of strength. Therefore, one should exhale the air very slowly through the left channel, the Moon Channel <term>ida</term>. || 49 ||

<summary>This page explains the final steps of Surya-bhedana pranayama, emphasizing full-body breath retention and the necessity of exhaling slowly through the left nostril to avoid physical injury and disease.</summary>
<keywords>Surya-bhedana, Kumbhaka, Pingala, Ida, Siddhasana, breath retention, gradual practice, leprosy prevention</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Surya-bhedana (Sun-Piercing Breath), emphasizing the necessity of intense breath retention and the correct method of exhalation. It then transitions into the benefits of this practice before introducing the second breathing technique, Ujjayi.</meta>

( 61 )
## Second Lesson

## **Root Verse: It is a cleanser of the skull <term>kapalashodhana</term>, a destroyer of wind-humor disorders <term>vatadosha</term>, and a remover of the affliction of worms. || 50 ||**
## **This excellent Sun-Piercing breath should be performed again and again. || 50 ||**

### Now, Ujjayi.

## **Closing the mouth, one should slowly draw in the breath through both nostrils <note>original: "nadibhyam"</note> so that it is felt from the throat down to the heart with a sound. || 51 ||**

### ->|| Commentary ||<-

If one asks, "Is it not contradictory to the statement 'it goes' <note>referring to a potential loss of control</note> to say that breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> should be performed with great effort?"—the answer is no. The statement "this breath is restrained by force" <note>original: "hatha-niruddhah prano'yam"</note> implies a mental resolve: "I shall conquer the breath shortly." In this way, because the practitioner is dedicated to intense practice, the breath is tamed gradually, like a wild elephant <note>original: "aranya-hastivat"</note>; this analogy illustrates the proper sense. Therefore, the instruction to hold the breath to one's full capacity after practicing with the sun and moon channels, and to restrain it, is consistent. 

Thus, breath retention must be performed with great effort. The more effort one applies to the retention, the greater the benefits produced. Conversely, if the retention is lax, the benefits will be meager. The experience of yogis is the authority on this matter. As for the inhalation <term>puraka</term>, it may be done very slowly or it may be done quickly. There is no fault in performing the inhalation quickly. However, the exhalation <term>rechaka</term> must only be performed very slowly. If the exhalation is done quickly, there is a risk of losing one's strength. Therefore, "one should exhale very slowly and not with speed," as stated by the author in many places. 

Then, after the retention has reached its limit, one should very slowly and gradually exhale the air through the left-side channel <note>original: "savya-nadi"</note>, also known as the <term>ida</term>. The repetition of the word "slowly" is for the sake of emphasis, meaning it *must* be done slowly. As it is said: "In wonder, in sorrow, in distress, in emphasis, as well as in propitiation and joy, a single word is spoken twice." || 49 ||

Regarding "Cleansing of the skull": This refers to the purification of the head. It is a "destroyer of wind-humor disorders" <note>original: "vatadoshaghnam"</note> because it destroys the eighty types of disorders born from the wind humor <term>vata</term>. It is a "remover of the affliction of worms" because it removes the disorders caused by worms born in the abdomen. It should be performed "again and again," meaning repeatedly. By following the method of filling through the "Sun" (right nostril), retaining, and exhaling through the "Moon" (left nostril), this practice is called the excellent Sun-Piercing Breath. "By yogis" is the implied subject here. || 50 ||

The author describes the *Ujjayi* breath in one and a half verses. "The mouth": Having restrained or closed the mouth...

### ->|| Vernacular Translation ||<-

The qualities of Sun-Piercing breath are described. "Cleansing of the skull": It purifies the head and removes the eighty types of disorders arising from the wind humor. It also removes the disorders caused by worms that have entered the stomach. Therefore, one should do this repeatedly. Fill the breath through the "Sun" (right) and exhale the air through the "Moon" (left); this is the excellent method. Among the yogis, Sun-Piercing was described first. || 50 ||

Now, the second breath, *Ujjayi*, is described. "The mouth": Closing the mouth, taking the breath from the throat...

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of Sun-Piercing Breath (Suryabhedana), detailing its medicinal benefits for wind disorders and parasites, and begins the instructions for Ujjayi breath.</summary>
<keywords>Suryabhedana, Ujjayi, Kumbhaka, Vata disorders, Kapalashodhana, breath retention, Hatha Yoga, yoga therapy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for Ujjayi (The Victorious Breath), detailing the retention phase, exhalation through the left nostril, and the extensive physiological benefits of the practice, including its unique suitability for practice during movement.</meta>

( 62 )
# In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika

**Root Verse: One should hold the breath <term>kumbhaka</term> as before and then exhale through the Ida <note>the left nostril</note>. This removes phlegm disorders in the throat and increases the body’s digestive fire. || 52 ||**

**It destroys disorders of the channels <term>nadis</term>, dropsy <term>jalodara</term>, and defects residing in the bodily tissues <term>dhatu</term>. This breath retention known as Ujjayi should be performed whether walking or standing. || 53 ||**

->### || Commentary ||<-

The phrase "having closed" <note>original: "mudrayitva"</note> means closing the mouth. The breath should be drawn from the throat down to the heart in such a way that it is accompanied by a sound. Both "from the throat" and "to the heart" describe the action. This means the air should graze or touch the surface of the throat. In this manner, one should slowly draw the air in through both channels—the Ida <note>left</note> and Pingala <note>right</note>—and fill the lungs. || 51 ||

Regarding the phrase **"as before"** <note>original: "purvavat"</note>: One should hold or restrain the breath <term>kumbhaka</term> in the same manner as described for the Sun-Piercing breath <term>surya-bhedana</term>. As stated previously: "one should hold the breath until it is felt from the hair of the head to the tips of the nails." After this retention, one should exhale or release the breath through the Ida, which is the left channel.

The author describes the benefits of Ujjayi in the next verse and a half. **"Removes phlegm disorders"**: In the region of the throat, it removes disorders of the phlegm <term>shleshma</term>, such as coughing and similar ailments. **"Increases the body's fire"**: This specifically refers to the "fire in the belly" <term>jatharagni</term>, meaning it stimulates and kindles the digestive power. || 52 ||

**"Channels and so on"**: This refers to the veins or channels <term>nadis</term>, "water" <note>original: "jalam"</note> referring to the disease of dropsy <note>fluid retention in the abdomen</note>, and the "tissues" <term>dhatus</term> which are present throughout the body. The compound word describes the destruction of those defects or disorders that have entered these systems. A person may perform this practice called Ujjayi while "walking" <note>moving</note> or while "standing" <note>stationary</note>. The word "but" <note>original: "tu"</note> highlights the unique distinction of this practice. It is something that should be done <note>original: "karyam"</note>. In some readings, the name is given as "Ujjayi." However, when practiced while walking or standing, it should be performed without the chin and root locks <term>bandhas</term>. The word "kumbhaka" <note>retention</note> can be used in all three grammatical genders; in masculine readings, the adjectives must also follow the masculine form. || 53 ||

->### || Vernacular Translation ||<-

One should slowly draw the breath in through both the Ida and Pingala <note>both nostrils</note> with sound, feeling it from the throat to the heart, and perform the inhalation <term>puraka</term>. Then, perform breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> until the sensation reaches the hair and nails. Afterward, exhale through the Ida, which is the left nostril. || 51 ||

The benefits of Ujjayi are stated: **"Removes phlegm disorders"**. It removes the faults of phlegm in the throat. It also kindles the digestive fire within the body. || 52 ||

It destroys the pain of excess water in the channels, and the disorders and defects in the tissues currently in the body. This Ujjayi is worthy of being performed by a person whether they are walking or standing still. || 53 ||

<summary>This page explains the Ujjayi breathing technique, involving a resonant inhalation through both nostrils, retention, and exhalation through the left nostril. It is highlighted as a cure for throat ailments, digestive issues, and fluid retention, and is unique for being permissible while moving.</summary>

<keywords>Ujjayi, Kumbhaka, digestive fire, jatharagni, phlegm, shleshma, dropsy, jalodara, Ida, Pingala, breath retention, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

( 63 )
<header>Second Instruction.</header>

## Now, Sitkari <term>The Hissing Breath</term>

> **Root Verse: One should make the sound "sit" in the mouth during inhalation; through the nose alone, one should perform the exhalation <note>original: "vijṛmbhikām"</note>. By the practice of this yoga, one becomes a second God of Love <term>Kamadeva</term>. || 54 ||**
>
> **He is held in high esteem by the circle of Yoginis and becomes the creator and destroyer of the universe. Neither hunger, nor thirst, nor sleep, nor laziness will ever arise for him. || 55 ||**

->### || Commentary ||<-

The author now describes the Sitkari retention <term>kumbhaka</term> with the verse beginning **"One should make the sound 'sit'."** In the "mouth" <note>original: "vaktre"</note>, one should produce the sound *sit*—this "hissing" is the *sit-kara*. The meaning is that one should perform the inhalation <term>puraka</term> through the mouth with the tongue touching the space between the lips, accompanied by the "sit" sound. The phrase **"through the nose alone"** <note>original: "ghrāṇenaiva"</note> specifies that the exhalation <term>rechaka</term> must be performed through both nostrils.

The word "alone" <note>original: "eva"</note> excludes the use of the mouth for exhaling. One must never release the air through the mouth, even after long practice, because doing so causes a loss of physical strength. The term **"exhalation"** <note>original: "vijṛmbhikām"</note> is also connected to this instruction. Although the retention of breath <term>kumbhaka</term> itself is not explicitly mentioned in the root verse, it must be understood to be present because Sitkari is classified as a form of *kumbhaka*.

Now, the praise of Sitkari is given. **"Practice"** <note>original: "abhyāsa"</note> refers to the repeated performance of the technique; this is the "yoga" because it is a means to achieve yoga. Through this, one becomes a "second God of Love" <term>Kamadeva</term>. This is due to the extraordinary increase in the practitioner's physical beauty and charm, making them resemble the deity of desire. || 54 ||

Regarding **"The circle of Yoginis"**: This refers to the assembly or group of Yoginis <note>female practitioners or semi-divine guardians of yoga</note>. The practitioner becomes "common" to them, meaning he is fit to be served or honored by them. He becomes the "creator and destroyer," meaning he gains the power over the manifestation <note>original: "sṛṣṭi"</note> and the dissolution <note>original: "saṃhāra"</note> of the world. **"Hunger"** is the desire to eat; it does not occur. **"Thirst"** is the desire to drink water; it does not occur. **"Sleep"** <note>slumber</note> does not occur. **"Laziness"** is the absence of the impulse to act, caused by a heaviness of body and mind. Heaviness of the body is caused by phlegm <term>kapha</term>, while heaviness of the mind is caused by the quality of darkness <term>tamas</term>. These do not manifest. The phrases "by the practice of yoga" and "does not arise" should be applied to each of these benefits. || 55 ||

->### || Language Translation ||<-

Now the third breath, Sitkari, is described. Regarding **"One should make a 'sit' sound"**: One should place the tongue between the lips in the mouth, make the "sit" sound, and draw the air in (inhale). Then, one should exhale through both nostrils. One should not release the air through the mouth even after the practice is mastered, as it harms one's strength; therefore, one should perform the "yawning" exhalation <note>original: "vijṛmbhikā"</note>, meaning the mouth remains closed while exhaling through the nose. Even though retention <term>kumbhaka</term> is not explicitly mentioned here, one should hold the breath after the Sitkari inhalation. By doing this repeatedly, one's beauty and grace increase so much that they become like a second God of Love. || 54 ||

He becomes worthy of being served by the groups of Yoginis. He becomes the master of creation and destruction. Hunger, thirst, sleep, and laziness do not affect him. || 55 ||

<summary>This page introduces Sitkari Pranayama, a cooling breath technique where inhalation occurs through the mouth with a "hissing" sound. The text emphasizes exhaling only through the nose to preserve strength and claims the practice grants physical beauty and mastery over bodily needs and cosmic forces.</summary>
<keywords>Sitkari, Hissing Breath, Kamadeva, Yogini, Kumbhaka, Pranayama, Hatha Yoga, beauty, vitality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the benefits of Sitkari (The Hissing Breath) and introduces the next pranayama technique, Shitali (The Cooling Breath). It details the physical execution involving the tongue and the specific diseases this practice is said to cure.</meta>

( 64 )
<header>On the Hathayogapradipika</header>

> **Root Verse: The body becomes strong <note>original: "satvaṃ"</note> and free from all disturbances. By this method, truly, the practitioner becomes a king among yogis on this earth. || 56 ||**

### Now, Shitali <term>The Cooling Breath</term>

> **Drawing in the air with the tongue, one should perform the practice of breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> as described before. The wise practitioner should then slowly exhale the air through the nostrils. || 57 ||**

> **This retention, known as Shitali, destroys diseases such as abdominal tumors <term>gulma</term> and spleen enlargement <term>pliha</term>, as well as fever, excess bile, hunger, thirst, and the effects of poisons. || 58 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

Regarding the verse beginning **"The body..."**: The strength <note>original: "balam"</note> and vitality of the physical body are increased. By following the method of practice described, the practitioner becomes like a king among yogis <note>original: "yogīndro"</note> on the surface of the earth, remaining free from all afflictions. Following the logical principle that "every statement implies a certainty" <note>original: "sarvaṃ vākyaṃ sādhāraṇam iti nyāyād"</note>, the results described are indeed true. || 56 ||

The author now describes the Shitali breath retention: **"With the tongue..."**. This means one should draw in the air slowly to perform the inhalation <term>puraka</term> by extending the tongue slightly past the lips and shaping it like the lower beak of a bird. The phrase **"as described before"** implies that one should follow the procedure for breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> as explained in the section on *Surya Bhedana* <term>The Sun-Piercing Breath</term>. The "wise practitioner"—one possessed of refined intellect—should then exhale the air slowly through both "openings of the nose" <note>original: "ghrāṇaraṃdhrābhyāṃ"</note>, meaning the nostrils. || 57 ||

The author now lists the virtues of Shitali: **"Abdominal tumors..."**. *Gulma* and *pliha* refer to specific chronic ailments such as abdominal distension or enlargement of the spleen. It also cures the disease known as fever, disorders caused by excess bile <term>pitta</term>, the desire to eat <note>hunger</note>, and the desire to drink water <note>thirst</note>. It even counteracts the disorders arising from the "poisons" <note>original: "viṣāṇi"</note> of snakes and similar creatures. "Shitali" is the well-known name for this practice. This described retention "destroys" <note>literally: "nihanti"</note> or completely removes these conditions. || 58 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Regarding the verse **"The body..."**: The body gains strength. By this aforementioned method of practice, the yogi becomes like Indra <note>the King of Gods</note> among practitioners upon this earth, remaining free from all disturbances. The fruits of practice described here are the absolute truth. || 56 ||

Now the fourth retention, Shitali, is described: **"With the tongue..."**. Shaping one's tongue like the lower beak of a bird and extending it outside the lips, one should draw in the air to perform inhalation. Then, just as previously described for *Surya Bhedana*, one should perform the breath retention. Finally, the one of beautiful intellect should exhale the air very slowly through the holes of the nostrils. || 57 ||

The virtues of Shitali are described: **"Abdominal tumors..."**. This includes diseases like *gulma* <term>chronic enlargement of the glands or abdomen</term> and *pliha* <term>spleen disorders</term>, as well as fever, bile disorders, the urge to eat, the urge to drink, and the poison from snake bites or other toxins. This retention called Shitali removes all of these. || 58 ||

<summary>This page concludes the benefits of Sitkari and introduces Shitali, the "Cooling Breath," performed by inhaling through a curled tongue and exhaling through the nose. It claims the practice cures digestive issues, fever, and neutralizes poisons.</summary>
<keywords>Shitali, Cooling Breath, Kumbhaka, Gulma, Spleen disorders, Bile, Fever, Yoga therapy, Pranayama technique</keywords>

<header>Second Lesson</header>

# Now, Bhastrika <term>The Bellows Breath</term>

> **Root Verse: Placing both auspicious soles of the feet upon the opposite thighs, this is the Lotus Pose <term>Padmasana</term>, which destroys all sins. || 59 ||**

> **Having properly assumed the Lotus Pose, keeping the neck and abdomen in a straight line, the wise practitioner should close the mouth and exhale through the nostrils with effort. || 60 ||**

> **In such a way that the air is felt from the heart and throat up to the skull, making a sound, one should also rapidly fill the heart-lotus with air. || 61 ||**

## ॥ Commentary ॥

The previous discussion on the nature of signs <note>original: "lingo'pi"</note> concludes. As the poet Shri Harsha says: "Having raised the crocodile, then the gold-born..." <note>original: "udasya kumbhīratha śātakuṃbhajā"</note> || 58 ||

Because the practice of Bellows retention <term>Bhastrika Kumbhaka</term> is performed only after first assuming the Lotus Pose, the author first describes that posture: **"Upon the thighs..."**. This means placing both pure, clean soles of the feet firmly upon the upper part of the thighs and remaining there. This is the Lotus Pose. What is its nature? It is the "destroyer of all sins" <note>original: "sarvapāpaprakaṛṣeṇa nāśanaṃ"</note>. Here, the word "above" <note>original: "upari"</note> is an indeclinable indicating that the feet are turned upward <note>original: "uttāna"</note>. Similarly, in the grammatical work *Manorama*, in the section on case-relations <term>karaka</term>, the phrase "above the intellects" is explained as "with upturned intellects." || 59 ||

Now the author describes the Bellows Breath <term>Bhastrika Kumbhaka</term>: **"Properly..."**. The neck and the abdomen together are called the "neck-abdomen." Due to being parts of a living being, they are treated as a single unit in grammar. One whose neck and abdomen are aligned <note>meaning the spine is straight</note> is "straight-necked-and-bellied." One whose intellect is steady is a "wise practitioner" <note>original: "sudhīḥ"</note>. Having firmly assumed the Lotus Pose and keeping the mouth restrained <note>tightly closed</note>, one should exhale the breath <note>prana</note> residing within the body through one of the nostrils with great effort. || 60 ||

He describes the method of exhalation: **"In such a way..."**. The heart and the throat together are the "heart-throat." He should exhale in such a way that the air is felt from the heart-throat up to the skull <term>kapala</term>, accompanied by a sound <note>original: "sasvanaṃ"</note>.

## ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥

Now the fifth variety, the Bellows Breath <term>Bhastrika Kumbhaka</term>, is described: **"Upon the thighs..."**. Placing both soles of the feet upturned upon the thighs and remaining still is the Lotus Pose. What is it like? It is the destroyer of all sins. || 59 ||

**"Properly..."**. A man whose neck and abdomen are aligned, and whose intellect is beautiful, should firmly bind the Lotus Pose. Closing the mouth, he should exhale the air through one of the nasal passages with effort. || 60 ||

One should exhale so that the air is felt in the heart and throat, accompanied by sound, reaching up to the skull. Then, one should rapidly fill the "heart-lotus" with air through inhalation <term>puraka</term>. || 61 ||

<summary>This page introduces Bhastrika (Bellows Breath), detailing the requirement of Padmasana (Lotus Pose) and the technique of forceful, rhythmic breathing that makes a sound from the throat to the skull.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Bhastrika (The Bellows Breath). The previous page established the physical posture (Padmasana) and the initial rapid movement of air from the heart to the skull.</meta>

( ६६ )
<header>On the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Verse: Again one should exhale in the same way, and inhale <note>original: "pūrayet", literally "to fill"</note> again and again, just as a blacksmith moves his bellows with speed. || 62 ||**

**In that very manner, one should move the air residing within their own body with intelligence. When fatigue arises in the body, one should then inhale through the Sun <term>Surya Nadi, the right nostril</term>. || 63 ||**

**In such a way that the abdomen becomes full of air quickly, one should then hold the nose firmly, without using the middle and index fingers. || 64 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The breath <note>original: "prāṇa"</note> reaches [the destination]. This is the meaning. One should exhale in that way. "Up to the heart-lotus" <note>original: "hṛtpadmāvadhi"</note> refers to the action where one fills the air with speed and force. The word "and" <note>original: "ca-api"</note> is used here to complete the poetic meter. || 61 ||

**"Again..."** means one should exhale and inhale repeatedly just as before. The author provides an illustration for this meaning: **"Just as..."**. A "blacksmith" is a maker of iron tools. A "bellows" <note>original: "bhastrā"</note> is a leather tool used for fanning the fire. The verse describes the manner in which it is moved with speed. || 62 ||

**"In that very manner..."** means in that same way, one should move the air (the breath) located within one's own body using "intelligence" <note>original: "dhiyā"</note> or the intellect. The author then describes the limit of this movement—how long one should continue the rapid cycles of exhalation and inhalation: **"When fatigue..."**. This means at the time when weariness or exhaustion occurs in the body due to the constant repetition of exhaling and inhaling. || 63 ||

**"In such a way..."** means in the manner that the abdomen becomes full of air very "quickly" <note>original: "laghu"</note>, one should inhale through the Sun channel <term>the right nostril</term>. The ancient dictionary *Amarakosha* defines "laghu" as "quick, swift, or rapid." After the inhalation, the author explains what must be done: **"One should hold..."**. One should hold the nose firmly, excluding the middle and index fingers, by using the thumb, the ring finger, and the little finger. This means one should grip the nose firmly by closing the right nostril with the thumb and the left nostril with the ring and little fingers. <note>This hand position is often called Vishnu Mudra in modern yoga practice.</note> || 64 ||

->॥ Translation in the Common Tongue <note>Hindi/Bhasha</note> ॥<-

**"Again..."** means first exhale, then inhale, then exhale, then inhale. Just as a blacksmith moves a leather bellows with great speed, one should perform rapid inhalations and exhalations. || 62 ||

**"When fatigue..."** means while performing these cycles of inhalation and exhalation continuously, when the body becomes tired, one should inhale through the Sun channel <term>the right nostril</term> in such a way that the belly is filled with air very quickly. || 63 ||

**"One should hold..."** means after inhaling, one should block the right nostril with the thumb and the ring finger... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the instructions for finger placement on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the rapid "bellows" breathing technique (Bhastrika), comparing it to a blacksmith's tool, and explains how to transition to breath retention (Kumbhaka) when tired.</summary>
<keywords>Bhastrika, Bellows Breath, Pranayama, Surya Nadi, Right Nostril Inhalation, Blacksmith Metaphor, Hand Mudra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific technical instructions for Bhastrika Kumbhaka (The Bellows Breath), moving from the rapid breathing phase to the final retention and exhalation. It explains the physiological benefits of balancing the three humors and increasing digestive fire.</meta>

( 67 )
<header>Second Instruction</header>

**Root Verse: Having performed breath retention according to the prescribed rules, one should exhale the air through the Ida <term>the lunar channel, the left nostril</term>. This practice removes imbalances of wind, bile, and phlegm, and increases the fire of the body. || 65 ||**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

**"According to the rules"** means having performed breath retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> while applying the locks <term>bandhas, internal physical seals used to direct energy</term>. One should then exhale the air through the Ida, the lunar channel. This is the sequence for the Bellows Breath <note>original: "bhastrā-kumbhaka"</note>.

The procedure is as follows: Closing the left nostril with the ring and little fingers of the right hand, one should perform rapid exhalations and inhalations through the right nostril, moving like a bellows. When fatigue sets in, one should inhale through that same nostril and, closing the right nostril with the thumb, hold the breath <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> for as long as possible. Afterward, one should exhale through the Ida <term>the left nostril</term>.

Then, closing the right nostril with the thumb, one should perform rapid exhalations and inhalations through the left nostril, just like a bellows. When fatigue sets in, one should inhale through that same nostril, close the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, hold the breath as long as possible, and then exhale through the Pingala <term>the solar channel, the right nostril</term>. This is the first method.

A second method is also taught: Closing the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, one inhales through the right nostril, quickly closes it with the thumb, and exhales through the left nostril. After repeating this a hundred times and becoming tired, one should inhale through that same side, perform the retention with the locks, and then exhale through the Ida. Then, closing the right nostril with the thumb, one inhales through the left nostril, quickly closes the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, and exhales through the Pingala like a bellows. After repeating this cycle of exhalation and inhalation until fatigue arises, one inhales through the left nostril, holds the breath using the ring and little fingers, and finally exhales through the Pingala.

The author now describes the benefits of the Bellows Breath: **"Wind, bile, and phlegm..."**. These are the three humors <note>original: "vāta-pitta-śleṣmā"</note>; this practice removes their disorders. Furthermore, it specifically increases and ignites the fire of the body, which is the digestive fire <note>original: "jaṭharānala"</note>. || 65 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

Closing the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, one should take the breath through the right... || 64 ||

**"According to the rules"** means that after performing the retention with the locks, one should exhale the air through the moon, which is the Ida channel. Know that this is the method for the Bellows Breath. One should close the left nostril with the ring and little fingers of the right hand, then perform rapid exhalations and inhalations through the right nostril with the speed of a blacksmith’s bellows. When fatigue comes, one should inhale through that same nostril, close the right side with the thumb, and hold the breath according to one's strength. Then, exhale through the Ida. 

Next, closing the right nostril with the thumb, one should quickly perform exhalations and inhalations through the left nostril like a bellows. When fatigue comes, one should inhale through that left nostril, close it with the ring and little fingers, perform the retention, and then exhale through the Pingala. This is one— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, continuing the description of the first method.</note>
