( 68 )
<header>In the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Verse: It quickly awakens the Kundalini, is purifying, brings happiness, and is beneficial. It destroys the obstruction of phlegm <note>original: "kaphārgala"</note> situated at the mouth of the Brahma-channel <term>Sushumna, the central energetic channel of the spine</term>. || 66 ||**

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

**"Kundalini"** and so on. This practice is the "awakener" <note>original: "bodhaka"</note> because it quickly rouses the sleeping Kundalini. It is "purifying" <note>original: "pavana"</note> and "gives happiness." It is "beneficial" because it removes the three humors <term>tridosha: the three biological energies of wind, bile, and phlegm</term>; thus, it is good for everyone at all times. While all types of breath retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> are generally beneficial, the Sun-Piercing <note>original: "Surya-bhedana"</note> and the Victorious <note>original: "Ujjayi"</note> breaths are heating in nature and are mostly beneficial in cold conditions. The Hissing <note>original: "Sitkari"</note> and the Cooling <note>original: "Shitali"</note> breaths are cold and are mostly beneficial in heat. The Bellows Breath <note>original: "Bhastrika"</note> is temperate <note>balanced in heat and cold</note> and is beneficial at all times. Although all retentions remove disease, Sun-Piercing primarily removes wind <note>original: "vata"</note>, the Victorious breath primarily removes phlegm <note>original: "shleshma"</note>, and the Hissing and Cooling breaths primarily remove bile <note>original: "pitta"</note>. The retention known as the Bellows should be understood to remove all three humors. 

The **Brahma-channel** is the Sushumna, so called because it leads one to the Absolute <note>Brahman</note>. As the Sacred Scripture <note>Shruti</note> says: 

> "There are one hundred and one channels of the heart; one of them leads up to the crown of the head. Going upward by it, one attains immortality; the others lead in various directions at the time of death." 

The **"obstruction"** <note>original: "argala," literally a door-bolt</note> consisting of phlegm and other substances is situated firmly at the mouth or the entrance of this channel, blocking the movement of the vital breath; this practice acts as its "destroyer." || 66 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

Now the second method is described: Closing the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, one should perform inhalation through the right nostril. Quickly closing it with the thumb, one should exhale through the left nostril. Repeat this several times. If fatigue sets in, then inhale through the left. After performing inhalation with the locks <term>bandhas, internal seals</term>, exhale through the Ida <term>the left nostril</term>. Then, closing the right nostril with the thumb, inhale through the left nostril; quickly closing the left nostril with the ring and little fingers, exhale through the Pingala <term>the right nostril</term>. Just like a bellows, perform such exhalations and inhalations repeatedly. When tired, inhale through the left nostril, perform breath retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> by closing with the ring and little fingers, and exhale through the Pingala. These are the qualities of Bhastrika: it removes wind, bile, and phlegm, and it ignites the digestive fire <term>jatharagni</term> in the body. || 69 || <note>The verse numbering here follows the vernacular tradition's sequence.</note>

**"Kundalini"** and so on: It also quickly awakens the sleeping Kundalini, acts as a purifier, and brings happiness. Because it removes the three humors, it is beneficial for all. How it is beneficial compared to other retentions is explained thus: Sun-Piercing and the Victorious breath are both heating; they are beneficial when performed in the winter. The Hissing and Cooling breaths are both cold; these are more beneficial in the summer. The Bellows breath is balanced, containing both heat and cold; it is beneficial at all times. While all retentions remove all diseases, the Sun-Piercing mostly removes wind-based diseases, and the Victorious breath mostly...

<summary>This page explains the benefits of Bhastrika (Bellows Breath), specifically its ability to awaken Kundalini and clear phlegm from the Sushumna channel. It also provides a second technical method for the practice involving alternate nostril breathing and compares the thermal effects of various Pranayamas.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Bhastrika (Bellows Breath) and introduces Bhramari (Bee Breath). It concludes the explanation of how Bhastrika pierces the three energetic knots (granthis) and begins the description of the sixth type of breath retention.</meta>

69
### Second Chapter

**Root Verse: This Bellows-style breath retention <term>Bhastrika Kumbhaka</term> should be performed with special care, as it is the breaker of the three knots <note>original: "granthi-traya"</note> that arise thoroughly within the body. || 67 ||**

-> Now, the Bee Breath <-

**By inhaling rapidly with a resounding drone like a male bee, and exhaling very slowly with the sound of a female bee, a certain playful bliss <note>original: "ānanda-līlā"</note> arises in the hearts of the lords of yogis through this practice. || 68 ||**

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

**"Thoroughly"** <note>original: "samyak"</note> and so on. This Bellows-style retention <note>original: "Bhastrakhya-kumbhaka"</note> should be performed with special care—meaning it is an essential duty—because it specifically causes the piercing of the three knots: the Knot of Brahma, the Knot of Vishnu, and the Knot of Rudra <term>granthis: three psychic blockages in the subtle body that prevent the full rise of Kundalini</term> which are thoroughly produced within the body <note>original: "gātra-madhye"</note>, specifically within the central channel <term>Sushumna</term>. Other practices like the Sun-Piercing breath <note>original: "Surya-bhedana"</note> should be performed as circumstances allow, but this one is vital. || 67 ||

Now the author describes the Bee retention <note>original: "Bhramari-kumbhaka"</note>. **"Rapidly"** and so on. One should perform the inhalation <note>original: "puraka"</note> rapidly and forcefully with a resonance <note>original: "ghosha"</note> that sounds like the humming of a male bee <note>original: "bhṛnga"</note>. Then, one should perform the exhalation <note>original: "rechaka"</note> very, very slowly, making a sound like a female bee <note>original: "bhṛngī"</note>. Although the retention of breath <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note> following the inhalation is naturally established by the fact that this is a "Kumbhaka" practice, it is not explicitly mentioned here as there is no unique instruction for it. Instead, the specific techniques for the inhalation and exhalation are highlighted. Through the application of this practice, an indescribable playful bliss arises in the hearts of the great yogis. || 68 ||

-> ॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥ <-

The Victorious breath <note>original: "Ujjayi"</note> removes phlegm <note>original: "shleshma"</note>, and the Hissing <note>original: "Sitkari"</note> and Cooling <note>original: "Shitali"</note> breaths—these two remove bile <note>original: "pitta"</note>. One should know that the Bellows breath <note>original: "Bhastrika"</note> removes all three humors <term>tridosha: the three biological energies of wind, bile, and phlegm</term>. Furthermore, it is the means to attain the Absolute <note>original: "Brahma"</note>. Therefore, the central channel <note>original: "Sushumna"</note> is called the "Brahma-conduit." At the mouth—that is, the front portion—of that central channel, it destroys the obstruction of phlegm and other humors that block the movement of the breath. || 66 ||

**"Thoroughly"** and so on. In the perceived body, this Bellows breath specifically pierces the three knots—the Knot of Brahma, the Knot of Vishnu, and the Knot of Rudra—which are produced in the central channel. Therefore, this is a worthy and necessary practice. Breath retentions like the Sun-Piercing should be done as one is able. || 67 ||

Now the sixth breath retention, named the Bee Breath <note>original: "Bhramari"</note>, is described. **"Rapidly"** and so on. If the inhalation is done quickly, there is a sound like a male bee; if done gently, it sounds like a female bee. After performing the inhalation with the male bee sound, one should exhale very slowly with the female bee sound. Because the inhalation and exhalation have these specific characteristics, they are detailed here. The retention itself follows the inhalation as a natural part of the Bee Breath, so no special instruction is written for it. By this method of practice, a playful bliss that cannot be described in words arises in the hearts of the great yogis. || 68 ||

<summary>This page details how Bhastrika (Bellows Breath) clears energetic blockages (knots) in the central channel and introduces Bhramari (Bee Breath), a practice characterized by humming sounds that induce a state of meditative bliss.</summary>
<keywords>Bhastrika, Bhramari, Kumbhaka, Sushumna, Brahma-granthi, Vishnu-granthi, Rudra-granthi, Bee Breath, Bellows Breath, three knots, playful bliss</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of eight specific breath retentions (kumbhakas) in the Hatha Yoga tradition. Following the descriptions of the Sun-Piercing, Ujjayi, Sitkari, Sitali, Bhastrika, and Bhramari breaths on previous pages, it now introduces the final two: Murcha (Fainting) and Plavini (Floating), before categorizing the general types of Pranayama.</meta>

( 71 )

# Now, the Fainting Breath

**Root Verse: At the end of the inhalation <term>puraka: the drawing in of breath</term>, perform the chin lock <term>jalandhara bandha: a technique where the chin is pressed against the chest to lock energy</term> very firmly; then exhale slowly. This is known as the Fainting breath <note>original: "mūrchā"</note>; it causes the mind to swoon <note>meaning a suspension of mental fluctuations</note> and bestows great bliss. || 69 ||**

# Now, the Floating Breath

**With the belly completely filled by drawing a generous amount of air inside, a person floats easily like a lotus leaf, even in deep, bottomless water. || 70 ||**

**Breath regulation <term>pranayama</term> is said to consist of three parts: exhalation <term>rechaka</term>, inhalation <term>puraka</term>, and retention <term>kumbhaka</term>. Furthermore, retention is considered to be of two types: "Accompanied" <note>original: "sahita"</note> and "Absolute" <note>original: "kevala"</note>. || 71 ||**

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

The author describes the Fainting retention <note>original: "mūrchā-kumbhaka"</note>. **"At the end of inhalation"** and so on. At the conclusion of the inhalation, one should apply the lock called *Jalandhara* extremely firmly, and then exhale very, very slowly. This retention is called "the Fainting" <note>original: "mūrchanā"</note> because it makes the mind faint or become still. By this, the author explains the result of the practice through the very definition of the word. Furthermore, it is "bliss-bestowing," meaning it grants happiness. || 69 ||

The author describes the Floating retention <note>original: "plāvanī-kumbhaka"</note>. **"Inside"** and so on. When a person’s belly is completely filled in all directions by a generous amount of air <note>original: "māruta"</note> drawn into the interior of the body, that person floats, moves, or travels with ease and without effort upon water—even water that is bottomless—just like a lotus leaf. || 70 ||

Next, the author describes the different types of breath regulation. **"Breath regulation"** <note>original: "prāṇāyāma"</note> and so on. *Pranayama* is the restraint <note>original: "āyāma"</note> or halting of the *Prana*, the vital air that moves within the body. The definition of *Pranayama* was stated by Gorakshanatha <note>the legendary founding master of Hatha Yoga</note>: "Prana is the living breath of one's own body; Ayama is its restraint." This is of three types through the divisions of: emptying <term>rechaka</term>, filling <term>puraka</term>, and retaining <term>kumbhaka</term>.

-> ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥ <-

Now the seventh retention called Murcha is described. **"At the end of inhalation"** and so on. After performing the inhalation, apply the lock called *Jalandhara* and then exhale very slowly. This retention is named Murcha. It causes the mind to "faint" <note>enter a state of meditative suspension</note>, which is why it is called the "Mind-Faint." What is it like? It is a giver of bliss. || 69 ||

Now the eighth Floating retention <note>original: "plāvinī-kumbhaka"</note> is described. **"Inside"** and so on. A man who has filled his belly in all four directions with the abundant air drawn inside the body can move with ease upon deep water, just like a lotus leaf. || 70 ||

Now the divisions of breath regulation <term>pranayama</term> are described. **"Breath regulation"** and so on. Stopping the *Prana*, which is the air inside the body, is called *Pranayama*. This *Pranayama* is of three types through the divisions of *Rechaka*, *Puraka*, and *Kumbhaka*. When one empties the air out from the belly, it is called *Rechaka*. When one fills the belly with air from the outside, it is called *Puraka*. And when, after the inhalation, one holds the air like a jar <note>original: "ghaṭa-kī-sīnāī"; the word "kumbhaka" literally means "pot-like"</note>...

<summary>This page describes the final two of the eight classical Hatha Yoga breath retentions: Murcha (fainting for mental stillness) and Plavini (filling the stomach with air to float). It also defines Pranayama and its components: inhalation, exhalation, and retention.</summary>
<keywords>Murcha, Plavini, Pranayama, Jalandhara Bandha, Gorakshanatha, Kumbhaka, Puraka, Rechaka, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on breath regulation (pranayama), specifically transitioning from the different varieties of retention (kumbhaka) to the two primary categories: Accompanied (sahita) and Absolute (kevala).</meta>

( 71 )

-> Instruction II <-

## **Root Verse: One should practice the Accompanied breath as long as the Absolute is not achieved. || 72 ||**
## **Abandoning exhalation and inhalation, that which is the comfortable holding of breath [is Kevala]. || 72 ||**

### ॥ Commentary ॥

Breath regulation <term>pranayama</term> is of three types: the exhalation-breath <term>rechaka</term>, the inhalation-breath <term>puraka</term>, and the retention-breath <term>kumbhaka</term>. The sage Yajnavalkya <note>A legendary Vedic sage credited with the Yoga Yajnavalkya text</note> describes the characteristics of exhalation: "That which is the emptying of the air from the belly is known as the Exhalation breath." Furthermore, the characteristic of the Exhalation breath is: "Having exited from the nostrils entirely, staying still without obstructing the air as if the breath were void—that is the great restriction called the Exhalation <note>original: "rechaka"</note>."

The characteristic of inhalation: "Filling the belly with air from the outside is indeed Inhalation." The characteristic of the Inhalation breath: "Drawing the air through the nostrils slowly from all sides and filling all the energy channels <term>nadis</term> completely—that is the great restriction called the Inhalation <note>original: "pūraka"</note>."

The characteristic of retention: "After filling, the holding of the air like a jar <note>original: "kumbhavat"; this is why the practice is called "kumbhaka," from the word for a water pot or jar</note> is Retention." This Retention is not different from the Inhalation breath [when practiced together]. However, it is distinct in this way: "Where there is neither exhalation nor inhalation, the sage steadily holds the air already present in the nostrils; the experts call this 'the Jar' <note>original: "kumbhakhya"</note>."

Now, the author classifies breath regulation in another way: **"The Accompanied."** Retention is of two kinds: Accompanied <note>original: "sahita"</note> and Absolute <note>original: "kevala"</note>. This is the opinion of the yogis. There, the Accompanied is of two kinds: those preceded by exhalation and those preceded by inhalation. As it is said: "Having exhaled or having inhaled, that which one performs is the Accompanied Retention." In that context, the one preceded by exhalation is not different from the Exhalation breath, and the one preceded by inhalation is not different from the Inhalation breath. The Absolute Retention is not different from the [pure] Retention breath. The previously mentioned varieties such as the Sun-Piercing <note>original: "sūryabhedana"</note> should be understood as variations of the Accompanied Retention preceded by inhalation. || 71 ||

The author describes the duration for the practice of the Accompanied Retention: **"As long as..."** until the perfection of the Absolute—meaning Absolute Retention—is achieved.

### ॥ Common Language Version ॥

This is called "the Jar" <note>kumbhaka</note>, and it is of two types: one is Accompanied and the other is Absolute. This is agreed upon by the yogis. Within these, the Accompanied is of two types: preceded by exhalation and preceded by inhalation. The one preceded by exhalation is not distinct from the Exhalation breath. The retention preceded by inhalation is inseparable from the Inhalation breath. The Absolute retention is not distinct from the pure Retention breath. The previously described practices like the Sun-Piercing should be understood as types of the retention preceded by inhalation. || 71 ||

**As long as:** Until the perfection of the Absolute Retention is attained, one should practice the Accompanied Retentions like the Sun-Piercing. After the piercing of the central channel <term>sushumna: the main energy channel of the spine</term>, when a sound like a pot <note>possibly a resonance or internal vibration</note> occurs within the central channel, then the Absolute Retention is perfected. After that, perform ten Accompanied retentions, then twenty, increasing the count up to eighty. If one has the strength, one may perform more than eighty. Now, the characteristic of Absolute Retention is stated: **Abandoning exhalation and inhalation, holding the breath with ease is called Absolute Retention.** || 72 ||

<summary>This page defines the three parts of breath regulation—exhalation, inhalation, and retention—and categorizes retention into "Accompanied" (connected to breathing) and "Absolute" (pure suspension of breath).</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Kumbhaka, Rechaka, Puraka, Sahita, Kevala, Sushumna, Yajnavalkya, breath retention, yoga practice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on breath retention (kumbhaka), moving from the definitions of inhalation and exhalation to the ultimate practice of "Absolute Retention" (kevala kumbhaka) and its spiritual fruits.</meta>

( 72 )

-> ### In the Light on Hatha Yoga <-

## **Root Verse: This is called breath regulation; it is truly the Absolute Retention. When Absolute Retention is perfected, devoid of exhalation and inhalation... || 73 ||**

## **...there is nothing in the three worlds <note>The three worlds usually refer to the physical earth, the intermediate atmospheric realm, and the heavens</note> that is difficult for him to obtain. Through the power of Absolute Retention, because he can hold the breath as he wishes... || 74 ||**

## **...he also attains the state of Royal Yoga; of this there is no doubt. From retention comes the awakening of the Kundalini <note>The "Coiled One," the latent spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note>; from the awakening of Kundalini, success follows. || 75 ||**

-> ### ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

One should practice the Accompanied Retention <term>sahita kumbhaka</term>—such as the "Sun-Piercing" breath <note>original: "sūryabhedādi," referring to the specific techniques taught earlier in the text</note>—until the Absolute <note>original: "kevala"</note> state is achieved. After the central channel <term>sushumna</term> is pierced, when the internal sounds <note>The "unstruck" sounds or Anahata Nada heard during deep meditation</term> arise within that channel, then Absolute Retention is perfected.

Following that, one may perform ten or twenty Accompanied Retentions, but the completion of the count of eighty <note>Traditional yoga manuals often prescribe eighty breath cycles per session, performed four times a day</note> must be done with Absolute Retentions alone. If one has the capacity, more than eighty Absolute Retentions may be performed. He now gives the definition of Absolute Retention: "Exhalation..." etc. Avoiding and abandoning exhalation <term>rechaka</term> and inhalation <term>puraka</term>, the holding of the breath which occurs comfortably and without effort is [Absolute] Breath Retention. || 72 ||

"Breath regulation..." etc. This indeed is the "mixed" <note>The commentator clarifies that even though it is "absolute," it is the culmination of the mixed practices</note> Absolute Retention described as breath regulation <term>pranayama</term>; he praises the Absolute state. "In the Absolute..." etc. When the Absolute Retention is perfected, it is free from both exhalation and inhalation. || 73 ||

"Nothing..." etc. For that yogi, there is nothing whatsoever in the three worlds that is hard to reach or difficult to obtain. Holding the breath as he pleases is no longer a challenge. The meaning is that everything becomes easy for him. "Capable..." etc. Capable and empowered by the practice of Absolute Retention, he holds the breath as he desires. || 74 ||

"Royal..." etc. He attains the state characterized by Royal Yoga <term>Rajayoga</term> <note>The "highest" or "royal" path of yoga focusing on the mind and samadhi, as opposed to the physical rigors of Hatha Yoga</note>. There is no doubt here; the meaning is that this is certain. He describes how the practice of retention leads to ultimate liberation <term>kaivalya</term> through a succession of stages: "From retention..." etc. From the practice of retention...

-> ### ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥ <-

"Breath regulation..." etc. When Absolute Retention <term>kevala kumbhaka</term> is perfected, it is devoid of exhalation and inhalation. || 73 ||

"Nothing..." etc. Nothing in the three worlds is difficult for that yogi to obtain. By becoming capable through the practice of Absolute Retention, he holds the breath as is appropriate. || 74 ||

"Royal..." etc. The state of Royal Yoga is attained; there is no doubt in this. It is certain. And through the practice of retention, the awakening of the Kundalini—which is the foundational power—occurs. And from the awakening of Kundalini, sleep, laziness, and other such obstacles are destroyed. || 75 ||

<summary>This page defines Absolute Retention (Kevala Kumbhaka) as the effortless holding of breath without inhalation or exhalation. It explains that mastering this leads to the awakening of Kundalini energy and the attainment of the highest state, Rajayoga.</summary>

<keywords>Kevala Kumbhaka, Absolute Retention, Rajayoga, Kundalini awakening, Pranayama, Sushumna, Sahita Kumbhaka</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page concludes the specific instructions on breath retention (kumbhaka) and emphasizes the symbiotic relationship between physical Hatha Yoga and the spiritual Raja Yoga, leading toward the final state of liberation.</meta>

### Second Instruction ( 73 )

**Root Verse:** The central channel <term>sushumna</term> becomes unobstructed, and success in Hatha Yoga is born. Without Hatha Yoga, there is no Royal Yoga <term>raja yoga</term>; without Royal Yoga, there is no Hatha. || 76 ||
Since success is impossible with only one, a practitioner should practice the pair together until the state of perfection is achieved. At the end of the breath restraint in retention <term>kumbhaka</term>, one should make the mind "supportless" <note>original: "nirāśraya," meaning a state of meditation where the mind does not lean on any physical or mental object</note>. By this method of practice, one reaches the state of Royal Yoga. || 77 ||

-> ॥ Commentary ॥ <-

This refers to the "Base Power" <note>original: "ādhāraśakti"</note> which is the Kundalini; her awakening is the "breaking of sleep." Success comes from this awakening of Kundalini. || 75 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with **"Unobstructed"**: The central channel, the <term>sushumna</term>, becomes free from "latches" <note>original: "argala," meaning a bolt or obstruction</note> such as phlegm and other blockages. Success in Hatha <note>original: "haṭhasiddhi"</note>—the success in the form of absolute liberation <term>kaivalya</term> through the traditional sequence beginning with sensory withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term>—is born. 

He now describes the mutual relationship where Hatha Yoga and Royal Yoga help one another: **"Without Hatha..."** Without the physical practices of Hatha Yoga, the spiritual state of Royal Yoga cannot be perfected, and without Royal Yoga, Hatha Yoga is not successful; thus, success is not found in either one alone. Therefore, making "perfection"—the success of Royal Yoga—the goal, one should practice the pair of Hatha Yoga and Royal Yoga together until that perfection is reached.

The term "Royal Yoga" is used here for the means of achieving it, whether directly or indirectly. This is like using the word "life" to refer to a "plow," since the plow is the means of sustaining life. The practices for Royal Yoga will be explained in the Fourth Instruction, consisting of states like the "Mindless State" <term>unmanī</term>, the "Seal of Shambhu" <term>śāmbhavī mudrā</term>, and the various systems of yoga having fifteen or ten "limbs" <note>Yoga systems were often categorized by how many stages or "limbs" they contained, such as the famous eight-limbed Ashtanga Yoga</note> mentioned in the text *The Essence of the Great Sayings* <note>original: "Vākya Sudhā"</note> regarding the perception of internal and external reality. || 76 ||

He now explains the method of attaining Royal Yoga through Hatha practice: **"At the end of retention..."** After the restraint of the breath through retention <term>kumbhaka</term>, one should make the mind <note>original: "citta," the "heart-mind" or inner instrument</note> supportless. This means that once "Recognized Absorption" <term>samprajñāta samādhi</term> is achieved, after resting in the form of the Infinite <term>brahman</term>, one should dissolve the mind through supreme detachment <term>vairāgya</term>. 

This is done by the "method of practice" <note>original: "abhyāsayoga"</note>. The dictionary states: "Yoga means armor, means, meditation, connection, and method." By this, one reaches the state or position of Royal Yoga. || 77 ||

-> ॥ Vernacular Translation ॥ <-

And the phlegm and other "latches" of the <term>sushumna</term> channel are removed. And success in Hatha occurs, meaning liberation <term>moksha</term> is achieved. Without Hatha Yoga, success in Royal Yoga does not occur. And without Royal Yoga, Hatha Yoga is not perfected. Until the success of Royal Yoga occurs, one should practice these two—Hatha Yoga and Royal Yoga—together. || 76 ||

**Regarding retention:** Restraining the breath through retention, at its conclusion, one should make the mind without a support. In this manner, through the method of practice, one attains the state of Royal Yoga. || 77 ||

<sig>10</sig>

<summary>This page explains that Hatha Yoga (physical discipline) and Raja Yoga (spiritual absorption) are inseparable; the physical practice clears the central channel to allow the mind to reach a "supportless" state of meditation. It emphasizes practicing both until the final stage of liberation is reached.</summary>

# ( 74 ) In the Lamp of Hatha Yoga

**Root Verse:** Slimness of body, brightness in the face, clarity of the inner sound <note>original: "nāda," the mystical resonance heard in deep meditation</note>, very clear eyes, freedom from disease, victory over the vital essence <term>bindu</term>, stimulation of the digestive fire, and the complete purification of the channels <term>nadis</term> are the signs of success in Hatha Yoga. || 78 ||

Thus ends the Second Instruction in the *Lamp of Hatha Yoga*. || 2 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He now describes the indicators that grant knowledge of success in Hatha: **"Slimness of body..."** *Vapuṣaḥ* refers to the body; its leanness or slimness. In the face <note>original: "vadane"</note>, there is a sense of pleasant brightness or grace. Regarding the inner sound <term>nāda</term>, there is "clarity," meaning it manifests clearly. The eyes <note>original: "nayane"</note> become exceptionally clear and pure. "Freedom from disease" refers to the state of health. Victory over the vital essence <term>bindu</term> consists of the absence of its loss or depletion. The stimulation of the fire refers to the brilliance of the internal gastric fire. "Purification of the channels" means specifically the removal of impurities from the <term>nadis</term>. These are the "signs" <note>original: "lakṣaṇam"</note>—that by which the impending success of the Hatha Yoga practice is characterized and recognized. || 78 ||

Thus ends the Second Instruction in the commentary on the *Lamp of Hatha Yoga* named "The Moonlight" <note>original: "Jyotsnā"</note>, composed by the scholar Brahmananda. || 2 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

**Regarding "Slimness of body":** The body becomes lean, the face appears bright and pleasant, the inner sound is revealed, and the eyes become clear. One becomes free from disease and achieves victory over the vital fluids <term>dhatu</term>. The gastric fire in the stomach increases—meaning it is stimulated—and the channels are purified. These are the characteristics of Hatha Yoga. || 78 ||

Thus ends the Second Instruction in the vernacular commentary of the *Lamp of Hatha Yoga*. || 2 ||

# Third Instruction

### Root Verse: As the King of Serpents is the support of the earth with its mountains and forests, so too is the Kundalini the support of all yoga practices. || 1 ||
### When the sleeping Kundalini is awakened by the grace of the Guru, then all the lotuses <note>the chakras or energy centers</note> and the knots <term>granthis</term> are pierced. || 2 ||
### Then the path of the void becomes the Royal Road for the breath; then the mind is without support <note>original: "niralamba," a state of objectless meditation</note>, and one cheats Time <note>original: "kala," which also signifies Death</note>. || 3 ||

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He now describes how the Kundalini is the refuge of all yoga. **"With mountains..."** (sashaila): The phrase "with mountains and forests" refers to the earth <note>original: "dhātrīṇāṃ"</note> which contains mountains, forests, and lands. Although the earth is essentially one, the plural is used here to account for the diversity of different regions. Just as the **King of Serpents** (ahināyakaḥ)—referring to Shesha <note>the primordial serpent of myth who balances the world on his hood</note>—is the "support" or foundation, so too the power of the Kundalini is the support of all the "instruments of yoga" <note>original: "yoga-tantrāṇi"</note>, meaning the various methods of yoga. The inner meaning is that without the awakening of Kundalini, all other methods of yoga are futile. || 1 ||

He describes the result of awakening the Kundalini in two verses. **"Sleeping..."** (suptā): When the sleeping Kundalini is awakened or "realized" through the grace of the Guru, then all the **lotuses**, meaning the six energy centers <term>chakras</term>, are "pierced" or opened. Furthermore, the **knots** (granthayaḥ)—specifically the Knot of Brahma, the Knot of Vishnu, and the Knot of Rudra—are also pierced, meaning they are successfully transcended. || 2 ||

**"Of the breath..."** (prāṇasya): At that time, the **path of the void** <note>original: "śūnyapadavī"</note>, which is the central channel <term>sushumna</term>, acts as a **Royal Road** (rājapathāyate) for the vital breath <note>original: "vayu"</note>. It is called a "Royal Road" because the breath can now travel through it with ease and without obstruction. Then the **mind** <note>original: "citta"</note> becomes **without support** (nirālambaṃ), meaning it is liberated from focusing on external objects. At that moment, there is the **cheating of Time**, which is to say, one escapes the grasp of Death. || 3 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Paraphrase ॥<-

Now he describes the Kundalini as the foundation of all methods. **"With mountains..."**: Just as the earth, along with its mountains, forests, cities, and villages, is supported by Shesha-ji, the King of Serpents, so too is the Kundalini the support of every single method of yoga. Without the awakening of the Kundalini, all yoga practices are useless. || 1 ||

Now he describes the fruit of awakening the Kundalini. **"Sleeping..."**: When the sleeping Kundalini rises through the favor of the Guru, then all the six chakras are pierced. Following this, the three knots—the Knot of Brahma, the Knot of Vishnu, and the Knot of Rudra—attain the state of being pierced. || 2 ||

**"Of the breath..."**: At that time, the *Sushumna* channel conducts the breath in the manner of a Royal Highway, and...

# In the Light of Hatha Yoga

### Root Verses
> **The Suṣumṇā, the Path of the Void, the Opening of Brahman <note>original: "brahmarandhra"</note>, the Great Path, the Cremation Ground, the Path of Śāmbhavī, and the Middle Path—these are all synonyms.** || 4 ||
> **Therefore, with every possible effort, to awaken the Goddess <note>the Kundalini</note> who sleeps at the entrance of the Gate of Brahman, one should thoroughly practice the Mudras.** || 5 ||
> **The Great Seal <term>Mahāmudrā</term>, the Great Bond <term>Mahābandha</term>, the Great Piercing <term>Mahāvedha</term>, and the Space-Walker <term>Khecarī</term>; the Upward Flying <term>Uḍḍiyāna</term>, the Root Bond <term>Mūlabandha</term>, and the bond named the Net-Bearer <term>Jālandhara</term>;** || 6 ||
> **The practice called the Reverse Action <term>Viparītakaraṇī</term>, the Thunderbolt <term>Vajrolī</term>, and the Stirring of Power <term>Śakticālana</term>. These are indeed the ten Mudras which destroy old age and death.** || 7 ||

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

He now provides the synonyms for the central channel. **"The Suṣumṇā..."**: These words all describe the same single reality; they are synonyms <note>original: "paryāyāḥ"</note>. The meaning of the verse is clear. || 4 ||

**"Therefore..."**: Since the piercing of the six energy centers <note>the chakras</note> only occurs through the awakening of the Kundalini, one should exert every effort to awaken her. "Brahman" refers to the state of Being, Consciousness, and Bliss <note>original: "sac-cid-ānanda"</note>, and the "Gate" is the means to attain it—the **Suṣumṇā**. By placing the "mouth" or the tip of the Kundalini into the opening of the Suṣumṇā, one awakens the sleeping Goddess. To do this "thoroughly" or perfectly, one must repeatedly practice the techniques beginning with the **Mahāmudrā**. || 5 ||

He identifies the Mudras in the following verse and a half. The meaning is clear. || 6 ||

He describes the benefits of the Mudras in the next two and a half verses. **"These..."**: This refers to the group of ten Mudras just mentioned, which overcome decay <note>old age</note> and death.

---

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

At that time, the mind-seed becomes free from worldly objects. Then, one transcends Time—meaning death—or in other words, one is saved from death. || 3 ||

**"The Suṣumṇā..."**: The Path of the Void, the Opening of Brahman, the Great Path, the Cremation Ground, Śāmbhavī, and the Middle Path are all names used to describe the **Suṣumṇā**. || 4 ||

**"Therefore..."**: Because the piercing of the six chakras happens through the awakening of the Kundalini, one should use all their strength to awaken that sleeping Kundalini. This is done by blocking the "mouth" or front part of the Suṣumṇā—the gate to Brahman—and awakening her through the practice of the Great Seal <note>Mahāmudrā</note> and the others. || 5 ||

**"The Great Seal..."**: 1. Mahāmudrā, 2. Mahābandha, 3. Mahāvedha, 4. Khecarī, 5. Uḍḍiyāna, 6. Mūlabandha, and 7. the bond named Jālandhara. || 6 ||

8. The practice known as the Reverse Action <note>Viparītakaraṇī</note>, 9. Vajrolī, and 10. Śakticālana. These are the ten Mudras.

<summary>This page defines the synonyms for the Suṣumṇā (the central energy channel) and lists the ten primary Mudras (seals or gestures) of Hatha Yoga used to awaken the Kundalini energy and achieve immortality.</summary>
<keywords>Suṣumṇā, Brahmarandhra, Kundalini awakening, Mudras, Mahāmudrā, immortality, energy channels</keywords>

# Lesson Three

### Root Verse
**Declared by the Primal Lord <note>original: "Ādinātha," a title for Shiva</note>, these divine practices bestow the eight-fold sovereignty. They are beloved by all perfected masters <term>Siddhas</term> and are difficult even for the gods to attain.** || 8 ||

---

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

[Referring to verse 7:] They destroy and prevent old age and death. || 7 ||

**"Declared by the Primal Lord..."**: This means they were spoken by the Primal Lord, who is Shambhu <note>another name for Shiva</note>. "Divine" means of celestial origin or most excellent. The "eight-fold sovereignty" <term>Ashta-Aishvarya</term> refers to the eight supernatural powers known as: Minuteness, Vastness, Heaviness, Lightness, Accessibility, Irresistible Will, Mastery, and Subjugation.

1. **Minuteness <term>Aṇimā</term>**: The ability of the body to become as small as an atom through mere will, by setting aside material density.
2. **Vastness <term>Mahimā</term>**: The state of becoming as immense as space by filling oneself with the essence of nature.
3. **Heaviness <term>Garimā</term>**: The quality of making even a very light thing, like a tuft of cotton, as heavy as a mountain.
4. **Lightness <term>Laghimā</term>**: Making even a very heavy thing, like a mountain, as light as cotton.
5. **Accessibility <term>Prāpti</term>**: Being present everywhere at once. For example, while standing on the earth, one can touch the moon with the tip of a finger.
6. **Irresistible Will <term>Prākāmya</term>**: Having no obstacle to one's desires. For example, diving into and emerging from the solid earth as if it were water.
7. **Mastery <term>Īśatā</term>**: The power over the creation, dissolution, and specific arrangement of all elements and their products.
8. **Subjugation <term>Vaśitva</term>**: Bringing all elements and beings under one's complete control.

The Mudras "bestow" these—meaning they grant them in abundance. They are "beloved" or dear to all perfected masters <note>original: "Siddhas"</note> such as Kapila and others. They are "difficult to attain" or rare even for the gods; how much more so for others? || 8 ||

---

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

These Mudras destroy old age and death. || 7 ||

Furthermore, these divine practices were spoken by the Primal Lord, who is Shiva; they are the group of ten Mudras. They grant the eight-fold sovereignty: 1. Minuteness, 2. Vastness, 3. Heaviness, 4. Lightness, 5. Accessibility, 6. Irresistible Will, 7. Mastery, and 8. Subjugation. These are the eight perfections <term>Siddhis</term>. Now, the characteristics of these eight perfections are described:

Through the mere will of the yogi, the density of nature is set aside, and the body becomes as subtle as an atom; this is called **Minuteness** <term>Aṇimā</term>. || 1 ||

By filling oneself with the essence of nature—that is, drawing nature into oneself—one becomes vast and immense like the sky; this is called **Vastness** <term>Mahimā</term>. || 2 ||

Taking very light things like cotton and giving them the heaviness of a mountain; this is **Heaviness** <term>Garimā</term>. || 3 ||

Taking heavy things like mountains and making them as light as cotton; this is **Lightness** <term>Laghimā</term>. || 4 ||

When all things become close at hand—for instance, standing on the earth and touching the moon with the tip of a finger; this is called **Accessibility** <term>Prāpti</term>. || 5 ||

The non-obstruction of will—just as one might dive into water and come out, one might appear or disappear into the earth; know this to be **Irresistible Will** <term>Prākāmya</term>. || 6 ||

And the power over the birth and death of all material things...

<summary>This page describes the ten Mudras as divine techniques revealed by Lord Shiva (Adinatha) that grant the practitioner the eight classical Siddhis or supernatural powers. It provides detailed definitions for each power, ranging from changing body size and weight to total mastery over the material world.</summary>

<keywords>Adinatha, Shiva, Ashta-Aishvarya, Siddhis, Anima, Mahima, Garima, Laghima, Prapti, Prakamya, Ishata, Vashitva, Mudras, supernatural powers</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the text completes the definitions of the eight supernatural powers (Siddhis) before moving into the specific practice of the "Great Seal" (Maha Mudra).</meta>

( 78 )

->**In the Light of Hatha Yoga**<-

**Root Verse: This [practice] should be kept secret with great effort, like a casket of jewels. It must never be told to anyone, just as one keeps the intimacy of a well-born woman private.** || 9 ||

### ### The Great Seal (Maha Mudra)

**Pressing the perineum <note>original: "yoni," referring here to the space between the anus and the genitals</note> with the left heel, and stretching the right leg out, one should hold it firmly with both hands.** || 10 ||

**Applying the lock at the throat <note>the Jalandhara Bandha</note>, one should hold the breath [and direct it] upward. Just as a snake struck by a rod becomes straight like a staff, [so the energy becomes straight].** || 11 ||

### ### Commentary

**"Should be kept secret..."**: This means it should be protected with "great effort," meaning intense exertion. He provides an example for this secrecy: **"Like a..."** just as a casket of jewels—containing diamonds and such—is kept hidden, so should this be. It should not be told to any person; indeed, it should not be spoken of even to the creator god Brahma, much less to anyone else. Another example is given: like the **"intimacy of a well-born woman,"** meaning her private union, so too is this practice [to be kept private]. || 9 ||

Now, he describes the **"Great Seal"** (Maha Mudra), as it is the first mentioned among the ten types of seals (*Mudras*). **"With the base of the foot..."**: with the left heel. **"Pressing the perineum"** means firmly joining the left heel to the perineal region, which is the middle part between the anus and the genitals, by contracting it. The **"right leg"**—the leg other than the left—is **"stretched out"** like a staff, with the heel touching the ground and the toes pointing upward. According to tradition, one should **"hold it firmly"** with both hands, specifically by gripping the big toe with the index fingers. || 10 ||

**"At the throat..."**: This means properly applying a "binding" or lock in the throat region. This refers to performing the Throat Lock <term>Jalandhara Bandha</term>.

### ### Vernacular Translation

The power to be capable of creation is called Mastery <term>Īśatā</term>. || 7 || And the power to bring elements and beings under one's own control is called the perfection of Subjugation <term>Vaśitva Siddhi</term>. || 8 || These [Mudras] are the givers of these powers; they are dear to perfected masters like Kapila, and are difficult even for the gods of the air to attain. || 8 ||

**"Like a..."**: Just as one keeps a box of jewels hidden, so should these be kept secret and not told to anyone. Just as one does not speak of intimate relations with a wife from a noble family, so too is this practice not fit to be spoken of. || 9 ||

Now the first, the Great Seal, is described: **"With the base of the foot..."** By pressing the middle part between the anus and the genitals (the perineum) with the left heel, one should stretch the right leg out long. Placing the heel on the earth and pointing the toes upward like a staff, one should hold the big toe of the right foot firmly with the thumb and index finger. || 10 ||

**"At the throat..."**: Then, having applied the Throat Lock, one should hold the breath upward within the central channel <term>Suṣumṇā</term>.

<summary>This page concludes the description of the eight supernatural powers and introduces Maha Mudra (The Great Seal), emphasizing the absolute secrecy required for these practices and detailing the specific physical posture and breath control needed for the first Mudra.</summary>

### ### Third Lesson

**Root Verse: That power, the Kundalini, suddenly becomes straight. Then, she attains the "state of death" while resting upon the two vessels.** || 12 ||

**Then, one should exhale very slowly, and never with force. For this very reason, the most excellent of the wise call this the "Great Seal" (Mahamudra).** || 13 ||

**This Great Seal was demonstrated by the great adepts. Through it, the "Great Afflictions" and defects such as death are overcome.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

One should hold the wind—the breath—upward within the **Sushumna** <term>the central energetic channel of the spine</term>. This implies the use of the **Mula Bandha** <term>the "Root Lock," performed by contracting the pelvic floor</term>. According to the practitioners of this tradition, this is accomplished by pressing the perineum and "binding the tongue" <note>likely referring to Kechari Mudra, where the tongue is turned back</note>. Just as a snake struck by a rod becomes straight—meaning it loses its coiled shape and becomes like a staff—so too does the energy become straight. || 11 ||

**"That power, the Kundalini..."**: Likewise, the energy at the base, the **Kundalini** <term>the "coiled" latent spiritual energy</term>, suddenly and quickly becomes straight. **"Then, she..."**: The "state of death" occurs in the two "vessels" <note>original: "puta," referring to the nostrils or the Ida and Pingala channels</note> that serve as her support. When the Kundalini is awakened and the breath enters the central channel, the breath is "extinguished" or "dies" in the two side channels <note>original: "Ida" and "Pingala"</note>. || 12 ||

**"Then..."**: After that, one should exhale very slowly. This refers to the breath. One should not exhale with force or speed. Exhaling with force leads to the risk of losing one's strength. **"This Great Seal..."**: This practice was demonstrated—shown clearly—by the great adepts such as **Adinatha** <note>the "First Lord," a title for the god Shiva as the first teacher of Yoga</note> and others. He explains the meaning of the name "Great Seal": The "Great Afflictions" (*Kleshas*) refer to the five mental poisons: ignorance, egoism, attachment, aversion, and the fear of death. These, along with other defects...

->॥ Vernacular Paraphrase ॥<-

Perform the Root Lock here. By pressing the perineum and binding the tongue, the Root Lock is achieved. Just as a snake, when struck by a staff, leaves its coiled shape and becomes straight, you should understand the energy does the same. || 11 ||

**"That power, the Kundalini..."**: In the same way, the Kundalini—the foundational power—quickly becomes straight. Through the awakening of the Kundalini, the breath enters the central channel. Because the breath departs from both the **Ida** and **Pingala** <term>the lunar and solar energy channels</term>, the "state of death" occurs in those two supports. || 12 ||

**"Then..."**: Afterward, exhale the breath slowly. Do not do it with force, or your strength will be harmed. For this reason, the Great Adepts like Adinatha have shown this Great Seal. The "Great Afflictions" like ignorance, attachment, and aversion, as well as the defects of sorrow and delusion, are destroyed. Even death and old age are overcome.

<summary>This page describes the physiological and energetic effects of Mahamudra, specifically how it forces the Kundalini energy to straighten into the central channel (Sushumna), causing the breath to cease in the side channels. It emphasizes the necessity of slow, controlled exhalation to preserve vital strength and lists the spiritual and physical benefits of the practice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the benefits and specific technical practice of Mahamudra (the Great Seal), detailing how the practice must be balanced between the left (lunar) and right (solar) sides of the body.</meta>

### ### On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika

**Root Verse: Therefore, the most excellent of the wise call this the Great Seal.** || 14 ||
**Having practiced on the lunar limb, one should then practice on the solar limb. Until the count of breaths is equal, one should continue; then, one may release the seal.** || 15 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

For those who practice, the defects arising from their actions—such as grief and delusion—are diminished. Even those things beginning with death and including old age are diminished and destroyed. It is for this reason that the "most excellent of the wise" <note>original: *vibudhotttamāḥ*, meaning the highest among the awakened or learned ones</note> call this the **Great Seal** (*Mahamudra*). The etymological meaning is that it "seals" <note>original: *mudrayati*, meaning to seal, close, or settle</note> and pacifies the "Great Afflictions" (*Mahaklesha*) such as death. || 13 || 14 ||

Now the sequence of practicing the Great Seal is described. **"On the lunar limb..."**: The **lunar limb** (*Chandranga*) refers to the side characterized by the lunar channel <term>the *Ida* nadi, or the left side of the body</term>. The word *tu* <note>a Sanskrit particle</note> is used here to complete the meter of the verse. Having practiced thoroughly on that left side, one should then practice on the **solar limb** (*Suryanga*), which is the side characterized by the solar channel <term>the *Pingala* nadi, or the right side of the body</term>. After practicing on the left side, one should practice on the right for as long as it takes to reach an equal number of breath retentions <term>known as *Kumbhaka*</term>. Once the count is equal, one should release the Great Seal.

The sequence is as follows: Press the contracted heel of the left foot into the **perineum** <term>original: *yonisthana*, the "place of the source" located between the genitals and the anus</term>. Grasp the big toe of the extended right foot with the index finger and thumb, and practice. This is "practice on the left side." During this practice, the inhaled air remains in the left side. Then, press the contracted heel of the right foot into the perineum, grasp the big toe of the extended left foot with the index finger and thumb, and practice. This is "practice on the right side." During this practice, the inhaled air remains in the right side. || 15 ||

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

For this reason, the great wise ones call this the **Great Seal**. || 13 || 14 ||

The sequence for practicing this Great Seal is now told. **"On the lunar limb..."**: Having practiced in the lunar limb, which is the left side, one should then practice in the solar limb, which is the right side. After practicing on the left side, one should continue until the number of breath retentions is equal to that performed on the left. When the count is equal, one may release the Great Seal.

The method is this: Applying the left heel to the perineum, stretch the right leg out long. Holding that toe with the index finger and thumb, perform the practice; this is called "practice on the left." In this practice, the breath that is filled remains situated in the left side. Then, pulling in the right leg and applying its heel to the perineum, stretch the left leg out long. Holding that toe with the index finger and thumb, perform the practice; this is called "practice on the right." In this practice, the breath that is filled remains only in the right side. || 15 ||

<vocab>महामुद्रा (Mahamudra), हठयोग (Hatha Yoga), चंद्रांग (Chandranga), सूर्यांग (Suryanga), कुंभक (Kumbhaka), विबुधोत्तमाः (Vibudhotttamah), योनिस्थान (Yonisthana)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines Mahamudra as the "Great Seal" because it seals away the afflictions of death and aging, and provides specific physical instructions for balancing the practice between the left (lunar) and right (solar) energy channels.</summary>
<keywords>Mahamudra, lunar limb, solar limb, breath retention, Kumbhaka, perineum, yoga posture, purification, Ida, Pingala</keywords>

### ### The Third Chapter

**Root Verse: There is no distinction between wholesome or unwholesome food; all tastes, even those lacking flavor, are digested. Even if a terrible poison has been consumed, it is digested as if it were the nectar of immortality.** || 16 ||
**Consumption, leprosy, constipation, abdominal tumors, and indigestion—these and other primary ailments are destroyed for the person who practices the Great Seal.** || 17 ||
**This Great Seal has been described as the bringer of great success to humanity. It must be guarded with effort and should not be given to just anyone.** || 18 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The author explains the virtues of the Great Seal in three verses. **"There is no..."**: The word "for the practitioner of the Great Seal" is implied here. There is no distinction between **wholesome** <note>original: *pathyam*, referring to food that is beneficial to the yogic path</note> or **unwholesome** <note>original: *apathyam*, referring to prohibited or harmful food</note>. This means there is no need for strict dietary considerations. Therefore, all tastes that are consumed, such as bitter, sour, and so on, are digested; this connects through a grammatical shift in the sentence. Even **flavorless** <note>original: *nīrasāḥ*</note> substances—meaning items from which the essence has departed or those that are stale—are digested. Even if **terrible** <note>original: *ghoram*, meaning difficult to digest or deadly</note> **poison** <note>original: *shvedam*</note> is eaten as food, it is digested and becomes like **nectar** <note>original: *pīyūṣam*, the drink of the gods</note>. The implication is: what then need be said of ordinary food? || 16 ||

**"Consumption..."**: For the person who practices the Great Seal, **consumption** <note>original: *kshaya*, often referring to tuberculosis or a general wasting of the body</note>, **leprosy** <note>original: *kustha*, referring to various chronic skin diseases</note>, **constipation** <note>original: *gudavarta*, a condition where the downward wind is obstructed</note>, and **abdominal tumors** <note>original: *gulma*, internal swellings or chronic gas</note>—these specific diseases, as well as **indigestion** <note>original: *ajirna*, the failure to cook or process food in the stomach</note>, are destroyed. These illnesses are the "forerunners" or the primary causes of other conditions like dropsy <term>severe swelling of the body</term> and fever; such defects and the diseases born from the three humors reach their end and are destroyed. || 17 ||

Concluding the section on the Great Seal, the author speaks of its secrecy. **"This..."**: This Great Seal has been described as... [text continues to next page]

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

The virtues of the Great Seal are described. **"There is no..."**: For the practitioner of the Great Seal, there is no consideration of wholesome or unwholesome food. Therefore, all tastes—bitter, sour, and so on—that are eaten will be digested. Even substances where the essence has dried up or those that are a day or two old will be digested. And even if a terrible, hard-to-digest poison is eaten, it will be digested just like nectar. || 16 ||

**"Consumption..."**: For the man who practices the Great Seal, diseases such as consumption, leprosy, abdominal tumors, and indigestion—which are the leaders of other conditions like fever, urinary disorders, and stomach ailments—these many diseases and defects will be destroyed. || 17 ||

**"This..."**: I have described this Great Seal which brings **Great Success** <term>the *Mahasiddhis*, such as *Anima* or the power to become minute</term> to those men who practice it. This should be kept secret; it is not worthy of being given to anyone who is unqualified. || 18 ||

<vocab>Mahamudra (Great Seal), Siddhi (Success/Power), Kshaya (Consumption), Kustha (Leprosy), Gudavarta (Constipation), Gulma (Abdominal tumor), Ajirna (Indigestion), Pathya (Wholesome), Apathya (Unwholesome), Piyusha (Nectar), Shveda (Poison), Anima (The power of smallness)</vocab>

It concludes by emphasizing that this powerful practice must remain a secret shared only with the qualified.</summary>

### On the Hatha Yoga Pradipika

**Root Verse: One should place the heel of the left foot at the perineum. Then, place the right foot upon the left thigh.** || 19 ||

->Thus begins the Great Lock (Mahabandha).<-

**Having filled the body with breath, one should press the chin firmly against the heart. Contracting the perineum, one should focus the mind on the middle channel.** || 20 ||
**Having held the breath according to one's capacity, one should exhale the air slowly. Having practiced on the left side, one should then practice on the right side.** || 21 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The state [of the Great Seal] has been described. The word "by me" is implied. What kind of state is it? For the men who practice it, it is the **maker** of the **great successes** <note>original: *siddhis*, referring to the eight supernatural powers such as *anima*, the power to become minute</note>. It is to be **guarded** with **great effort**; it is not **to be given** to just anyone who is unworthy or disconnected from the practice. The use of the genitive case here denotes a general prohibition—meaning it is not fit to be shared with the unqualified. || 18 ||

Now the author describes the Great Lock. **"The heel..."**: One should place the **heel** <note>original: *pārṣṇi*, the lower part of the ankle</note> of the **left** foot at the **perineum** <note>original: *yonisthāne*, the space between the anus and the genitals</note>. The *Amarakosha* dictionary defines the heel as the part below the ankle joints. One should firmly place the **right foot** upon the **left thigh**. The word "thus" <note>original: *tathā*</note> is used here to complete the meter of the verse. || 19 ||

**"Having filled..."**: Thereafter, having inhaled the breath and pressing the **chin** firmly against the **heart**, one should fix it steadily. By this, the **Chin Lock** <note>original: *Jālandhara Bandha*</note> is described. By contracting the perineum, the **Root Lock** <note>original: *Mūla Bandha*</note> is indicated. However, some suggest that the Root Lock need not be performed separately as its purpose is fulfilled by the Tongue Lock <note>original: *jihvābandha*</note>. One should then direct the **mind** into the **middle** channel <note>original: *madhyanāḍyām*, referring to the Sushumna</note>. || 20 ||

**"Having held..."**: Without exceeding one's capacity, after holding the breath (retention), one should slowly...

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

Now the second practice, the Great Lock <term>Mudra</term>, is described. **"The heel..."**: Placing the heel of the left foot at the perineum, and then placing the right foot upon the left thigh. || 19 ||

**"Having filled..."**: Afterward, having performed an inhalation <term>Puraka</term>, one should press the chin firmly against the heart—this is called the Chin Lock <term>Jalandhara Bandha</term>. By slightly pressing the perineum with that heel, the Root Lock <term>Mula Bandha</term> is performed. One should then direct the mind to act within the central energy channel <term>Sushumna</term>. || 20 ||

**"Having held..."**: Having performed breath retention <term>Kumbhaka</term> according to one's strength, one should then perform a slow exhalation <term>Rechaka</term>. Having completed a round starting with the left side, one should then practice an equal number of times starting with the right side. || 21 ||

<summary>This page details the physical positioning and breath control required for Mahabandha (the Great Lock), involving the simultaneous engagement of the chin and root locks to direct energy into the central channel.</summary>
<keywords>Mahabandha, Jalandhara Bandha, Mula Bandha, Perineum, Sushumna, Kumbhaka, Yoga Posture, Breath Control</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Mahabandha (The Great Lock). While the previous page focused on the physical posture of the feet and the basic application of the lock, this page introduces a variation regarding the throat and tongue positions, emphasizing the movement of vital energy through the channels.</meta>

### Third Lesson

**Root Verse: However, in the opinion of some, one should avoid the throat lock.**
**The lock of the tongue placed at the "royal teeth" is said to be better.** || 22 ||
**This [lock] blocks the upward movement of vital energy in all the channels.**
**Truly, this Great Lock (Mahabandha) is the giver of great accomplishments.** || 23 ||
**It is expert at releasing one from the great snare of death.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

One should exhale the <term>Anila</term><note>The word *anila* means air or breath</note> slowly. Having practiced thoroughly on the left side of the body, one should then practice on the right side for an exactly equal number of repetitions. || 21 ||

Now, the author explains why the contraction of the throat in <term>Jālandharabandha</term><note>The Throat Lock</note> might be unnecessary. **"The opinion..."**: This is the opinion of certain teachers. What is it? In this context of the Great Lock, one should **avoid**—specifically set aside—the **locking** or contraction of the **throat**. Why? Because the "kings of teeth" are the **royal teeth** <note>original: *rājadantha*, referring to the upper front incisors or the alveolar ridge just behind them</note>. The **tongue** situated at these royal teeth—meaning the connection of the tongue to that upper region—is **better**. It is considered superior compared to the usual contraction of the throat. || 22 ||

**"This [lock]..."**: This locking of the tongue at the royal teeth is the **inhibitor** or blocker of the **upward movement** of the breath within **all the channels** <note>original: *nāḍīs*</note>—numbering seventy-two thousand—excluding the central channel <note>original: *suṣumnā*</note>. By this, the benefit described for the Throat Lock—that "it binds the network of vessels"—is achieved through this tongue-lock alone. Now, the author describes the result of the Great Lock. **"Truly, this..."**: This aforementioned practice is famous for bestowing **great accomplishments** <note>original: *mahāsiddhis*, the supernatural powers gained through yoga</note> in an excellent manner. || 23 ||

**"Time..."**: The **snare** of **Time** <note>original: *kāla*, which denotes both Time and the personification of Death</note> is like a hunter's net; this technique is specifically [skilled at releasing one from that bondage].

->॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥<-

**"The opinion..."**: There is an opinion among some teachers that in the <term>Jālandharabandha</term>, one should specifically avoid the throat lock. That is to say, one should not press the chin against the heart. Why? Because the "royal teeth" (*rajadanta*) are the two front teeth. Placing the tongue against these teeth is considered superior. || 22 ||

**"This [lock]..."**: This tongue-lock restrains the movement of the breath upward through all seventy-two thousand channels, except for the central <term>Suṣumnā</term> channel. By doing this, the network of veins and nerves is bound, which is the very reason it is called a "lock." Now its fruit is described: this Great Lock grants great spiritual perfections (*siddhis*). || 23 ||

**"Time..."**: Furthermore, it is expert in removing the bondage caused by the snare of death. The <term>Iḍā</term>, <term>Piṅgalā</term>, and Su... <note>The text transitions here to discuss the three primary energy channels—the lunar, solar, and central paths.</note>

<summary>This page details a specific variation of Mahabandha (The Great Lock) where a tongue-lock at the upper teeth replaces the chin-to-chest throat lock. This technique is said to redirect energy from 72,000 minor channels into the central path, ultimately overcoming the power of death.</summary>
<keywords>Mahabandha, Jalandhara Bandha, Rajadanta, Nadis, Sushumna, Siddhis, breath control, yoga anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Mahabandha (The Great Lock). While the previous page focused on the physical posture and the basic application of the lock, this page concludes the benefits of Mahabandha and introduces Mahavedha (The Great Piercing), emphasizing that the previous techniques are incomplete without this third stage.</meta>

८४
हठयोगप्रदीपिकायां

**Root Verse: [The Great Lock] maintains the confluence of the three rivers and leads the mind to Kedāra.** || 24 ||
**Just as a woman endowed with beauty and charm is without purpose without a man, so too the Great Seal and the Great Lock are fruitless if they are devoid of the Great Piercing.** || 25 ||

### Now, the Great Piercing (Mahāvedha)

**The yogi, remaining in the Great Lock, should perform inhalation with a focused mind, and restrain the movement of the breaths firmly with the throat lock.** || 26 ||

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

The practitioner is **expert**—skilled—in **releasing** or liberating [the breath]. The **confluence of the three rivers** <note>original: *triveṇī-saṃgama*</note> refers to the meeting of the three primary energy channels <note>the Iḍā, Piṅgalā, and Suṣumṇā</note>; this is what the lock maintains, like the sacred confluence at **Prayāga**. One should lead the **mind**—the inner sense—to **Kedāra**. The word *Kedāra* refers to the "Seat of Shiva" located between the eyebrows. Through the grammatical rule of "movement and knowledge," the mind is the agent that is directed to this goal. || 24 ||

To explain the **Great Piercing** <note>original: *Mahāvedha*</note>, the author first describes its excellence. **"Endowed with beauty..."**: **Beauty** <note>original: *rūpa*</note> is a quality pleasing to the eye, while **charm** <note>original: *lāvaṇya*</note> is a specific kind of radiance. As it is said: "That which appears in the limbs like the shimmering luster found in pearls is called charm." A young woman endowed with these two qualities is **without purpose**—or fruitless—without a **man** (a husband). In the same way, the **Great Seal** <note>original: *Mahāmudrā*</note> and the **Great Lock** <note>original: *Mahābandha*</note> are **fruitless**—meaning useless—if they are **devoid** of or lacking the Great Piercing. According to the Great Commentator’s <note>Patañjali</note> rule on the dropping of the first part of a compound, the word "Great" is implied here. || 25 ||

The author now describes the **Great Piercing**. **"Remaining in the Great Lock..."**: One who is established in the posture of the Great Lock. A yogi with a **focused mind**—one whose intellect is one-pointed—should perform **inhalation** <note>original: *pūraka*</note> by taking in breath through the nostrils. By applying the **throat lock**—the <term>Jālandhara-bandha</term>—one should firmly block the **movement** of the <term>Prāṇas</term> <note>the vital life forces</note>... || 26 ||

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

[The Great Lock] maintains the confluence of the three rivers—the <term>Iḍā, Piṅgalā, and Suṣumṇā</term>—and leads the mind to **Kedāra**, which is the seat of Shiva between the eyebrows. || 24 ||

**Beauty and Charm:** Just as a woman may be possessed of beauty, charm, and grace, but is without purpose if she has no husband, so too the **Great Seal** and **Great Lock** are fruitless and useless if they are not joined with the **Great Piercing**. || 25 ||

Now the third practice, the **Great Piercing**, is described. A yogi established in the Great Piercing posture, with a focused intellect, should inhale through the nostrils. Then, applying the **throat lock** (the <term>Jālandhara-bandha</term>), he should restrain the upward and downward movement of the breath through <term>Kumbhaka</term> <note>breath retention</note>. || 26 ||

<summary>This page explains the spiritual geography of the body (Triveni and Kedara) and introduces Mahavedha, arguing that Mahamudra and Mahabandha are incomplete without it. It begins the technical instructions for Mahavedha, focusing on breath control and the throat lock.</summary>
<keywords>Mahavedha, Mahabandha, Mahamudra, Triveni, Kedara, Jalandhara Bandha, Prana, breath retention, beauty analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the physical instructions for Mahāvedha (The Great Piercing). Following the breath retention and throat lock described on the previous page, the yogi now uses a specific physical movement—striking the ground—to force energy into the central channel, concluding the sequence of the three great locks.</meta>

( 85 )
# Third Instruction

**Root Verse: With both hands placed evenly on the ground, one should gently strike the buttocks <note>original: *sphicau*</note> [against the floor]. Bypassing the two channels <note>the nostrils or the Iḍā and Piṅgalā</note>, the breath flashes as it enters the middle path.** || 27 ||

**The connection of Moon, Sun, and Fire <note>the three primary energy channels</note> is formed for the sake of immortality. A death-like state <note>original: *mṛtāvasthā*</note> arises; then, one should exhale the breath.** || 28 ||

**This Great Piercing <note>original: *Mahāvedha*</note>, through constant practice, bestows great supernatural powers <note>the *siddhis*, such as becoming infinitely small</note>.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...by obstructing the upward and downward movement [of the breath], and holding it silently and motionlessly—meaning, by performing <term>Kumbhaka</term> <note>breath retention</note>. || 26 ||

Regarding **"With both hands"**: This means having both hands placed evenly on the ground or floor. One whose palms are firmly attached to the ground and whose arms are straight. **Buttocks** <note>original: *sphicau*</note> refers to the hips/rear; according to the *Amarakośa* lexicon: "the terms *sphicau* and *kaṭiprothau* refer to the buttocks of a woman." By supporting oneself on the palms of the hands on the ground, and along with the left foot whose heel is pressed against the **perineum** <note>original: *yoni-sthāna*</note>, one should lift oneself slightly from the ground and **slowly strike**—properly thump—[the buttocks against the floor]. On the ground itself, the breath—bypassing the two channels of the <term>Iḍā</term> and <term>Piṅgalā</term>—enters the middle; thus the "middle-going" breath flashes within the <term>Suṣumṇā</term>. || 27 ||

Regarding **"Moon..."**: Moon, Sun, and Fire refer to the channels presided over by them: the Iḍā, Piṅgalā, and Suṣumṇā. Their **connection** <note>original: *saṃbandha*</note> refers to the meeting of these breaths. This union **occurs for the sake of immortality**—for liberation. The word "indeed" <note>original: *vai*</note> is an indeclinable used for emphasis. A **death-like state**—the condition of one whose life force has departed—**is produced**. This is because the breath is no longer flowing through the Iḍā or Piṅgalā. **Then**, after that, one should **exhale the air**—slowly release it through the nostrils. || 28 ||

Regarding **"The Great Piercing"**: This **Great Piercing** <note>original: *Mahāvedha*</note>, through **practice**—meaning repeated performance—bestows **great supernatural powers** <note>original: *mahāsiddhi*</note> such as <term>Aṇimā</term> <note>the power to become as small as an atom</note> and others...

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

**With both hands:** Placing both hands evenly on the earth so the palms are touching. Then, with the support of both hands and the heel <note>original: *pāṣṇi*</note> which is pressed into the perineum, one should rise up like a **ball** <note>original: *kanduka*</note> and then slowly strike [the ground]. In doing so, the breath bypasses both the Iḍā and Piṅgalā and is obtained in the middle of the Suṣumṇā. || 27 ||

**Moon...:** The connection of the channels presided over by the Moon, Sun, and Fire—the Iḍā, Piṅgalā, and Suṣumṇā—happens for the sake of liberation. Certainly, a death-like state, where the life-breath seems to depart, is produced; that is, one becomes like a dead person. After that, one should slowly perform **exhalation** <note>original: *recaka*</note> of the breath through the nostrils. || 28 ||

**The Great Piercing:** This which is the Great Piercing, by practicing it, brings about the great supernatural powers like <term>Aṇimā</term> and the rest...

<vocab>Mahāvedha (The Great Piercing), Kumbhaka (Breath retention), Iḍā (Left energy channel), Piṅgalā (Right energy channel), Suṣumṇā (Central energy channel), Aṇimā (Power of minuteness), Sphicau (Buttocks), Kanduka (Ball/jumping movement), Mṛtāvasthā (Death-like state)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the physical practice of Mahāvedha (The Great Piercing), where the yogi strikes the ground with their buttocks while holding the breath to force energy into the central Suṣumṇā channel. This process creates a "death-like state" of stillness that leads to supernatural powers and spiritual liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Mahāvedha, Suṣumṇā, breath retention, Kumbhaka, energy channels, supernatural powers, yoga practice, liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three great practices: Mahā-Mudrā, Mahā-Bandha, and Mahā-Vedha. The previous page concluded the physical description of Mahā-Vedha (The Great Piercing), and this page now details the benefits and frequency of practicing these three combined techniques.</meta>

# In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika

**Root Verse: [This practice] is the destroyer of wrinkles, gray hair, and trembling; it is practiced by the best of seekers.** || 29 ||

**These three are a great secret, the destroyers of old age and death. They cause the increase of the [digestive] fire and bestow the supernatural qualities such as <term>Aṇimā</term> <note>the power to become as small as an atom</note>.** || 30 ||

**It should be performed in eight ways [or eight times], watch by watch <note>original: *yāme yāme*; a yāma is a three-hour period</note>, day after day. It consistently accumulates a store of merit and always shatters the flood of sins.**

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

...[These practices] are the bestowers of those [attainments]; they are powerfully effective. **Wrinkles** <note>original: *valī*</note> refers to the shriveling of the skin due to old age. **Gray hair** <note>original: *palitaṃ*</note> refers to the whitening of the hair due to age. **Trembling** <note>original: *vepaḥ*</note> refers to shaking. Because it destroys these, it is the "destroyer of wrinkles, gray hair, and trembling." Therefore, it is practiced—meaning habitually performed—by the **best of seekers**, those who are the most advanced among practitioners. || 29 ||

The author now describes the extreme secrecy of these three starting with the Great Seal <note>Mahā-Mudrā</note>. **Regarding "These"**: These three, the triad of Great Mudrās, are a **great secret**, an ultimate mystery. The following descriptions explain why: Because they are the specific destroyers of **old age** (senescence) and **death** (the final separation of the life-force from the body). They are the "makers" or causes of the increase—the brilliance—of the **fire** <note>original: *vahneḥ*</note> of the stomach (digestion). They bestow the qualities starting with **Aṇimā** <note>the eight *siddhis* or supernatural powers</note> in a superior way, thus they are "bestowers of Aṇimā and other qualities." The word "and" (*cakāra*) is used to include other benefits like health and victory over the <term>Bindu</term> <note>the preservation of vital essence</note>. The word "indeed" (*eva*) is used for emphasis. || 30 ||

Now he describes the specific method of practice for these three individually. **Regarding "Eight-fold"**: **Day after day** means every day. **Watch by watch** <note>original: *yāme yāme*</note> refers to every three-hour period; the word is repeated to show frequency. It is performed in eight ways, or **eight times** <note>original: *aṣṭadhā*</note>. The word "and" (*ca*) here also adds emphasis. This applies to all "three" practices. What is its nature? It is the "accumulator" or performer of a **store of merit**. Furthermore, what else? It is like a thunderbolt in **shattering the flood of sins**—meaning the entire heap of past mistakes. It is "always" effective; whenever it is practiced, it destroys sin at that very moment.

->॥ Vernacular Commentary ॥<-

They are capable of bestowing those [attainments]. Wrinkles (*vali*), grayness (*palita*), and trembling (*kampa*): specifically, when one becomes old, three folds appear on the body, which are called *valī*. And when the hair becomes white due to old age, that is called *palita*. And when the body starts shaking due to old age, that is called *kampa*. It destroys these three. Therefore, those who are the best among practitioners should practice this. || 29 ||

**Regarding "These"**: These three mudrās, beginning with Mahā-Mudrā, are highly secret. They specifically remove old age and death. They increase the digestive fire. They grant supernatural powers like *Aṇimā*. || 30 ||

**Regarding "Eight-fold"**: In one day there are eight watches (*prahara*); so one should perform eight [repetitions] daily in every watch, and

<meta>catchword: and</meta>
