# CHAPTER THREE

**Root Verse: For those who are well-instructed, this initial practice should be performed modestly in this manner.** || 31 ||

## NOW, KHECHARĪ ##

**Root Verse: When the tongue is turned backward and enters the hollow of the skull, and the gaze is fixed between the eyebrows, this is Khecharī Mudrā.** || 32 ||

**Through cutting, moving, and milking, one should gradually increase [the length of] the tongue until it touches the space between the eyebrows. Then, the perfection of Khecharī is attained.** || 33 ||

### ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

**"Those who are well-instructed"** refers to those who possess the traditional teaching—the instructions of a Guru. **"In this manner"** refers to the method previously stated: day by day, in every <term>yāma</term> <note>a three-hour period</note>, totaling eight times. The **"initial practice"** should be performed only **"modestly"** or bit by bit. || 31 ||

Desiring to explain **Khecharī**, the author first describes its form in the verse beginning with **"In the skull."** In the **"hollow of the skull"** refers to the cavity within the head. Within that hollow, the **"tongue"** (the organ of taste) should be **"turned backward,"** meaning it is directed in the opposite direction <note>pointing back toward the throat rather than forward</note>. The **"gaze"** (the act of seeing) should be **"between the eyebrows,"** meaning it enters the space between the brows. This is **Khecharī Mudrā**. The definition is established: Khecharī consists of fixing the gaze between the eyebrows while simultaneously inserting the tongue into the hollow of the skull. || 32 ||

The author now describes the signs of perfecting Khecharī in the verse beginning with **"Cutting."** **"Cutting"** <note>original: *chedana*; the surgical snipping of the frenulum (the membrane under the tongue)</note> will be explained in the very next verse. **"Moving"** <note>original: *cālana*</note> refers to grasping the tongue with the thumb and forefinger and moving it from side to side. **"Milking"** <note>original: *doha*</note> refers to pulling the tongue with the thumb and forefinger, similar to how one milks a cow. Through these actions, one should **"increase"** or lengthen the **"tongue"** until it is long enough. How long? **"Until it touches the space between the eyebrows"**—specifically the external point between the brows. When it reaches that, the **"perfection of Khecharī"** occurs. || 33 ||

### ॥ VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ॥

This practice increases the store of merit and, like a thunderbolt, destroys the flood of sins. For a person who is well-instructed, it is proper to perform this initial practice modestly, following the routine of eight times a day, every three hours. || 31 ||

Now the fourth Mudrā, **Khecharī**, is described. **"In the skull"** means the tongue enters the hole or cavity there in a reversed position, and one looks with the eyes into the middle of the eyebrows. This is Khecharī Mudrā. || 32 ||

Now the sign of perfecting Khecharī is described. **"Cutting"** will be explained ahead. **"Moving"** means catching the tongue with the thumb and forefinger and shaking it. **"Milking"** means pulling and stretching the tongue with the thumb and forefinger of both hands, just as one milks the teats of a cow. How much should it be lengthened? It should be increased until it can reach out and touch the middle of the eyebrows. Then, the perfection of Khecharī is achieved. || 33 ||

<summary>This page introduces Khecharī Mudrā, a practice involving reversing the tongue into the nasal cavity and focusing the gaze between the eyes. It details the physical preparation of the tongue through "cutting, moving, and milking" to achieve the necessary length for success.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, tongue stretching, milking the tongue, hollow of the skull, gaze, third eye, Hatha Yoga, Guru's instruction</keywords>

# IN THE HAṬHAYOGA PRADĪPIKĀ

**Root Verse: Taking a blade as sharp, smooth, and clean as the leaf of the Snuhi plant, one should cut [the frenulum] by the breadth of a single hair.** || 34 ||

**Then, one should rub the area with a powder made of Saindhava and Pathyā. After seven days have passed, one should again cut it by the breadth of a hair.** || 35 ||

**Following this sequence, one should practice daily with diligence for six months. After six months, the binding of the vein at the root of the tongue is destroyed.** || 36 ||

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

The author explains the means for this practice in the verse beginning with **Snuhi**. **Snuhi** is the *Guda* plant <note>Euphorbia</note>; its **leaf** is its petal. **Resembling a Snuhi leaf** means shaped like it. One should take an **extremely sharp**, **smooth**, and **stainless** instrument—a tool for cutting—and after taking it, one should **cut** by the **measure of a hair**. The object of this action—**the vein at the root of the tongue** <note>the lingual frenulum</note>—is implied. The *Amarakośa* <note>a famous 4th-century Sanskrit lexicon</note> says: "Sihuṇḍa, Vajrasnuk, Snuhi, and Guḍā are synonymous names for this plant" <note>original: "miśrayāpyatha sihuṇḍo vajrasnuk strī snuhī guḍe"</note>. || 34 ||

**Then** means after the cutting. One should **rub** the cut area of the vein with two **powdered** substances: **Saindhava**, which is salt originating from the Indus region <term>Saindhava: rock salt</term>, and **Pathyā**, which is Haritakī <term>Pathyā: the Yellow Myrobalan fruit, used in Ayurveda for healing</term>. This cutting and the rubbing with salt and Haritakī should be performed every morning and evening for seven days. Because practitioners of yoga are generally forbidden from consuming salt, some practitioners use a powder of **Khadira** <term>Khadira: the astringent wood of Acacia catechu</term> and Haritakī instead. The mention of **Saindhava** in the root verse is intended specifically for the preparation of Khecharī before the main practice of Haṭha. **After seven days have passed** means when they are gone, on the eighth day. Terms denoting "attaining" here imply "passing." One should then cut a hair's breadth more than the previous cut. || 35 ||

**In this manner** refers to following this sequence: cutting a hair's breadth and then rubbing with salt and Haritakī morning and evening for seven days...

->|| TRANSLATION INTO THE COMMON TONGUE ||<-

Now the method for Khecharī is described. **Snuhi**: Taking a blade as sharp, smooth, and clean as the leaf of the **Thūhar** <note>The Hindi name for the Snuhi or Euphorbia plant</note> plant, one should cut the vein beneath the tongue by the breadth of a hair. || 34 ||

**Then**: After cutting, one should grind rock salt and **Harade** <note>The Hindi name for Haritakī</note> into a powder and rub it on the cut. This should be done both in the evening and the morning. Because salt is forbidden for a yogi, one should grind **Khādira** and Harade and rub them instead. Do this for seven days, and then after seven days, on the eighth day, cut it slightly more again. || 35 ||

**In this manner**: By this sequence, one should rub with salt and Harade for another seven days. A practitioner of Yoga...

<summary>This page describes the surgical process of lengthening the tongue for Khecharī Mudrā, involving minute weekly incisions of the frenulum followed by the application of healing salts and herbs over a six-month period.</summary>
<keywords>Khecharī Mudrā, tongue lengthening, Snuhi, rock salt, Haritakī, Khadira, frenulum cutting, anatomical preparation, Haṭhayoga technique</keywords>

### THIRD CHAPTER
12

**Root Verse: Having turned the tongue backwards, one should join it to the triple path. That is the Khecharī Mudrā; it is called the Sky-Circle.** || 37 ||

**If the yogi remains even for half a moment with the tongue turned upwards, he is freed from poisons, diseases, death, and old age.** || 38 ||

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

...the cutting and the rubbing. On the eighth day, one should perform more cutting. Following the prescribed order, one should practice this daily with diligence for six months. The words "cutting" and "rubbing" are the actions implied here. After six months, the bond of the vein—the *nādi* at the root of the tongue (the lower part)—which acts as an obstacle to the connection between the tongue and the hollow of the skull, is completely destroyed. || 36 ||

The author now explains what should be done once the tongue has lengthened through the process of cutting, in the verse beginning with **Kala**. **Kala** means the tongue. By making it **turned backwards**—that is, facing the internal opening—one should **join** it to the **triple path** <term>Tripatha: the junction of the three principal subtle energy channels—Ida, Pingala, and Sushumna—located in the upper palate</term>. This act of joining it to the triple path in the hollow of the skull is the **Khecharī Mudrā**. It is also spoken of as **Vyomachakra** <term>Vyomachakra: the "Sky-Circle" or "Ether-Wheel," referring to the internal space behind the palate</term>. || 37 ||

Now, the benefits of Khecharī are stated in the verse beginning with **Rasana**. By turning the **tongue** so that it goes upwards into the cavity above the palate, if the **Khecharī Mudrā** is maintained for even **half a moment** <note>original: "kshana-ardham." A *muhurta* is 48 minutes; its half, a *ghatika*, is 24 minutes</note>, the yogi is **freed from poisons**, such as the venom of snakes and scorpions. He is also freed from **disease** (the imbalance of the bodily humors), **death** (the final separation of the life-force from the body), and **old age** (senility), as well as symptoms such as wrinkles and grey hair. The word **moment** (*kshana*) has multiple meanings: "It is used to refer to a festival, a center, a short time, a rule, a fixed order, and time itself" <note>This is a quotation from a Sanskrit dictionary (Nānārtha) to clarify the timeframe of the practice</note>. || 38 ||

->|| VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ||<-

On the eighth day, one should cut more. If one does this daily with discipline for six months, then after six months, the vein at the root of the tongue—which prevents the tongue from joining with the hollow of the skull—will be destroyed. || 36 ||

**Kalamiti** || Having turned the tongue backwards and sideways, one should place it in the path of the three channels <note>the hollow of the skull</note>. This is Khecharī Mudrā; this is what is called the Sky-Circle. || 37 ||

Now the benefits of Khecharī are told. **Rasanamiti** || If the tongue enters the cavity above the palate and remains in Khecharī Mudrā for about twenty-four minutes, the yogi is freed from the poisons of creatures such as snakes and scorpions. And he is freed from things like disease, death, and old age, as well as wrinkles and grey hair. || 38 ||

<summary>This page describes the final stage of Khecharī Mudrā, where the lengthened tongue is inserted into the nasal cavity (the "triple path"), and lists its miraculous benefits, including immunity to poison and the reversal of aging.</summary>
<keywords>Khecharī Mudrā, Vyomachakra, Tripatha, Yoga benefits, Hatha Yoga, rejuvenation, poison immunity, tongue-stretching</keywords>

### LIGHT ON HAṬHA YOGA
13

**Root Verse: Disease, death, sloth, sleep, hunger, and thirst do not occur; nor does fainting happen to one who knows the Khecharī Mudrā.** || 39 ||

**He is not afflicted by disease, he is not smeared by the effects of actions (karma), and he is not constrained by Time (death), he who knows the Khecharī Mudrā.** || 40 ||

**Because the mind moves in the "sky" (the internal space) and the tongue has entered and moves in the "sky," this Mudrā is described by the perfected masters as Khecharī (The Sky-Goer).** || 41 ||

**For the one who has sealed the cavity above the uvula with Khecharī, his vital fluid (*bindu*) does not fall, even when embraced by a passionate woman.** || 42 ||

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

**No disease, etc.** || The one who knows the Khecharī Mudrā does not experience disease, death, or **sloth** <note>original: *tandrā*</note>—which is a specific dull state of the internal mind. There is no sleep, no hunger, no **thirst** <note>original: *tṛṣā*</note>, and no **fainting** <note>original: *mūrcchā*</note>—which is a specific state where the mind is overwhelmed by **darkness** <term>Tamas: the quality of dullness, heaviness, or ignorance in Samkhya philosophy</term>. || 39 ||

**Afflicted, etc.** || The one who knows the Khecharī Mudrā is not joined with disease, such as fever or similar ailments. || 40 ||

**Mind, etc.** || For this reason: the **mind** <note>original: *citta*</note> moves in the **sky** <note>original: *khe*</note>, meaning the space between the eyebrows; and the tongue, having gone there, also moves in that same "sky." For that reason, this Mudrā is described as **Khecharī**, well-known as Khecharī. The word "nāma" (name) is used here as an indeclinable meaning "well-known." It was defined by the **perfected masters** <term>Siddhas: enlightened beings who have attained liberation and supernatural powers</term> such as Kapila and others. The name is explained by its parts: "Khe" refers to the space or void within the eyebrows, and "charī" refers to the mind and tongue moving or going there. Regarding the repetition of terms like "disease" in these three verses, it is not considered a literary fault because these verses are part of a traditional collection of wisdom. || 41 ||

**By Khecharī, etc.** || By the yogi, with the Khecharī Mudrā, the opening **above the uvula** <note>original: *lambikā-ūrdhvataḥ*</note> is sealed...

->|| VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

**No disease, etc.** || For the one who knows the Khecharī Mudrā: disease, death, laziness, sleep, hunger, thirst, and fainting specifically do not occur. || 39 ||

**Afflicted, etc.** || He who knows the Khecharī Mudrā is not pained by disease, is not stained by actions (*karma*), and does not succumb to the obstacles of Time (death). || 40 ||

**Mind, etc.** || The internal mind wanders in the opening within the eyebrows, and the tongue wanders in the middle of the eyebrows; because of this, perfected masters like Kapila and others call this Khecharī. || 41 ||

**By Khecharī, etc.** || If a yogi has covered the opening above the palate with the Khecharī Mudrā, then...

<summary>This page details the transcendental benefits of Khecharī Mudrā, including immunity to disease, hunger, and death, while explaining that the name "Sky-Goer" refers to the mind and tongue moving in the internal space of the brow.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Siddhas, Kapila, Bindu, Karma, Tamas, immortality, internal space, uvula</keywords>

### THIRD LESSON

**Root Verse: Even if the vital fluid has moved and reached the genital region, it travels upward when struck by power and bound by the Yoni Mudrā.** || 43 ||

**He whose tongue is turned upward and remains steady, drinking the lunar nectar—that knower of Yoga undoubtedly conquers death within half a month.** || 44 ||

**The yogi whose body is daily filled with the lunar essence...**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The suffix *-tasi* is used here in a universal sense. The **tongue** <note>original: *lambikā*</note> is the palate; the **opening** <note>original: *vivara*</note> or cavity situated above it is **sealed** <note>original: *pihitam*</note> or closed. Even when **embraced** <note>original: *ālingitasya*</note> by a young, passionate woman—the word "and" implies "even then"—his **vital fluid** <note>original: *bindu*</note> does not leak or fall. || 42 ||

**Even if it has moved, etc.** || Even if the vital fluid has slipped or moved, at the time it has reached the **genital region** <note>original: *yonimaṇḍalam*</note>, it is immediately **bound** <note>original: *nibaddho*</note>—firmly held—by the **Yoni Mudrā** <term>Yoni Mudrā: here referring to a specific physical contraction of the lower pelvic and urethral muscles</term>, which involves the contraction of the male organ. By this, **Vajrolī Mudrā** <term>Vajrolī: a practice where the yogi draws fluids upward through the urethra to preserve vital energy</term> is indicated. Drawn by the **power of attraction** <note>original: *ākarṣaṇa-śaktyā*</note>, the fluid is pulled up and travels to the **highest point** <note>original: *prakṛṣṭa*</note>. It goes to the seat of the vital fluid via the **path of the central channel** <term>Suṣumṇā: the primary energetic channel in the spine through which prana and kundalini rise</term>. || 43 ||

**He whose tongue is turned upward, etc.** || He whose tongue is directed toward the upper cavity of the palate is one with an **upward-turned tongue** <note>original: *ūrdhvajihvaḥ*</note>. Becoming **steady** <note>original: *sthira*</note> or motionless, he performs **Soma-pāna** <term>Soma-pāna: the practice of "drinking" the internal nectar or lunar fluid believed to drip from the brain</term>, which is the drinking of the moon-nectar falling from that upper cavity. That **knower of yoga** <note>original: *yogavit*</note> conquers or overcomes death within **half a month** <note>original: *māsārdha*</note>, meaning a fortnight. There is no doubt; this is a certainty. || 44 ||

**Daily, etc.** || The yogi whose body is **daily** <note>original: *nityam*</note>—every single day—filled with the **lunar essence** <note>original: *somakalā*</note>—filled with the moon-nectar—... || 45 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ॥<-

When a man is in an embrace with a woman, his vital fluid does not leak. || 42 ||

**Even if it has moved, etc.** || And if the vital fluid has already moved and reached the genital region, then the yogi performs the contraction of the male organ, which is the **Yoni Mudrā** shown here as **Vajrolī Mudrā**. By being bound and pulled by this power, he draws it upward through the **path of the central channel** <note>original: *suṣumṇā-mārga*</note>; that is, it reaches the place of the vital fluid again. || 43 ||

**He whose tongue is turned upward, etc.** || Placing the tongue facing the cavity above the palate and remaining steady, the yogi drinks that **lunar nectar** <note>original: *candrāmṛta*</note> which falls from the cavity above the palate. This nectar trickles from the moon located in the center of the eyebrows. The knower of yoga who drinks this overcomes death in half a month—which is a fortnight. In this, there is no doubt; it is certain. || 44 ||

**Daily, etc.** || And for that yogi whose body is filled daily with the lunar nectar...

<summary>This page describes the preservation and reclamation of vital fluid (bindu) through Vajrolī and Yoni Mudrā, and the rejuvenation of the body by drinking the internal lunar nectar (Soma).</summary>
<keywords>Bindu, Yoni Mudrā, Vajrolī Mudrā, Suṣumṇā, Soma-pāna, lunar nectar, immortality, vital energy preservation</keywords>

( ९२ )
### HATHA YOGA PRADĪPIKĀ

**Root Verse: Even if bitten by Takshaka himself, his poison does not spread.** || 45 ||
->**Just as fire does not leave fuel and a lamp does not leave its wick,**<-
->**so the soul does not leave a body filled with the lunar essence.**<- || 46 ||
->**One should daily eat "cow-meat" and drink "divine wine";**<-
->**I consider such a person to be of noble birth; others are destroyers of their lineage.**<- || 47 ||

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

For one whose body is so filled, even if bitten or stung by **Takshaka** <note>a legendary king of serpents in Hindu mythology</note>, a specific type of serpent, the poison—the venom and the suffering it causes—does not spread or circulate through the yogi's body. || 45 ||

**Just as fire, etc.** Just as fire does not abandon **fuel** <note>original: *īndhanāni*</note> such as wood, and a lamp <note>original: *dīpakaḥ*</note> does not abandon a **wick** <note>original: *tailavartti*</note> soaked in oil, so the **soul** <note>original: *dehī* or *jīva*</note> does not abandon or leave the **body** <note>original: *deham*</note> when it is filled with the **lunar essence** <term>Somakala: the sixteen digits or nectar-drops of the moon located in the head</term>. || 46 ||

**Cow-meat, etc.** Regarding the **technical definition** <note>original: *pāribhāṣika*</note> of "cow-meat" which will be explained in the following verses—he who eats this daily, and also drinks the **divine wine** <term>Amaravaruni: literally "liquor of the immortals," referring to the nectar that flows during deep yoga</term> which will also be explained—I, the author, consider that yogi to be **noble** <term>Kulina: literally "of a good family," but here redefined as one who belongs to the spiritual lineage of yoga</term>. This is supported by the **Brahmavaivarta Purana**: "The parents are fulfilled, the land is blessed, and that family is noble where a practitioner of Yoga is born; any gift given there becomes imperishable. One who possesses the discernment between Spirit <note>original: *Purusha*</note> and Matter <note>original: *Prakriti*</note>, if seen, spoken to, or touched, purifies the sins of men accumulated over millions of lifetimes." In the **Brahmanda Purana**: "A practitioner of Yoga is superior to a thousand householders, a hundred forest-dwellers <note>those in the third stage of life, vanaprastha</note>, and a thousand celibate students." In the **Raja Yoga**, Shiva says to **Vamadeva**: "Who knows the true greatness of Raja Yoga? The land where such a knower dwells becomes a vessel of merit. By seeing or worshiping him, twenty-one generations of one’s family, even if ignorant, attain the state of liberation—what then to say of those devoted to him? He who knows the distinction between internal yoga and external yoga..."

->॥ VERNACULAR ॥<-

Even if the body is bitten by the serpent Takshaka, the poison has no effect and no pain occurs. || 45 ||

**Just as fire, etc.** Just as fire does not leave the wood and a lamp does not leave the wick combined with oil, so the soul does not abandon the body that is full of the lunar nectar. || 46 ||

**Cow-meat, etc.** The author says: the yogi who daily eats "cow-meat" and drinks "divine wine"—I consider him to have been born into an excellent family. And he who is without this eating and drinking of "cow-meat" and "divine wine" is a non-yogi; they are the destroyers of their lineage—

<summary>This page describes how a yogi filled with internal nectar becomes immune to snake venom and achieves a state where the soul will not leave the body. It introduces the esoteric yogic definitions of "cow-meat" and "divine wine," elevating the practitioner to a status superior to all other social and religious classes.</summary>
<keywords>Takshaka, venom immunity, Somakala, lunar nectar, Gomamsa, Amaravaruni, Kulina, Brahmavaivarta Purana, Raja Yoga, Shiva, Vamadeva</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the esoteric metaphors introduced on page 92. Having stated that a yogi should "eat cow-meat," the author now provides the yogic definition of these terms, shifting from literal dietary taboos to the internal alchemy of Khecharī Mudrā.</meta>

### THIRD CHAPTER

**Root Verse:** The word "Go" <note>original: *go-śabdena*</note> refers to the tongue; its entry into the palate is the "eating of cow-meat." This indeed destroys the greatest sins. || 48 ||
The nectar that flows from the Moon, produced by the heat generated by the entry of the tongue, is known as "Divine Wine" <term>Amaravaruni: literally 'the liquor of the immortals'</term>. || 49 ||
If the tongue constantly kisses the tip of the uvula and flows with fluid...

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

"By you, by me, and by others this bond [is known], but what of the physician?" Thus it is said in the **Kurma Purana** <note>one of the eighteen major Hindu Puranas</note>: "Whether practiced once, twice, three times, or daily, those who engage in this Great Yoga are to be recognized as **Great Lords** <note>original: *Maheśvarāḥ*, an epithet of Shiva</note>." Others, who are devoid of the "eating of cow-meat" and the "drinking of divine wine" to be described, are not yogis; they are "destroyers of their lineage" because their birth into a noble family is rendered useless. || 47 ||

**He explains the meaning of the term "cow-meat": By the word "Go," etc.** By the word "Go"—that is, by the term "Go"—the tongue is denoted or spoken of. "In the palate" uses the locative case to indicate proximity. The entry of that tongue into the upper cavity near the palate is called "eating cow-meat." Such "eating of cow-meat" is the destroyer of **great sins** <term>Mahapataka: the five most heinous sins in Hindu law, such as stealing gold or killing a priest</term>. || 48 ||

**He explains the meaning of the term "Divine Wine": The tongue, etc.** From the entry of the tongue into the upper cavity of the **uvula** <note>original: *lambika*</note>, a certain fire or heat is produced. By that [heat], the **essence** <note>original: *sāra*</note> is distilled or produced. That which flows or drips from the **Moon** <note>original: *candrāt*</note> located in the left part between the eyebrows is the "Divine Wine"; it should be designated by the term **Amaravaruni**. || 49 ||

**Kissing, etc.** If it kisses or touches the tip of the uvula, the upper cavity of the uvula, constantly...

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

Those who are [without this practice], even if born in a noble family, their birth is in vain. || 47 ||
**The meaning of the term "cow-meat" is explained: By the word "Go," etc.** Here, the tongue is called "Go." The entry of the tongue into the opening near the palate is called "eating cow-meat." This "eating of cow-meat" destroys great sins. || 48 ||
**The meaning of the term "Divine Wine" is explained: The tongue, etc.** The entry of the tongue into the opening above the palate creates a fire or heat. The essence produced by that heat, which flows from the Moon situated in the left part within the eyebrows, is called "Divine Wine." || 49 ||
**Kissing, etc.** That which constantly touches the opening above the palate, and in which there is the flow of the lunar nectar...

<summary>This page decodes the symbolic language of Hatha Yoga, explaining that "eating cow-meat" is actually the practice of folding the tongue into the palate (Khecharī Mudrā) and "drinking wine" is the consumption of internal secretions.</summary>
<keywords>Gomamsa, Amaravaruni, Khechari Mudra, tongue, palate, lunar nectar, Mahapataka, Kurma Purana, esoteric anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Khecharī Mudrā, moving from the symbolic "eating of cow meat" to the physical sensations of the practice—specifically the tastes of the nectar (amrita) and the resulting supernatural powers (siddhis).</meta>

( 94 )
### THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

**Root Verse:** It is salty, pungent, sour, like milk, like honey, and like ghee. It removes diseases, ends old age, wards off the strike of weapons, grants eight-fold immortality, and the power to attract celestial maidens. || 50 ||
From the head, the nectar drops from the sixteen-petaled lotus <note>The Vishuddha or throat chakra, where the nectar is said to collect</note>, obtained through Hatha and the breath. Holding the tongue in the cavity with the face upturned, contemplating the Supreme Power <note>Shakti/Kundalini</note>, one who drinks the pure, wavy, stream-like "water of the arts" <note>original: *kalājalam*, referring to the digits/phases of the moon</note> becomes free from disease, possesses a body as soft as a lotus stalk, and lives a long time. || 51 ||

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

...and more. Therefore, the "juice-dripper" is she whose tongue possesses the dripping or flowing of the nectar of the moon's digits. **Salty** means combined with the taste of salt. **Pungent** refers to substances like black pepper. **Sour** refers to the tamarind fruit and similar things. **Milk** refers to cow's milk; it is similar or equal to these. **Honey** <note>original: *kshaudram*</note> and **Ghee** <note>clarified butter</note> are likewise equal. The word "likewise" is used for conjunction. Because the nectar has many flavors—being sweet and oily—these adjectives are applied to the tongue as it drips this essence. 

Then, for such a person, there is the **removal of diseases**, meaning their departure. The **ending of old age** means the destruction of the elderly state. The **approach of weapons** means that even if weapons are brought toward him, he can ward them off or neutralize them. The **eight-fold immortality** refers to the state of an immortal being attained by one who has acquired the eight supernatural powers <term>Siddhis: includes powers like becoming infinitely small (Anima) or infinitely large (Mahima)</term>. **Attraction of celestial maidens** <note>original: *Siddhanganā*</note> means he gains the power to attract the women of the perfected beings. || 50 ||

**Regarding "In the head," etc.:** Fixing the tongue in the cavity of the skull... [The text explains] turning the face upward.

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

When the tongue is like this, and when it naturally perceives the tastes of the nectar flow—such as salt, pepper-like pungency, tamarind-like sourness, milk, honey, and ghee—after the "severing of the root" <note>referring to the surgical preparation of the tongue for Khecharī Mudrā</note>, then the Yogi gains the power to distance himself from disease and destroy old age. He can ward off weapons coming toward him, obtain the eight supernatural powers, attain a divine state, and attract celestial maidens. Such power is achieved. || 50 ||

**Regarding "In the head," etc.:** Having fixed the tongue in the hole of the skull and turning the face upward, the practice is shown here in reverse. While meditating on the Kundalini, the pure, wavy nectar-juice of the moon is obtained through Hatha Yoga. It falls from the two-petaled lotus <note>The Ajna chakra between the eyebrows</note> down into the sixteen-petaled lotus located in the throat. Whoever drinks this stream-like nectar, that Yogi is freed from afflictions like fever, his body becomes as soft as a lotus stalk, and he lives for a very long time. || 51 ||

<summary>This page details the sensory experiences of Khecharī Mudrā, specifically the various flavors of the nectar (amrita) that a yogi tastes, and lists the resulting physical and supernatural benefits such as longevity and invulnerability.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Amrita, nectar, sixteen-petaled lotus, Vishuddha Chakra, Siddhis, immortality, Kundalini, rejuvenation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Khecharī Mudrā and the resulting internal alchemy. It transitions from the physical tastes of the nectar to the physiological and spiritual significance of the "hollow" in the skull where the nectar resides, linking the macrocosm (Mount Meru) to the microcosm (the spine).</meta>

( 95 )
### LESSON THREE

**Root Verse:** The wise one says the Essence <note>original: *tattva*, meaning the ultimate reality or the Self</note> resides in that hollow situated at the summit of Meru <note>The spinal column is called Mount Meru, the axis of the world, in yogic anatomy</note>, where the nectar <note>original: *prāleyaṃ*, literally "frost" or "snow-like," referring to the cool lunar nectar</note> is placed; it is the source of the rivers. The essence of the body flows from the moon, and by this, death comes to men. Therefore, one should practice the "good method" <note>Khecharī Mudrā</note>, for otherwise, the perfection of the work is not achieved. || 52 ||

### ->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

...and he whose face is upturned. By the term "upturned face," the Inverted Action <note>term: *Viparīta-karaṇī* — a yogic posture where the body is inverted to change the flow of internal fluids</note> is suggested. While contemplating and meditating on the Supreme Power <note>Kundalini</note>, using the breaths as the means... the sixteen-petaled lotus located in the throat... by force, or through Hatha Yoga, one obtains the pure, wavy "water of the lunar digits" <note>original: *kalājalam*, the nectar of the moon</note>. The man who drinks or sucks this becomes a Yogi free from disease—meaning free from fevers and the like—or one from whom various mental afflictions <note>original: *ādhi*</note> have departed. Possessing a body as soft and tender as a lotus stalk, he lives for a very long time. This is attained through Hatha Yoga, using the breaths as a means; some readings say "by the breaths." || 51 ||

Regarding the verse **"That which is like frost..."**: The Sushumna <note>the central energy channel of the spine</note> is the highest of all, like Mount Meru. That which stays within the upper part of it is the "lunar nectar." The "hollow" or "aperture" is that in which this nectar is placed. In that cavity, one whose intellect is "wise"—meaning a mind whose essence is not overwhelmed by passion or ignorance <note>the qualities of *rajas* and *tamas*</note>—proclaims the Truth of the Self <note>original: *ātma-tattva*</note>.
> "In the midst of that flame, the Supreme Self is established."
This is from the revealed scripture <note>original: *śruti*</note>. Although the Self is all-pervading, its manifestation occurs there in the practice of Khecharī Mudrā...

### ->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

One becomes free from disease and possesses a body soft like the inner fibers of a lotus; such a one lives for a long time. || 51 ||

Regarding the verse **"That which is like frost..."**: Like Mount Meru, the Sushumna is the highest of all. In its upper part is situated the water in the form of lunar nectar. In that opening, he whose intellect is filled with purity <note>original: *satoguṇī*</note> speaks of the Essence of the Self. And the rivers—Ganga, Yamuna, Saraswati, and Narmada—which are the energy channels known as Ida, Pingala, Sushumna, and Gandhari, have their origin in that hollow. From the lunar circle, the essence of the body flows. Because of the flowing of that lunar nectar, death comes to men. Therefore, one should first master the "good method" called Khecharī Mudrā. By binding this Khecharī, the nectar does not flow away, and then death does not occur. If there is an absence of Khecharī Mudrā, then the perfection of the body—being beautiful, strong, and firm like a diamond <note>original: *vajra*</note>—cannot happen. || 52 ||

<summary>This page describes the "hollow" at the top of the Sushumna (spine) as the source of lunar nectar and the dwelling place of the Self. It emphasizes that Khecharī Mudrā is essential to stop this nectar from being "consumed" by the body's internal fire, thereby preventing death and disease.</summary>
<keywords>Sushumna, Meru, lunar nectar, Khechari Mudra, Atma-tattva, Hatha Yoga, nectar flow, immortality, subtle anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and spiritual justification for Khecarī Mudrā, moving from the physical practice to its metaphysical significance as the "supreme" among all yogic techniques. It explains the "hollow" in the skull and identifies it as the seat of the divine void.</meta>

( 96 )
### IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADĪPIKĀ

**Root Verse:** The hollow is the producer of knowledge, joined with the five streams. In that void, the stainless <note>original: *nirañjane*, meaning without blemish or without the "ink" of worldly attachment</note>, resides the Khecarī Mudrā. || 53 ||

**Root Verse:** There is one seed full of creation, and one Mudrā: Khecarī. There is one God, the independent <note>original: *nirālamba*, literally "without support," referring to the absolute reality that does not depend on anything else</note>, and one state: the mind-transcending <note>term: *manonmanī* — a state of high samadhi where the mind is dissolved into pure consciousness</note>. || 54 ||

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

...as it has been said. Regarding the "downward-flowing" rivers—known by the names Ganges, Yamunā, Sarasvatī, Narmadā, and others—these refer to the [energy channels known as] the Iḍā, Piṅgalā, Suṣumṇā, Gāndhārī, and so on. Their mouth or opening is located within that hollow <note>original: *vivare*, referring to the *brahmarandhra* or the hollow space in the upper palate/skull</note>. From the body of the Moon, the essence of the body flows or trickles down. By this trickling of the lunar essence, the death of men occurs. For this reason, one should bind [practice] the "good method" <note>original: *sukaraṇam*</note> previously mentioned, called Khecarī Mudrā. The intent is that when the "good method" is bound, death does not occur because of the absence of the lunar essence trickling away. Otherwise, without binding the "good method," the perfection of the body <note>term: *kāya-siddhi* — the attainment of a perfected, immortal body</note>—consisting of beauty, grace, strength, and an adamantine constitution <note>original: *vajra-saṃhanana*, a body as hard and indestructible as a diamond</note>—would not exist. || 52 ||

Regarding the verse **"The hollow..."**: It is joined with or followed by the five "streams," which are the flows of the Iḍā and the other channels. In some readings, it is said to be "joined with seven streams." This hollow is the "producer of knowledge," meaning it produces the extraordinary realization of the Self. In that hollow, which is "stainless" <note>original: *nirañjanam*</note>—meaning that from which ignorance <note>original: *avidyā*</note> and its effects, such as grief and delusion, have departed—and in that "void" <note>term: *śūnya* — the empty space of the inner sky</note> or the space of the hollow, the Khecarī Mudrā resides and becomes steady. <note>The commentator notes a grammatical point here regarding the use of the Atmanepada verb form for "stays."</note> || 53 ||

Regarding the verse **"There is one..."**: There is one primary "seed" <note>term: *bīja* — a causal seed-syllable or source</note> which is the nature of creation, called the *Pranava* <note>The sacred syllable OM</note>. As stated in the *Māṇḍūkya Upaniṣad*: "OM, this syllable, is all this." There is one primary Mudrā: Khecarī. There is one primary God: the Independent, who is without any support. This is because the Self remains in its own nature by abandoning all supports. There is one primary state: the mind-transcending <note>*unmanī*</note> state. The lexicon *Amarakośa* defines "Eka" as primary, chief, or alone. The meaning is that among seeds, the *Pranava* is primary, and among Mudrās, Khecarī is primary. || 54 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR PARAPHRASE ॥<-

Regarding the verse **"The hollow..."**: The flow of the five vessels—Iḍā, Piṅgalā, Suṣumṇā, Gāndhārī, and Hastijivhā—is directed upwards. These flow upwards, and joined with their flow is the hollow <note>original: *vivar*</note> which manifests the direct realization of the Soul. In that hollow, which is like a void where ignorance and the effects of ignorance—such as grief and delusion—are removed, the Khecarī Mudrā becomes steady. || 53 ||

Regarding the verse **"There is one..."**: The seed in the form of creation is the one named *Pranava* [OM]. It is the chief among all seeds. And the Lord God is the chief among all deities. Just as the mind-transcending <note>*manonmanī*</note> state is the primary state, so too is the Khecarī Mudrā the primary among all Mudrās. || 54 ||

<summary>This page defines Khecarī Mudrā as the supreme yogic practice, explaining how it seals the "hollow" in the skull to prevent the loss of lunar essence (vitality) and facilitates the mind-transcending state (Manonmanī).</summary>
<keywords>Khecharī Mudrā, Manonmanī, Śūnya, Nirañjana, lunar essence, Kāya-siddhi, Praṇava, OM, inner rivers, yoga anatomy</keywords>

<header>THIRD INSTRUCTION</header>
<sig>13</sig>

# Now, the Uḍḍīyāna Bandha

**Root Verse:** Because the breath <note>original: *prāṇa*</note> is bound and then flies up <note>original: *uḍḍīyate*</note> into the central channel <note>term: *suṣumṇā* — the primary energy channel of the subtle body, located along the spine</note>, this practice is called "Uḍḍīyāna" by the masters of yoga. || 55 ||

**Root Verse:** Because the "Great Bird" <note>original: *mahā-khaga*, a metaphor for the breath or life-force</note> flies up through it without ceasing, it is called Uḍḍīyāna. Its specific lock <note>term: *bandha* — a physical "lock" or "binding" used in Hatha Yoga to control energy flow</note> is now described. || 56 ||

**Root Verse:** One should perform a backward pull <note>original: *paścimam tānam* — literally "western stretch," referring to the back of the body</note> of the abdomen, above the navel. This Uḍḍīyāna Bandha is a lion to the elephant of death. || 57 ||

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Wishing to explain the Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, the author first explains the meaning of the word "Uḍḍīyāna" with the verse beginning: **"Because the breath..."** The reason it is called so is that by this lock, the breath is bound or restrained and then "flies up" <note>original: *uḍḍīyate*</note> into the central channel, the middle artery. That is, it leaves the other channels and travels through the sky of the *suṣumṇā*. For this reason, this lock has been called "Uḍḍīyāna" by the masters of yoga, such as Matsyendra <note> Matsyendra was a legendary 10th-century yogi and co-founder of the Natha lineage</note> and others, using a precise etymological derivation. "Uḍḍīyana" refers to that by which the bound breath flies up in the *suṣumṇā*. It is derived from the prefix *ut* (up) and the root *ḍīṅ* (to fly through the sky) with the suffix *lyuṭ* denoting the instrument of the action. || 55 ||

Regarding **"The Great Bird..."**: The "Great Bird" is the breath <note>original: *prāṇa*</note>, because it constantly moves within the space of the body. Because of this lock, it "flies" or moves like a bird without fatigue. The phrase "in the central channel" should be understood here as implied. That specific lock is called Uḍḍīyāna. "In that regard," or on that subject, "the lock is described"—meaning the nature of the lock is now being stated by me <note>the commentator</note>. || 56 ||

He describes the Uḍḍīyāna Bandha with the verse: **"In the abdomen..."** In the belly, both above and <note>noted by the word "and" in the original</note> below the navel, one should perform a "western stretch" or backward pull. One should perform this "stretching"—meaning a contraction—in such a way that both the upper and lower parts of the navel appear to be pressed against the back <note>the spine</note>. The causative form of the verb "to do" is used here without intending a causative meaning <note>a grammatical note indicating the practitioner does it themselves</note>. This Uḍḍīyāna lock, which consists of stretching the regions above and below the navel, is what kind of thing? It is a "lion for the elephant of death." Just as a lion overcomes an elephant, this lock turns back or defeats death itself. || 57 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

Now he wishes to describe the Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, so he first explains the meaning of the word Uḍḍīyāna. **"Because the breath..."** For the reason that, by this binding, the restrained air flies up through the central channel <note>original: *suṣumṇā*</note> and travels through the path of the sky, this lock is called Uḍḍīyāna by the yogis like Matsyendra and others. || 55 ||

**"The Great Bird..."** The Great Bird is the breath. By performing this lock, the breath moves through the *suṣumṇā* like a bird's flight without effort. This binding is called Uḍḍīyāna, and here its nature is described. || 56 ||

**"In the abdomen..."** The upper part of the navel and the lower part should be drawn into the abdomen so that they seem to touch the back...

<summary>This page introduces and defines Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, explaining that its name comes from the way it causes the breath (the "Great Bird") to fly up the central channel. It provides the physical instruction to pull the abdomen toward the spine and claims this practice can defeat death.</summary>
<keywords>Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, abdominal lock, prana, suṣumṇā, Matsyendra, Hatha Yoga, breath control, victory over death</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, moving from its definition to the specific physical requirements, the duration of practice, and its ultimate spiritual results, including the reversal of aging and the attainment of natural liberation.</meta>

( 98 )
<header>IN THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

**Root Verse: The Uḍḍīyāna lock is taught by the Guru <note>original: *guruṇā kathitaṃ*</note> to be always natural. One who practices it constantly, even if old, becomes like a youth.** || 58 ||

**Root Verse: One should carefully perform the stretch <note>original: *tānam*</note> above the navel and also below it. If one practices for six months, they will certainly conquer death—of this there is no doubt.** || 59 ||

**Root Verse: Among all the locks <note>original: *bandhānām*</note>, Uḍḍīyāna is indeed the best. When the Uḍḍīyāna lock is perfected, liberation occurs naturally.** || 60 ||

## ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

**Regarding "The Uḍḍīyāna lock...":** The Guru is the teacher of beneficial knowledge. By that Guru, Uḍḍīyāna is said to be always, at all times, "natural" <note>original: *sahajam*</note>, meaning it relates to the spontaneous outward movement of the breath <note>original: *prāṇasya bahirgamanam*</note>, because this movement occurs at all times for everyone. Whoever—meaning any person—practices this constantly and without interruption, even if they are an old man <note>original: *sthaviro'pi*</note>, they "act like a youth" <note>original: *taruṇāyate*</note>, meaning they behave like a young person. || 58 ||

**Regarding "Above the navel...":** Above the navel, in the upper part, and also below it, in the lower part, one should perform the "backward stretch" <note>term: *paścimatānam* — literally "western stretch," referring to pulling the abdomen back toward the spine or "the west" of the body</note> with great effort. "With effort" means through a special exertion of will. The first half of the verse describes the nature of Uḍḍīyāna. Now follows its praise: for "six months"—meaning for a duration of six months—one should practice Uḍḍīyāna repeatedly. Such a person certainly conquers death; there is no doubt. The meaning is that there is no uncertainty in this matter. || 59 ||

**Regarding "Among all...":** Among all the various locks, the Uḍḍīyānakah <note>The suffix '-ka' here is used in the sense of the word's own meaning without changing it</note> or Uḍḍīyāna Bandha is the best or most excellent. This is because when the Uḍḍīyāna lock is made firm or perfected, liberation <note>term: *mukti* — release from the cycle of suffering and rebirth</note> becomes "natural," meaning it is established by one's own nature. When the Uḍḍīyāna lock is performed, the breath travels through the central channel <note>term: *suṣumṇā*</note> to the head with the gait of a bird. Based on the teaching that "one attains liberation in meditative absorption," the intent is that liberation becomes spontaneous. || 60 ||

## ॥ VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ॥

This lock, which involves pulling backward, is named Uḍḍīyāna. What is it like? It is like a lion that turns away the elephant of death. <note>This refers back to the metaphor in verse 57</note> || 57 ||

**Regarding "The Uḍḍīyāna lock...":** As taught by the Guru, the giver of beneficial instruction, if one constantly practices Uḍḍīyāna, which is described as having a natural character, then even an old man becomes a youth. || 58 ||

**Regarding "Above the navel...":** One should perform the stretch such that the upper and lower parts of the navel are pressed toward the back with effort; that is, one pulls them backward. If one practices this Uḍḍīyāna repeatedly in this way for six months, they will conquer death; of this there is no doubt. || 59 ||

**Regarding "Among all...":** Among all the locks, the Uḍḍīyāna lock is the most excellent. When this Uḍḍīyāna lock is firm...

<summary>This page describes the benefits of Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, asserting that six months of practice can rejuvenate the elderly and conquer death. It identifies Uḍḍīyāna as the superior lock because it forces the life-force into the central channel, leading to natural liberation.</summary>

<keywords>Uḍḍīyāna Bandha, Abdominal Lock, rejuvenation, Suṣumṇā, conquest of death, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Guru, liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the final benefits of the Uḍḍīyāna lock (the abdominal lift) to the detailed instructions for Mūlabandha (the root lock). THIRD CHAPTER ( 99 )

## Now, the Root Lock <note>original: *mūlabandha*</note>

**Root Verse: Pressing the perineum <note>original: *yonim*</note> firmly with the heel, one should contract the anus. By pulling the downward-moving breath <note>term: *apāna* — the vital energy responsible for elimination and downward movement</note> upward, this is called the Root Lock.** || 61 ||

**Root Verse: That which by force makes the downward-moving *apāna* move upward through contraction—yogis call that the Root Lock.** || 62 ||

**Root Verse: Pressing the anus with the heel, one should forcefully contract the breath...**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The Root Lock is explained: **"With the part of the heel..."** The "part of the heel" refers to the region below the ankles. With that, one should thoroughly press the "perineum" <note>original: *yonisthānam*</note>—which is the middle part between the anus and the genitals—and then contract the anus. By pulling the *apāna*—the breath that naturally moves downward—upward, it is called the "Root Lock" <note>original: *mūlabandha*</note>. This means that the contraction of the anus, preceded by pressing the perineum with the heel, is called the Root Lock. || 61 ||

**"The downward-moving..."** That *apāna* breath, whose movement is naturally downward or outward, is forced to go upward through the contraction of the "Root Center" <note>term: *mūlādhāra* — the fundamental energy center at the base of the spine</note>. It then becomes "upward-moving," meaning it tends to move up into the central channel <note>term: *suṣumnā* — the primary spiritual path for energy in the body</note>. The word *vai* is an indeclinable used for emphasis. Yogis—those who practice yoga—call this the "Root Lock." The name is literal <note>original: *anvartha*</note> because it is the "binding" or "locking" of the root location. This explains the meaning of the term "Root Lock," whereas the previous verse explained the method of performing it; thus, there is no repetition. || 62 ||

Now, the Root Lock is described according to the *Yogabīja* <note>a classical text on the "Seed of Yoga"</note>: **"The anus..."** This means one should thoroughly press and engage the anus and the breath with the heel (the part below the ankles). The word "but" <note>original: *tu*</note> distinguishes this from the previous...

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

...if this occurs, then liberation is achieved naturally. By performing the Uḍḍīyāna lock, with a movement like that of a bird, the breath enters the central channel <note>original: *suṣumnā*</note>. By taking the breath to the head, liberation is achieved in deep meditation <note>term: *samādhi* — a state of complete spiritual absorption</note>. In this way, natural liberation occurs. || 60 ||

Now the Root Lock <note>original: *mūlabandha*</note> is described: **"With the part of the heel..."** Pressing the perineum with the heel, contract the anus. Then, pull the *apāna* breath—which usually goes downward—upward. This is called the Root Lock. || 61 ||

**"The downward-moving..."** That *apāna* breath, whose path is downward—by contracting the Root Center <note>original: *mūlādhāra*</note>, one should force it to move upward, meaning it is made to enter the central channel. Yoga practitioners call this the Root Lock. Because the "locking" happens at the "root" location, it is called the Root Lock. || 62 ||

The method of the Root Lock as stated in the *Yogabīja* is described: **"The anus..."** With the heel...

<summary>This page defines Mūlabandha (the Root Lock), explaining the physical technique of pressing the perineum with the heel and contracting the anus to redirect the downward-moving Apāna energy upward into the Suṣumnā channel.</summary>
<keywords>Mūlabandha, Root Lock, Apāna, Suṣumnā, perineum, Mūlādhāra, Hatha Yoga, Yogabīja</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Mūlabandha (the Root Lock), moving from the physical action to the subtle energetic results. It explains the merging of the upward and downward vital breaths and the role of internal sound and light (Nāda and Bindu) in achieving the goals of Hatha Yoga.</meta>

# In the Light of Hatha Yoga <note>original: *Haṭhayogapradīpikā*</note>

**Root Verse: Repeatedly, so that the equalizing breath <note>original: *samīkaraṇaḥ*</note> moves upward, || 63 ||**

**Root Verse: The Prāṇa and Apāna, and the Sound <note>term: *nāda* — the internal mystic sound heard in meditation</note> and the Point <note>term: *bindu* — the concentrated seed of energy or light</note>, reach unity through the Root Lock. Having attained this, they grant the perfection of Yoga; of this there is no doubt. || 64 ||**

**Root Verse: The union of Apāna and Prāṇa [leads to] the reduction of urine and excrement...**

---

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The word "specifically" clarifies the intent. Just as, or in whatever manner, the equalizing breath goes or moves to the upper part of the central channel <note>term: *suṣumnā* — the primary spiritual path for energy in the body</note>, in that same manner, one should forcefully and repeatedly contract the downward-moving breath <note>term: *apāna*</note> by contracting the anus. The phrase "This is the Root Lock" is understood to complete the sentence. || 63 ||

Now, he speaks of the benefits of the Root Lock: **"Prāṇa and Apāna..."** *Prāṇa* and *Apāna* are the upward and downward moving breaths. Sound is the "unstruck" sound <note>term: *anāhata-dhvani* — the internal vibration not caused by two objects hitting each other</note>. The Point is the nasal vibration <note>term: *anusvāra*</note>. These become unified through the Root Lock and grant the perfection of Yoga, which is complete success. This refers to the practitioner—this word is implied. There is no doubt in this matter; it means there is no uncertainty. 

The essence is this: when the Root Lock is performed, the *apāna* breath becomes unified with the *prāṇa* and enters the central channel. From that, the internal Sound is manifested. Then, both the *prāṇa* and *apāna* move above the heart along with the Sound, and having attained unity with the Point, they reach the head. From that, the perfection of Yoga arises. || 64 ||

**"Of Apāna and Prāṇa..."** Through the constant practice of the Root Lock gesture, the unity of *apāna* and *prāṇa* [is achieved]...

---

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

Pressing the anus, in the manner that the air reaches the upper part of the *suṣumnā*, one should forcefully and repeatedly contract the *apāna* air by pulling the anus. This is the Root Lock. || 63 ||

Now the qualities of the Root Lock are stated: **"Prāṇa and Apāna..."** *Prāṇa* is the upward-moving air and *apāna* is the downward-moving air. Sound <note>original: *nāda*</note> refers to a sound like a thundercloud, and the Point <note>original: *bindu*</note> refers to the nasal vibration. These four become one through the Root Lock and grant the success of Yoga. There is no doubt in this. 

The meaning is: by performing the Root Lock, the *apāna* air joins with the *prāṇa* air to become one and enters the *suṣumnā*. From that, the internal Sound manifests. Along with that Sound, both the *prāṇa* and *apāna* airs go above the heart, and by unifying the Sound with the Point, they reach the head. From that, the perfection of Yoga occurs. || 64 ||

**"Of Apāna and Prāṇa..."** By constantly performing the Root Lock gesture, the *apāna* air and the *prāṇa* air...

# Chapter Three <note>original: *tṛtīyopadeśaḥ*</note>

**Root Verse: Even an old person becomes young through the constant practice of the Root Lock. || 65 ||**

**Root Verse: When the downward-moving breath <note>term: *apāna* — the vital force responsible for elimination and downward movement</note> is forced upward and reaches the fire-region <note>term: *vahnimaṇḍala* — a subtle center of heat located in the lower abdomen</note>, then the flame of that fire grows long, fanned by the breath. || 66 ||**

**Root Verse: Then, the fire and the downward breath reach the upward-moving breath <note>term: *prāṇa* — here referring to the specific vital air located in the chest/heart</note>, which is hot by nature. Because of this, the internal fire of the body becomes intensely radiant. || 67 ||**

### ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

**"Becomes."** The waste matter <note>original: *mūtrapurīṣayoḥ* — urine and feces</note> that has accumulated is reduced or expelled. Even an old person <note>original: *sthaviro'pi*</note> becomes a youth. || 65 ||

**"The downward breath..."** Through the Root Lock, the breath that usually tends to move downward instead moves upward. When this happens, it reaches the fire-region. This fire-region is a triangle located below the navel. This was stated by the sage Yajnavalkya:
> “In the center of the body is the place of fire, shining like molten gold. It is a **triangle** for humans, a **square** for four-legged animals, and a **circle** for birds—I tell you this truly. In the center of that fire, a slender flame always resides.” 

At that time, joined with the downward breath, the flame of the gastric fire <note>term: *jaṭharāgni* — the "digestive fire" believed in Ayurveda and Yoga to reside in the stomach</note> becomes long and extended. Some versions of the text read "increases" <note>original: *vardhate*</note> instead of "becomes long." || 66 ||

**"Then..."** Following that, the fire and the downward breath move together. They reach the upward-moving breath <note>term: *prāṇa*</note>, which has become hot in nature due to the length of the flame. [Another way to interpret the grammar is:] Or, they reach the breath from the heat produced by the elongated flame. Through this meeting with the upward breath, the fire born within the body <note>original: *dehaja*</note> becomes extremely and excessively radiant. The word "likewise" <note>original: *tathā*</note> is used here to complete the poetic meter. The meaning is that while the fire is already brightened by the upward movement of the downward breath, it becomes even more intensely fanned into a blaze by its union with the upward breath. || 67 ||

### ॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥ <note>This section translates the Braj Bhasha summary provided in the manuscript.</note>

These energies achieve unity. Then, the urine and feces that have accumulated are expelled. By performing this Root Lock, an old man becomes a youth. || 65 ||

**"The downward breath..."** By performing the Root Lock, the downward breath begins to move upward. Below the navel, there is a triangle; that is the region of fire where the fire resides. When the downward breath enters that fire-region, the flame of the gastric fire, joined with the wind, grows into a great flame. || 66 ||

**"Then..."** After that, the fire and the downward breath both enter the upward breath, which is hot. Because of this, the fire that exists within the body becomes extremely and intensely bright. || 67 ||

<summary>This page describes the rejuvenating effects of Mūlabandha, explaining how directing the Apāna breath upward ignites the internal gastric fire (Jaṭharāgni), leading to physical purification and increased vitality.</summary>
<keywords>Mūlabandha, Root Lock, Apāna, Prāṇa, Vahnimaṇḍala, Fire Region, Jaṭharāgni, Gastric Fire, Rejuvenation, Yajnavalkya, Yoga Anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mūlabandha (the Root Lock) from page 101, explaining how the intensified internal fire awakens the dormant spiritual energy (Kuṇḍalinī) and forces it into the central channel. It then introduces the next practice, Jālandharabandha (the Throat Lock).</meta>

# In the Lamp on Hatha Yoga <note>original: *Haṭhayogapradīpikāyāṃ*</note>

**Root Verse: By that fire, the sleeping Goddess <note>term: *kuṇḍalinī* — the primordial energy coiled at the base of the spine</note>, being intensely heated, is fully awakened. Like a serpent struck by a stick, she hisses and becomes straight. || 68 ||**

**Root Verse: Then, she enters the Brahmanadi <note>term: *brahmanāḍī* — another name for the *suṣumṇā*, the central energy channel of the spine</note> as if entering a hole. Therefore, the Root Lock should always be performed daily by yogis. || 69 ||**

**Root Verse: Contract the throat and firmly place the chin on the heart <note>note: specifically the upper chest area</note>. This is called the Jalandhara Lock <note>term: *jālandharabandha* — the "Chin Lock" or "Throat-Clearing Lock"</note>, the destroyer of old age and death. || 70 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

**"By that."** By that intense lighting of the fire, the sleeping—that is, the slumbering—**Kundalini** energy <note>original: *kuṇḍalinī śaktiḥ*</note> becomes thoroughly heated and is fully awakened. Just as a female serpent <note>original: *bhujangī*</note> that is struck by a stick might hiss and then become straight or upright, so too does this energy. || 68 ||

**"Entering the hole."** After achieving that straightness, she enters the center of the **Brahmanadi**—the **Sushumna** <note>term: *suṣumṇā* — the primary channel for spiritual ascent</note>—just as a serpent enters its burrow. For this reason, the Root Lock is "worthy of being performed" <note>original: *kartavyaḥ*</note> by practitioners of yoga every day and at all times. || 69 ||

**Explaining the Jalandhara Lock.** **"The throat."** After contracting the opening of the throat, one should place the chin firmly and steadily upon the heart—specifically the region of the chest about four finger-widths <note>original: *caturaṅgulāntarita*</note> below the throat. This practice, characterized by contracting the throat and the effort of bending the chin down toward the chest, is known by the name **Jalandhara**. What is its nature? It is a "destroyer of old age and death," meaning it specifically acts as the one who brings an end to the aging process and the cycle of dying. || 70 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

**"By that."** By the extreme heat of that internal fire, the sleeping **Kundalini** energy reaches a state of heat and wakes up. Just as a sleeping snake, when struck by a stick, takes deep breaths and becomes straight, so it is with this energy. || 68 ||

**"Entering the hole."** After that, just as a snake enters its hole, the **Kundalini** enters the **Sushumna**. For this reason, those practicing yoga should perform the Root Lock day by day at all times. || 69 ||

**Now the Jalandhara Lock is described.** **"The throat."** Bending the throat downward, one should use effort to firmly fix the chin four finger-widths from the heart. This is the lock named **Jalandhara**. What is it like? It destroys old age and death. || 70 ||

<summary>This page describes how the heat generated by Mūlabandha awakens the Kundalini energy, causing it to rise into the central channel (Sushumna), and introduces Jālandharabandha (the Throat Lock) as a means to overcome aging and death.</summary>
<keywords>Kundalini, Mulabandha, Jalandharabandha, Sushumna, Brahmanadi, Chin Lock, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, rejuvenation</keywords>

### Third Instruction

**Root Verse: It binds the network of vessels and the downward-flowing nectar from the "sky" <note>original: *nabhojalam* — referring to the "lunar nectar" believed to drip from the cranial cavity</note>. || 71 ||**
**Root Verse: Therefore, it is the Jalandhara Lock, the destroyer of the multitude of throat ailments. When the Jalandhara Lock, characterized by the contraction of the throat, is performed, || 72 ||**
**Root Verse: the nectar does not fall into the fire, nor does the breath become disordered. By the very contraction of the throat, one should firmly restrain the two channels.**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The author explains the meaning of the term Jalandhara. **"It binds."** Because it binds—or restrains—the **network of vessels** <note>original: *śirājālam*</note>, meaning the entire collection of energy channels or nerves. It also binds—preventing from flowing downwards—the **water of the sky**, which is the nectar <note>original: *amṛtam*</note> from the hollow of the skull. Therefore, for this reason, it is called the **Jalandhara Lock**, a name whose meaning corresponds to its function: the word *jala* means a "collection" or "web" (of nerves) as well as "water" (nectar), and *dhara* means "holding." What is its nature? It is the **destroyer of the multitude of throat ailments**, meaning it acts as the healer for the various disorders and pains that arise in the neck and throat region. || 71 ||

He describes the qualities of Jalandhara. **"The Jalandhara [Lock]."** This lock is defined by the **contraction** or shrinking of the throat. When this Jalandhara Lock is performed, the **nectar** <note>original: *pīyūṣam*</note> does not fall or flow into the **fire** <note>original: *agnau*</note> of the digestive system <note>term: *jaṭharāgni* — the internal fire responsible for digestion and the "consumption" of the body's vital essence</note>. Furthermore, the **breath** <note>original: *vāyuśca*</note>—the vital life force—does not "become disordered," meaning it does not enter the wrong channels or cause agitation. || 72 ||

**"By the contraction of the throat."** By the mere act of contracting the throat, one should **firmly restrain** the **two channels**—the **Ida** and **Pingala** <note>term: *iḍā* and *piṅgalā* — the left and right energy channels representing lunar and solar energies</note>. It is understood that "one performs" this Jalandhara Lock. This practice, situated in the throat region...

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

Now the meaning of the term Jalandhara is explained. **"It binds."** It binds the network of vessels—the collection of channels—and it binds the nectar from the "sky-hollow" <note>original: *kapāla-kuhara* — the opening in the upper palate</note> which otherwise flows downwards. Thus it is called the Jalandhara Lock. A collection of waters is called a *jala*. Because it holds the network of nerves, it is called Jalandhara. This Jalandhara lock destroys the collection of pains and all disorders occurring in the throat. || 71 ||

Now the qualities of Jalandhara are described. **"The Jalandhara [Lock]."** This lock's very nature is the bowing of the neck. When one performs this, the nectar from above does not fall into the digestive fire. Then, the **vital breath** <note>original: *prāṇavāyu*</note> does not travel inside the channels in a way that causes disorder. || 72 ||

**"By the contraction of the throat."** By the mere firm contraction of the throat, one restrains both channels, the **Ida** and the **Pingala**. This is the Jalandhara Lock. One should know that the **Chakra** <note>term: *cakra* — an energy center in the subtle body</note> located in the throat region is named **Vishuddha** <note>term: *viśuddha* — the "pure" center, the fifth primary chakra</note>; it is the middle center.

<summary>This page defines Jālandharabandha (the Throat Lock) and explains how it preserves vital nectar from being "burned" by the digestive fire while regulating the flow of energy through the Ida and Pingala channels. It identifies the practice with the Vishuddha Chakra in the throat.</summary>
<keywords>Jalandhara Bandha, Throat Lock, Nectar, Amrita, Ida, Pingala, Vishuddha Chakra, Hatha Yoga, Vital Breath</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Hatha Yoga, specifically focusing on the "Middle Center" (Viśuddha chakra), the sixteen vital supports of the body, and the combined application of the three locks—Mūla, Uḍḍiyāna, and Jālandhara—to direct the breath into the central channel (Suṣumṇā).</meta>

( 104 )
### In the Light on Hatha Yoga

**Root Verse: This should be known as the Middle Center, the binding of the sixteen supports. || 73 ||**
**Root Verse: Contracting the root place, one should perform the Uḍḍiyāna lock. Binding the Iḍā and Piṅgalā, one should cause the breath to flow through the western path. || 74 ||**
**Root Verse: By this very method, the breath reaches dissolution. Consequently, death, old age, disease, and so forth do not arise. || 75 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The chakra named **Viśuddha** <note>original: *viśuddha-ākhyaṃ cakraṃ* — the "purification" center located at the throat</note> is to be known as the **Middle Center** <note>original: *madhyacakraṃ*</note>. What is its nature? It is the **binding of the sixteen supports**, meaning it is the factor that binds or controls the sixteen specific locations in the body starting from the big toes and ending at the aperture of Brahma <note>original: *brahmarandhra* — the crown of the head</note>. These sixteen supports are: "The big toes, the ankles, the knees, the thighs, the perineum <note>original: *sīvanī*</note>, the genitals, the navel, the heart, the base of the neck, the throat region, the uvula <note>original: *lambikā*</note>, the nose, the space between the eyebrows, the forehead, the skull, and the aperture of Brahma." These are the **sixteen supports** described by the **greatest of yogis**. The specific results of focusing one's concentration <note>original: *dhāraṇā*</note> on these supports should be understood from the *Treatise of Gorakṣa* <note>original: *Gorakṣasiddhānta* — a foundational text of the Nath yoga tradition</note>. || 73 ||

The author now describes the application of the three aforementioned locks. **"The root place."** After **contracting**—thoroughly pulling inward—the **root place** <note>original: *mūlasthāna*</note>, which is the base support, one should **perform** the **Uḍḍiyāna** lock, which consists of pulling the navel backward toward the spine. The causative form of the verb here is not intended to change the meaning <note>A grammatical note: the author implies the yogi does it themselves</note>. One should **bind** the **Iḍā and Piṅgalā**, which are metaphorically the Ganges and Yamuna rivers. This is done by means of the **Jālandhara lock**, as it has been stated: "By the very contraction of the throat, one should restrain the two channels." One should then **cause to flow** the breath—meaning the vital energy <note>original: *prāṇa*</note>—into the **western path**, which is the path of the **Suṣumṇā** <note>The central energy channel running along the spine</note>. || 74 ||

**"By this."** By this very method just described, the **breath** <note>original: *pavana*</note> or vital force reaches **dissolution** <note>original: *laya*</note>, meaning stability. The dissolution of the breath is its state of remaining in the aperture of the crown after its outward movement has ceased. Because of this dissolution of the breath, **death, old age, disease, and so forth** are overcome.

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

This chakra should be understood as the "Middle Center." It involves sixteen supports, starting from the big toes up to the aperture of Brahma. These sixteen are counted as: the big toes, the ankles, the knees, the thighs, the perineum, the genitals, the navel, the heart, the base of the neck, the throat, the uvula, the nose, the eyebrow center, the forehead, the skull, and the aperture of Brahma. These sixteen supports are spoken of by the best among yogis. The specific fruits of concentration on these supports should be learned from the *Treatise of Gorakṣa*. || 73 ||

**"The root place."** One should perform the Uḍḍiyāna lock by pulling the navel backward, and by performing the Jālandhara lock, one should bind the Iḍā and Piṅgalā—meaning one should bow the neck and restrain these two channels. Then, one should direct the vital breath into the western path, which is the path of the Suṣumṇā. || 74 ||

**"By this."** Through this method, the vital breath becomes stable; that is, the movement of the air is stopped...

<vocab>Middle Center (madhyacakra), Sixteen Supports (ṣoḍaśādhāra), Root Place (mūlasthāna), Flying-Up Lock (uḍḍiyāna), Left Channel (iḍā), Right Channel (piṅgalā), Central Channel (suṣumṇā), Dissolution/Absorption (laya), Treatise of Gorakṣa (gorakṣasiddhānta), Throat Lock (jālandharabandha), Vital Breath (prāṇavāyu)</vocab>

It explains how to combine the Root, Navel, and Throat locks to force the breath into the central channel (Suṣumṇā) to achieve a state of absorption and conquer death.</summary>

### Third Instruction

**Root Verse: This triad of locks is the most excellent, practiced by the Great Adepts. Yogis know it to be the means of success for all Hatha systems. || 76 ||**

**Root Verse: Whatever nectar flows from the divine Moon, all of that is swallowed by the Sun; thereby the body is overcome by old age. || 77 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Regarding the previous verse <note>Verse 75 from the preceding page</note>: "And in that sense," the phrase **does not arise** means it does not originate. By the word **beginning** <note>original: *ādipadena* — referring to "and so forth" in the previous verse</note>, we should include wrinkles, graying hair, drowsiness, laziness, and similar ailments. || 75 ||

**This triad of locks:** This previously mentioned **triad of locks** <note>original: *bandhatrayam* — referring to the Root, Navel, and Throat locks</note> is the **most excellent** and highly praised among the locks involving the sixteen supports <note>referring to the vital points mentioned on the previous page</note>. It is **practiced by the Great Adepts** <note>original: *mahāsiddhaiḥ*</note> such as Matsyendra and others, as well as by sages like Vasiṣṭha. Yogis such as Gorakṣa and others **know** it as the **means**—the producer of success—for **all Hatha systems**, meaning all Hatha yoga methods. || 76 ||

Wishing to describe the practice of the "Inverted Action" <note>term: <term>Viparītakaraṇī</term> — a yoga posture where the body is inverted to reverse the flow of internal fluids</note>, the author first explains the cause of the body's aging as an introduction. **Whatever nectar:** "Divine" means excellent or composed of nectar. From that **divine Moon** <note>original: *divyarūpiṇaścandrāt*</note>—the lunar source located at the root of the palate—whatever **nectar** <note>original: *amṛtaṃ* or *pīyūṣa*</note> **flows** or falls, all of that nectar is **swallowed** by the **Sun** located at the navel, which is fiery in nature. As stated by Lord Gorakṣanātha:

> "The Sun, which is of the nature of fire, is always situated in the navel region. The Moon, which is of the nature of nectar, is always situated at the root of the palate. The Moon rains downwards, while the Sun, facing upwards, swallows. That practice should be performed by which the nectar is obtained."

Because of this swallowing of the nectar by the Sun, the **body** <note>original: *piṇḍo* — literally "the lump" or microcosm</note> becomes **overcome by old age**. || 77 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ॥<-

When the breath remains steady in the aperture <note>original: *randhra* — the opening at the top of the head</note>, it is called the "dissolution of breath." Through this dissolution, death, old age, disease, the three wrinkles of the skin, white hair, fainting, and laziness do not occur. || 75 ||

**This triad of locks:** These three locks previously mentioned are the best. They have been practiced by the Great Adepts like Matsyendra and by sages like Vasiṣṭha. They reveal the success of all Hatha methods. This is known by the Adepts, beginning with Gorakṣa. || 76 ||

**Whatever nectar:** From the Moon, which is divine in form and situated at the root of the palate, some nectar flows. That nectar is swallowed by the Sun, which is in the form of fire and situated in the navel. Because of the Sun swallowing the nectar, this body is joined with **old age**, which is the state of being elderly. || 77 ||

१४ <note>Manuscript marker: 14</note>

<vocab>bandhatraya: the three locks; mahāsiddha: great adept; Matsyendra: a founding master of the Nath tradition; Vasiṣṭha: a legendary sage; Gorakṣanātha: the principal teacher of Hatha Yoga; viparītakaraṇī: inverted practice; piṇḍa: the physical body; jarā: old age/decay; haṭhatantra: the system of Hatha Yoga; pīyūṣa: nectar of immortality</vocab>

It also introduces the esoteric physiology of the body, where a "Sun" in the navel consumes the life-preserving "nectar" dripping from a "Moon" in the head, leading to aging and death.</summary>
