<meta>This page continues the instructions for purifying the subtle body and awakening the spiritual energy. While the previous page focused on the physical act of "Stimulating the Power" (Śakticālana) through the Bellows breath, this page moves toward the mental state of meditation and the ultimate goal of Hatha Yoga: the achievement of Raja Yoga (the "Royal Yoga" or highest state of consciousness).</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<header>THIRD INSTRUCTION</header>

**Root: Through posture <term>āsana</term>, breath restraint <term>prāṇāyāma</term>, and the seals <term>mudrā</term>, [the central channel] becomes straight. || 24 ||**

**Root: In the practice of those who have overcome sleep, holding the mind in meditative absorption <term>samādhi</term>, when the Rudrāṇī seal <note>likely referring to the Śāmbhavī mudrā or the Kundalinī itself</note> is mastered, it bestows auspicious success. || 25 ||**

**Root: Without Royal Yoga <term>rājayoga</term>, the Earth does not shine; without Royal Yoga, the Night does not shine; without Royal Yoga, even the most wondrous Seal <term>mudrā</term> does not shine. || 26 ||**

### ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

Breath restraint <term>prāṇasaṃyāma</term> refers to breathing exercises <term>prāṇāyāma</term>. Seals <term>mudrā</term> refer to the Great Seal <term>mahāmudrā</term> and others. By these, the central channel becomes "straight," meaning direct and unobstructed. || 24 ||

Regarding the practice: "By meditative absorption" <term>samādhi</term> means by one-pointedness that restricts other mental fluctuations. By "holding the mind," one makes the internal sense steady in concentration. For those "whose sleep has vanished" <note>original: vinidrāṇāṃ</note>—this word "sleep" also implies laziness; thus, it refers to those who are not lazy. The Rudrāṇī seal—which is the Śāmbhavī seal—or another higher state like the "mindless" state <term>unmanī</term>, bestows or gives "auspicious success," meaning the perfection of yoga. By this, it is said that Hatha Yoga is a helpful tool for reaching Royal Yoga <term>rājayoga</term>. || 25 ||

He explains the uselessness of postures and other techniques without Royal Yoga through a poetic double-meaning <note>a literary device called śleṣa, where words have two meanings</note>:

"Without Royal Yoga": Royal Yoga is the continuous flow of thoughtless awareness toward the Self, preceded by the restriction of other mental fluctuations. This was suggested in the phrase "Royal Yoga cannot be achieved without Hatha." Without the practice of those means, "the Earth"—by which the quality of steadiness is meant—represents **posture** <term>āsana</term>. This is because the result of the ultimate human goal is not achieved.

"Without Royal Yoga, the Night": "Night" <term>niśā</term> represents **breath retention** <term>kumbhaka</term>, because just as people generally do not move about at night, in retention, the movement of the life-force ceases. This does not shine without the goal of Royal Yoga.

"Without Royal Yoga, the Seal": Even the various and extraordinary "Seals" <term>mudrā</term> like the Great Seal do not shine.

On the other hand <note>interpreting the verse literally</note>: Without the "Union of a King" <term>rājayoga</term> (the presence of a monarch), the "Earth" (the land) does not shine, because without a ruler, various calamities can occur. A King...

### ॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥

The middle channel, the Suṣumṇā, becomes straight. || 24 ||

Regarding the practice: Through meditative absorption <term>samādhi</term>, which is one-pointedness, one holds the mind in practice. For those whose sleep and laziness have gone, the Rudrāṇī seal—which is the coiled power <term>kuṇḍalinī</term>—gives them the auspicious success of yoga. || 25 ||

Without Royal Yoga, posture <term>āsana</term> does not attain beauty; without Royal Yoga, the "Night," which is breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term>, does not shine. Without Royal Yoga, various and diverse seals <term>mudrā</term> do not shine. || 26 ||

<summary>This page explains that physical yoga techniques like postures and breath control are meant to clear the Suṣumṇā channel, but they are ultimately meaningless unless they lead to Rājayoga (the highest state of meditative union).</summary>
<keywords>Suṣumṇā, Rājayoga, Samādhi, Mudrā, Kumbhaka, Rudrāṇī, Hatha Yoga Pradīpikā, mental one-pointedness</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the physical techniques of breath and posture to the mental requirements of Hatha Yoga. While the previous page established that the "Seals" (mudrās) are ineffective without the "Royal Yoga" (Rāja Yoga) of the mind, this page specifies that breath control must be practiced with total mental focus and concludes by praising the spiritual lineage through which these secrets are passed.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>ON THE LAMP ON HATHA YOGA</header>

**Root: One should practice every method of the breath <term>māruta</term> with the mind fully engaged. A wise person should not let their mental activity wander elsewhere. || 27 ||**
**These ten seals <term>mudrā</term> were spoken of by the Primal Lord <term>ādinātha</term>, Śiva <note>literally "the auspicious one"</note>. Each one of them grants great success <term>mahāsiddhi</term> to those who practice restraint. || 28 ||**
**He who gives the traditional instruction of these seals—instruction coming from the established lineage—he is the Venerable Teacher <term>śrīguru</term>, the Master, and is indeed God <term>īśvara</term> manifest. || 29 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

The Moon: Referring to the scripture <note>from the Rigveda</note> stating "Soma (the Moon) is the King of us Brahmins." Without a connection <term>yoga</term> to that, the Night does not shine. Without "Royal Yoga" <term>rājayoga</term>—meaning without a connection to the King—the Seal <note>original: mudrā; here playing on the word for a king's physical signet or stamp</note> does not shine, even if it is wondrous. From the perspective of the Earth: even if it is extraordinary because it produces jewels and the like, it does not shine without the king. From the perspective of the Night: even if it is wondrous because of the planets and stars, it does not shine without the moon. From the perspective of the Seals: even if they are marked with wondrous lines and forms, they do not shine [without the highest yoga]. || 26 ||

**Regarding "Of the breath" <note>verse 27</note>:** One should thoroughly practice <term>abhyāsa</term> every method of the breath <term>māruta</term>—specifically the method of breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> and the seals—with the mind fully engaged. A wise and intelligent person should not allow the activity of the mind to flow toward any object other than the method of the breath. || 27 ||

Concluding the section on the seals: **Regarding "These..." <note>verse 28</note>:** By the Primal Lord, the Master of All, Śiva <note>the name Śambhu is explained as "one from whom happiness arises"</note>. In the manner described, ten seals have been spoken of. Among those seals, even a single one gives "Great Success" <term>mahāsiddhi</term>—such as the power to become as small as an atom <term>aṇimā</term>—to the self-restrained yogis. || 28 ||

Praising the teacher who instructs in the seals: **Regarding "Instruction..." <note>verse 29</note>:** The person who provides the instruction on the seals—such as the Great Seal and others...

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

**Regarding "Of the breath":** One should practice the whole method of the breath <term>māruta</term>, which is the technique of breath retention <term>kumbhaka</term> and the seals, by joining the mind to it. A wise person should not allow the mind’s activity to wander toward other subjects outside of the breath control <term>prāṇāyāma</term> method. || 27 ||

**Regarding "These":** Ten seals have been spoken of by the Primal Lord, the Sovereign Śiva. Among those seals, each individual seal is the giver of great success <term>mahāsiddhi</term>, such as the power of miniaturization <term>aṇimā</term>, to the yogis. || 28 ||

**Regarding "Instruction":** The person who gives instruction on the Great Seal and others according to the tradition <term>sampradāya</term> (the lineage of yoga teachers)—that person is the greatest among all teachers, the Master, and is indeed the manifest, living God <term>īśvara</term>. || 29 ||

<summary>This page emphasizes that physical breath control must be accompanied by total mental focus and validates the ten Great Seals (mudrās) as gifts from the god Śiva. It concludes by elevating the yoga teacher who possesses traditional lineage knowledge to the status of a divine being.</summary>
<keywords>Mudra, Breath Control, Adinatha, Shiva, Mahasiddhi, Guru, Ishvara, Kumbhaka, Yoga Lineage</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page concludes the third chapter (Instruction) of the text. It transitions from the technical details of the ten seals (mudrās) to the fruit of their practice: the conquest of death and the attainment of supernatural powers. It emphasizes that these results are only possible through total devotion to the Guru's specific instructions on lifestyle and practice.</meta>

<page-num>( 127 )</page-num>
<header>THIRD INSTRUCTION</header>

**Root: Being devoted to his word and focused on the practice of the seals <term>mudrā</term>, one attains the deception of time <note>original: kālavancanam; refers to escaping or "cheating" death</note> along with the qualities of becoming small <term>aṇimā</term> and other powers. || 130 ||**

**Thus ends the third instruction, titled "The Procedure for the Seals," in the Lamp on Hatha Yoga <term>Haṭhapradīpikā</term> composed by the venerable Svātmārāma, the king of yogis. || 3 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

He gives the traditional instruction <note>original: upadeśa</note> that has come down through the lineage of teachers to the practitioners. That person is the "Venerable Teacher," meaning the most excellent of all teachers. He is the Master, the Lord, and is indeed God <term>īśvara</term> manifest. This means he is non-different from God himself. || 29 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with "His" <note>referring to verse 130</note>: This refers to the word of the teacher who instructs one in the seals. Being "devoted to his word" means being dedicated and respectful toward the teacher's instructions regarding the performance of postures <term>āsana</term> and breath-retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>, as well as proper diet, lifestyle, and conduct. Having become so, and being "focused"—that is, careful and attentive—in the practice of the seals <note>the repeated performance of the Great Seal and others</note>, one attains "the deception of time." This means one achieves the tricking or evading of death, while simultaneously gaining the eight supernatural powers <term>siddhi</term>, beginning with the power to become as small as an atom <term>aṇimā</term>. || 130 ||

**Thus ends the third instruction, titled "The Explanation of the Seals," in the commentary called *The Moonlight* <term>Jyotsnā</term> on the Lamp on Hatha Yoga, written by Brahmananda. || 3 ||**

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

Regarding "His": One who is respectful and dedicated to the words of the Guru—the instructor of the seals—concerning the methods for performing postures, breath-retentions, and the proper rules for diet and behavior. By being careful in the practice of the Great Seal <term>mahāmudrā</term> and others, one attains the deception of time—which is the crossing over of death—together with the supernatural powers like becoming small. || 130 ||

**Thus ends the third instruction, titled "The Explanation of the Seals," in the vernacular commentary on the Lamp on Hatha Yoga. || 3 ||**

<summary>This page concludes the third chapter on Mudras (Seals), declaring that faithful practice under a Guru leads to the conquest of death and the attainment of the eight supernatural powers (siddhis). It reinforces the status of the yoga teacher as a divine figure whose instructions on diet and conduct are as vital as the physical techniques themselves.</summary>

<keywords>Mudras, Siddhis, Anima, Conquest of Death, Guru Lineage, Svatmarama, Brahmananda, Hatha Yoga, Spiritual Discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the fourth and final chapter of the text. Having completed the instructions on physical postures, breath control, and seals in the previous three chapters, the author now transitions to the ultimate goal of Hatha Yoga: the attainment of the state of Samādhi (meditative absorption) and Royal Yoga (Rāja-yoga).</meta>

# IN THE LAMP ON HATHA YOGA
<page-num>138</page-num>

**Root Text: Salutation to Shiva, the Guru, who is the essence of Sound <term>nāda</term>, the Point <term>bindu</term>, and the Part <term>kalā</term>. One who is constantly devoted to Him attains the stainless state <note>original: nirañjana-padam; the state of pure consciousness free from the "stain" of illusion</note>. || 1 ||**

**Now I shall describe the supreme sequence of Meditative Absorption <term>samādhi</term>, which destroys death, serves as a joyful means, and bestows the supreme bliss of the Absolute <term>brahmānanda</term>. || 2 ||**

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

The author, Svātmārāma, wishing to speak of Royal Yoga <term>Rāja-yoga</term>—which is the fruit of the postures <term>āsana</term>, breath-retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>, and seals <term>mudrā</term> described in the first, second, and third instructions—performs an auspicious salutation consisting of a bow to the Guru who is non-different from Lord Shiva. He does this to remove any obstacles, acknowledging the proverb that "good works face many obstacles."

**Salutation to Shiva:** This means to the one whose form is bliss, or who is non-different from the Lord <term>īśvara</term>. As it has been said: "Salutations to You, O Lord Shiva, manifest in the form of the Guru." **To the Guru:** To the spiritual preceptor <note>original: deśikāya</note>, or rather, to Shiva as the Lord who acts as the inner ruler and universal teacher of all. This is echoed in the Yoga Sutras of Patañjali: "He is the Guru even of the ancients, being unconditioned by time."

Let there be a state of humble bowing. What is the nature of this Shiva-Guru? He is **the essence of Sound, the Point, and the Part.** **Sound** <term>nāda</term> is the resonance similar to the ringing of a bronze bell. **The Point** <term>bindu</term> is the subtle vibration following the nasal sound <note>original: anusvāra</note>. **The Part** <term>kalā</term> is a specific portion or aspect of that sound. He is the one whose essential nature <term>ātman</term> consists of these three; he exists in the form of Sound, Point, and Part.

In that Shiva-Guru, the man who is **constantly**—meaning every day—**devoted** or attentive, attains the "Stain-less State." The word "stain" <term>añjana</term> refers to the limiting adjunct of Illusion <term>māyā</term>; that which is devoid of it is "Stain-less" <term>nirañjana</term>, meaning pure. This is the "State" or the Absolute <term>brahman</term> that is reached or understood by yogis. The author will further explain this later in verses regarding "one who enjoys absorption through the cultivation of inner sound." By using the phrase "Shiva, the Guru," the author also indicates the non-difference between the spiritual teacher and the Divine. || 1 ||

He now promises to explain the sequence of absorption. **"Now"**: After the description of postures, breath-retention, and seals, he will now...

->।। VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ।।<-

In the first, second, and third instructions, the author described postures, breath-retention, and seals. Now, Svātmārāma wishes to describe Royal Yoga <term>Rāja-yoga</term>, which is their result. To remove obstacles, he performs an auspicious salutation that demonstrates the non-difference between Lord Shiva and the Guru.

**Salutation to Shiva:** Salutations to the Guru who is the form of Shiva, non-different from the blissful Lord, and who is the giver of instruction. What is this Shiva-like Guru like? He is the sound of the ringing bell, the Point <term>bindu</term> which is the nasal vibration, and the Part <term>kalā</term> which is a portion of that sound. These constitute his very self. The man who is daily devoted to these Gurus in the form of Shiva shall reach the "Stainless State"—the state of the Absolute <term>brahman</term>—which is fit to be attained by pure yogis who are free from the limitations of Illusion <term>māyā</term>. || 1 ||

**Now:** After the teaching of postures, breath-retention, and seals, he now begins the description of Meditative Absorption <term>samādhi</term> in the form of sensory withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term> and the rest...

<summary>This page introduces the fourth chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, opening with a salutation to Shiva as the Supreme Guru and the source of inner mystic sounds (Nada). It defines Samādhi as the ultimate goal that leads to the bliss of Brahman and the conquest of death.</summary>
<keywords>Samādhi, Rāja-yoga, Śiva, Guru, Nāda, Bindu, Kalā, Nirañjana, Brahman, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Svātmārāma</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the opening verses of the fourth chapter. The previous page ended while explaining the nature of the Guru as the inner ruler and universal teacher. We now move into the specific explanation of verse 2, which defines the sequence and benefits of meditative absorption (Samādhi).</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
### FOURTH INSTRUCTION

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

In this context, the phrase "I shall speak" <note>original: "pravakṣyāmi"</note> connects to the "sequence of meditative absorption" <term>samādhi-krama</term>, which consists of practices such as sensory withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term>. I shall explain this by distinguishing its various aspects. What kind of sequence is this? It is **supreme** <note>original: "uttama"</note>, meaning it is the most excellent among the millions of varieties of absorption described by the Primordial Lord, Shiva <term>Ādinātha</term>. 

Furthermore, what is its nature? It is the **destroyer of death** <note>original: "mṛtyughnaṃ"</note>, for it wards off Time <note>Kāla</note> and allows the practitioner to depart from the body at their own will. It is the **means for attaining the happiness** of "liberation while living" <term>jīvanmukti</term> through the dawning of essential knowledge, the dissolution of the mind, and the exhaustion of latent tendencies <term>vāsanas</term>. 

Again, what is its effect? It **bestows the supreme bliss of the Absolute** <term>brahmānanda</term>. When the effects of past actions <term>prārabdha-karma</term> are exhausted, it leads to "liberation beyond the body" <term>videha-mukti</term>, which is the attainment of absolute bliss in the non-duality of the individual soul and the Absolute <term>Brahman</term>. 

In that state, there is "restraint" <term>nirodha</term>. Through absorption, the mind's fluctuations <term>vṛtti</term>—including their subconscious imprints—are entirely restrained. When the states of peace, agitation, or delusion are removed, the yogi attains a state of "abiding in their own changeless nature." This is liberation while living, as stated in the scriptures: "The wise one, even while living here, is freed from joy and sorrow" <note>original: "jīvanneveha vidvān harṣaśokābhyāṃ vimucyate"</note>. 

Supreme liberation, however, occurs at the end of one's experienced destiny. It is the absolute abiding in one's own nature, achieved through the "reverse-resolution" <note>original: "pratiprasava"; the process where effects dissolve back into their causes</note> of the qualities of the internal organ <term>antaḥkaraṇa</term>. In the transition between worldly consciousness and absorption, the mental imprints dissolve into the mind, the mind into the sense of "I-ness," the "I-ness" into the Great Principle <term>Mahat</term>, and the Great Principle into Primordial Nature <term>Pradhāna</term>. This is the reverse-evolution or dissolution into the original cause.

One might ask: "If a person liberated while living is seen to engage in worldly behavior—thinking 'I am a man' and so on—does his nature not become corrupted by these mental impositions, just as milk is turned sour by acid?" 

The answer is **no**. This is because the imprints of the Self experienced in "conscious absorption" <term>samprajñāta-samādhi</term> have been confirmed as the ultimate reality. A false or superficial appearance of change does not affect the changeless nature of the Self. The turning of milk into curd by acid is a real, substantial change <note>original: "tāttvika"</note>, but the analogy is different here. For the Soul <term>puruṣa</term>, worldly behavior like thinking "I am a Brahmin" is merely an imposition caused by the proximity of the internal organ. It is like a clear crystal appearing red because of a red hibiscus flower placed nearby; the color is not real. Just as the crystal returns to its own clear state when the flower is removed, the Soul's abiding in its own nature remains unshaken when all fluctuations of the internal organ are restrained. || 2 ||

---

->।। VERNACULAR VERSION ।।<-

Now I shall describe the sequence after deep deliberation. What is this sequence of absorption? It is the best among the millions of types of absorption taught and perfected by the Primordial Lord, Shiva <term>Ādinātha</term>. Furthermore, what does this sequence do? It wards off death, which is Time itself. By the power of this absorption, the yogi may leave the body at their own will. It is the means for attaining the happiness of liberation while living <term>jīvanmukti</term> through the dawn of essential knowledge and the destruction of the mind's cravings. What else is this sequence? It causes the exhaustion of past karma, removes the distinction between the individual soul and the Absolute, and bestows the experience of absolute, eternal bliss <term>brahmānanda</term>. || 2 ||

<sig>17</sig>

<vocab>Sequence of Absorption (Samādhi-krama), Commentary (Ṭīkā), Vernacular (Bhāṣā), Primordial Lord (Ādinātha), Liberation while living (Jīvanmukti), Liberation beyond the body (Videha-mukti), Bliss of the Absolute (Brahmānanda), Restraint of mental fluctuations (Citta-vṛtti-nirodha)</vocab>

<summary>This page explains verse 2 of the fourth chapter, defining Samādhi as the supreme state taught by Shiva that leads to liberation while living (Jīvanmukti) and ultimate bliss. It uses the analogy of a crystal and a hibiscus flower to explain how the soul remains pure even when the mind appears active.</summary>
<keywords>Samādhi, Jīvanmukti, Ādinātha, Brahmānanda, Citta-vṛtti-nirodha, crystal analogy, reverse-resolution, Prārabdha-karma</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Samādhi. Following the introductory remarks on the sequence of practice, the text now enumerates the various synonyms for the state of meditative absorption and provides three specific definitions involving the union of mind, breath, and the individual soul with the absolute.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>On the Hatha Yoga Pradīpikā</header>

**Royal Union <term>Rājayoga</term>, Meditative Absorption <term>Samādhi</term>, the state beyond mind <term>Unmanī</term>, the transcendence of mind <term>Manonmanī</term>, Immortality <term>Amaratva</term>, Dissolution <term>Laya</term>, Reality <term>Tattva</term>, the Void and non-Void <term>Śūnyāśūnya</term>, and the Supreme State <term>Paraṃ Pada</term> [are synonyms]. (3)**

**Mindlessness <term>Amanaska</term>, Non-duality <term>Advaita</term>, the Supportless <term>Nirālamba</term>, the Spotless <term>Nirañjana</term>, Liberation while living <term>Jīvanmukti</term>, the Natural state <term>Sahaja</term>, and the Fourth <term>Turyā</term>—all these terms denote the same state. (4)**

**Just as salt <note>original: "saindhava," referring to rock salt from the Indus region</note> when joined with water becomes one with it, so too the union of the Self and the mind is called Meditative Absorption. (5)**

**When the life-force <term>prāṇa</term> becomes exhausted <note>meaning quieted or still</note> and the mind dissolves, that state of "equal-flavor" <note>original: "samarasatva," meaning a state of total equilibrium or oneness</note> is called Meditative Absorption. (6)**

**That state of equality and the union of the two—the individual soul <term>jīvātman</term> and the Supreme Soul <term>paramātman</term>—where all mental constructs and intentions <term>saṅkalpa</term> are destroyed, is called Meditative Absorption. (7)**

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

The author explains the synonyms of Meditative Absorption <term>Samādhi</term> in detail through the two verses beginning with **Royal Union**. This is clear. [Verses 3-4]. He then explains the nature of Samādhi through the three verses beginning with **Just as salt...** [Verses 5-7]. Just as salt—specifically that which originates from the Indus region <note>original: "sindhudeśodbhavaṃ lavaṇaṃ"</note>—attains oneness and total identification with water through contact and mixing, so too does the mind when held within the Self. The mind takes the form of the Self and attains equality with the Self. Such union of Self and mind is what is expressed by the term "Samādhi." [5, 6, 7].

->।। VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ।।<-

Now the synonyms for Samādhi are spoken of in the two verses starting with **Royal Union**. Royal Union <term>Rājayoga</term>, Meditative Absorption <term>Samādhi</term>, the state beyond mind <term>Unmanī</term>, the transcendence of mind <term>Manonmanī</term>, Immortality <term>Amaratva</term>, Dissolution <term>Laya</term>, and the Supreme State of Void and non-Void. (3) Mindlessness, Non-duality, the Supportless, the Spotless, Liberation while living, the Natural state, and the Fourth—these are all names for Samādhi; these distinctions will be explained further. (4)

Regarding the verses **Just as salt...** and **When...**: Just as salt from the Indus region <note>original: "saindhava lavaṇa"</note> becomes of the same nature as water through union, so too the mind, when applied to the Self, becomes identical to the Self. This oneness of the Soul and the mind is called Samādhi. (5) When the life-force <term>prāṇa</term> diminishes and dissolves into the mind, one attains the state of immortality; that too is called Samādhi. (6) And when the individual soul and the Supreme Soul reach a state of equal union, then all thoughts and intentions <term>saṅkalpa</term> are destroyed; that is called Samādhi. (7)

<summary>This page lists the various technical synonyms for Samādhi and provides three essential metaphors and definitions: the salt-in-water analogy for the union of mind and self, the dissolution of breath and mind, and the non-dual union of the individual and universal soul.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi synonyms, Rajayoga, Unmani, Jivanmukti, Saindhava salt metaphor, Jivatman, Paramatman, dissolution of mind, union of breath and mind</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Chapter (Samādhi-lakṣaṇam) of the Haṭha Yoga Pradīpikā. After defining Samādhi and its synonyms on the previous page, the text now transitions into praising the greatness of Royal Union (Rājayoga) and emphasizing the necessity of a teacher’s grace to achieve these advanced states.</meta>

### FOURTH CHAPTER | (131)

**Who truly knows the greatness of Royal Union <term>Rājayoga</term> in its essence? Knowledge, liberation, stability, and supernatural powers <term>siddhi</term> are obtained through the words of the teacher. (8)**

**Renunciation of sense-objects is difficult to attain; the vision of Reality <term>Tattva</term> is difficult to attain; the natural state <term>Sahajavastha</term> is difficult to attain without the compassion of a true teacher. (9)**

**Through various postures <term>āsana</term>, diverse breath-retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>, and many different seals <term>karaṇa</term>, when the Great Power <note>refers to the Kuṇḍalinī</note> is awakened, the life-force <term>prāṇa</term> dissolves into the Void <term>śūnya</term>. (10)**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

Now, the praise of Royal Union <term>Rājayoga</term>. Regarding the verse **"Who truly knows..."**: Who indeed knows the greatness—the influence—of Royal Union, which was just mentioned, in its essence—that is, in its true nature? The implication is that no one knows it [fully]. Although it is impossible to describe the greatness of Royal Union in its entirety, he describes a portion of its influence. Knowledge—the direct experience of one's own nature; liberation—liberation after the death of the body <term>videhamukti</term>; stability—the state of being liberated while living <term>jīvanmukti</term> in the form of remaining in an unchanging nature; and perfection <term>siddhi</term>—powers like becoming as small as an atom <term>aṇimā</term> and others; these are all obtained through the teacher's word—the speech of the guru. The phrase "from Royal Union" is understood as the source. (8)

Regarding the verse **"Renunciation is difficult..."**: Those things that specifically bind and constrain the observer through association are "sense-objects" <term>viṣaya</term>. These include earthly things like a wife and family, as well as heavenly things like nectar <term>sudhā</term>. The renunciation of these—the absence of the desire for enjoyment—is difficult to attain. The "vision of Reality" <term>tattva-darśana</term>—the direct experience of the Self—is difficult to attain. The "natural state" <term>sahajavastha</term>—the Fourth state <term>turyā</term>—is difficult to attain without the compassion—the mercy—of a "True Teacher" <term>sadguru</term>. Such a teacher is defined by the characteristic to be described later: "one whose gaze is steady even without an object of sight." This requirement of grace applies to all the preceding points. They are difficult—impossible—to obtain [on one's own]. The dictionary <note>the Amarakośa</note> states that the prefix *duḥ* denotes difficulty or prohibition. The inner meaning is that through the guru's grace, everything becomes easy to attain. (9)

Regarding the verse **"Through various..."**: Through various—many kinds—of postures <term>āsana</term> such as the Matsyendra seat; through diverse—manifold—breath-retentions <term>kumbhaka</term>. The word "diverse" applies to both postures and breath-retentions, following the "logic of the crow’s eye" <note>original: "kākākṣi-golaka-nyāya," a Sanskrit idiom for a word that looks in two directions, qualifying both the word before and after it</note>. Through diverse—many types—of "actions" <term>karaṇa</term>—referring to the Great Seals <term>mudrā</term> like Mahāmudrā which are excellent aids for success in Hatha Yoga; when the Great Power <term>Mahāśakti</term>—the Serpent Power <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term>—is awakened...

->|| VERNACULAR EXPOSITION ||<-

Now the praise of Royal Union is spoken. Regarding the verse **"Who truly knows..."**: No one knows the essence of the greatness—the influence—of Royal Union. Knowledge, liberation, stability (which is liberation while living), and perfections such as the power of minuteness—all these are obtained from Royal Union through the words of the teachers. (8)

Regarding the verse **"Renunciation is difficult..."**: Without the grace of an excellent teacher, the renunciation of sense-objects—the absence of desire for enjoyments—is difficult to attain. The vision of reality—the direct experience of the Soul—is difficult to attain. The natural state, which is the Fourth state, is also difficult to attain. Yet, through the grace of the teacher, everything becomes easily obtained. (9)

Regarding the verse **"Through various..."**: Postures of many kinds like the Matsyendra seat, and breath-retentions of many kinds, and...

<summary>This page highlights the supreme importance of the Guru's grace in achieving success in Yoga, asserting that renunciation and the highest state of Samādhi (Sahajavasthā) are nearly impossible to reach alone. It also explains that physical practices like postures and breath-retention are intended to awaken the Kuṇḍalinī, leading the life-force to dissolve into the spiritual void.</summary>
<keywords>Rājayoga, Guru's grace, Sahajavasthā, Kuṇḍalinī, Prāṇa, Siddhi, renunciation, Samādhi, Tattva-darśana</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Chapter of the Haṭha Yoga Pradīpikā, moving from the necessity of the Guru's grace to the specific internal states of the yogi when the Kuṇḍalinī is awakened. It elaborates on the transition from physical and mental activity to the "Natural State" (Sahajāvasthā) and the eventual destruction of all past actions (karma).</meta>

### ON THE HAṬHA YOGA PRADĪPIKĀ

**Root Text:**
**For the yogi whose awareness of the Power <note>the Kuṇḍalinī</note> has arisen, and who has abandoned all actions without exception, the Natural State <term>Sahajāvasthā</term> arises spontaneously. (11)**
**When the life-force <term>prāṇa</term> flows through the Suṣumṇā and the mind enters the Void, then the knower of Yoga uproots all karmas. (12)**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

[Continuing from the previous verse:] When sleep occurs, the life-force—the vital air—dissolves into the Void, the "Opening of Brahman" <term>Brahmarandhra</term>; it reaches a state of dissolution. The dissolution of the life-force is simply the absence of its activity. (10)

**Regarding Verse 11 ("For the yogi whose awareness..."):** "Arisen" means born. The "awareness of the Power" refers to the awakening of the Kuṇḍalinī. For that yogi by whom all actions—the entirety of them—have been abandoned or discarded. By means of physical posture <term>āsana</term>, physical activities are abandoned, yet activity remains in the life-force and the senses. When mental activities are abandoned through withdrawal <term>pratyāhāra</term>, concentration <term>dhāraṇā</term>, meditation <term>dhyāna</term>, and cognitive absorption <term>samprajñāta-samādhi</term>, activity still remains in the intellect <term>buddhi</term>. This is because the pure Self <term>puruṣa</term> is unchanging and "is indeed unattached" <note>original Sanskrit: *asaṅgo hyayaṃ puruṣaḥ*, a famous Upanishadic quote</note>, whereas the intellect, which is composed of the quality of purity <term>sattvaguṇa</term>, is subject to change. (11)

Through highest dispassion <term>paravairāgya</term> or through the long-term practice of cognitive absorption <term>samprajñāta-samādhi</term>, once the activities of the intellect are abandoned, one remains in a state of unchanging self-nature. That itself is the Natural State <term>Sahajāvasthā</term>, the Fourth State <term>Turyāvasthā</term>, and Liberation While Living <term>Jīvanmukti</term>. It manifests spontaneously, without any further effort. This is supported by the scripture: "By that which you renounce, renounce that as well; one should become unattached through wisdom." <note>This suggests a total letting go, even of the tool of renunciation itself.</note> **Regarding Verse 12 ("When the life-force..."):** When the life-force—the vital air—is flowing in the Suṣumṇā, the middle channel...

->|| VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

Through various types of Great Seals <term>mahāmudrā</term> and other practices, when the Great Power—the Kuṇḍalinī—is awakened, the life-force then dissolves into the Void of the "Opening of Brahman" <term>Brahmarandhra</term>. (10)

**Regarding Verse 11:** For the yogi in whom the awareness of the Kuṇḍalinī has arisen and who has removed all karmas: through the practice of posture <term>āsana</term>, the activities of the body are removed. However, activity still resides in the breath and senses. Through withdrawal, concentration, meditation, and cognitive absorption, the activities of the mind are removed. At that point, activity remains only in the intellect, and the intellect becomes pure <term>sattvic</term>. Through dispassion and the long practice of absorption, the activities of the intellect are also removed. Then, one is established in the unchanging self-nature. This is called the Natural State <term>Sahajāvasthā</term>. This is also called the Fourth State <term>Turyāvasthā</term>. In this state, the yogi attains Liberation While Living <term>Jīvanmukti</term> spontaneously, without needing to make further effort. (11)

**Regarding Verse 12:** And when the life-force begins to move in the Suṣumṇā, which is the middle channel, then the internal faculty <term>antaḥkaraṇa</term> enters the Void of Brahman. At that time, the knower of yoga uproots all those karmas that were destined to be experienced <term>prārabdha karma</term>. (12)

<summary>This page describes how the awakening of the Kuṇḍalinī leads a yogi from physical and mental stillness to the "Natural State" (Sahajāvasthā), where effort ceases and all past karmas are destroyed. It explains the hierarchy of stillness, moving from the body to the senses, then the mind, and finally the intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Sahajāvasthā, Jīvanmukti, Kuṇḍalinī, Samprajñāta Samādhi, Suṣumṇā, Brahmarandhra, Turyāvasthā, Karma</keywords>

### FOURTH INSTRUCTION | ( 133 )

**Root Text:**
**Salutations to you, the Immortal One, by whom even Time <note>original: *kāla*, meaning both Time and Death</note> has been conquered—that Time into whose mouth this entire world of moving and unmoving things has fallen. (13)**

**When the mind attains equanimity and the breath travels through the middle path <term>Suṣumṇā</term>, then the states of Amaroli, Vajroli, and Sahajoli arise. (14)**

**How can knowledge arise in the mind as long as the breath still lives <note>i.e., continues to fluctuate</note> and the mind has not yet died <note>i.e., reached a state of complete stillness</note>?...**

### || COMMENTARY ||

When the mind <term>mānasa</term>—the internal instrument—enters into the Absolute <term>Brahman</term>, which is the "Void" <note>original: *śūnye*, a state free from the limitations of space, time, and physical objects</note>, at that moment, the knower of Yoga <note>one who understands the restraint of mental fluctuations</note> uproots all past actions <term>prārabdha-karma</term>. The word "uproots" <note>original: *nirmūlayati*</note> is derived from the word for "rootless" <note>A linguistic note on the Sanskrit verb formation *ṇic* used here</note>. (12)

Through the practice of deep absorption <term>samādhi</term>, one overcomes even those actions already set in motion. The author now offers salutations to the yogi who has thus conquered Time.

**Regarding Verse 13 ("Salutations to the Immortal..."):** "Immortal" <term>amara</term> means one who does not die. Salutations to you, such a long-lived yogi. Even Time <note>or Death, *mṛtyu*</note>, though difficult to ward off, has been conquered or defeated by you, the yogi. This sentence provides the reason for the salutation. Who is this Time? It is that into whose "mouth" this visible world of the moving <note>animals, humans</note> and the unmoving <note>plants, minerals</note> has fallen. The meaning is that the yogi has conquered even that which devours the entire universe. (13)

The previously mentioned practices of Amaroli and the others only truly manifest when Samādhi is perfected. Therefore, after describing Samādhi, the author explains that these states arise through its perfection.

**Regarding Verse 14 ("When the mind..."):** When the mind <term>citta</term> attains "equanimity"—meaning it becomes a continuous flow of awareness focused entirely on the object of meditation—and when the breath <term>vāyu</term> travels through the middle path <note>the *suṣumṇā* channel</note>, this equanimity is achieved. At that time, the states of Amaroli, Vajroli, and Sahajoli described previously are produced. The implication is that these cannot be perfected by one who has not conquered their breath or their mind. (14)

The author further states that without the practice of Haṭha Yoga, neither knowledge nor liberation can be achieved.

**Regarding Verse 15 ("How can knowledge..."):** As long as the breath...

### || HINDI COMMENTARY ||

Now, by the practice of Samādhi, one disregards even those actions already in motion <term>prārabdha</term>. Therefore, salutations are offered to that yogi who has conquered Time.

**Regarding Verse 13:** The world, including everything stationary and moving, has fallen into the mouth of Time. That Time devours the world and cannot be turned away by anyone else. Yet, you yogis have overcome that Time, that Death. Therefore, salutations to you, the Immortal Yogis. (13)

**Regarding Verse 14:** When the mind <term>citta</term>—the internal organ—attains a state of equalness within the Self <term>Ātman</term>, and the vital breath begins to move within the central channel <term>Suṣumṇā</term>, then Amaroli, Vajroli, and Sahajoli manifest. (14)

The author explains that without the practice of Haṭha Yoga, knowledge <term>jñāna</term> and liberation <term>mokṣa</term> are not perfected.

**Regarding Verse 15:** The breaths of the left and right channels <note>original: *iḍā* and *piṅgalā*</note>...

<vocab>Samādhi (absorption), Amaroli (yogic ritual/state), Vajroli (yogic practice/state), Sahajoli (natural state), Prārabdha (past karma unfolding), Suṣumṇā (central energy channel), Kāla (Time/Death), Jñāna (knowledge), Mokṣa (liberation)</vocab>

<summary>This page highlights the yogi's conquest of Death/Time through the perfection of Samādhi. It explains that advanced states like Amaroli and Vajroli only truly occur once the mind is stilled and the breath enters the central Suṣumṇā channel.</summary>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Haṭha Yoga Pradīpikā, specifically focusing on Verse 15 and its extensive commentary. It builds on the previous discussion of how the breath and mind are interconnected and must both be stilled to reach liberation.</meta>

( 134 )
->IN THE LIGHT ON HAṬHA YOGA<-

## **Root Text: The man who brings these two—the breath and the mind—to dissolution, he attains liberation; no other does, by any means whatsoever. (15)**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

[The breath and mind] continue to live. By the use of the word "also," it is implied that the senses continue to "live" and do not die as long as the mind does not die, but continues to live. The "life" of the breath is its flow through the *Iḍā* and *Piṅgalā* <note>original: *iḍāpiṅgalābhyāṃ vahanaṃ*, referring to the left and right nostrils/energy channels</note>. The "life" of the senses is the grasping of their respective objects. The "life" of the mind is the production of fluctuations in the form of various objects. What is intended here by "death" is the cessation of those respective states. 

Now, one might ask: as long as these are not destroyed in their current form, how can knowledge—the direct experience of the Self—arise in the internal instrument <term>antaḥkaraṇa</term>? It cannot. The point is that the activities of the breath, senses, and mind act as obstacles to knowledge. 

That yogi who leads these two—the breath and the mind—to dissolution <term>vilaya</term> or destruction, attains liberation <term>mokṣa</term>, which is characterized by remaining in one's ultimate, true form. The dissolution of the breath is its state of non-activity within the crown of the head <term>Brahmarandhra</term>. Through absorption in the form of the object of meditation, the mind is dissolved when it is no longer occupied by other, endless objects. This means that a person whose breath and mind have not dissolved cannot attain liberation even through a hundred different methods. 

This is stated in the *Yoga-bīja* <note>an ancient text on the "Seed of Yoga"</note>: "The mind cannot be mastered through various types of philosophical reasoning. Therefore, the only way to conquer it is to conquer the breath." It also says that the supreme state of isolation <term>kaivalya</term>, which is beyond pleasure and pain, is "attained through the path of the Adepts <term>Siddha-mārga</term>, and not otherwise; so says Lord Shiva." The "path of the Adepts" is the path of Yoga. By this, it is established that without Yoga, neither knowledge nor liberation can be achieved. 

This is well-known in the scriptures, tradition, histories, and ancient lore <note>original: *śruti-smṛti-itihāsa-purāṇa*</note>. For instance: "Now, the means to that Vision is Yoga," where "that Vision" refers to the Vision of the Self. "Through the attainment of spiritual yoga <term>adhyātma-yoga</term>, the wise man realizes the Divine and leaves behind both joy and sorrow." Also: "One knows the Divine through faith, devotion, and the yoga of meditation." 

And: "When the five senses of knowledge <note>original: *jñānāni*, the five perceptions</note> stand still together with the mind, and the intellect does not stir—that, they say, is the Supreme Goal. They consider that steady control of the senses to be 'Yoga.' Then one becomes focused." 

"When, by means of the reality of the Self, a disciplined person sees the reality of the Absolute <term>Brahman</term> as one would see a lamp, then, knowing the unborn, eternal Divine who is untainted by all realities, he is freed from all bonds." "For the sake of the Absolute, one should unite the Self with the Great Sound *OM*." "Holding the body straight, with the three upper parts <note>chest, neck, and head</note> aligned, and drawing the senses into the heart by means of the mind, the wise man should cross [the world] by the boat of the Absolute..."

->|| VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

When the air moves through the channels, that is the "life" of the breath. When the senses grasp their various objects, that is the "life" of the senses. When many mental states regarding various objects are manifested, that is the "life" of the mind. So, as long as the breath lives, the senses live, and the mind lives—as long as these do not die—one cannot have the direct experience of the Self. The yogi who destroys both the breath and the mind, that yogi attains liberation. He whose breath and mind have not dissolved cannot attain liberation even if he tries a hundred different ways. (15)

<vocab>Prāṇa (breath/energy), Manas (mind), Mokṣa (liberation), Yoga-bīja (an esoteric yoga text), Adhyātma-yoga (inner yoga), Brahmarandhra (the spiritual opening at the crown of the head), Haṭha-yoga-pradīpikā (Light on Hatha Yoga), Kaivalya (absolute freedom/isolation)</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that liberation is impossible without the "dissolution" of the breath and the mind, citing various scriptures to prove that Yoga is the essential practical method for achieving spiritual knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>dissolution of mind, breath control, liberation, Yoga-bija, Upanishadic quotes, Brahmarandhra, sensory withdrawal</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the extensive commentary on Verse 15 of the Fourth Instruction. Having established that the dissolution of breath and mind leads to liberation, the commentator now provides a massive list of scriptural authorities (Shruti, Smriti, and Puranas) to prove that Yoga is the essential and universal path to the highest state, regardless of one’s social status or gender.</meta>

( 135 )
<header>THE FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

## || COMMENTARY ||

The wise one understands that "all streams <gloss>si</gloss> are fearsome." The scriptures <term>Shruti</term> state: "Meditate upon the Self as Om," and so on. In the section on the duties of ascetics, the lawgiver **Manu** says: "One should look upon the past and the future through the Yoga of the Supreme Self. By quickly abandoning the two bodies <note>original: *dehadvayaṃ*, referring to the physical and the subtle bodies</note>, one becomes liberated from bondage." 

In the **Yajnavalkya Smriti**, it is written: "Among all actions such as sacrifices, proper conduct, restraint, non-violence, charity, and study of the Vedas, this is the supreme duty: the vision of the Self through Yoga." 

The great sage **Matanga** says: "The best of the twice-born <note>meaning the higher castes who have undergone initiation</note>, having abandoned all rituals like the *Agnishtoma* sacrifice, and remaining peaceful and devoted to the practice of Yoga, attains the Supreme Brahman. Yoga is the purifier for Brahmins, Kshatriyas, Vaishyas, women, and even Shudras. For the sake of peace from the fruits of actions, there is no other way to liberation than Yoga." 

In the **Daksha Smriti**, it is stated by way of contrast: "That Brahman is indeed self-knowable, just as the pleasure of a maiden with her lover is known only to her. One who is not a yogi never knows It, just as one born blind cannot know what a water-pot looks like." These and other traditional texts <term>Smriti</term> support this.

In the **Mahabharata**, regarding the path of Yoga, the sage **Vyasa** says: "Even one of low birth or a woman desiring righteousness may reach the supreme goal through this path. Whether a man knows all laws or is unrefined, whether he is the most righteous or the greatest of sinners, whether he is a 'tiger among men' <note>a hero</note> or one who bears the mark of impotence—if he serves this path, he crosses the great sorrow that is the ocean of old age and death. Even one who is merely curious about Yoga transcends the word-form of God <term>Shabda-Brahman</term> <note>meaning they go beyond the ritualistic sounds and rules of the Vedas</note>."

In the **Bhagavad Gita**: "Thus constantly uniting himself with the Self, the yogi of subdued mind reaches the peace that culminates in Nirvana and which subsists in Me." Also: "That place which is reached by the followers of Samkhya <note>the path of intellectual inquiry</note> is also reached by the yogis," and so on.

In the **Aditya Purana**: "From Yoga, knowledge is born; Yoga is the state of having the mind focused solely on Me." 

In the **Skanda Purana**: "Liberation comes from knowledge of the Self, but that does not happen without Yoga. And that Yoga is perfected only through practice over a long period of time."

In the **Kurma Purana**, the words of Shiva: "Now I shall speak of the supreme Yoga, which is very difficult to attain, by which men see the Self as the Lord, shining like the sun. The fire of Yoga quickly burns away the entire cage of sins. Then, clear knowledge is born, and through knowledge, one attains Nirvana."

In the **Garuda Purana**: "The wise man should strive in such a way that supreme cessation is attained. That is obtained through Yoga and by no other means. Yoga is indeed the supreme medicine for those scorched by the heat of worldly existence. He whose mind is not attached to things high or low finds this impossible. But he who has burned away the collection of all miseries with the fire of Yoga surely attains the supreme, eternal Nirvana—of this there is no doubt. One who has attained perfection in Yoga is fulfilled by the vision of the Self; there is no further work for him to do, for he has accomplished everything. Always delighting in the Self, he is full and has reached ultimate bliss. Therefore, even for him who is filled with supreme joy, there is a turning away from worldly desires <term>nirveda</term>. The great-souled yogis, whose selves are purified by austerity and whose senses are restrained, cross the great ocean of existence by Yoga alone."

In the **Vishnu Dharma**: "Tell us that supreme good which helps all beings, including women, and even insects and moths." Thus was the sage **Kapila** questioned long ago by the gods and divine seers, and he told them: "Yoga alone is their supreme good."

In the **Vasistha**: "O Rama, the cholera-like poison of the unbearable speed of worldly existence is cured by the purifying 'Garuda-mantra' <note>an antidote to poison</note> of Yoga." 

Now, one might object: "Since direct knowledge can be gained through Great Sayings <term>Mahavakya</term> such as 'That Thou Art' <note>original: *Tat Tvam Asi*</note>, why should one labor in Yoga, which is achieved only through great effort?"

<summary>This page lists numerous scriptural citations from the Vedas, Smritis, and Puranas to prove that Yoga is the only certain path to liberation for all people, regardless of caste, gender, or past sins. It concludes by introducing a common philosophical objection: why practice physical Yoga if knowledge can be gained through scriptures alone?</summary>
<keywords>scriptural authority, liberation, universal path, Manu, Yajnavalkya, Vyasa, Puranas, Nirvana, Self-knowledge, fire of yoga</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical philosophical debate from the commentary on Verse 15. While the previous page focused on scriptural authorities (Smritis) praising Yoga, this page delves into a sophisticated logical defense regarding how "direct knowledge" of the Self is attained, specifically debating the role of Upanishadic statements versus meditative practice.</meta>

( 136 )
<header>THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

-> || COMMENTARY || <-

This practice must be performed. It should not be argued that there is no evidence for the directness <term>aparoksha</term> of the knowledge arising from scriptural sentences. The knowledge produced by sentences such as "That Thou Art" <note>original: *Tat Tvam Asi*, a famous Upanishadic "Great Saying" equating the individual soul with the Absolute</note> is direct knowledge, because its subject matter—the Self—is always directly present. This is supported by the logical inference that it is like the direct visual perception of an object like a water-pot. 

It cannot be said that the "directness" of the subject is unproven because it lacks a physical form. Its directness is well-established, as it is identical to the Consciousness that is the very object of our ignorance. For example, when the mind's activity <term>vritti</term> connects with a pot through the eyes, it removes the ignorance covering the underlying Consciousness of that pot; at that moment, the pot becomes direct to us. Similarly, when the sentences like "That Thou Art" awaken a mental state shaped like Pure Consciousness <term>chaitanya</term>, that very removal of ignorance makes the Reality "direct." Therefore, the logical reason is not unproven. 

Furthermore, this directness is not dependent on being produced by the physical senses. If directness required physical senses, then our internal experiences like "pleasure" would not be direct, because the mind is not traditionally counted as a physical sense organ. Alternatively, we can define the directness of an object as its appearing as non-different from the manifested Consciousness. In cases where knowledge is merely indirect <term>paroksha</term>, the "veil" of ignorance is not removed, so there is no over-extension of this definition. 

In the case of a mistake—such as thinking a rope is a snake—the sentence "This is not a snake, but a rope" creates a mental state, but it does not fully remove the underlying veil of ignorance for someone still suffering from a visual defect; thus, the object remains indirect in a sense. However, knowledge born from the teachings of Vedanta <term>Vedanta</term> is truly direct because it removes the veil of ignorance. This knowledge is superior to any previous misconceptions. 

Furthermore, the scripture <term>Shruti</term> says: "I ask about that Person known only through the Upanishads." If the Self could be known only through Yoga, this scriptural statement—that the Self is known through the Upanishads alone—would be contradicted. 

If one objects, "The logical inference is irrelevant," we respond: No. In the common view, "direct perception" is defined as knowledge produced by the sense organs. But in our view, the senses are the cause for specific types of perception, and specific Words are the cause for other types of direct perception. There is no obstacle to considering the mind <term>manas</term> as a sense organ. The scriptures say the mind is the "Lord of the Senses" <note>original: *manonātha*</note>, just as one might say a man is the "King of Men" to show his excellence, not to say he is not a man. The mind is a specific internal faculty. Therefore, authoritative dictionaries <term>kosha</term> state: "The organs of action are the anus, etc., while the mind and eyes are the organs of knowledge," and "Perception is that which follows the senses; the mind is also a means of perception." 

The words of the **Bhagavad Gita**—"The senses are ten and one <note>original: *indriyāṇi daśakaṃ ca*, referring to the five senses of perception, five of action, and the mind as the eleventh</note>"—serve as proof that the mind is a sense organ. Furthermore, knowledge born from sentences like "That Thou Art" is verbal <term>shabda</term> knowledge. Just as the sentence "One should sacrifice" produces verbal knowledge, this scriptural knowledge is not in conflict with direct experience. This is the most efficient explanation.

<summary>This page features a highly technical philosophical defense of how scriptural study (Vedanta) leads to direct realization of the Self, arguing that the mind acts as a sense organ and that "That Thou Art" provides immediate, not just theoretical, knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Aparoksha (direct knowledge), Tat Tvam Asi, Manas (mind), Indriya (senses), Vedanta, Chaitanya (consciousness), Vritti (mental state), Bhagavad Gita</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical philosophical debate from the commentary on Verse 15 regarding the nature of "direct knowledge" (aparoksha). The previous page introduced the argument that scriptural statements like "That Thou Art" can produce immediate, direct experience of the Self. This page refutes that claim, arguing instead that direct realization is achieved through a mind purified by disciplined yoga practice (samadhi), rather than through words alone.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION (137)</header>

## || COMMENTARY ||

...from logic that is favorable. In your view, however, by accepting direct perception even from words, you create the **logical burden** <term>gaurava: a term in Indian logic referring to an unnecessary complexity or a "heaviness" in an argument</term> of imagining two distinct sets of cause-and-effect relationships. Furthermore, [knowledge] is produced even before reflection and deep meditation. In your view, even indirect knowledge does not remove ignorance; therefore, you face the burden of claiming that knowledge only removes ignorance in its capacity as "negating knowledge" <note>original: *badha-jnanatvena*</note>.

In my view, however, when the Self is seen by the **internal organ** <term>antahkarana: the mind or "inner instrument"</term>—which has reached the ripeness of the practice of meditative absorption <term>samadhi</term> and is free from all impurities such as doubt—ignorance is removed by the mere act of seeing. Thus, there is no room for any logical burden. 

This point is confirmed by the teaching of Yama in the *Katha Upanishad*: "This Self, hidden in all beings, does not shine forth. But it is seen by subtle seers through a sharp and subtle intellect. The wise man should restrain speech in the mind..." <note>original: *Esha sarveshu bhuteshu...* (Katha Upanishad 1.3.12-13)</note>, and concluding with the phrase "he is released from the jaws of death" <note>original: *mrityu-mukhat pramuchyate*</note>, which refers to the removal of ignorance. There is no dispute on this matter.

If one adopts the view that knowledge produced before reflection is merely indirect <term>paroksha</term>, and thus there is no "burden" caused by obstruction, even then, a problem remains. Once the mind is purified through hearing the teachings and other practices, the vision of the Self is possible immediately afterward. To imagine that one must then recall specific scriptural sentences *after* that vision would involve a great logical burden.

Now, an opponent might say: "Surely, we do not claim that knowledge born from words is direct based on logic alone, but also based on scripture <term>Shruti</term>." For instance, the scripture says, "I ask about that Person known through the Upanishads" <note>original: *tam tvaupanishadam purusham pucchami* (Brihadaranyaka Upanishad 3.9.26)</note>. The "Upanishadic" nature of the Supreme Person does not merely mean being an object of the intellect's knowledge through the Upanishads; for if something could be known through direct perception and yet be called "Upanishadic," it would lead to an absurdity in common usage. 

Just as in a ritual involving twelve pots, even though eight pots are present, the ritual is specifically referred to as the "twelve-pot" ceremony. Or, just as a man with two sons might be referred to in a specific context as having "one son." So it is here. The intended meaning is that the Self is known **only** through the Upanishads. The opponent argues that if the Self were known by the mind, this scriptural title "Upanishadic" would be logically impossible.

To this, we reply: No. The suffix [in "Upanishadic"] does not exclude everything other than the Upanishads from being a cause. Even you, who advocate for direct knowledge via words, accept that the mind and other factors act as instruments in the indirect perception of the Self. Rather, the term "Upanishadic" excludes other types of words, such as the *Puranas* <note>ancient legendary and mythological texts</note>. This is supported by the traditional law <term>Smriti</term> which says: "He is to be heard from the Vedic sentences" <note>original: *shrotavyah shruti-vakyebhyah*</note>. Since I also accept this meaning, your objection amounts to nothing. 

It is only proper to propose that other means of knowledge are excluded once it has been proven that words are the authoritative source for the **direct** perception of the Self. The intention to exclude other *words*, however, is proper to assume because it is consistent with scripture. This being the case, scriptures such as "It is to be seen by the mind alone" <note>original: *manasaivanudrashtavyam*</note> and "This is to be attained by the mind alone" can be explained with perfect clarity.

Some people argue that these scriptures—which say the mind is the cause—are not contradicted because they only mean the mind is the "material cause" <note>original: *upadana*</note> of the vision-state. This is entirely untenable upon investigation. Since those texts were written in response to the need for a "means of knowledge" <term>pramana</term>, how could they suddenly refer to a material cause? Furthermore, since scriptures like "Desire, determination, doubt..." <note>original: *kamah sankalpo vicikitsa...* (Brihadaranyaka Upanishad 1.5.3)</note> already teach that *all* mental states have the mind as their material cause, why would there be a need to describe it as such here?

A doubt arises because in the previous section <note>the second "garland" or chapter</note>, the sacred syllable **Om** <term>Pranava</term> was described as the revealer of Brahman; thus, could it not also be a cause of direct knowledge?

<summary>This page presents a philosophical defense of the mind (Manas) as the primary instrument for direct realization of the Self, countering the view that scriptural words alone provide direct knowledge. It uses logical principles (Gaurava) and Upanishadic citations to argue that while scripture points the way, the actual "vision" occurs through a mind purified by Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Aparoksha Jnana, Manas, Shruti, Katha Upanishad, Gaurava, Samadhi, Brahman, Antahkarana, Vedantic epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of Yoga as the primary means of Self-realization. While the previous page argued that scriptural study alone is insufficient for direct experience, this page focuses on the mind (manas) as the valid instrument for perceiving the Self when purified by Yoga. It addresses technical objections from the Nyaya (logic) school and cites the Yoga Sutras and the Yoga Bija to prove that "truth-bearing" wisdom only arises in the state of meditative absorption (samadhi).</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika</header>

## || COMMENTARY ||

To prevent [errors], scriptural statements with restrictive emphasis such as "It is to be seen by the mind alone" <note>original: *manasaivanudrashtavyam* from the Brihadaranyaka Upanishad 4.4.19</note> can be explained in this way; enough with this excessive talk. In reality, the knowledge of extremely distant objects possessed by yogis in **meditative absorption** <term>samadhi: a state of intense concentration where the observer and the object become one</term> is well-known in all scriptures; it is not **indirect** <term>paroksha: knowledge gained through a medium, like words or inference, rather than direct experience</term>. This is because, at that time, the apparatus for indirect knowledge is absent. Nor is it mere memory, because those yogis had no prior specific experience of those objects. Nor is it a "witness-perception" like the feeling of pleasure or pain, for that would contradict established doctrine. Nor is it without proof, because the rule is that every instance of valid knowledge requires an instrument. Nor is it produced by the eyes or other senses, since there is no physical contact with the objects. 

Therefore, it must be said that this is a **mental valid knowledge** <note>original: *manasiki prama*</note>. Thus, any attempt to deny that the mind acts as a sense organ and an instrument of proof is far-fetched. Even those who attempt a synthesis of Yoga and Scripture still face the same mass of faults mentioned previously. Therefore, it is established: the Self is known through the mind alone, aided by the **latent impressions** <term>samskaras: psychological traces or "grooves" left by past actions and intense practice</term> produced by Yoga. 

One might object: just as a man lustfully dwelling on a woman might "see" her before him even if she is far away, would not the "seeing" of the Self also be an **invalid perception** <term>aprama</term> since it is born of intense imagination? No, because the object (the Self) is never **sublated** <term>badhita: "cancelled" or proven false by a later, more accurate experience</term> and it is not born of a mental defect. The lustful man’s vision of a woman is invalid precisely because it is sublated by reality and is born of the defect of passion. 

Furthermore, if we say that meditative absorption acts as the revealer of truth, we are not inventing a new instrument of proof. Because the mind acts as a cooperative cause, even the **Logicians** <term>Naiyayikas: followers of the Nyaya school of Indian philosophy, known for their rigorous analysis of logic and perception</term>, who are experts in defining the instruments of proof, include "Yoga-born perception" within the category of **extraordinary perception** <term>alaukika-pratyaksha</term>. Through this extraordinary Yogic contact, yogis see distant, hidden, or subtle objects, and even the Self, exactly as they are. This is stated in a **Patanjali Sutra** <note>original: *Yoga Sutra 1.48*</note>:

> "In that state, the wisdom is 'truth-bearing.'"

In that state of meditative absorption, the wisdom that arises is different in its objects from the wisdom gained through **hearing** <note>original: *shrutam* — scriptural knowledge</note> or **inference** <note>original: *anumanam* — logical deduction</note>. Why? Because it pertains to **particulars** <note>original: *vishesha*</note>. The sense is: this wisdom has for its object the "particular," which is **non-conceptual** <term>nirvikalpa: direct experience without the filter of names, categories, or mental labels</term>. Words are restricted to producing knowledge through the "limitations of the word-meaning" (generality), and inference is restricted to producing knowledge through the "limitations of universal concomitance." Therefore, those methods cannot grasp the pure, specific subject. 

On this point, the **commentary by Vyasa** <note>original: *Badarayana-krityam-bhashyam* — though the text says "Badarayana," this quote is typically attributed to Vyasa's commentary on the Yoga Sutras</note> says: "Scriptural knowledge is a science of generalities; indeed, a 'particular' cannot be expressed by scripture. Why? Because a word cannot have a convention (meaning) assigned to a specific particular." It continues to say that the particularity found in subtle elements or in the **Consciousness** <term>Purusha: the true Self or pure awareness</term> can only be grasped by the wisdom of meditative absorption. As stated in the *Yoga Bija*:

> "Though one be established in knowledge, dispassionate, a knower of duty, and a master of the senses—without Yoga, even a god does not attain liberation, O Dear One."

Furthermore, as the scripture says: "To whatever his mind is attached, to that he goes together with his karma" <note>original: *Brihadaranyaka Upanishad 4.4.6*</note>, and as the **Tradition** <term>Smriti: specifically the Bhagavad Gita 14.14-15</term> says: "Attachment to the qualities is the cause of his births in good and evil wombs." At the time of the body’s end, whatever passion or thought is awakened, the soul attains that very womb. Thus, for one lacking Yoga, another birth is certain, because at the moment of death, a non-yogi is unable to prevent the mental agitation that arises. It is said in the *Yoga Bija*:

> "Whatever one contemplates in the mind at the time of the body's end, that very thing the soul becomes; thus is birth created—"

<summary>This page argues that the mind, refined by Yoga, is the only instrument capable of direct Self-realization, as words and logic only deal with generalities. It also warns that without the mental control gained through Yoga, one is subject to the cycle of rebirth based on their final thoughts at death.</summary>

<keywords>manas, samadhi, yogaja-pratyaksha, truth-bearing wisdom, ritambhara pragna, Yoga Sutras, Yoga Bija, liberation, rebirth, perception</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument regarding the necessity of Yoga for achieving direct perception of the Self. It builds on the previous discussion by asserting that intellectual inquiry (jnana) into the Great Sayings of the Upanishads is only a preliminary step; the actual realization of liberation (moksha) requires the practical application of Yoga to transform theoretical knowledge into direct experience.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>Fourth Instruction</header>

## || COMMENTARY ||

...cause. Humans do not know what kind of birth might occur at the end of the body’s life. Therefore, mere intellectual knowledge, renunciation, and chanting are nothing more than physical labor. If a person is distracted when a mere ant <note>original: *pipilika*</note> crawls on their body, losing their focus on knowledge, then how could someone bitten by scorpions—referring to the intense pains at the moment of death—remain peaceful or happy? However, for Yogis, by the power of Yoga, even at the time of death, there is only liberation <term>moksha: release from the cycle of rebirth</term> through the constant contemplation of the Self; thus, there is no future birth. This was stated by the Lord:

> "At the time of departure, with a steady mind, endowed with devotion and the power of Yoga..." <note>original: *prayana-kale manasa’calena...* from the Bhagavad Gita 8.10</note>

This is further supported by the scripture: "There are a hundred and one channels of the heart" <note>original: *shatam chaika hridayasya nadyah* from the Katha Upanishad 6.16; it describes the soul exiting through the crown of the head</note> and so on. One cannot argue that inquiry into the Great Sayings like "Thou Art That" <note>original: *Tat Tvam Asi*</note> is useless if it is meant to produce direct knowledge. The knowledge produced by investigating those statements becomes the means for direct realization specifically through the practice of Yoga.

On this subject, there is a great dialogue between Gauri and Ishvara <note>the goddess Parvati and the god Shiva</note> in the text *The Seed of Yoga* <note>original: *Yoga Bija*</note>; a portion of it is written here:

> ->The Goddess said:<-
> What is the fate of those "knowers" <term>jnanins: those who pursue liberation through intellectual or philosophical study</term> who have died? Tell me, O Lord of Gods, O Ocean of the Nectar of Compassion!
> ->Ishvara said:<-
> At the end of the body's life, the knower receives the fruits of their merits and sins. Whatever those fruits may be, the knower must experience them and be born again. Afterward, through merit, one gains the company of a **Perfected One** <term>Siddha: an accomplished master of Yoga</term>. Then, by the grace of the Siddha, one becomes a Yogi, and not otherwise. Only then is **the wandering cycle of existence** <term>samsara: the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth</term> destroyed; this is the truth spoken by Shiva.
> ->The Goddess said:<-
> The knowers always say that liberation comes from knowledge alone. Why is it not so without the perfection of Yoga? Can Yoga alone grant liberation?
> ->Ishvara said:<-
> It is true that liberation comes through knowledge; their statement is not wrong. But everyone says victory is won by the sword—yet how can one achieve victory without valor and the act of fighting? Likewise, knowledge devoid of Yoga does not lead to liberation.

And so on. Now, one might ask: "If we hear that figures like King Janaka attained liberation and unimpeded knowledge even without Yoga, then how can you say that such knowledge and liberation only come from Yoga?" 

It is replied: We hear in the **Ancient Histories** <term>Puranas: a genre of Indian literature covering myths, history, and philosophy</term> that their attainment of knowledge was due to the imprints <note>original: *samskaras*</note> of Yoga practice performed in previous lives. For example:

> "Just as the Brahmin Jaigishavya, and Asita and others; the warrior-kings like Janaka and others; and the merchants like Tuladhara and others; all attained the highest perfection through their own Yoga practiced in previous lives. The seven 'hunters' like Dharma-vyadha, though born as servants, and women like Maitreyi, Sulabha, Sharngi, and the ascetic Shandili—these and many others, even those born into low stations, attained the highest state of devotion to knowledge through their own Yoga practiced in previous lives."

Furthermore, depending on the degree of merit from Yoga practice performed in previous lives, some have attained the status of the Creator (Brahma), or the sons of Brahma, or celestial sages, or royal sages, or silent monks, or great devotees. In those cases, they may possess the direct experience of the Self even without instruction in this life. Thus, we hear in the Puranas that beings like Hiranyagarbha, Vishnu, Narada, Sanatkumara, Vamadeva, and Shuka were "perfected from birth" <note>original: *janma-siddha*</note>. As for the scriptural statements that only a Brahmin is eligible for liberation, that refers specifically to those who are Yogis. This is stated in the *Garuda Purana*:

> "For those men who do not possess the practice of Yoga carried over from another birth, for the sake of attaining Yoga, they..."

<vocab>Yoga, Jnana (Knowledge), Moksha (Liberation), Tika (Commentary), Bhagavad Gita, Yoga Bija, Gauri-Ishvara-Samvada (Dialogue of Shiva and Parvati), Janaka and others, Purana, Garuda Purana, Janma-siddha (Perfected from birth)</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that philosophical knowledge (Jnana) alone is insufficient for liberation without the practical power of Yoga, citing a dialogue between Shiva and Parvati. It explains that famous figures who seemed to achieve liberation through knowledge alone were actually benefiting from Yoga practices mastered in their previous lives.</summary>
