<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>In the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

### [Root Verses]

**Verse 16: Having known the true piercing of the Sushumna <term>Sushumna: the central energy channel of the subtle body</term> and having directed the breath into that middle path, one should always remain in a suitable place and restrain the breath in the Brahmarandhra <term>Brahmarandhra: the 'opening of Brahman' located at the crown of the head</term>.**

**Verse 17: The Sun and the Moon <note>referring to the right and left nostrils/energy channels, Ida and Pingala</note> create the time consisting of day and night. The Sushumna is the consumer of time; this is declared to be a great secret.**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

The sequence of Shudra, Vaishya, and others is described as follows: "By being a woman, one reaches the state of a Shudra; from there, one reaches the state of a Vaishya; then one becomes a Kshatriya, and then a Brahmin <term>Brahmin: the priestly or scholarly class</term> who is initially without grace. Then one becomes a learned scholar, then a performer of sacrifices, and then a renouncer of actions. After that, one attains the state of a Knower, and finally, the Yogi attains liberation in due order." <note>original: *shudra-vaishyad-ikramah...* citing a traditional progression of soul-evolution through different social and spiritual stages</note> This means that by becoming a Yogi through this sequence of Shudra, Vaishya, etc., one attains liberation. 

In this way, since the scriptures declare that everyone has the right to Yoga, it is established that everyone is liberated through the knowledge of reality produced by Yoga. Even for a Yogi who has "fallen" <note>original: *yogabhrashta*; one who dies before completing the path</note>, they do not return to the lower sequence of Shudra and others. As the Lord stated: "The one fallen from Yoga is born in the house of the pure and prosperous, or else in the family of Yogis themselves." <note>original: *shuchinam shrimatam gehe...* from the Bhagavad Gita 6.41-42</note> This is sufficient explanation. || 15 ||

It has been stated that liberation is not achieved without the dissolution <term>laya: the process of absorption or dissolution of the individual consciousness into the universal</term> of both the breath and the mind. Here, the author explains the method of that dissolution, noting that by the dissolution of the breath, the dissolution of the mind is also achieved. 

**"Having known"** etc.: Always, in a "suitable place"—meaning a place with the characteristics previously described, such as a "good kingdom and a righteous country"—having established a residence; having known from the mouth of a Guru <term>Guru: a spiritual teacher</term> the "true piercing," which is the excellent method of opening the **Sushumna** (the middle channel); and having made the **Vayu** (the breath) "middle-dwelling," meaning moving within that middle channel; one should "restrain it," or hold it firmly, in the **Brahmarandhra** (the space at the top of the head). 

When the breath is restrained in the Brahmarandhra, dissolution occurs. When the breath is dissolved, the mind is also dissolved. As stated in the *Yoga Vasistha*: "When the movement of the breaths reaches exhaustion through practice, the mind attains peace, and only Nirvana <term>Nirvana: ultimate liberation and peace</term> remains." When the breath and mind are dissolved, the internal instrument <note>original: *antahkarana*</note>, aided by the state of meditative absorption <term>Samadhi: a state of intense concentration and union</term>, attains the unhindered direct perception of the Self. At that moment, the person becomes liberated while still living. || 16 ||

The author now explains how the mastery over time occurs upon the dissolution of the breath. **"The Sun and the Moon"** etc.: The Sun and the Moon...

->## || HINDI TRANSLATION ||<-

**"Having known"**: One should reside in a beautiful kingdom, a righteous country, and a beautiful place. Then, having learned from the mouth of a Guru the beautiful method of piercing the **Sushumna** (the middle channel), and having acted so that the life-breath begins to move within that Sushumna, one should then dissolve the breath in the **Brahmarandhra**. As soon as the breath is dissolved, the mind is also dissolved. || 16 ||

Now, it is said that when the breath is dissolved, there is also victory over Time. **"The Sun and the Moon"**...

<summary>This page details the technical process of directing the breath into the Sushumna channel and the Brahmarandhra to achieve mental dissolution (laya) and liberation. It also asserts that Yoga is a universal path accessible to all, regardless of social origin, leading to a state that transcends time and the cycle of rebirth.</summary>

<keywords>Sushumna, Brahmarandhra, Pranalaya (breath dissolution), Hatha Yoga, Moksha (liberation), Samadhi, Kalajaya (victory over time), yoga-bhrashta</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on Verse 17 regarding the conquest of time and introduces Verse 18, which emphasizes the supremacy of the central channel (Sushumna) over the tens of thousands of other energy channels in the human body.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>Fourth Instruction</header>

**Root Verse: There are seventy-two thousand openings for the channels in this cage [the body]. The Sushumna is the power of Shambhu <term>Shambhavi Shakti: the energy associated with Lord Shiva</term>; the rest are indeed useless. || 18 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the Sun and the Moon: [The commentator notes a grammatical rule] "In a compound of two deities..." <note>original: *devatadvandve ca...* referencing Panini’s Sanskrit grammar rules for dual compounds</note> the lengthening of the vowel occurs. "Night and day" refers to the period of a full day. One whose nature is comprised of night and day is "of the nature of night and day." The Sun and the Moon establish this time consisting of night and day. The Sushumna, also called Sarasvati, is the consumer—the devourer or destroyer—of this time created by the Sun and the Moon. This is declared to be a profound secret.

The meaning is as follows: The Sun [the right-channel breath] flows for two and a half *ghatikas* <term>ghatika: a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes; 2.5 ghatikas equals one hour</term>, and the Moon [the left-channel breath] flows for two and a half *ghatikas*. When the Sun flows, it is called "day." When the Moon flows, it is called "night." Within a span of five *ghatikas* [two hours], a complete cycle of "day and night" occurs. Within one worldly day and night [24 hours], there are twelve such cycles of "day and night" for the yogis. The lifespan of living beings is measured according to this reckoning of time. 

When the breath becomes absorbed in the Brahmarandhra <term>Brahmarandhra: the spiritual 'aperture of Brahman' at the crown of the head</term> through the path of the Sushumna, time—consisting of day and night—ceases to exist. Thus, it is said that "Sushumna is the consumer of time." As long as the breath remains absorbed in the Brahmarandhra, the yogi's lifespan increases. A yogi who has practiced Samadhi <term>Samadhi: a state of deep meditative absorption</term> for a long time can perceive the moment of death in advance, draw the breath into the Brahmarandhra to ward off time, and leave the body at his own will. || 17 ||

Regarding the verse "Seventy-two...": In the "cage"—the body, which is bound by bones and ribs like a cage—there are seventy-two thousand...

->## || VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

The Sun and the Moon create the time consisting of day and night. The Sushumna, which is Sarasvati, is the destroyer of that time created by the Sun and Moon. This is spoken of as a secret. The meaning is this: The Sun-breath flows for two and a half *ghatikas*, and the Moon-breath flows for two and a half *ghatikas*. When the Sun flows, it is called day; when the Moon flows, it is called night. A "day and night" cycle consists of five *ghatikas*. In the time a worldly person experiences one day and night, the yogi experiences twelve such cycles. The lifespan of beings is measured by this time. 

When the breath is absorbed in the Brahmarandhra via the Sushumna path, the time of day and night is absent. For this reason, the Sushumna is called the destroyer of time. As long as the breath is absorbed in the Brahmarandhra, the yogi's life increases. A yogi who practices Samadhi for a long duration, knowing the time of death beforehand, brings the breath into the Brahmarandhra and wards off time, then leaves the body by his own will. || 17 ||

Regarding "Seventy-two...": In the body, which is bound by nerves/veins like a cage, there are seventy-two thousand channels...

<summary>This page explains the yogic measurement of time through breath cycles and describes the Sushumna channel as the "consumer of time," allowing a yogi to transcend mortality and achieve voluntary death. It also introduces the anatomical concept of the body containing 72,000 nadis (energy channels).</summary>
<keywords>Sushumna, Nadi, Brahmarandhra, Time (Kala), Shambhavi Shakti, Samadhi, Ghatika, Breath control</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 72,000 nadis (energy channels) from the previous page, specifically explaining why the central channel (Sushumna) is considered the only one of true importance for the advanced yogi.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>On the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

**Root Text: Because the breath, once mastered, awakens the Kundalini along with the fire and enters the central channel without obstruction. || 19 ||**

**When the breath flows through the central channel, the state of "mind-beyond-mind" <term>Manonmani: a state of samadhi where the mind transcends ordinary thought</term> is surely attained. Otherwise, all other practices are merely an exertion for yogis. || 20 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

"Seventy-two thousand" refers to the total number of channels or pathways for the entry of breath. The *Sushumna* <note>the central energy channel of the spine</note> is the "Power of Shambhu" <term>Shambhavi Shakti: the energy associated with Lord Shiva</term>. *Sham* means "bliss" or "well-being," which comes to devotees from *Shambhu* (the Lord); therefore, this power is called *Shambhavi*. It is so named because it leads one to attain *Shambhu* through meditation, or because it causes the manifestation of *Shambhu*. Alternatively, *Shambhu* is the Self (*Atman*) which resides in the form of bliss; this power is *Shambhavi* because it is the place where consciousness is expressed and the means for the direct realization of the Self through meditation. The remaining channels, such as the *Ida* <note>the left/lunar channel</note> and *Pingala* <note>the right/solar channel</note>, are "useless"—meaning they are devoid of purpose compared to the central goal. || 18 ||

**Regarding the breath:** Because the breath has been mastered through practice, it awakens the *Kundalini* power along with the digestive fire <term>jatharagni: the internal fire used in yoga to heat the life-force</term>. Then, without obstruction or restraint, it enters the *Sushumna*, also called the *Sarasvati* <note>the central channel is sometimes named after the goddess of wisdom and flow</note>. The meaning is that practice must be undertaken specifically for the purpose of the breath entering the *Sushumna*. || 19 ||

**Regarding the central channel:** When the life-force (*prana*) flows through the *Sushumna*, the state of *Manonmani*—the "mind-beyond-mind" condition—is certainly achieved. Otherwise, if the breath does not flow through the central channel, all other various practices of the yogis are only for the sake of effort, meaning they result in nothing but exhaustion. || 20 ||

->## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

The pathways are the entry routes for the breath. The *Sushumna* is the power of *Shambhu*. Because bliss (*Sham*) comes from *Shambhu*, the Lord, this power is called *Shambhavi*. Through meditation, it brings one to *Shambhu* or makes Him manifest. Or, "Shambhu" is the name of the Self that exists as the nature of bliss; this power realizes the Self through meditation on consciousness. Thus, it is called *Shambhavi*. Other channels like the *Ida* and *Pingala* are now without further use, as their purpose has been fulfilled. || 18 ||

**Regarding the breath:** Through the breath that has been practiced, the *Kundalini* is awakened by the digestive fire. This breath, which cannot be stopped by any other force, enters the *Sarasvati* (the *Sushumna*). One must practice for the purpose of making the breath enter the *Sushumna*. || 19 ||

**Regarding the central channel:** When the life-breath begins to move in the *Sushumna*, then the state of *Manonmani*...

<summary>This page explains that the goal of yogic breathing is to awaken the Kundalini and direct the breath into the central channel (Sushumna). Once the breath flows there, the yogi achieves the state of Manonmani (higher consciousness), making all other physical practices redundant.</summary>
<keywords>Sushumna, Kundalini, Shambhavi Shakti, Manonmani, Prana, Jatharagni, Nadi, Shambhu</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between the breath and the state of the mind, specifically focusing on how the central channel (Sushumna) serves as the path to Samadhi.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>The Fourth Instruction</header>

**Root Text: By whatever the breath is bound, by that the mind is also bound. By whatever the mind is bound, by that the breath is also bound. || 21 ||**

**The mind has two causes: latent tendencies and the breath. When even one of these is destroyed, both of them are destroyed. || 22 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the verse "The breath": By whichever practice a yogi binds the breath <note>original: *pavana-pranavayu*</note>—meaning it is made steady or controlled—by that same practice, the mind is bound. By whatever means the mind is bound, the breath is also bound. The meaning is that when either the mind or the breath is restrained, both become restrained. || 21 ||

Regarding the verse "The two causes of the mind": There are two causes or reasons for the activity of the mind. What are they? He says they are: **latent tendencies** <term>Vasana: the deep-seated impressions or psychological imprints that drive our desires and habits</term> and the **breath** <term>Samirana: a technical term for the life-force or Prana</term>. When one of these—either the tendencies or the breath—is destroyed (meaning it is diminished or quieted), then both of them are destroyed.

The intention is this: when the latent tendencies are diminished, the breath and the mind also become diminished. When the breath is diminished, the mind and the tendencies become diminished. When the mind is diminished, the breath and the tendencies become diminished. This is stated in the *Vasistha* <note>The *Yoga Vasistha*, an ancient philosophical text known for its teachings on the nature of reality and the mind</note>: "O Rama, there are two seeds of the mind: the movement of the breath and the latent tendencies. When one of these is destroyed, both are quickly destroyed."

The same text explains the inverse: "As long as the mind is not dissolved, the tendencies do not perish. As long as the tendencies have not perished, the mind does not find peace. As long as higher wisdom <note>original: *vijnana*</note> is not attained, the doubts of the mind do not cease. As long as the mind is not quieted, there is no realization of the Truth. Without the destruction of tendencies, how can the knowledge of Truth arise? As long as the Truth is not attained, the tendencies are not destroyed. Knowledge of the Truth, the dissolution of the mind, and the destruction of tendencies—these three are mutually the cause of each other, and are therefore very difficult to achieve. Unless these three are practiced together, repeatedly and over time, the realization of Truth will not occur, even if one seeks refuge in these practices." || 22 ||

->## || VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ||<-

This is true. Otherwise, if the breath does not flow through the central channel <term>Sushumna</term>, then all other practices besides this are merely a source of exhaustion for the yogi. || 20 ||

Regarding the verse "The breath": By whatever means the yogi binds the breath, the mind is bound. And by whatever the mind is bound, the breath is bound. || 21 ||

Regarding the verse "The two causes of the mind": There are two causes for the mind's activity. Which ones? One is the latent tendencies and the other is the breath. If even one of these two is diminished, then both reach destruction. The sense is: if the destruction of tendencies occurs, then both breath and mind are diminished. If the breath is diminished, the mind and tendencies both diminish. And when the mind is diminished, then the breath and tendencies are both diminished. || 22 ||

<summary>This page explains the deep physiological and psychological link between the breath and the mind, asserting that controlling one inevitably leads to the control of the other. It introduces "latent tendencies" (vasanas) as the root of mental activity and emphasizes that spiritual liberation requires the simultaneous silencing of breath, thought, and desire.</summary>
<keywords>breath control, Prana, mind, Chitta, Vasana, Yoga Vasistha, latent tendencies, self-realization, Tattva-jnana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the physiological and psychological connection between the breath and the mind, transitioning from the methods of restraint to the metaphorical description of their unified nature and its role in achieving liberation.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>On the *Hatha Yoga Pradipika*</header>

**Root Text: Wherever the mind is dissolved, the breath is dissolved there. Wherever the breath is dissolved, the mind is dissolved there. || 23 ||**

**Like milk and water joined together, the mind and the breath are truly equal in their activities. Where the breath moves, there the mind acts; where the mind acts, there the breath moves. || 24 ||**

**Among them, by the destruction of one, the other is destroyed; from the activity of one, the other acts. When these two are not destroyed, the senses continue their activity; when both are destroyed, the state of liberation is attained. || 25 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the verse "the mind": In whichever support <note>original: *adhara*—referring to the internal focal points of meditation</note> the mind is dissolved, the breath <note>original: *pavana*</note> is dissolved in that same support. This is the logical connection. || 23 ||

Regarding the verse "like milk and water": Like milk and water <note>original: *dugdhambu*</note>, which are perfectly blended together, both the mind and the breath <note>original: *manasamarutau*—a compound of mind (*manas*) and wind/breath (*maruta*)</note> are equal in their activity. That is to say, their movement or tendency is the same. He explains this equality of action: "Where the breath moves," etc. In whichever **chakra** <term>Chakra: an energy center or "wheel" within the subtle body</term> the breath acts, the activity of the mind occurs in that same chakra. In whichever chakra the mind acts, the activity of the breath occurs in that same chakra. 

This is also stated in the *Vasistha* <note>The *Yoga Vasistha*</note>: "The life-breath and the consciousness of living beings are eternally inseparable. Like the fragrance is mixed with the flower, or like oil stays within the sesame seed, these two are joined. By their destruction, they perform the highest task called **liberation** <term>Moksha: the final release from the cycle of rebirth and suffering</term>." || 24 ||

Regarding the verse "among them": Between those two, the mind and the breath, by the destruction of one—either the mind or the breath...

->## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

Regarding "the mind": In whichever support the mind becomes absorbed, in that same **chakra** the breath also becomes absorbed. And in whatever the breath is absorbed, the mind is absorbed there as well. || 23 ||

Regarding "like milk and water": Just as water and milk are mixed, the mind and the life-breath are two things whose tendencies become equal. Now their equal tendency is explained: in whichever chakra the air moves, the mind's activity occurs there. And in whichever chakra the mind moves, the breath's activity occurs in that chakra. || 24 ||

Regarding "among them": Out of these two—the mind and the breath—by the dissolution of one, the other also becomes dissolved. Similarly, if there is activity in either the mind or the breath, there will be activity in the other. If these two, the mind and the breath, are not dissolved, then the group of **senses** <term>Indriya: the five faculties of perception and five of action</term> continues to move toward their respective objects. But if the mind and the life-breath are both thoroughly dissolved, then the attainment of the state of liberation occurs...

<summary>This page explains the inseparable bond between the mind and the breath, using the metaphor of milk and water to show that when one is controlled or dissolved, the other follows. It concludes that the cessation of both is necessary to quiet the senses and achieve the final goal of liberation (Moksha).</summary>

<keywords>Mind-Breath Connection, Prana, Chakra, Yoga Vasistha, Liberation, Senses, Hatha Yoga, Samadhi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical discussion on the relationship between the breath and the mind, specifically introducing the alchemical analogy of mercury (rasa) to describe the "binding" of the vital forces.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>
<sig>19</sig>

**Root Text: Both mercury and the mind are fickle by nature. If mercury is bound and the mind is bound, what cannot be accomplished on this earth? || 26 ||**

**When "swooned," it removes diseases; when "dead," it grants life itself. When "bound," both mercury and the breath, O Parvati, bestow the power of flight! || 27 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

Until the destruction or dissolution of the other—meaning the breath or the mind—takes place, they remain linked. From the activity of one—the mind or the breath—the activity of the other occurs. While both the mind and the breath are undestroyed and not yet dissolved, the activity of the senses <note>original: *indriya-vritti*</note> continues, as the collection of senses remains engaged with their respective objects. When both are destroyed and dissolved, the attainment of the state of liberation <term>Moksha: the state of spiritual liberation or release from the cycle of rebirth</term> is accomplished. This is because, upon their dissolution, the individual resides in their true, essential nature. 

"In that regard, the destruction of the breath is to be accomplished through the practice of the six-limbed yoga <note>original: *shadanga-yoga*—a system of yoga focusing on posture, breath, withdrawal, concentration, meditation, and absorption</note> and similar methods. However, the destruction of the mind is easily accomplished in a mere moment through the grace of the Guru." This verse from the *Yogabija* <note>A classical text on Hatha Yoga</note> follows the previous root verse. || 25 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with "Mercury": Mercury <term>Rasa: literally 'essence,' referring here to quicksilver or mercury, used in Tantic alchemy as a physical parallel to the mind</term> and the mind <note>original: *manasa*</note> are fickle or unstable by their very nature. If mercury is "bound" <note>original: *baddha*—in alchemy, this refers to fixing mercury so it does not evaporate; in yoga, it refers to restraining the mind</note>, the mind becomes bound. Therefore, what cannot be accomplished on the surface of the earth? Everything becomes possible. || 26 ||

He explains this further in the verse "When swooned": Mercury is called "swooned" <note>original: *murchita*</note> when its natural fickleness is removed through the application of specific medicinal herbs. Similarly, the breath is said to be "swooned" when, at the conclusion of retention <term>Kumbhaka: the practice of holding the breath</term>, the practitioner refrains from exhalation <term>Rechaka: the act of breathing out</term>. 

"O Parvati!" is an address by Lord Shiva used to make the teaching easy for Parvati to understand. "Swooned" mercury and "swooned" breath <note>original: *prana*</note> take away or destroy diseases. Mercury that has been "killed" <note>original: *mrita*—reduced to a medicinal ash or bhasma</note> or breath that has been dissolved in the **Brahmarandhra** <term>Brahmarandhra: the 'aperture of Brahma' located at the crown of the head</term> grants life "by itself"—meaning by its own inherent power—for a long duration. 

Mercury that is "bound" by specific processes into the form of a pill, and breath that is "bound" or held through specific concentration <term>Dharana: focused mental cultivation</term> in the space between the eyebrows, bestows the power of flight <term>Khecharata: the yogic power to move through the sky or the ethereal space</term>. || 27 ||

->## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

This means that from the dissolution of both of these, the individual resides in their true nature. || 25 ||

Regarding the verse "Mercury": Mercury and the mind are both fickle by nature. If mercury is bound and the mind is bound, then that living being achieves all success and powers on the surface of the earth. || 26 ||

He says the same: Mercury that has lost its fickleness through the use of herbs is called "swooned." And the breath that one refrains from exhaling at the end of retention is called "swooned." Lord Shiva says: O Parvati! The ash of "swooned" mercury removes diseases. And "swooned" breath, when directed again into the crown of the head...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga, moving from the alchemical metaphors of the previous page (mercury and breath) to the psychological and physiological chain of command that leads to the state of dissolution (Laya) and liberation.</meta>

<page-num>146</page-num>
<header>ON THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

**Root: When the mind is steady, the breath becomes steady; from that, the vital seed <term>Bindu: literally 'drop' or 'point,' referring here to the vital essence or semen, which yogis believe must be preserved and drawn upward</term> becomes steady. From the steadiness of the seed, constant strength and steadiness of the body <note>original: *pinda-sthairyam*—literally 'stability of the mass/body'</note> are produced. || 28 ||**

**The mind is the lord of the senses, and the breath is the lord of the mind. Dissolution <term>Laya: a state of deep meditative absorption where the individual mind dissolves into the universal</term> is the lord of the breath, and that dissolution rests upon the internal sound <term>Nada: the mystic inner resonance heard during advanced meditation</term>. || 29 ||**

**This very state may be called liberation, or perhaps it is not so called in other systems of thought. However, when the mind and breath dissolve, a certain indescribable bliss unfolds. || 30 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

As stated in the *Goraksha Shataka* <note>The "Hundred Verses of Goraksha," an influential early text of the Hatha Yoga tradition</note>: "That which is like a mass of split collyrium <note>A dark, eye-paint pigment, suggesting a deep blue-black color</note> situated in the middle of the eyebrows is the element of air, associated with the letter 'Pa' and the deity Ishvara. One should hold the mind and the breath dissolved there for five *ghatikas* <note>A traditional unit of time; five *ghatikas* is approximately two hours</note>; for the self-controlled yogi, this practice of concentrating on the air grants the power of movement through the sky." || 27 ||

Regarding the verse **When the mind is steady**: When steadiness of the mind is achieved, the breath <note>original: *prana*</note> becomes steady. Then, through the steadiness of the breath, the vital seed—the semen—becomes steady. From the steadiness of the seed, strength and the steadiness of the physical body are always produced. || 28 ||

Regarding the verse **The mind is the lord**: The mind, or the internal faculty, is the "lord" or the driver of the senses, such as hearing and the rest. The "lord" of the mind is the breath. The "lord" of the breath is dissolution—the melting away of the mind. That dissolution, in turn, is dependent on the internal sound; this means the mind becomes dissolved within the mystic sound. || 29 ||

Regarding the verse **This very state**: This very dissolution of the consciousness is what is meant by the term "liberation" <note>original: *moksha*</note>. In other systems or doctrines, this may or may not be the case. Because the dissolution of the consciousness also exists in deep, dreamless sleep <term>Sushupti: the state of deep sleep where the 'I' is temporarily absent</term>, when both the mind and the breath are dissolved, a certain [indescribable bliss arises].

->## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

[A yogi] who is dissolved [in meditation] can, by his own power, restore life to a dead person for a long time. Furthermore, by holding mercury that has been bound into the form of a small pill <note>original: *gutika*</note> in the mouth, one gains the power of movement through the sky. And by performing concentration, holding the breath bound in the middle of the eyebrows, one achieves the power of moving through the heavens. || 27 ||

Regarding the verse **When the mind is steady**: If the mind becomes still, the breath becomes still. And when the breath is stilled, the vital seed becomes steady. By the seed becoming steady, the strength of the body is forever made firm. || 28 ||

Regarding the verse **The mind is the lord**: The internal faculty is the master of the senses, such as the ears and so on; that is, it is what sets them in motion. The master of the mind is the breath. The master of the breath is dissolution, and this dissolution takes refuge in the internal sound. That is to say, the mind becomes absorbed into the sound. || 29 ||

Regarding the verse **This very state**: The dissolution of the consciousness into the sound is itself liberation. In other systems, they say that in deep sleep...

<summary>This page explains the hierarchical relationship between the senses, mind, breath, and inner sound, leading to a state of meditative dissolution (Laya). It emphasizes that physical and spiritual stability begins with the mind and results in the preservation of vital essence and the attainment of bliss.</summary>
<keywords>Mind-breath connection, Bindu (vital seed), Laya (dissolution), Nada (inner sound), Moksha (liberation), Goraksha Shataka, Breath control</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 146 regarding the state of dissolution (Laya). The previous text established that when the mind and breath are stilled, a state of bliss emerges which characterizes liberation.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root: That dissolution <term>Laya: the state where the individual mind merges into the universal consciousness</term> of the yogis triumphs, in which inhalation and exhalation have vanished, the grasping of sensory objects is destroyed, and which is motionless and free from mental modifications. || 31 ||**

**Root: When all mental constructs <term>Sankalpa: the formative faculty of the mind that creates desires and intentions</term> are cut off and every movement has ceased, a certain extraordinary dissolution arises—knowable only by one’s own experience—which is beyond the reach of speech. || 32 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

An indescribable bliss proceeds and becomes fully manifest. The sense is that when this inexpressible bliss appears, the happiness of "liberation while living" <term>Jivanmukti: the state of being spiritually liberated while still inhabiting a physical body</term> surely occurs. || 30 <note>The commentator begins by finishing the explanation of the previous verse.</note> ||

Regarding the verse beginning with **"vanished"** <note>original: *pranasta*</note>... Inhalation <note>original: *shvasa*</note> and exhalation <note>original: *nishvasa*</note> are the two types of breath. That state in which these two are "vanished" or dissolved is the subject. Inhalation is the drawing of external air into the body; exhalation is the release of internal air to the outside. "Destroyed" <note>original: *pradhvasta*</note> means the forceful destruction or disappearance of the "grasping of sensory objects" such as sound and the other senses. That in which bodily movement <note>original: *kayakriya*</note> has departed is "motionless." That in which the activity of the internal organ <note>the mind</note> has departed is "free from modifications." Such is the dissolution of the yogis; the mental state which has taken the form of the object of meditation <note>original: *dhyeyakara-vritti*</note> triumphs, existing in supreme excellence. || 31 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with **"cut off"** <note>original: *ucchinna*</note>... That in which all "mental constructs"—the transformations of the mind—are cut off or destroyed. That in which "every movement" has ceased without any remainder. "Knowable only by oneself" <note>original: *svavagamya*</note> means it can only be understood through one's own realization. It is "beyond the reach of speech," meaning it is not an object that words can describe. A "certain extraordinary" <note>original: *ko'pi vilakshana*</note> dissolution is produced or manifested for the yogis. || 32 ||

->## || VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ||<-

In this state, the dissolution of the mind is itself liberation. Some say this is not liberation, but that is answered here: it is indeed so. Why? Because when the mind and the vital breath are dissolved, then such a bliss proceeds that it cannot be described to anyone. When this indescribable bliss is manifested, then the happiness of being liberated while living occurs. There is no doubt in this. || 30 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with **"vanished"**... That in which inhalation and exhalation have been removed. Drawing the outside air inside is inhalation, and expelling the internal air outside is exhalation. Also, the grasping of sensory objects is removed. That in which the actions of the body are removed and which is free from modifications—such a dissolution of the yogis, for the sake of attaining the Absolute <term>Brahman: the ultimate, infinite reality in Hindu philosophy</term>, prevails through the total excellence of the mental state. || 31 ||

Regarding the verse beginning with **"cut off"**... That in which all mental constructs are destroyed, in which all movement has ceased, which can only be known by one's own self, and which cannot be expressed through speech—such an extraordinary dissolution is manifested to the yogis. || 32 ||

<summary>This page describes the state of Laya (dissolution), characterized by the cessation of breath, sensory perception, and mental activity, leading to an indescribable bliss and liberation while living.</summary>
<keywords>Laya, Jivanmukti, Sankalpa, Breath Control, Inhalation, Exhalation, Brahman, Mental Stilling</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 146 regarding the state of dissolution (Laya). The previous section defined Laya as the cessation of breath and mental constructs, leading to a state of bliss beyond speech. This page further defines the nature of Laya and contrasts the secrecy of yogic practice with public scripture.</meta>

### ON THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

**Root: Wherever the gaze is fixed, dissolution <term>Laya: the absorption of the mind into a state of pure consciousness</term> occurs; therein lies the eternal [power] of the elements and the senses. That is the power of living beings; when those two unperceivable things have dissolved, [the state of Laya is reached]. || 33 ||**

**Root: "Dissolution, dissolution," they say—but what is the defining characteristic of dissolution? Dissolution is the forgetting of sensory objects <note>original: *vishaya-vismriti*</note> because latent impressions <term>Vasana: the subtle subconscious inclinations or "perfumes" left by past actions that drive current desires</term> do not rise again. || 34 ||**

**Root: The Vedas, the technical scriptures <term>Shastras: the six traditional systems of Indian philosophy</term>, and the ancient histories <term>Puranas: the eighteen major collections of Hindu mythology and lore</term> are like common prostitutes <note>original: *ganika*</note>. Only this Shambhavi Mudra <term>Shambhavi Mudra: a highly regarded yogic seal where the eyes are fixed on an external point or the space between the eyebrows while the mind remains focused internally</term> is kept secret, like a high-born wife <note>original: *kulavadhu*</note>. || 35 ||**

->## || COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the verse **"Wherever the gaze"**... In whatever object—namely, Brahman <note>The ultimate, infinite reality in Hindu philosophy</note>—the gaze (the movement of the internal organ) is fixed, dissolution occurs right there. The "elements" are earth and the others; the "senses" are hearing and the others. These are "eternal" or perpetual because, according to the doctrine of the pre-existing effect <term>Satkaryavada: the theory that an effect already exists within its cause before it manifests</term>, the entire collection of effects exists within primordial ignorance <note>original: *avidya*</note>. This "power" of living beings (ignorance) and "knowledge" <note>original: *vidya*</note>—these two unperceivable things—dissolve into Brahman for the yogis. This is the implied meaning. || 33 ||

Regarding the verse **"Dissolution"**... Many people speak of "dissolution, dissolution." Through a question, the author explains the nature and true form of dissolution. Because latent impressions <note>original: *vasana*</note> no longer arise, there is a "forgetting of sensory objects." This means the forgetting of sounds and other sensations, or the forgetting of the object of meditation itself. This is the meaning of the word "dissolution." || 34 ||

Regarding the verse **"The Vedas"**... The four Vedas, the six Shastras, and the eighteen Puranas are like "common prostitutes" <note>This metaphor emphasizes that book-learning is "public" and accessible to anyone, whereas true spiritual experience is intimate and protected.</note>. This is because they are frequented by many men (seekers). Only the one Shambhavi Mudra is kept secret, like a "high-born wife" or a lady of the house. This is because it is only accessible to a "distinguished man" (the advanced practitioner). || 35 ||

->## || VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

Regarding the verse **"Wherever the gaze"**... When the movement of the internal organ <note>the mind</note> is in Brahman, it dissolves into that Brahman. The five great elements and the ten senses constantly reside in primordial ignorance. This ignorance and the power of the living being (knowledge)—both of these dissolve into Brahman for the yogi. || 33 ||

Regarding the verse **"Dissolution"**... Many people call this "dissolution," and here is the nature of that dissolution: when latent impressions no longer rise, there is a forgetting of sensory objects like sound and others. This is called dissolution. || 34 ||

Regarding the verse **"The Vedas"**... The four Vedas, the six Shastras, and the eighteen Puranas are like prostitutes, because they are obtained by many men. Therefore, the Shambhavi Mudra is the only one that is worthy of being kept hidden, like a wife of a noble family. || 35 ||

<summary>This page defines Laya (dissolution) as the state where subconscious impressions (vasanas) cease to trigger thoughts of sensory objects. It emphasizes the esoteric nature of the Shambhavi Mudra, contrasting its required secrecy with the public nature of traditional scriptures.</summary>
<keywords>Laya, Shambhavi Mudra, Vasana, Brahman, Satkaryavada, Avidya, Vidya, esoteric practice versus scripture</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Shambhavi Mudra, transitioning from its comparison to a "high-born wife" on the previous page to its technical definition and the state of consciousness it produces. It focuses on the internal and external mechanics of the practice.</meta>

### FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root: The internal focus, the external gaze, free from the closing and opening of the eyelids—this is that Shambhavi Mudra, kept secret in the Vedas and technical scriptures. || 36 ||**

**Root: When the yogi remains with the mind and breath dissolved in the internal focus, the gaze and pupils unmoving, looking outward and downward while yet not seeing—verily, this is Shambhavi Mudra. Obtained through the grace of the Guru, that supreme Shambhavi reality <note>The state of consciousness associated with Lord Shiva</note> shines forth, which is distinct from both the void <term>Shunya: emptiness or the absence of mental constructs</term> and the non-void <term>Ashunya: the world of names and forms</term>. || 37 ||**

->## || SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ||<-

Desiring to explain the seal <term>Mudra: a physical or mental "seal" used to channel energy and focus the mind</term> which serves as a means for the dissolution of the breath by way of the dissolution of the mind, [the author] introduces the Shambhavi Mudra with the words: **"The internal focus..."** Internal target refers to the movement of the internal organ <term>Antahkarana: the mind's inner faculties</term> directed toward a preferred energy center <term>Chakra: focal points of the subtle body</term> located between the root center <note>original: *Adhara*</note> and the crown of the head <note>original: *Brahmarandhra*</note>. The **external gaze** refers to the connection of the eyes with the space outside the body. What kind of gaze? It is **free from blinking and opening**—blinking being the meeting of the eyelids and opening being their separation. When the mind enters into the form of the object of meditation, the gaze naturally becomes free from blinking and opening. This aforementioned seal is called **Shambhavi** because it belongs to Shambhu <note>A name for the god Shiva</note>, is dear to Shiva, or causes the manifestation of Shiva. What is its status? It is **kept secret in the Vedas** (such as the Rig Veda and others) and the **technical scriptures** (such as Samkhya, Patanjali’s Yoga, and others). || 36 ||

[The author] demonstrates the practice of Shambhavi Mudra with the words: **"The internal focus..."** When, in a particular state, the mind and breath are dissolved in the internal target—such as the heart center <note>original: *Anahata*</note>—be it the form of a deity or the absolute reality <note>original: *tattvam-asi*: "Thou art That"</note> which marks the oneness of the individual and the divine...

->## || VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ||<-

**The internal focus...** The internal target means the movement of the mind toward any desired chakra from the base <note>original: *Adhara*</note> to the crown of the head. When the gaze outside the body is free from the opening and closing of the eyelids, that is Shambhavi Mudra. What is it like? It is secret in the Vedas, beginning with the Rig Veda, and in the technical scriptures like Samkhya and Patanjali’s Yoga. || 36 ||

**The internal focus...** When the mind and breath are dissolved in the internal target—such as the heart center <note>original: *Anahata*</note>—whether it be a deity with form or the absolute reality, such a yogi remains steady. Even if the yogi, with this internal gaze, looks downward or upward outside the body, they do not perceive external sensory objects. This is Shambhavi Mudra, and it is obtained through the grace of the Guru. Then, the luminous state and the actual reality of Shambhavi come into one’s perception. || 37 ||

<summary>This page defines Shambhavi Mudra as a simultaneous internal focus on the chakras and a steady, unblinking external gaze. It emphasizes that this practice leads to a state of consciousness beyond ordinary perception, achievable only through a teacher's guidance.</summary>

<keywords>Shambhavi Mudra, internal focus, external gaze, chakras, Shiva, Guru's grace, void, non-void, yoga practice</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the Shambhavi Mudra, contrasting it with the Khechari Mudra. It concludes the commentary on the previous verse regarding the "neither void nor non-void" state of consciousness and introduces the Unmani Mudra, focusing on the bliss arising from the dissolution of the mind.</meta>

# IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

## **Root: Through the difference of states and locations of the illustrious Shambhavi and Khechari [mudras], the bliss of the mind’s dissolution arises in the Void, which consists of the happiness of pure consciousness. || 38 ||**

## **Fixing the pupils on the Light, one should slightly raise the eyebrows...**

->## || SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ||<-

The yogi whose mind and breath <note>original: *manomarutau*</note> are dissolved in Brahman—the reality signified by the Great Saying "I am Brahman"—exists in such a state. With an unmoving gaze, where the pupils are steady and fixed, he remains looking at the space outside the body. Even though he connects his eyes to the outside, he "does not see," for he does not perceive external objects. The word "verily" <note>original: *khalu*</note> is a rhetorical ornament. This is the described Shambhavi Mudra. A Mudra <term>Mudra: literally a "seal"; a practice that seals the energy and "seals away" suffering</term> is so named because it "seals away" afflictions. If this is obtained through the loving grace and favor of the Guru—the guide—then that Shambhavi state <note>original: *padam*</note> shines forth. This state, reached by yogis, is the very nature of the Self. It is distinct from both the void <term>Shunya: emptiness or absence of thought</term> and the non-void; it is distinct from the void because the mental activity directed toward the object of meditation remains present, yet it is also distinct from the non-void because there is no perception of even that activity. This is the actual reality. 

As it is said: "Seeing the internal target with an unswerving mind, the self-controlled one joyfully gazes without opening or closing the eyes—this is the Shambhavi Mudra. Hidden by Girisha <note>A name for Lord Shiva, "Lord of the Mountains"</note>, the knower of the Tantras, for those seeking the essential truth of the Tantras, this Mudra dissolves the mind of the yogis, grants liberation, and is difficult to obtain." || 1 || "With gaze upward, gaze downward, piercing upward, and the head below, through the practice of the Radha-yantra <note>An advanced, technical yogic posture or internal configuration</note>, one becomes liberated while still alive on this earth." || 2 || [This completes the commentary on Verse 37].

**Through the illustrious Shambhavi** [referring to Verse 38]: From the difference in the **states and locations** of the illustrious Shambhavi Mudra and the Khechari Mudra. In Shambhavi, there is an external focus with the gaze remaining outward; in Khechari, the focus is at the space between the eyebrows. In Shambhavi, the location for meditation is the heart; in Khechari, the location is only the space between the eyebrows. Through these two differences, the **bliss of the mind's dissolution** occurs in the void—that which is void of limitations of space, time, and matter, and void of differences—within the Self, which is **the form of the bliss of consciousness**. The meaning is that Shambhavi and Khechari differ in the practice-based aspects of state and location, but not in the resulting bliss. || 38 ||

**He now describes the Unmani Mudra** <term>Unmani: literally "mind-less" or "beyond mind"; a state of samadhi where mental fluctuations cease</term>: **The pupils**, etc. Regarding the pupils of the two eyes, in the Light, the pupils at the tip of the nose...

->## || VERNACULAR EXPOSITION ||<-

**Through the illustrious Shambhavi**, etc.: Through the difference in states and locations of the Shambhavi Mudra and the Khechari Mudra, the bliss of the mind's dissolution occurs in the Self, which is the form of conscious bliss. || 38 ||

**He describes the Unmani Mudra**: **The pupils**, etc. One should fix the gaze on the tip of the nose, in the Light...

<summary>This page compares the Shambhavi and Khechari Mudras, noting that while they differ in physical focus and location, both lead to the same state of conscious bliss and mental dissolution. It also introduces the Unmani Mudra, characterized by a specific gaze aimed at achieving a state beyond the mind.</summary>
<keywords>Shambhavi Mudra, Khechari Mudra, Unmani Mudra, Chitta-laya (mind dissolution), Brahman, Girisha (Shiva), Radha-yantra, Guru's grace</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Shambhavi Mudra, transitioning into the description of the Unmani state (the mindless or "beyond-mind" state). It builds upon the previous page's discussion of the "neither void nor non-void" reality by explaining how this state is achieved in an instant through focused practice.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root:** Fixing the mind in the previously mentioned yoga, one becomes the creator of the mindless state <term>Unmani: literally "no-mind"; a state of high meditative absorption where the mental faculties are transcended</term> in an instant. || 39 ||

Some are deluded by the net of the Agamas <term>Agama: traditional scriptures, often referring to Tantric texts</term>, and some by the complexities of the Nigamas <term>Nigama: the Vedas</term>. Some are deluded by logic <term>Tarka: reasoning or dialectics</term>; they do not know the Savior <term>Taraka: that which carries one across the ocean of worldly existence</term>. || 40 ||

With half-open eyes, a steady mind, and a gaze fixed on the tip of the nose; bringing the Moon and the Sun <note>The "Moon" and "Sun" represent the Ida and Pingala energy channels, or the cooling and heating breaths.</note> to dissolution through a state of total stillness...

->## || SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the method: having joined the gaze to the brilliant light <note>original: *tejasi*</note>, one should slightly raise the eyebrows upward. The "previously mentioned yoga" refers to the practice described before, characterized by an internal target and an external gaze. By joining the mind <note>original: *antahkarana*</note> to this, the yogi attains the state of Unmani—the mindless state—within a single moment <note>original: *muhurtat*</note>. || 39 ||

He now explains that there is no other means of "crossing over" <note>liberation</note> without this Mindless state. **"Some..."** refers to those who know the scriptures and Tantras. The Agamas are those texts from which meanings "come to" (ascend) the intellect; their "nets" are the means of bondage, like a fisherman's net. People become deluded by the fruits <note>rewards</note> described in them. The implication is that by being attached to those rituals, they remain bound. Some followers of the Vedas are deluded by the "complexities of the Nigamas"—the overwhelming abundance of rewards promised in Vedic texts. Others, like the followers of the Vaisheshika school <note>an ancient school of Indian logic and atomism</note>, are deluded by logic, which consists of specific types of self-concocted reasoning. They do not know the "Savior," the means of crossing over. The meaning is that they do not know that only the aforementioned Mindless state is the true means of salvation. || 40 ||

Regarding **"half-open eyes"**: This refers to one who has the eyes half-opened...

->## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

Fixing the sight in that very light which is the Measure <note>original: *maan tej*</note>, one should raise the eyebrows slightly. When the mind is joined to the previously described yoga of the internal target and external gaze, the yogi reaches the state of Unmani in an instant. || 39 ||

He says that there is no other way to cross over besides Unmani. **"Some..."** refers to those who are learned in the scriptures and Tantras; they are caught in the delusion of the fruits promised by those texts. Those who are knowers of the Vedas remain deluded by the many rewards mentioned in the Vedas. The logicians of the Nyaya school <note>original: *nyaya-shastri*</note> remain deluded by their own reasoning. They do not know that only the previously mentioned Unmani is the way to cross over. || 40 ||

Regarding **"half-open eyes"**: The eyes are half-opened, the mind is steady, and the tip of the nose...

<summary>This page describes the transition from the physical practice of Shambhavi Mudra to the mental state of Unmani (no-mind). It criticizes those who rely solely on scriptural study or logic, identifying the Unmani state as the only true "Taraka" or savior that leads to liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Unmani, Agama, Nigama, Tarka, Taraka, Shambhavi Mudra, liberation, mental dissolution, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Shambhavi Mudra and the transition into the Unmani state. The previous page ended with the description of the yogi's physical posture—eyes half-open and mind steady—as they begin to dissolve the dualities of the "Moon" and "Sun" (the Ida and Pingala energy channels).</meta>

# IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

### Root: That supreme Reality is the form of light, the seed of all, entirely radiant; it is the highest state. It is the ultimate object. What more can be said than this? || 41 ||

### One should not worship the Symbol <term>Linga: literally "sign" or "mark"; in this context, it refers to the internal object of meditation or the pillar of light representing supreme consciousness</term> by day, nor should one worship it by night. One should always worship the Symbol by restraining both day and night. || 42 ||

->।। SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ।।<-

"He of the half-open eyes" means one whose eyes are partially opened. One whose mind is "steady" means his mind is immobile. His "gaze is fixed on the tip of the nose" <note>original: *nasagradattekshanah*</note> means the gaze is directed to the tip of the nose or up to a distance of twelve finger-breadths from the nose.

As Vasistha said: "Up to twelve finger-breadths from the tip of the nose, in the pure sky <note>original: *vimalambare*; refers to the space/ether in front of the face</note>, as the consciousness and the vision become tranquil, the vibration of the breath <note>original: *pranaspandah*</note> is restrained."

The "state of stillness" <note>original: *nispandabhavah*</note> refers to the immobility of the body, the senses, and the mind. By this, he "brings the Moon and the Sun to dissolution"—meaning he causes the cooling and heating breaths to merge into a state of absorption. By the stillness of the body, senses, and mind, he also arrests the movement of the breath. This was stated previously: "where the mind is dissolved," and so on.

The yogi who is endowed with these previously mentioned qualities attains the supreme "state" <note>original: *padam*; also means "step" or "position"</note>, which is the very essence of the Self. This state is the "form of light," meaning it illuminates everything. It is the "seed of all," being the cause of all through the manifestation of space and the other elements. it is "entirely radiant," meaning it shines intensely and is self-luminous. It is "supreme," acting as the witness to the body, senses, and mind. It is "Reality" <note>original: *tattvam*</note>, meaning it is actual and not superimposed. This is the ultimate object, which yogis reach <note>original: *padyate*</note> but which cannot be described as "this" or "that." In the state of Unmani <term>Unmanyavastha: the "mindless" state of Sahaja Samadhi</term>, the yogi remains established in his own true nature. What more needs to be said? This implies there is no higher object to be attained. || 41 ||

He now speaks of the absence of time-restrictions for the realization of Unmani: **"Not by day..."** "By day" refers to when the solar breath <note>original: *surya-sanchare*; the flow of breath through the right nostril, or Pingala</note> is active...

->।। VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ।।<-

With the eyes fixed on the tip of the nose, and through the steady state of the senses and the mind, the yogi causes the Moon and the Sun to dissolve. Like a light, he illuminates the entire universe. He attains the supreme state, the very form of the Self, which is the cause of everything, completely full, and excessively brilliant through its own self-effulgence. This state is what is worthy of being attained by yogis. What more can be said about attaining this? || 41 ||

**"Not by day..."** When the Sun-breath <note>the Pingala channel</note> flows, one should not meditate on the Self. And when the Moon-breath <note>the Ida channel</note> flows, one should also not meditate on the Self. Why? Because when the Moon or Sun breaths are moving, the mind does not remain steady. Therefore, by restraining both the Sun and the Moon, one should meditate on the Self at all times. By restraining these, the Susumna <note>the central energy channel</note>...

<summary>This page describes the pinnacle of Shambhavi Mudra, where the yogi achieves the Unmani (mindless) state by fixing the gaze and breath. It emphasizes that the supreme reality is a self-luminous light and instructs the practitioner to transcend the duality of the solar and lunar breaths to achieve constant meditation.</summary>

<keywords>Unmani, Linga, Shambhavi Mudra, Jyotirupa, Prana, Vasistha, Nasagra, Moon and Sun channels, Samadhi</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for stabilizing the mind through yoga. It transitions from the Shambhavi Mudra described on the previous page to the Khechari Mudra. The commentary continues to emphasize that true worship is internal meditation and explains the physiological process of the breath entering the central channel.</meta>

( 133 )
Fourth Instruction
20

# Now, the Khechari Mudra

**Root:** When the breath <term>Maruta: the vital air or life force</term> which usually resides in the left and right channels <term>Nadi: energy channels, specifically the Ida and Pingala</term> moves in the middle, then the **Khechari Mudra** is established in that place; of this, there is no doubt. || 43 ||

**Root:** In the middle of the **Ida** and **Pingala**, one should swallow the breath into the **Void** <term>Shunya: literally "emptiness"; refers to the etheric space or the central channel</term>. There, **Khechari Mudra** truly resides, again and again. || 44 ||

->।। SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ।।<-

The Self is the cause of everything. As the scripture <note>original: *Shruti*</note> says: "From this very Self, space was born" <note>original: *Etasmadatmana akashah sambhutah*</note>. One should "not worship" or "not meditate" upon it in the traditional sense. Meditation itself is the true worship of the Self. This is stated in the *Vasistha Samhita*: "The Self is the offering of meditation; meditation is its great worship. Without this, the Self is not attained by any other means."

One should not worship or meditate during the "night," which refers to the flow of the breath through the Moon channel <note>original: *Ida*</note>. This is because the mind lacks stability during the active flow of either the Moon or the Sun channels. As it has been said: "When the breath moves, the mind moves." By "restraining both day and night," one stops the flow of the Sun and Moon breaths. <note>The commentator explains a grammatical point here: "Restraining" acts as a gerund implying the removal of dualities.</note> At all times, one should worship or meditate upon the "Symbol" <term>Linga: the internal mark of consciousness</term> which is the Self. When the Sun and Moon are restrained, and the breath enters the **Sushumna** <term>the central energy channel of the spine</term>, the mind becomes steady. As it is said: "When the air resides within the **Sushumna**, stillness of the mind is produced." || 42 ||

Now he describes **Khechari**: When the breath—situated in the left and right channels—moves "in the middle" (meaning the space between the two), then in that specific place, the **Khechari Mudra** becomes steady. <note>The commentator notes a specific Sanskrit verb form *atmanepadam* used here to denote a state of being.</note> There is no doubt regarding this statement. || 43 ||

Regarding the verse "In the middle of **Ida** and **Pingala**": The "Void" is the space between the left and right channels. One should "swallow" the breath there. Swallowing here means the stabilization of the breath within that Void. In that emptiness, the **Khechari Mudra** is established. The phrase "this is the truth" is repeated for emphasis. || 44 ||

->।। VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ।।<-

When the breath is internal, the mind becomes still. || 42 ||

Now the **Khechari Mudra** is described: The air situated in the left and right channels moves to the middle region of the eyebrows; in that very place, the **Khechari Mudra** remains situated. || 43 ||

Between the **Ida** and **Pingala** is the "Sky" (the Void), which swallows the breath. In that sky, the breath becomes still. This is what is meant by "swallowing." In that Void, the **Khechari Mudra** resides. This is the truth. || 44 ||

<summary>This page defines the Khechari Mudra as the state where the breath leaves the side channels and enters the central "Void," leading to mental stillness. It emphasizes that internal meditation is the highest form of worship, surpassing external rituals performed by day or night.</summary>

<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Sushumna, Ida, Pingala, Prana, Fourth Chapter, Vasistha, mental stability, Shunya, Linga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Khechari Mudra, following the previous page's description of positioning the breath in the central channel. It focuses on the physiological and spiritual integration of the "Sun" and "Moon" energy channels and the resulting state of higher consciousness.</meta>

( 154 )
# In the Light on Hatha Yoga

**Root:** In the middle of the Sun and the Moon, in that unattached internal space, sits the one located in the Wheel of Ether <term>Vyoma-chakra: the 'space-center' usually associated with the upper palate or brow</term>; that is known as the **Khechari Mudra**. || 45 ||

**Root:** The stream that arises from the Moon <note>original: *Soma*</note> is truly the Beloved of Shiva. One should fill the peerless, divine Central Channel <term>Sushumna</term> at its posterior opening. || 46 ||

**Root:** When it is also filled from the front, the **Khechari** is surely perfected. Through the practice of **Khechari Mudra**, the state of Mindlessness <term>Unmani: a state of high meditative absorption where the mind transcends thought</term> is born. || 47 ||

->।। SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ।।<-

**On "Between the Sun and Moon":** This refers to the space between the **Ida** and **Pingala** <term>the left and right energy channels</term>. The phrase "unattached internal space" refers to the void or gap there. The term "Wheel of Ether" refers to the collection of openings at the brow-center <note>original: *bhru-madhye*</note> where all the channels converge. As it has been said: "It is joined with five streams." That which is situated there is the **Khechari Mudra**. || 45 ||

**On "From the Moon":** From the Moon <note>symbolizing the cool, lunar energy at the crown</note>, in this **Khechari** practice, a stream of nectar <note>original: *amrita-dhara*</note> arises. This is called the "Beloved of Shiva" because it is dear to the Lord. One should fill the peerless, pure, and divine **Sushumna**—the best of all channels—at its posterior opening. The text implies this is done <gloss>using the tongue</gloss>. || 46 ||

**On "From the front also":** This means the channel should also be filled from the front <gloss>with the vital breath or Prana</gloss>. If this is done, then the **Khechari Mudra** is said to be "certain" or "undoubted." However, if the channel is not filled with breath from the front, and one only blocks the back with the tongue, it produces a state of dullness or delusion <note>original: *mudha-avastha*</note>. In that case, it is not the true **Khechari**. When **Khechari Mudra** is practiced correctly, the state of **Unmani** arises. This means the "Fourth State" <term>Turyavastha: the state beyond waking, dreaming, and deep sleep</term> occurs because the mind enters into the form of the object of meditation. || 47 ||

---

->।। VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ।।<-

**On "Between the Sun and Moon":** Between the Sun and Moon—which are the **Ida** and **Pingala** channels—lies the "Sphere of Space." That which is situated there is called the **Khechari Mudra**. || 45 ||

**On "From the Moon":** The stream of nectar that appears from the Moon during **Khechari** is very dear to Lord Shiva. One should block the **Sushumna**, which is pure and the best of all channels, at the inner opening of the mouth using the tongue. || 46 ||

**On "From the front also":** If one also blocks the **Sushumna** from the outside using the breath <term>Prana</term>, then it is certainly the **Khechari Mudra**. If one does not block the breath from the outside, but only uses the tongue inside, it produces a state of foolishness or stupor. That is not the true **Khechari Mudra**. By practicing **Khechari**, the state of **Unmani** is achieved. Through the absorption of the mind into the object of meditation, the "Fourth State" <term>Turya</term> is attained. || 47 ||

<summary>This page explains the internal mechanics of the Khechari Mudra, emphasizing that it requires both the physical positioning of the tongue and the energetic control of the breath to reach the state of Unmani.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Sushumna, Ida, Pingala, Unmani, Turyavastha, Nectar of the Moon, Breath Control, Yoga Philosophy</keywords>

### FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root:** In the middle of the eyebrows is the **Abode of Shiva**; there, the mind dissolves. That state should be known as the **Fourth** <term>Turiya: the fourth state of consciousness, beyond waking, dreaming, and deep sleep</term>; in that state, Time does not exist. || 48 ||

**Root:** One should practice **Khechari** until one becomes "Yoga-sleeping" <term>Yoga-nidra: a state of conscious deep sleep or profound meditative absorption</term>. For one who has attained **Yogic Sleep**, Time <note>meaning death or the passage of temporal life</note> never exists. || 49 ||

**Root:** Making the mind **supportless** <term>Niralamba: a state where the mind does not lean on any external or internal object of thought</term>, one should not think of anything at all. He remains fixed like a pot in the outer and inner space <note>original: *vyoman*</note>. || 50 ||

---

->॥ SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY ॥<-

**On "Between the brows":** In the middle of the eyebrows, in the interval between the brows, is the **Abode of Shiva**. This refers to the place of Shiva, the Supreme Lord; it is the remaining place of the Self, whose nature is pure bliss. There, in that Shiva, the mind dissolves <note>original: *viliyate*</note>. It becomes like a continuous flow of thought taking the very form of Shiva. This state of the mind's dissolution is the **Fourth State** <note>original: *turyam padam*</note>, so called because it is the fourth beyond the states of waking, dreaming, and deep sleep; this must be understood. In that state, Time—meaning Death—does not exist. Or, it means that because the "Sun" and "Moon" <note>the right and left breath channels</note> are restrained, the Time that causes the depletion of the lifespan does not exist. As it has been said: "The **Sushumna** <term>the central energy channel</term> is the consumer of Time." || 48 ||

**On "One should practice":** One should practice the **Khechari Mudra** for as long as it takes to become "Yoga-sleeping." Yoga is the cessation of all mental fluctuations; when that itself becomes one's "sleep," one is called "Yoga-sleeping." For one who has attained this **Yogic Sleep**, at no time whatsoever does Time/Death exist. || 49 ||

**On "Supportless":** One who makes the mind supportless—devoid of any object to lean on—should not think of anything. This means that while the **Khechari Mudra** is being perfected, one should even abandon the thought-fluctuation of "I am Brahman" <note>the Absolute Reality</note> through the power of supreme detachment. That Yogi, in the outer and inner space <note>meaning the external atmosphere and the internal space of the heart/head</note>, remains fixed like a pot...

---

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

**On "Between the brows":** In the middle of the brow-crease is the place of Lord Shiva. In that Shiva, the mind becomes absorbed. This state of mental dissolution should be known as the **Fourth State**, which is distinct from waking, dreaming, and deep sleep. In that state, Time—which is Death—is not encountered. || 48 ||

**On "One should practice":** One should practice the **Khechari Mudra** until the complete cessation of all mental fluctuations occurs. For the one whose fluctuations have all been stopped, never, at any time, does Time/Death occur. || 49 ||

**On "Supportless":** That Yogi who, with a mind free of any support or dwelling-place, does not think of anything, remains certainly fixed in the outer and inner space like a pot. Just as inside and outside a pot <note>The text likely continues to explain that space is the same inside and outside a jar; the Yogi similarly perceives the non-dual reality of the internal and external void</note>...

<vocab>भ्रुवोर्मध्ये (bhruvormadhye - between the brows), शिवस्थानं (shivasthanam - abode of Shiva), तुर्यपद (turyapada - the fourth state), योगनिद्रा (yoganidra - yogic sleep), खेचरीमुद्रा (khecharimudra - the tongue-lock seal), निरालंब (niralamba - supportless), निसेध (nisedha - cessation/restraint), हठयोग (hathayoga)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the transition from the physical Khechari Mudra to the mental state of Samadhi, where the mind dissolves into the "Abode of Shiva" at the brow-center. It emphasizes reaching the Fourth State (Turiya) and Yogic Sleep, which are said to liberate the practitioner from the effects of Time and Death.</summary>
