( 166 )
### ON THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

**Root:** Just as the external air is absorbed, so too is the internal [air], without a doubt. The breath, together with the mind, attains stability in its own proper place. || 51 ||

**Root:** For the yogi practicing in this way within the "path of the breath" day and night, the breath is consumed through practice, and the mind dissolves in that very same spot. || 52 ||

**Root:** One should drench the body with the nectars, from the soles of the feet to the top of the head.

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

...remains firm. This is certain. Just as a pot in the sky is filled with space both inside and out, so the yogi, through the practice of **Khechari** <term>Khechari Mudra: 'Moving in the sky,' a practice of turning the tongue back into the nasal cavity to seal the energy channels</term> and by abandoning all mental attachments <note>original: *alambana-parityagena*</note>, remains filled with Brahman <term>Brahman: the Ultimate Reality or Universal Consciousness</term>. || 50 ||

**On "External":** The external air—the air outside the body—becomes absorbed during the practice of Khechari. Because it no longer moves inward, the internal air within the body likewise becomes absorbed, as it no longer moves outward. There is no doubt in this matter. "Place" <note>original: *sthanam*</note> refers to the seat where one becomes steady; for the breath, this seat of stability is the **Brahmarandhra** <term>Brahmarandhra: 'The Opening of Brahman,' the spiritual center at the crown of the head</term>. There, the breath <note>original: *pavana*</note> along with the mind <note>original: *citta*</note> attains stillness. || 51 ||

**On "In this way":** In the manner described, within the "path of the breath"—meaning the **Sushumna** <term>Sushumna: the central energy channel of the subtle body that runs along the spine</term>. For the yogi practicing day and night, the breath <note>original: *prana*</note> is "consumed" <note>original: *jiryate*</note>, meaning it is diminished or dissolved into its foundation. In that very foundation where the breath is absorbed, the mind also dissolves. || 52 ||

**On "Nectars":** With the nectars flowing from the "cavity" <note>referring to the lunar center in the head</note>, from the soles of the feet to the top of the head...

->।। VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ।।<-

Just as the outer sky is full, so in **Khechari**, by abandoning all mental supports, the yogi remains filled with Brahman. || 50 ||

**On "External":** Just as the air outside the body is absorbed in **Khechari**—having no tendency to move inward—so too the air within the body is absorbed, having no tendency to move outward. There is no doubt about this. In the **Brahmarandhra**, which is the place for the breath to become steady, the breath and the mind become still together. || 51 ||

**On "In this way":** For the yogi who practices day and night in the **Sushumna** (the path of the breath), the breath is absorbed into its foundation through practice; and in that same place where the breath is absorbed, the mind also dissolves. || 52 ||

**On "Nectars":** It is excellent to drench the body from the feet to the head with nectar...

<summary>This page explains how the practice of Khechari Mudra leads to the simultaneous dissolution of the breath and the mind into the Brahmarandhra (the crown center). It emphasizes that when the breath stops moving in and out, the yogi becomes filled with the experience of Brahman, much like a pot submerged in space.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Brahmarandhra, Sushumna, dissolution of mind, breath control, nectar, Brahman, Samadhi</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions of the Fourth Chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, specifically concluding the section on the Khechari Mudra and moving into the internal meditative practices involving Shakti (Kundalini) and the concept of "Space" (Brahman).</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root:** He certainly becomes perfected, possessing a great body and great strength and valor. || 53 ||

### Thus concludes the Khechari Mudra.

**Root:** Having placed the mind in the middle of Shakti and Shakti within the mind, and observing the mind with the mind, one should hold the supreme state. || 54 ||

**Root:** Place the Self in the middle of space, and place space in the middle of the Self. Having made everything the nature of space, one should think of nothing at all. || 55 ||

### ।। COMMENTARY ।।

The phrase "of the breath, the mind, and the senses" <note>original: *pranituryasenanganam*</note> is used here in the singular sense to show their unification. From the soles of the feet to the top of the head <note>original: *apadatalamastakam*</note>, one should drench or flood the body. One who possesses an excellent or "great body" <note>original: *mahakayah*</note> and possesses "great strength and valor" <note>original: *mahabalaparakramah*</note> is a yogi who certainly becomes perfected. Through this drenching with nectar <term>Amrita: the "nectar of immortality," a fluid believed in Hatha Yoga to flow from the cranial vault, which the yogi "drinks" using the Khechari Mudra</term>, success is guaranteed. || 53 ||

**Regarding "In the middle of Shakti":** Shakti refers to **Kundalini** <term>Kundalini: the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine, visualized as a coiled serpent</term>. Placing the mind in her center means holding the mind upon her and making the mind take her form. By placing Shakti in the middle of the mind, one merges the energy of Shakti into the mind through the intensity of meditation, thereby awakening the Kundalini. As **Goraksha** <note>Gorakshanatha, the 11th-century founding master of the Nath lineage of Hatha Yoga</note> says: "She is awakened by the union with the fire <term>Vahni: the internal heat or digestive fire used to stir Kundalini</term> along with the great mind." By "observing the mind with the mind," it means stabilizing the intellect through internal observation. One should "hold the supreme state" <note>original: *paramam padam*</note>, meaning one should make the most excellent self-form the object of concentration <term>Dharana: the practice of fixed concentration</term>. || 54 ||

**Regarding "In the middle of space":** "Space" <note>original: *kha*</note> refers to the all-pervading **Brahman** <term>Brahman: the ultimate, infinite reality or universal consciousness</term>. Place your own Self <term>Atman: the individual soul or essence</term> within that space. The meaning is: contemplate the thought, "I am Brahman." And place space (the full Brahman) within the center of the Self. The meaning is: contemplate the thought, "Brahman is I." Having made everything the nature of space—viewing the entire universe as Brahman—one should not think of anything at all. This means one should even eventually abandon the meditation "I am Brahman" <note>The author suggests a progression from dualistic thought to a state of pure, non-conceptual existence (Samadhi)</note>. || 55 ||

### ।। VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ।।

The yogi whose body is great and who possesses strength and valor becomes perfected by being sprinkled with that nectar. || 53 ||

**Regarding "In the middle of Shakti":** By holding the mind in the Kundalini and holding the Kundalini in the mind, and through the intensity of meditation on the Kundalini, the mind becomes the Shakti. Thus, by uniting the Kundalini and the mind and awakening the Kundalini, one sees the mind through the inner faculty and holds that most excellent form. || 54 ||

**Regarding "In the middle of space":** Just as the sky is all-pervading, so is the complete Brahman. One should place the Self in that Brahman and cultivate the feeling "I am Brahman." Then, place the complete Brahman within the Self and cultivate the feeling "Brahman is I." Then, after contemplating everything as the nature of Brahman, do not let the mind think of anything; that is, one should even give up the specific meditation of "I am Brahman." || 55 ||

<summary>This page concludes the Khechari Mudra section, detailing how the yogi attains a perfected body through internal "nectar." It then introduces internal meditations that merge the mind with Kundalini energy and the individual self with the infinite Brahman (Space).</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Kundalini, Shakti, Brahman, Atman, Amrita, Goraksha, Meditation, Non-dualism, Samadhi</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, focusing on the state of Samadhi (meditative absorption) and the total cessation of mental activity. It follows the previous discussion on merging the mind with the "Space" of Brahman.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

**Root:** Empty within, empty without, like an empty pot in space. Full within, full without, like a full pot in the ocean. || 56 ||

**Root:** One should not perform external thinking, nor should one perform internal contemplation. Abandoning all thought, one should think of nothing at all. || 57 ||

**Root:** The entire world is but a creation of the will <note>original: *samkalpa*, meaning mental intention or volition</note>—

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

Now, he describes the state of one who is thus established in the Self. **"Empty within"** means that the internal instrument <term>Antahkarana: the collective functions of the mind, including intellect and ego</term> is "empty" because it lacks any thought-waves other than the realization of Brahman <term>Brahman: the ultimate, infinite reality or absolute consciousness</term>; it is "empty" of duality. **"Empty without"** means outside of the mind there is also emptiness, because one no longer perceives a second, separate object. 

As a **pot** <note>original: *kumbha*</note> in **space** <note>original: *ambare*</note> is empty both inside and out, similarly, in the "space of the heart," the mind is filled with the "air" of Brahman-consciousness. **"Full within"** refers to the presence of the thought-form of Brahman. **"Full without"** means that outside the heart and mind, everything is also full. This is because no reality exists other than Brahman, or because one is filled with the Absolute. 

Just as a pot in the **ocean** <note>original: *arnave*</note> is filled with water on all sides, the yogi established in **Samadhi** <term>Samadhi: a state of intense concentration and union with the divine</term> becomes filled with the Absolute. || 56 ||

**"External thinking"** refers to worldly objects; the yogi should not engage in such thoughts. Likewise, one should not engage in **"internal contemplation,"** meaning the mental fantasies one creates, such as imagining "palaces or gardens made of sweets" <note>original: *ashamodakasandhavatika*, an idiom for "castles in the air"</note>. Abandoning all thought, both external and internal, one should not think of anything at all. Through supreme dispassion, one should even let go of the mental form of the "Self." By abandoning even that, the state of **liberation while living** <term>Jivanmukti: the state of being spiritually liberated while still inhabiting a physical body</term> occurs. || 57 ||

To show that peace arises from abandoning internal and external thoughts, he cites the words of the sage Vasistha:

->।। VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ।।<-

**"Empty within"** means that within the mind there is nothing, because nothing is separate from the Absolute. And **"Empty without"** means that outside the mind, nothing else is seen, because nothing exists apart from the Absolute. An example for this is given: just as a pot in the sky has space inside it and space outside it. 

Furthermore, the mind is **"Full"** because the presence of the Absolute is there. And it is **"Full without"** because it is filled by the Absolute. The example given for this is: just as a pot in the ocean is filled with water on the outside and filled with water on the inside. In this same way, the yogi established in Samadhi becomes full of the Absolute. || 56 ||

**"External thinking"**: It is not proper for the yogi to worry about external subjects. Similarly, it is not proper to engage in the many contemplations and imaginings created by the mind. Having abandoned all internal and external worry, one should not think of anything. || 57 ||

Peace comes from the abandonment of internal and external worry. On this point, the words of Vasistha to Lord Rama are...

<summary>This page uses the analogies of a pot in space and a pot in the ocean to describe the yogi's state of Samadhi, where internal and external distinctions vanish into the fullness of Brahman. It emphasizes the total cessation of both worldly thoughts and mental fantasies to achieve Jivanmukti (liberation while living).</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Brahman, Jivanmukti, Antahkarana, Vasistha, emptiness, fullness, non-duality</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, focusing on the dissolution of the mind into the ultimate reality (Samadhi). The previous page concluded with the metaphor of the pot in the ocean, representing the yogi's immersion in Brahman-consciousness.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

->**The play of the mind is merely the construction of mental intent.**<-
->**O Rama! Abandon the belief in mere intent, and by taking refuge in the**<-
->**undifferentiated state, attain certain peace. || 58 ||**<-
->**Just as camphor dissolves in fire and salt dissolves in water,**<-
->**so the mind, when united with Reality, dissolves. || 59 ||**<-
->**All that is known and perceived, and even knowledge itself, is called the mind.**<-

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the word **intent** <note>original: *kalpa*</note>: **Intent** <term>Samkalpa: the formative or volitional faculty of the mind</term> is a mental operation. The "construction" of this intent is the entire perceived world; that is to say, the whole external universe is imagined by the mind alone. The **play** <note>original: *vilasa*</note> of the mind consists of the imagining of various objects and forms, like building "palaces of dreams" or "imaginary gardens" <note>literally: "palaces made of the sweets of hope"</note>. Even the internal world of the mind is merely the creation of mental intent.

**Abandon the belief** in the reality of this intent—the belief that the internal and external worlds are true. Then what should be done? He says: **in the undifferentiated state** <note>original: *nirvikalpam*</note>. **Differentiations** <term>Vikalpa: conceptual distractions or dualistic thoughts</term> are specific imaginings: the ideas of being a "doer," an "enjoyer," or "happy," and the distinctions of category, space, time, and object. That which is free from these is the "undifferentiated." By taking refuge in that Self—making it the object of one's meditation—O Rama! <note>The text here addresses Rama, as this verse is quoted from the *Yoga Vasistha*</note> attain **certain** and doubtless **peace**, which is supreme stillness. The implication is that you will then attain happiness. The Blessed Lord <note>referring to Krishna in the *Bhagavad Gita* 2.66</note> stated this through the negative: 
> "There is no peace for the one who does not meditate, and for the one without peace, where is happiness?" || 58 ||

**Camphor...** Just as **camphor** <note>original: *karparam*</note> when joined or united with **fire** <note>original: *anale*</note> **dissolves** completely—meaning it disappears and takes the form of the fire—and just as **salt** <term>Saindhava: literally "from the Indus," a common term for rock salt</term> when joined with **water** dissolves, abandoning its form as salt to take the form of the water; in the same way, the **mind** being joined and applied to **Reality** <note>original: *tattve*, the essential Truth or the Self</note> dissolves and takes the form of the Self. || 59 ||

When the dissolution of the mind occurs, duality also dissolves. He explains this in the next three verses. **"All that is known..."** Everything...

->|| VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||<-

Proof is now given for what was said previously. **Intent...** The external world is imagined by the mind alone. The mind's own expansion into many various forms is also a construction of intent. Abandon the belief in the truth of the world, both inside and out. By taking refuge in the Self, which is free from all distinctions, O Rama! You shall undoubtedly attain peace and happiness. || 58 ||

**Camphor...** Just as camphor placed in fire becomes the form of the fire, and salt placed in water abandons its identity as salt to become the form of the water, even so, if one applies the mind to the Self, the mind becomes the form of the Self. || 59 ||

**All that is known...** In all that is perceived, you reside within as the object of all knowledge. And "knowledge" itself is what is called the mind.

<summary>This page describes the mind as the sole creator of the perceived world and uses the metaphors of dissolving camphor and salt to explain how the mind must merge into the absolute Reality (the Self) to achieve peace.</summary>
<keywords>Samkalpa (intent), Vikalpa (distinction), Samadhi (absorption), Yoga Vasistha, Bhagavad Gita, Mind-dissolution, Rama, peace</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, building upon the previous discussion regarding the dissolution of the mind into the ultimate reality. While the previous page used the metaphor of salt dissolving in water, this page explores the philosophical implications: the total cessation of the subject-object distinction (duality) and the resulting state of absolute liberation.</meta>

# IN THE LAMP ON HATHA YOGA

Root text: Knowledge and the object of knowledge are both equally destroyed; there is no other second path. || 60 ||
All this that is seen by the mind—whatever exists, whether moving or unmoving—is merely the mind.
Indeed, through the mind's state of being "beyond-mind" <term>Unmanibhava: a state of consciousness where mental fluctuations have ceased</term>, duality is no longer perceived. || 61 ||
By the abandonment of all objects of knowledge, the mind attains dissolution.
When the dissolution of the mind has occurred, Absolute Liberation <term>Kaivalya: the state of pure, solitary consciousness</term> remains. || 62 ||

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

**The object of knowledge** <note>original: *jneya*</note>—that which is worthy of being known, that which is perceived and known—and **knowledge** <note>original: *jnana*</note>—all this is called the "mind." Because everything is merely a construction of the mind, it is referred to by the word "mind." If knowledge and the object of knowledge are dissolved together <note>original: *samam*</note> and destroyed along with the mind, then there is no **second path**; that is, there is no second "object" for the mind to follow. The resulting meaning is that duality no longer exists. || 60 ||

**All this that is seen by the mind:** This world that is perceived—whatever it may be, whether **moving** <note>animate beings</note> or **unmoving** <note>inanimate objects</note>—is together called "the moving and unmoving" <note>original: *sacharacharam*</note>. This entire universe is **seen by the mind**, meaning it is merely the mind's own intent <note>original: *sankalpa*</note>. Because the world is perceived when mental construction exists and is not perceived when it is absent, the entire world is an error <note>original: *bhrama*</note>. This is because an error exists only in its perception. 

This does not lead to the unwanted conclusion of the Buddhist doctrine <note>The commentator is defending the text against the Buddhist view of "emptiness" (Shunyavada). He argues that while the world is an illusion, it has a real foundation.</note>, because we accept the reality of Brahman as the substratum <note>the underlying basis</note> of the error. Through the **state of being beyond-mind** <note>original: *unmanibhava*</note>—which is the dissolution of the mind—**duality** <note>differentiation</note> is no longer perceived and does not appear. This is because the mind's intent, which is the cause of the error of duality, is absent. The word "indeed" <note>original: *hi*</note> is used here as an indeclinable to indicate this cause. || 61 ||

**The object of knowledge:** By the **abandonment of the object of knowledge**—that is, by the rejection of the entire visible world of moving and unmoving things, which consists of names and forms—the mind dissolves and takes the form of the Self, whose nature is Being, Consciousness, and Bliss <term>Satchidananda: the Vedantic definition of the ultimate reality</term>. When the dissolution of the mind has occurred, **Absolute Liberation** remains. This "Absolute Liberation" <note>original: *kaivalya*</note> is the state of the solitary Self. The meaning is that the nature of the non-dual Self alone remains. || 62 ||

->|| VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

Upon the dissolution of the mind, the world of duality also dissolves. || 60 ||
**All this that is seen by the mind:** The entire world, including the unmoving and the moving, exists only through the mental intent of the mind. Therefore, through the dissolution of the mind, the distinctions of the world are no longer perceived. || 61 ||
**The object of knowledge:** By abandoning the visible objects of the world, including all moving and unmoving things, the mind itself takes the form of the Self, which is Being, Consciousness, and Bliss. And as soon as the mind is dissolved, the non-dual nature of the Self remains. || 62 ||

<summary>This page explains that the entire perceived world is a projection of the mind (Sankalpa), and that true liberation (Kaivalya) is achieved when the mind dissolves, ending the illusion of duality.</summary>
<keywords>Unmanibhava, Kaivalya, duality, dissolution of mind, Satchidananda, Brahman, Sankalpa, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

Following the description of the mind's dissolution into the absolute, the text now transitions from theoretical descriptions to honoring the lineage of teachers and the energetic structures of the body that make Samadhi possible. It concludes by introducing the practice of Nadanusandhana (listening to the inner sound) as the most effective method for reaching this state.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION (Page 161)

**Root Text:** Thus, various methods, coupled with genuine personal experience, the paths to Samadhi <term>Samadhi: a state of intense concentration and union with the divine</term> have been described by the great-souled former teachers. || 63 ||

To the Sushumna <term>Sushumna: the central energy channel of the subtle body</term>, to Kundalini <term>Kundalini: the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</term>, to the nectar born of the moon, to Manonmani <term>Manonmani: "the state beyond mind," a synonym for the highest Samadhi</term>—salutations to you, the Great Power whose nature is Pure Consciousness. || 64 ||

[This path] is agreed upon even by those who are foolish and unable to perceive the True Reality...

### || COMMENTARY ||

**"Thus"** <note>original: *evam*</note>: By the methods previously described, such as "inner aim with outward gaze" <note>referring to *Shambhavi Mudra*</note>, the **great-souled former teachers** <note>original: *purvacharya*</note>—those whose internal instruments (the heart and mind) were purified by the great practice of Samadhi, such as Matsyendra and others—have described the **paths** (the means of attainment) for Samadhi, which is the cessation of the fluctuations of the mind.

What kind of paths to Samadhi? They are those of **various methods** <note>original: *nanavidhopayah*</note>, meaning they consist of various techniques and practices. Furthermore, they are **coupled with genuine personal experience**, meaning they are joined with a direct experience of the Self <note>original: *atmanubhava*</note> that is thorough and free from any doubt or error. || 63 ||

Having accomplished his goal, the author now bows to the Sushumna and the other divine aspects. **"To Sushumna"** <note>original: *sushumnayai*</note>:

To the **Sushumna**, which is the central channel. To **Kundalini**, the foundational power <note>original: *adhara-shakti*</note>. To the **nectar**, the ambrosia <note>original: *piyusha*</note> which has its birth from the "moon" located in the center of the eyebrows. To **Manonmani**, the "Fourth State" <term>Turiya: the state of pure consciousness transcending waking, dreaming, and deep sleep</term>. To her whose nature and true form is Pure Consciousness and Awareness. To that **Great Power**, who is the supreme Power of Consciousness in the form of the Spirit <note>original: *purusha-rupa*</note>, because she provides consciousness to the inert senses and the mind. The word **"to you"** <note>original: *tubhyam*</note> applies to each of these individually. Let there be a salutation—a bowing down in humility. || 64 ||

Having stated the various means for achieving Samadhi, he now introduces the primary method, which consists of the exploration of the inner sound <term>Nadanusandhana: the yoga of listening to internal vibrations</term>.

### || VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ||

**"Thus"** <note>original: *evam*</note>: In this way, those great-souled former teachers whose hearts and minds were purified by performing Samadhi have told the paths of Samadhi, which possess various practices and the experience of the Self that is free from doubt. || 63 ||

**"To Sushumna"** <note>original: *sushumnayai*</note>: Salutations to the **Sushumna**, which is the central channel. And salutations to **Kundalini**. Salutations to you who are of the form of **nectar**. Salutations to you, the Moon-born, whose birth is from the moon in the middle of the eyebrows. And salutations to you, **Manonmani**. And salutations to you whose nature is consciousness, the supreme power within the whole, in the form of the Spirit. || 64 ||

Now, having spoken of the various means of Samadhi, he now describes the primary method in the form of the exploration of the inner sound...

<summary>This page concludes the general discussion of Samadhi by honoring the lineage of teachers and the vital energies (Sushumna, Kundalini, Nectar) and introduces Nadanusandhana (inner sound) as the supreme yogic method.</summary>

Following the introduction of the various paths to Samadhi, the text now focuses on the specific practice of Nadanusandhana (meditation on the inner sound) as the most effective method, citing the authority of both Adinath (Shiva) and the sage Gorakshanath.</meta>

# HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA (Page 162)

**Root Text:** [The path] spoken by Gorakshanath <term>Gorakshanath: a legendary 11th-century yogic master and founder of the Kanphata lineage</term>, known as the worship of the inner sound <note>original: *nādopāsana*</note>, is now described. || 65 ||

Lord Adinath <term>Adinath: the "First Lord," identified as the god Shiva, the original teacher of Yoga</term> has described one and a quarter crore <note>12.5 million</note> varieties of dissolution <term>Laya: the process where the individual mind dissolves into the universal consciousness</term>; they prevail in their excellence. Yet, we consider meditation on the inner sound <note>original: *nādānusandhāna*</note> to be the single most important among all these methods of dissolution. || 66 ||

Seated in the Liberation Posture <note>original: *muktāsana*, a variation of the seated meditation pose</note>, the yogi should adopt the Shambhavi Mudra <term>Shambhavi Mudra: a practice where the eyes are fixed on an internal point while the mind is focused inward</term> and, with a one-pointed mind, listen to the internal sound in the right ear. || 67 ||

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

**"Even the foolish"**: This practice is accepted even by those who are dull-witted or unlearned, meaning those who have not yet attained the realization of Truth <note>original: *tattvabodha*</note> through formal study. By using the word "even," it is implied that it is certainly intended for the learned as well. Because it was spoken by the great **Gorakshanath**, its value and worthiness for practice are established. The **worship of the inner sound** <note>original: *nāda*</note> refers to the practice of constant contemplation on the "unstruck" internal resonance. || 65 ||

**"By Lord Adinath"**: Lord Adinath, who is Shiva, described **one and a quarter crore** <note>original: *sapādakoṭi*</note> methods—different ways to achieve the dissolution of the mind. These prevail in their greatness. However, among all these **methods of dissolution**, we consider **meditation on the inner sound** <note>original: *nādānusandhāna*</note> alone to be the very best. The essence is that because it is superior and is the favored view of Gorakshanath, meditation on the inner sound must certainly be practiced. || 66 ||

Regarding the practice of meditation on the inner sound through Shambhavi Mudra, [it is said]: **"In the Liberation Posture"**. Seated in the **Liberation Posture** <note>which is synonymous with the *Siddhasana* or Accomplished Pose</note>...

->।। VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ।।<-

Now it is said: This is accepted even by those in whom the knowledge of Truth has not arisen and who have not studied. What then to say of those who *do* study? This was spoken by Gorakshanath. The practice is described as the service of sound in the form of meditation. || 65 ||

**Lord Adinath** (Shiva) spoke of one and a quarter crore different methods for the mind to reach dissolution. These prevail in their excellence. But we know that the repeated contemplation of the inner sound is the most primary among all methods of dissolution. Since it is the preferred view of Gorakshanath, meditation on the sound is truly worthy of being performed. || 66 ||

**"In the Liberation Posture"**: Seated in the **Accomplished Pose** <note>original: *siddhāsana*</note>, the yogi becomes one-pointed through Shambhavi Mudra and hears the sound located within the **Sushumna channel** <term>Sushumna: the primary energy channel in the spine</term> in the right ear. || 67 ||

<note>1. In this section, the absence of grammatical joining (*sandhi*) is natural, as it is intended for those who are not scholars of grammar.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Nadanusandhana (meditation on internal sound) as the most effective of the 12.5 million methods of Laya (mind-dissolution) taught by Shiva. It provides the physical instructions for the practice: sitting in Siddhasana, performing Shambhavi Mudra, and focusing on the sound in the right ear.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. Following the introduction to meditation on the inner sound (Nada), the text now details the physical technique for hearing this sound and outlines the four stages of progress in yoga practice.</meta>

# FOURTH INSTRUCTION

Root Text: **One should perform the closing of the ear-holes, the pair of eyes, the nostrils, and the mouth. || 68a ||**
**In the path of the purified central channel <term>Sushumna: the primary energy channel in the subtle body, running from the base of the spine to the crown of the head</term>, the stainless Inner Sound <note>original: *nāda*</note> is clearly heard. || 68b ||**
**Commencement, Integration, Concentration, and Completion—these are the four stages that occur in all forms of Yoga. || 69 ||**

->।। COMMENTARY ।।<-

Then the yogi, having established the **Shambhavi Mudra** <term>Shambhavi Mudra: a practice where the eyes are fixed on an internal point while the mind is focused inward</term>, as described by the phrase "the internal target and external gaze," should proceed. Being "one-minded" or having a single-pointed focus, he should listen to the **Inner Sound** existing within the **Sushumna** channel in the right ear.

As stated in the *Tripurasara-samuccaya* <note>A classic Tantric text on the essence of the Goddess Tripura</note>: "In the beginning, this sound acts as a purifier of all latent impressions, resembling the hum produced by a swarm of intoxicated bees. Next, it is like the sound of air flowing through a resonant flute. After that, it imitates the striking of a bell, and then the deep, steady roar of the ocean. Finally, within this hollow of the Brahma-channel <note>another name for the Sushumna</note>, there resides the thundering sound of rain clouds." || 67 || <note>Note: The commentator is finishing the explanation of verse 67 started on the previous page.</note>

He now describes the practice of focusing on the Inner Sound through the "Outward-Facing Seal" <note>original: *parāṅmukhī-mudrā*, also known as *shanmukhi-mudrā* or the 'six-gated' seal</note> || **One should perform the closing** || Regarding the "ear-holes," the "pair of eyes," the "nostrils," and the "mouth": even though these are parts of a living being and could be grouped as a single grammatical unit, they are treated individually here for emphasis <margin>The commentator notes a grammatical choice in the Sanskrit compound here, allowing for the plural instead of the singular collective.</margin>. The closing of these should be performed using the fingers of the hands.

The method of closing is as follows: "Both ears with the thumbs, the eyes with the index fingers, the nostrils with the middle fingers, and the mouth with the remaining fingers—thus one covers the sensory organs." This covers the "closing" mentioned in the verse. Within the **Sushumna-pathway**, which has been rendered stainless and free from impurities through breathing exercises <term>Pranayama: yogic breath control</term>, the pure Inner Sound is heard clearly and distinctly. || 68 ||

Now he names the four stages of the Inner Sound || **Commencement and...** || These are the **Commencement Stage** <note>original: *ārambhāvasthā*</note>, the **Integration Stage** <note>original: *ghaṭāvasthā*</note>, the **Concentration Stage** <note>original: *paricayāvasthā*</note>, and the **Completion Stage** <note>original: *niṣpattyavasthā*</note>. In all yogas—that is, in all methods of restraining the fluctuations of the mind such as Shambhavi and others—these four stages exist. The extra particles in the verse <note>the Sanskrit words *ca, eva, tathā, api, ca*</note> are used simply to complete the meter of the poetry. || 69 ||

->।। VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ।।<-

**Closing the ears...** || One should block the pair of ears, the pair of eyes, the nostrils, and the mouth using the thumbs and fingers of the hands. Then, through the practice of breath control, when the path of the **Sushumna** becomes free of impurities, a clear and stainless Inner Sound comes to be heard. || 68 ||

**Commencement and...** || The Stage of Commencement, the Stage of Integration, the Stage of Concentration, and the Stage of Completion—these four stages are found in all systems of Yoga. || 69 ||

---

<note>Footnote 1: This is a scholarly critique by the commentator regarding the Sanskrit grammar of the compound word for the senses. He argues that the traditional rule for grouping body parts usually requires a singular form, but suggests the plural is used here either because the rule is optional or to fit the poetic meter of the verse.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical technique of blocking the senses (Shanmukhi Mudra) to hear the internal mystic sounds and introduces the four progressive stages of yoga mastery: Commencement, Integration, Concentration, and Completion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It moves from the general classification of the four stages of yoga progress into the specific details of the first stage, the Commencement Stage (Arambhavastha), and begins the description of the second, the Integration Stage (Ghatavastha).</meta>

# Now, the Commencement Stage

Root Text: **When the Knot of Brahma <term>Brahma-granthi: The first of three energetic "knots" along the spine that must be pierced for spiritual awakening; it is located at the heart center</term> is pierced, bliss arises from the void. A diverse, tinkling, Unstruck Sound <note>original: *anāhata dhvani*</note> is heard within the body. || 70 ||**

**The Yogi possesses a divine body—radiant, smelling of heavenly fragrance, and free from disease. With a heart that is both full and void <note>full of bliss, yet void of worldly thoughts</note>, he enters the Commencement Stage. || 71 ||**

# Now, the Integration Stage

-> In the second stage, having integrated the breath, it moves into the central channel. <-

## || COMMENTARY ||

Regarding this, he describes the **Commencement Stage** <note>original: *ārambhāvasthā*</note> with the verse beginning "From the Knot of Brahma." When the "piercing"—meaning the breaking through of the **Knot of Brahma** situated in the **Anahata Chakra** <term>Anahata Chakra: the heart energy center</term>—occurs through the practice of Breath Control <note>original: *prāṇāyāma*</note>, then [bliss arises]. The word "then" is implied to complete the "when." "Bliss" <note>original: *ānanda*</note> is that which causes joy. "Arising from the void" <note>original: *śūnya-sambhavaḥ*</note> means it is produced in the space of the heart <note>original: *hṛd-ākāśa*</note>. "Diverse" means of various types. "Tinkling" <note>original: *kvaṇakaḥ*</note> refers to a sound like the jingling of ornaments; it means it resembles the sound of jewelry. As the *Amarakosha* <note>The most celebrated ancient Sanskrit thesaurus</note> states: "The jingling of ornaments is called *shiñjita*, *nikvāṇa*, *nikvaṇa*, *kvāṇa*, *kvaṇa*, and *kvaṇana*." The **Unstruck Sound**—a sound produced without two things hitting each other—is heard within the body; that is, it becomes an object of hearing. || 70 ||

Regarding the "divine body": When this "commencement" of sound occurs in the heart-void, the yogis use the terms "Void," "Exceeding Void," and "Great Void" to refer to the heart-space, the throat-space, and the space between the eyebrows, respectively. "With a heart full" means his heart is completely filled with the vital breath <note>original: *prāṇa-vāyu*</note>, or rather, filled with bliss. The yogi possesses a "divine body," meaning a body endowed with beauty, grace, and strength. He is "radiant," meaning full of majesty. He has a "divine fragrance," meaning he possesses an excellent, heavenly scent. He is "free from disease," meaning healthy. This is how the description is connected. || 71 ||

He now describes the **Integration Stage** <note>original: *ghaṭāvasthā*</note> with the verse beginning "In the second." In the second stage, which is the Integration Stage, the breath...

## || VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||

Now the **Commencement Stage** is described. When the **Knot of Brahma** is pierced, then a bliss-giving, diverse sound resembling the jingling of ornaments is heard within the body, originating in the heart-space. || 70 ||

Regarding the "divine body": The commencement of the Sound <note>original: *nāda*</note> occurs in the void of the heart-space. The yogi whose heart is filled with the vital breath, or whose heart is full of bliss, becomes radiant, possesses a divine body and divine fragrance, and remains free from disease. || 71 ||

Now the **Integration Stage** is described. In the Integration Stage, the breath and the sound...

<summary>This page describes the 'Commencement Stage' (Arambhavastha) of Yoga, where the piercing of the heart's energy knot leads to the hearing of inner sounds and physical rejuvenation, then introduces the 'Integration Stage' (Ghatavastha).</summary>
<keywords>Arambhavastha, Brahma-granthi, Anahata Chakra, Nada, divine body, heart-space, Ghatavastha, Pranayama</keywords>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

[Root Verse]
**Having attained a steady posture, the yogi is then wise and becomes like a god. || 72 ||**
**Then, through the piercing of the Knot of Vishnu, there is an indication of supreme bliss. In the "Extreme Void" <note>Atishunya; the space within the throat.</note>, a crashing resonance arises, like the sound of a kettle-drum. || 73 ||**

## NOW THE STAGE OF INTEGRATION (PARICHAYAVASTHA)

**In the third stage, the sound of a *mardala* drum <note>A traditional double-headed drum.</note> is clearly heard in the sky. Then the breath enters the "Great Void" <note>Mahashunya; the space between the eyebrows.</note>, which is the dwelling place of all accomplishments. || 74 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Having united the downward breath (*apana*) with the individual self, and merging the <term>Nada</term> <note>The inner mystic sound.</note> and the <term>Bindu</term> <note>The point of concentrated energy or seed of consciousness.</note>, one enters the "middle center" located in the throat. This is stated right here regarding the <term>Jalandhara Bandha</term> <note>The "throat lock" practice where the chin is pressed to the chest.</note>: "This is to be known as the middle center, the binding of the sixteen supports." 

It should be understood that at this stage, the practitioner of yoga possesses a **steady posture**, meaning his seat is firm. He is **wise**, meaning his intellect is more refined than before. He is **like a god**, meaning he is equal to a deity due to his superior beauty and radiance. This is stated in the *Raja Yoga* taught by the Lord: "Because the <term>Prana</term> <note>Outward/upward breath.</note> and <term>Apana</term> <note>Inward/downward breath.</note>, the *Nada* and *Bindu*, and the individual soul and supreme soul meet and unite, it is called the 'Jar' (*Ghata*)." || 72 ||

Regarding the phrase **"The Knot of Vishnu"**: after the piercing of the Knot of Brahma, the <term>Vishnu Granthi</term> <note>The second major energetic blockage, associated with the throat and heart, representing the preservation of egoic identity.</note> located in the throat is pierced through the practice of breath-retention (*kumbhaka*). This serves as a **sign** or indicator of the coming supreme bliss of <term>Brahmananda</term> <note>The ultimate bliss of universal consciousness.</note>. In the **"Extreme Void"**, which is the space of the throat, there is a **crashing** or a collision of many sounds. The **sound of a kettle-drum** (*bheri*)—a resonance resembling a large drum—occurs at that time. || 73 ||

Now, the author describes the Stage of Integration (*Parichayavastha*) in two and a half verses. Regarding the phrase **"In the third stage"**: in the third stage of integration, the **sound of a *mardala* drum** is heard in the "sky" or space between the eyebrows (*bhrumadhya*). This sound is **to be known**, meaning it is especially worthy of recognition. At that time, the breath reaches the **"Great Void"**, the space between the eyebrows, which is the **dwelling place of all accomplishments**, the seat of all powers such as <term>Anima</term> <note>The yogic power to become as small as an atom.</note>. By performing <term>Samyama</term> <note>Combined concentration, meditation, and absorption.</note> there, one attains these powers. || 74 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

When the yogi is situated in the "middle center" of the throat, then in this state, his posture becomes firm. He becomes wise and his beauty increases so much that he becomes like a god. || 72 ||

Regarding the **Knot of Vishnu**: after the piercing of the Knot of Brahma, the Knot of Vishnu situated in the throat is pierced through breath-retention. Through this, the bliss of the Absolute is known. A collision of many sounds occurs, and a sound like that of a kettle-drum—the resonance of a large drum—is heard at that time. || 73 ||

Now the Stage of Integration is described. In the third stage, which is Integration, in the space between the eyebrows...

<summary>This page explains the progress from the "Jar" stage (Ghatavastha) to the "Integration" stage (Parichayavastha). It describes the physical transformation of the yogi into a god-like state and the mystical sounds—specifically the kettle-drum and mardala drum—that signify the piercing of the Vishnu energy knot.</summary>

<page-num>166</page-num>
<header>ON THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

## ROOT VERSES

->**Having then conquered the bliss of the mind, the arising of innate bliss occurs. ||**<-
->**Then the yogi is free from the three humors, pain, old age, disease, hunger, and sleep. || 75 ||**<-
->**When the breath, having pierced the Knot of Rudra, reaches the Seat of Shiva,**<-
->**In the Stage of Consummation, the sound of a flute and the resonance of a plucking lute arise. || 76 ||**<-
->**Then the mind, having become one, is called Raja Yoga. ||**<-

## ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

Regarding the phrase **bliss of the mind** (*chittananda*): having conquered or transcended the pleasure produced by the mental functions focused on the subject of the inner sound, the **arising of innate bliss** (*sahajananda*) occurs. This innate bliss is the manifestation of the natural joy of the Self. At that time, the yogi becomes **free from**—meaning devoid of—the **humors** (*doshas*), which are wind, bile, and phlegm <note>Vata, Pitta, and Kapha; the three biological energies in Ayurvedic medicine.</note>; **pain**, meaning the suffering born from physical or spiritual afflictions; **old age**, the state of senescence; **disease**, such as fever and other ailments; **hunger**; and **sleep**. || 75 ||

In response to the question of "when" this happens, the text says: **When the Knot of Rudra...** The <term>Knot of Rudra</term> <note>original: *Rudra-granthi*; the third and final psychic knot located at the brow center that blocks the flow of energy to the crown.</note> is located in the **Command Center** <note>original: *Ajna-chakra*; the energy center between the eyebrows.</note>. When the **breath** (the vital energy or *prana*) has **reached** that **Seat of Shiva** <note>original: *Sharva-pitha*; the "Seat of the Auspicious One," referring to the space between the eyebrows.</note> after piercing that knot, then it occurs.

Now, the author describes the **Stage of Consummation** (*Nishpatti-avastha*): In the state of **Consummation**, when the breath has entered the **Aperture of Brahma** <note>original: *Brahmarandhra*; the energetic opening at the crown of the head.</note>, the Stage of Consummation occurs. At that point, there is a **flute-like** (*vainavah*) sound—meaning a sound related to a bamboo flute—and the **resonance** or sound of a **plucking lute** <note>original: *veena*; a traditional Indian stringed instrument.</note> which is actively vibrating. || 76 ||

Regarding the phrase **having become one**: At that time, in that state, the **mind** (the internal faculty) has **become one**, meaning it has become unified with its object. This is due to the metaphorical non-difference between the observer and the observed. This state is given the **name Raja Yoga** <note>The "Royal Yoga," representing the highest state of meditative absorption or Samadhi.</note>.

## ॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥

In the midst of the commentary, it is explained that in the "sky" <note>The inner space of consciousness.</note>, the sound of these instruments should be recognized. In that state, which is the repository of all **perfected powers** <note>original: *siddhis*; such as the power to become tiny (*anima*), etc.</note>, the vital breath reaches the space in the center of the eyebrows. || 74 ||

Regarding **bliss of the mind**: By setting aside the pleasure produced by the fluctuations of the mind, the dawn of the natural bliss of the Self occurs. Then the yogi becomes free from the humors, pain, old age, disease, hunger, and sleep. || 75 ||

Regarding **Rudra**: The Knot of Rudra is in the brow center. When the vital breath pierces the Knot of Rudra and reaches the center of the eyebrows, the place of Lord Shiva, then the yogi's qualities become like that. Now the Stage of Consummation is described: when the vital breath enters the crown of the head, the Stage of Consummation occurs. When the state of Consummation is reached, a sound like a flute and a sound like a vibrating lute are produced. || 76 ||

Regarding **having become one**: In that state, the internal faculty becomes focused on a single object. This one-pointedness of the mind is what is called **Raja Yoga**. The yogi who is devoted to the pursuit of the inner sound is [involved in] creation and <meta>catchword: dissolution</meta>

<summary>This page describes the transition into the final stage of yoga, the Stage of Consummation (Nishpattiavastha), where the practitioner transcends physical needs and hears subtle flute-like sounds as the breath reaches the crown of the head.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Rudra-granthi, Nishpattiavastha, Ajna-chakra, Brahmarandhra, Sahajananda, Inner Sound, Yoga Physiology</keywords>

<page-num>( 187 )</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

### ROOT VERSES

**Such a person becomes a creator and destroyer of the universe, equal to the Lord of Yogis. || 77 ||**

**Whether liberation is achieved or not, here indeed is unbroken bliss. This bliss, which arises from absorption, is attained through Raja Yoga. || 78 ||**

**Those who do not know Raja Yoga and are merely practitioners of Hatha Yoga rituals—I consider these practitioners to be laboring without fruit. || 79 ||**

**For the rapid attainment of the Mind-transcending state, meditation on the space between the eyebrows is my recommendation. This is an easy method for even those of modest intellect to reach the state of Raja Yoga. Absorption born of the Inner Sound produces immediate realization. || 80 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Regarding the term **Raja Yoga**: it refers to the one-pointedness of the mind. **Creator and destroyer**: This practitioner, who is intent on the <term>Absorption in Sound</term> <note>original: *nādānusaṃdhāna*; the practice of listening to the internal mystical sounds to reach Samadhi.</note>, creates and destroys; in this way, he is a "creator and destroyer." Because of this, he becomes **equal to the Lord**—meaning he is like the Supreme Being himself. || 77 ||

**Whether liberation...** and **Those who do not know Raja Yoga...**: both of these verses have been explained previously. || 78 || 79 ||

**For the rapid attainment...**: For the quick or swift **attainment**—meaning the achievement—of **Unmani** or the <term>Mind-transcending state</term> <note>original: *unmany-avasthā*; a high state of Samadhi where the mind ceases to function as a separate entity and merges into pure consciousness.</note>, **meditation on the space between the eyebrows** is **favored by me**, Svatmarama <note>The author of the text.</note>. Raja Yoga is the "King of Yogas"; to **reach** or obtain that very state, which is also called the **Fourth State** <term>Turyavastha</term> <note>original: *turyāvasthā*; the transcendental state of consciousness that lies beyond waking, dreaming, and deep sleep.</note>, the previously mentioned meditation on the brow-center is an **easy means**—a method achieved with ease—even for **those of modest intellect**. If it is easy for them, then how much more so for others? This is the intent. The **absorption born from sound**—the dissolution of the mind arising from the Inner Sound—**produces immediate realization**; it brings about a direct, perceived experience quickly. || 80 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

...is done. This yogi becomes equal even to God. || 77 ||

**Whether liberation...**: Whether there is liberation or not, in this state there is unbroken bliss. The bliss that arises from absorption is also obtained through Raja Yoga. || 78 ||

**Those who do not know Raja Yoga...**: Those practitioners who do not know Raja Yoga and only perform the physical rituals of Hatha Yoga—I consider them to be devoid of the fruits of their toil. || 79 ||

**For the rapid attainment...**: For the purpose of quickly achieving the Mind-transcending state, meditation in the middle of the eyebrows is favored by me, Svatmarama. For reaching the state of Raja Yoga, which is the "Fourth State," meditation in the center of the eyebrows is an easy method even for those of duller intellect. And the dissolution of the mind that occurs through the Inner Sound produces immediate realization. || 80 ||

<summary>This page emphasizes that physical Hatha Yoga is fruitless without the mental state of Raja Yoga, and recommends meditation on the brow-center as an easy way for all practitioners to reach the "Fourth State" of consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Unmani, brow-center meditation, Turyavastha, inner sound, Svatmarama, spiritual absorption</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Chapter) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, detailing the internal practice of Nādānusaṃdhāna (meditation on the inner sound). It follows the previous page's discussion on the necessity of Raja Yoga for reaching the mind-transcending state.</meta>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA</header>

**The unique bliss that grows in the hearts of the great lords of Yoga who partake in the <term>Absorption in Sound</term> <note>original: *nādānusaṃdhāna*; the practice of listening to the internal mystical sounds to reach Samadhi.</note>—a bliss that words cannot reach—is known only by the illustrious Lord Guru. || 81 ||**

**Closing both ears with his hands, whatever sound the Sage hears, he should steady his mind upon it until he reaches the <term>Stable State</term> <note>original: *sthira-padam*; the "Fourth State" of consciousness beyond waking, dreaming, and deep sleep.</note>. || 82 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Regarding the verse **The unique bliss...**: The term **Exploration of Sound** <note>original: *nādānusaṃdhāna*</note> refers to the continuous contemplation of the <term>Unstruck Sound</term> <note>original: *anāhata-dhvani*; the mystic sound heard within the body without any external object striking another.</note>. By this, one partakes in **Absorption** <note>original: *samādhi*</note>, which is the one-pointedness of the mind. Those who enjoy this are the "Partakers in Sound-absorption." Among yogis, they are the **Lords**—those who are most capable and accomplished. In their **hearts**, this bliss grows. It is **beyond words**; it is impossible to describe it as "this" or "that." This unique, primary **bliss** or delight, famous in the scriptures of Yoga, is known only by the **illustrious Lord Guru**—the glorious Master alone. By this, it is suggested that the joy of absorbing oneself in the inner sound can only be reached through the guidance of a Guru. || 81 ||

The author now describes the state of Absorption that follows the practice of **withdrawal of the senses** <note>original: *pratyāhāra*</note> through Sound-meditation with the verse **Closing both ears...**: The **Sage** <note>original: *muni*</note> is the yogi who is reflective by nature. The phrase **with his hands** refers specifically to the thumbs. By **closing**—that is, by placing the thumbs into the openings of the ears—whatever **sound** (the internal Unstruck sound) he **hears**, he should make his wandering **mind steady** upon that sound. He should do this until he reaches the **Stable State**, which is the state called <term>Turya</term> <note>original: *turyākhya*; the pure state of consciousness.</note>.

As it has been said: "The state of Turya is the experiencing of the Sound that reveals pure Consciousness." Through this focus on the inner sound, the breath becomes steady, and the <term>Supernatural Powers</term> <note>original: *aṇimādayo*; the eight yogic powers such as becoming as small as an atom.</note> are attained. 

It is also stated in the *Tripurāsāra-samuccaya*: "Through this, the vital breath is conquered by him in whom the natural inner sound has arisen. The powers like becoming-small <note>original: *aṇimā*</note> belong to him, and he possesses infinite merit and the rising of great virtues. By blocking the <term>opening of the enemy of the son of the King of Devas</term> <note>original: *surarāja-tanūja-vairi-randhre*; a poetic riddle meaning the ear canal. The King of Devas is Indra; his son is Arjuna; Arjuna's rival is Karna; 'Karna' also means 'ear'.</note> with the fingers of his hands, a mortal man suddenly hears the deep roar of the ocean echoing within." The "King of Devas" is Indra; his "son" is Arjuna; his "enemy" is Karna—the rest of the riddle is clear. || 82 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

Regarding the verse **The unique bliss...**: Those great lords of Yoga who enjoy the one-pointedness of mind produced by the constant contemplation of the inner sound—the heart-swelling, primary bliss they experience cannot be described in speech. Only the Blessed Lord Guru knows this bliss. This implies that the joy of Sound-meditation is known only through the Guru. || 81 ||

Regarding the verse **Closing both ears...**: A yogi should place his thumbs in his ears (thereby closing them) and listen to the internal sound. He should steady his mind on that Unstruck sound until he attains the state of <term>Turya</term> <note>the fourth state of consciousness</note>. || 82 ||

<summary>This page explains Nādānusaṃdhāna, the practice of focusing on internal mystical sounds to achieve Samadhi, emphasizing that the resulting bliss is indescribable and only truly understood through a Guru's guidance.</summary>
<keywords>Nadanusandhana, internal sound, Samadhi, Guru, Turya, Anahata, Tripurasara-samuccaya, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, meditation techniques</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, focusing on the progression of sounds heard during Nādānusaṃdhāna (meditation on the internal sound). It elaborates on how the internal sound eventually eclipses external noise and the specific timeline for achieving mental stability.</meta>

<page-num>( 169 )</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

**Root Text: When this <term>Inner Sound</term> <note>original: *nāda*</note> is practiced, it drowns out all external noise. By conquering all <term>distraction</term> <note>original: *vikṣepa*; the wandering or flickering of the mind.</note> within a <term>fortnight</term> <note>original: *pakṣa*; a period of fifteen days or half a lunar month.</note>, the Yogi becomes happy. || 83 ||**

**In the initial practice, various loud sounds are heard. Thereafter, as the practice progresses, an increasingly subtle sound is heard. || 84 ||**

**At first, sounds arise like those of the ocean, thunder, the kettle-drum, and the <term>jharjhara</term> drum. In the middle stage, sounds arise like those from the <term>mardala</term> drum and the conch, as well as the bell and the gong. || 85 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Regarding the verse **When this...**: When this **Sound**, known as the **Unstruck** <note>original: *anāhata*</note>, is **practiced**—meaning continuously contemplated—it **drowns out** or covers the **external sound**, which is the noise born of the outside world, from the range of the ears. The **Yogi** who practices this sound, after a **fortnight** (half a month), **conquers** or overcomes **all distraction**, which is the flickering nature of the mind, and becomes **happy**, resting in the bliss of his own self. || 83 ||

Regarding the verse **In the initial...**: In the **initial practice** (the early stages of training), a **loud sound** of **various kinds** is **heard** or perceived, resembling the ocean, thunder, or a large kettle-drum. **Thereafter**, as the **practice** of contemplating the sound **progresses**, a **subtle sound**, becoming more and more refined, is **heard** and becomes the object of hearing. || 84 ||

The author describes the various types of sounds in two verses, beginning with **At first...**: In the **beginning**, when the <term>Vital Air</term> <note>original: *vāyu*; the breath or life-force.</note> starts entering the **Cranial Aperture** <note>original: *brahmarandhra*; the spiritual center at the crown of the head.</note>, the sounds are like the **ocean** <note>original: *jaladhi*</note>, a **cloud** <note>original: *jīmūta*; representing thunder</note>, or a **kettle-drum** <note>original: *bheri*; a specific type of percussion instrument</note>. The *Amarakosha* <note>An ancient Sanskrit lexicon or dictionary.</note> states: "The word *bherī* is feminine, and *dundubhi* (drum) is masculine." The **jharjhara** is also a specific type of drum. The *Amarakosha* further explains: "The various types of instruments include the *ḍamaru*, *maḍu*, *ḍiṇḍima*, and *jharjhara*. The *mardala* and *paṇava* are others." These sounds resemble those born from the ocean and so on. **In the middle**, once the breath has achieved **steadiness** within the Cranial Aperture, sounds arise like those from the **mardala** (a drum) and the **conch** (the shell-trumpet), and sounds like those born from the **bell** and the **gong** <note>original: *kāhala*; a large trumpet or gong</note> are produced. || 85 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ॥<-

Regarding **When this...**: The sound that has been practiced covers the external sounds. And the Yogi who practices this sound, in just a fortnight, conquers the total flickering of the mind and thereby becomes happy. || 83 ||

Regarding **In the initial...**: In the first stage of practice, many types of loud sounds like the ocean, thunder, and kettle-drums are heard. After that, when the practice of Sound-contemplation increases, then only increasingly subtle sounds come into the range of hearing. || 84 ||

He describes the various types of sounds: In the **beginning**, when the breath travels toward the Cranial Aperture, at that time, sounds like those of the ocean, clouds, kettle-drums, and the *damaru* drum occur. And in the **middle**, sounds like the *panava* drum and...

<sig>22</sig>

<vocab>Inner Sound (Nāda), Yoga, Unstruck (Anāhata), Cranial Aperture (Brahmarandhra), Kettle-drum (Bherī), Jharjhara, Mardala, Conch (Śaṅkha), Gong (Kāhala), Practice (Abhyāsa), Distraction (Vikṣepa)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the sensory progression of Nāda Yoga, where internal mystical sounds gradually drown out external noise, leading to mental stability within fifteen days. It categorizes the sounds heard by the yogi into loud, coarse sounds (like thunder) in the beginning and softer, more melodic sounds (like bells) in the middle stage.</summary>
