<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, detailing the specific internal sounds heard as the practitioner progresses in Nāda Yoga. It moves from the louder, "gross" sounds to the delicate "subtle" sounds, providing instructions on how to maintain mental focus during these auditory experiences.</meta>

<page-num>( 170 )</page-num>
<header>REGARDING THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

**Root Text: At the end, sounds like <term>tinkling bells</term> <note>original: *kiṅkiṇī*</note>, the flute, the lute, and the bee are heard; thus, many kinds of sounds arise within the body. || 86 ||**

**Even when great sounds like thunder and kettle-drums are heard, one should contemplate only the sound that becomes subtler and subtler. || 87 ||**

**Moving from the loud sound to the subtle, or from the subtle to the loud, the mind—even if restless while enjoying them—should not be allowed to wander elsewhere. || 88 ||**

### ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

Regarding the verse **At the end...**: **At the end**—meaning after the <term>vital breath</term> <note>original: *prāṇa*</note> has achieved great stability in the <term>opening of Brahman</term> <note>original: *brahmarandhra*; the energetic center at the crown of the head</note>—the sounds that are heard are like **tinkling bells** (small bells), the **flute** (reed pipe), the **lute** (stringed instrument), and the **bee** (honey-gatherer). These **various** types of sounds, previously mentioned, are **heard** as they have **arisen within the body**. || 86 ||

Regarding the verse **Even when...**: Even when a **great**, abundant **sound** is being **heard** or listened to—such as **thunder** and the **kettle-drum** (meaning the resonance produced by them)—one **should contemplate** or meditate only upon the **subtler and subtler** sound among them. The intention is that because a subtle sound remains for a long time, the mind attached to it will become steadily fixed for a long time. || 87 ||

Regarding the verse **Moving from...**: Leaving aside the **loud** (great) sound like thunder or drums to focus on the **subtle**, or leaving the **subtle** to focus on the **loud**—even if the **mind** is **restless** (extremely flickering due to the quality of <term>activity</term> <note>original: *rajas*</note>) while **playing** or alternating between these sounds, one **should not move** or drive it **elsewhere** toward other objects of the senses. The idea is that a restless mind attached to external objects cannot achieve <term>absorption</term> <note>original: *samādhi*</note>, but a mind playing among these internal sounds can. || 88 ||

### ॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥

The sounds of the conch, the bell, and the horn are similar to the sounds heard [in the middle stage]. || 85 ||

Regarding the verse **At the end...**: And in the end, after the breath has become very still in the **opening of Brahman**, various sounds like the **tinkling bell**, the **flute**, the **lute**, and the **bee** are heard as they arise within the body. || 86 ||

Regarding the verse **Even when...**: When hearing the sounds of instruments like thunder and kettle-drums, one should meditate on the sound that is even subtler than the subtle. || 87 ||

Regarding the verse **Moving from...**: Leaving the great sound of thunder and kettle-drums to focus on the subtle, or leaving the subtle for the great, one should not let the mind—which is extremely restless due to the quality of **activity**—be inspired toward other sense objects. || 88 ||

<summary>This page describes the final stages of internal sound meditation, where the practitioner hears delicate sounds like flutes and bees, and is instructed to keep the mind focused on these vibrations to prevent distraction.</summary>
<keywords>Inner Sound, meditation, concentration, flute sound, bee sound, mental stability, Hatha Yoga, Nāda Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It follows the discussion of internal sounds (nāda) heard during meditation, specifically focusing on how these sounds serve to anchor the mind and prevent it from wandering toward external sensory distractions. These verses use the metaphors of the honey-bee and the wild elephant to illustrate the power of Nāda Yoga.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

**Root Text: Wherever the mind first attaches itself to a <term>sound</term> <note>original: *nāda*</note>, it becomes thoroughly steady there and dissolves along with it. || 89 ||**

**Just as a bee drinking nectar does not care for the fragrance, so the mind—when attached to the internal sound—does not crave sensory objects. || 90 ||**

**For the wild elephant-king of the mind wandering in the garden of sensory objects, this internal resonance is the sharp <term>goad</term> <note>original: *aṅkuśa*; a hooked tool used by trainers to control and direct elephants</note> capable of restraining it. || 91 ||**

### ॥ COMMENTARY ॥

Regarding the verse **Wherever...**: Or, wherever—in whichever sound, whether it be a loud resonance or a subtle sound—the **mind first** (previously) **attaches** (becomes fixed), it becomes **thoroughly steady** in that very sound and **dissolves** (becomes absorbed) **along with** that sound. This is the meaning. Here, the first part of the sentence refers to <term>withdrawal of the senses</term> <note>original: *pratyāhāra*</note>, the second to <term>concentration</term> <note>original: *dhāraṇā*</note>, and through <term>meditation</term> <note>original: *dhyāna*</note>, the third part describes <term>total absorption</term> <note>original: *samādhi*</note>. || 89 ||

Regarding the verse **Just as a bee...**: Just as a **bee** (a honey-gatherer) **drinking** (consuming) **nectar** (flower juice) **does not care for** (desire) the **fragrance**, so too the **mind** (the inner faculty) **attached to the sound** (focused on the internal resonance) **does not crave sensory objects**. Sensory objects are things like flower garlands, sandalwood paste, and women, which "bind" or "entrap" the perceiver through association. The word "indeed" <note>original: *hi*</note> is used for emphasis. || 90 ||

Regarding the verse **For the mind...**: Sensory objects—such as sound, touch, etc.—are the **garden** (the forest), and the **mind** is the **wild elephant-king** that wanders there. It is described as such because it is difficult to restrain. The **sound** (the <term>unstruck resonance</term> <note>original: *anāhata-dhvani*</note>) is itself the **sharp goad** (a keen tool of restraint), which is **capable** (powerful enough) of **restraining** (turning back) that mind. Through these verses, the nature of **sensory withdrawal** <note>original: *pratyāhāra*</note> is explained, following the definition: "The withdrawal of the eyes and other senses as they range among objects in their respective order is known as *pratyāhāra*." || 91 ||

### ॥ VERNACULAR COMMENTARY ॥

Regarding the verse **Wherever...**: In whichever great sound or subtle sound the mind first becomes attached, it becomes stable in that very sound and is dissolved along with it. || 89 ||

Regarding the verse **Just as a bee...**: Just as a bee, while drinking the juice of a flower, does not desire its fragrance, so the mind that has become attached to the internal sound does not desire sensory objects like flowers, sandalwood, women, and so on. This is certain. || 90 ||

Regarding the verse **For the mind...**: The mind is like a wild elephant wandering in the forest of sensory objects; the internal sound is the sharp goad capable of driving it back. || 91 ||

<summary>This page explains how internal sounds (Nāda) act as an anchor for the mind, leading the practitioner through the stages of sensory withdrawal (Pratyahara) to total absorption (Samadhi). It uses vivid metaphors of a bee attracted to nectar and a goad restraining a wild elephant to describe the mind's focus.</summary>
<keywords>Nāda, meditation, Samadhi, Pratyahara, internal sound, mind control, elephant metaphor, bee metaphor, sensory withdrawal</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It follows the discussion of using internal sound (nāda) as a "goad" to control the wild elephant of the mind. The text now moves to specific metaphors for how sound binds the mind's restlessness, aligning these practices with the classical stages of Yoga: concentration (dhāraṇā) and meditation (dhyāna).</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA</header>

**Root Text: Bound by the bond of <term>sound</term> <note>original: *nāda*</note>, the mind abandons its restlessness and attains absolute stillness, just like a bird with clipped wings. || 92 ||**

**Renouncing all worries, one who desires the <term>sovereignty of Yoga</term> <note>original: *yoga-sāmrājyam*; the supreme state of a Master of Yoga, or Raja Yoga</note> should contemplate the sound alone with an attentive mind. || 93 ||**

**For binding the <term>deer</term> <note>original: *sāraṅga*</note> of the internal mind, the sound acts as a snare; it also acts as a hunter in slaying the deer of the internal mind. || 94 ||**

->॥ COMMENTARY ॥<-

Regarding the verse **Bound by...**: The sound itself is the "bond." It is called a bond because it is the means by which the mind is bound; through its own power, it brings the mind under its control. It has reached a state of being "bound," meaning it is fully attached to the practice of holding the internal sound. Consequently, the mind—having thoroughly abandoned its **restlessness** (the habit of seizing and discarding sensory objects moment by moment)—attains **absolute stillness** or reaches a state of unwavering fixedness. 

The author provides an illustration: just as a **bird** (literally: "sky-goer") whose **wings are clipped** becomes still. By this, the stage of <term>concentration</term> <note>original: *dhāraṇā*; the sixth limb of yoga</note> is explained according to the definition: "Having controlled the breath through <term>breath-control</term> <note>original: *prāṇāyāma*</note> and the senses through <term>withdrawal</term> <note>original: *pratyāhāra*</note>, one should then make the mind steady upon a blessed support." Fixing the mind on a blessed support is the defining characteristic of concentration. || 92 ||

Regarding the verse **Renouncing all worries...**: Renouncing or casting aside the **worries** (the thinking processes) regarding all external and internal sensory objects, a person who **desires** (longs for) the **sovereignty of Yoga** (the status of being a "Raja Yogi") should **contemplate** (meditate upon) the **sound alone**—the unstruck resonance <note>original: *anāhata-dhvani*</note>—with an **attentive** (one-pointed) **mind**. This means one should create a continuous flow of mental awareness in the form of that sound. 

By this, the stage of <term>meditation</term> <note>original: *dhyāna*; the seventh limb of yoga</note> is explained according to the definition: "Meditation is the focused, continuous flow of perception regarding that object, undisturbed by other thoughts, and it is perfected by the first six limbs of yoga." Here, the "one-pointedness of perception" is the defining characteristic of meditation. || 93 ||

Regarding the verse **For binding the deer...**: The **sound** acts as a **snare** <note>original: *vāgurā*; a net used by hunters</note> for the **binding** (the removal of flickering movement) of the **internal mind**, which is like a **deer**. Just as a net stops the movement of a deer by binding it, the sound stops the movement of the mind. Furthermore, it acts as a **hunter** in the **slaying** (destruction of the mind's fluctuations) of that same internal deer-like mind. || 94 ||

->॥ VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ॥<-

Regarding **Bound by...**: When the mind is bound by the bond of sound, it completely renounces its flickering nature. Such a mind attains great stability, just as a bird becomes still when its wings are clipped. || 92 ||

Regarding **Renouncing all worries...**: With a one-pointed mind, having abandoned all worries of the inner and outer worlds, the man who desires the rank of Raja Yoga should find his focus in the sound alone. || 93 ||

Regarding **For binding the deer...**: This sound acts like a snare for binding the deer of the internal mind, or for putting an end to its flickering...

<summary>This page uses vivid metaphors—a bird with clipped wings and a deer caught in a hunter's snare—to describe how internal sound (nāda) anchors the mind. The commentary explicitly links these practices to the classical yogic stages of Dhāraṇā (concentration) and Dhyāna (meditation).</summary>
<keywords>Nāda Yoga, Dhāraṇā, Dhyāna, Raja Yoga, bird metaphor, deer metaphor, mental stillness, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It expands on the metaphors for sound (nāda) as a tool for mental control, transitioning from the "deer" and "hunter" metaphors to "horse and gate-bar" and finally an "alchemical" metaphor involving mercury and sulfur.</meta>

<page-num>( 173 )</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

### Root Text

**For the horse of the internal mind belonging to the self-controlled yogi, sound acts as a gate-bar. Therefore, the <term>contemplation of sound</term> <note>original: *nādopāstir*</note> must be practiced daily by the yogi. || 95 ||**

**Bound and freed from its restlessness through the <term>calcination</term> <note>original: *jāraṇāt*; an alchemical term for processing or consuming a substance to change its state</note> by the sulfur of sound, the mercury of the mind attains the ability to roam in the sky known as the <term>Supportless</term> <note>original: *nirālamba*; the state of consciousness that rests on nothing but itself</note>. || 96 ||**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the previous verse [94]: Just as a hunter kills the deer, so sound—through its own power—removes the restlessness of the internal mind. The internal mind itself is the **deer** <note>original: *sāraṅgo hariṇas*</note>. Its "binding" consists of removing the production of various mental fluctuations; in this process, the sound acts as the **hunter** <note>original: *vyādhāyate*</note>. Just as a hunter kills a deer caught in a net, sound "slays" the mind that has become absorbed in it. || 94 ||

Regarding the verse **For the horse...** [95]: The internal mind of the self-controlled yogi is like a **horse** <note>original: *vājin*</note> because of its speed and agility. Sound acts as the **gate-bar** <note>original: *parigha*; a heavy wooden or iron bolt used to lock a stable door</note>. Just as a gate-bar prevents a horse from escaping the stable, the sound prevents the internal mind from wandering elsewhere. For this reason, the worship or contemplation of sound must be practiced daily by the yogi. The word **"hi"** <note>original: *hi*</note> is an indeclinable used for emphasis, meaning "certainly." || 95 ||

Regarding the verse **Bound...** [96]: Sound itself is like a specific secondary mineral called **sulfur** <note>original: *gandhaka*</note>. Through **calcination** <note>original: *jāraṇa*</note>—which means the removal of restlessness through the union with the "sulfur" of sound—the mind is "bound," meaning it is focused solely on the sound. <margin>In the context of alchemy, this refers to forming a pill (*guṭikā*).</margin> Consequently, the mind has **abandoned its restlessness**, which consists of transforming into the shapes of various sensory objects. <margin>In the context of alchemy, this means removing the volatility of mercury.</margin> The mind itself is **mercury** <note>original: *pārada*; also known as quicksilver</note>. It attains the state of "roaming in the sky" of the **Supportless** <note>original: *nirālamba*</note>, which is another name for the Absolute Brahman.

->|| VERNACULAR PARAPHRASE ||<-

Sound is like a net for removing restlessness. Just as a deer’s wandering is ended by the binding of a net, so sound—by its own power—removes the restlessness of the inner mind. The inner mind is the deer, and sound acts like a hunter in the process of binding it. || 94 ||

Regarding the verse **For the horse...**: The inner mind of the yogi is like a horse. Sound is like the iron gate-bar used to restrain it. Just as an iron bolt is placed on the door of a stable to stop a horse from going outside, sound is what stops the inner mind from going toward sensory objects. Therefore, it is proper for a yogi to firmly practice the contemplation of sound every single day. || 95 ||

Regarding the verse **Bound...**: Through the "calcination" by sound, the mind is bound and its restlessness is removed. Such a mind creates an unbroken flow of consciousness focused on the Supportless Brahman. This is just like how **mercury**—once bound through calcination with **sulfur**—allows one to fly through the sky if the resulting "pill" is kept in the mouth. || 96 ||

<summary>These verses describe sound as the ultimate restraint for the mind, using the metaphors of a hunter's net, a stable's gate-bar, and the alchemical stabilization of mercury to explain how meditation on internal sound leads to the state of Samadhi.</summary>
<keywords>Nada Yoga, mind control, mercury alchemy, sulfur, Niralamba, Samadhi, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, specifically focusing on the practice of Nāda Yoga (the Yoga of Inner Sound). It completes the commentary on verse 96 regarding alchemical metaphors for the mind and proceeds to explain verses 97, 98, and 99, which use metaphors of the serpent, fire, and the deer.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>IN THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA</header>

### Root Text

**Upon hearing the <term>Inner Sound</term> <note>original: *nāda*; the mystical internal resonance heard during meditation</note>, the serpent of the internal mind quickly forgets everything and, becoming one-pointed, does not run anywhere. || 97 ||**

**Fire kindled in wood is extinguished along with the wood. The mind applied to the Sound dissolves along with the Sound. || 98 ||**

**For the <term>deer</term> <note>original: *hariṇa*</note> of the internal mind, which is frozen and attached to the sound of bells and the like, it is easy to shoot if the archer is skilled in aiming. || 99 ||**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

Regarding the previous discussion of the "Supportless" [verse 96]: Because the divine <note>original: *deva*</note> space is indivisible, "roaming" within it means the flow of mental fluctuations takes on the form of that space. From another perspective, it means "roaming in the sky." Just as processed mercury <note>original: *baddha-pārada*; literally "bound mercury," referring to mercury stabilized through alchemy</note> allows one to travel through the sky, so the **bound mind** creates an uninterrupted flow of consciousness shaped as the **Absolute** <note>original: *brahmākāra-vṛtti*</note>. || 96 ||

Regarding the verse **Upon hearing the Sound...** [97]: From hearing the <term>Unstruck Sound</term> <note>original: *anāhata-svana*; the sound produced without two things striking together, heard in the heart center</note>, the internal mind—which is like a **serpent** <note>original: *bhujangama*</note>—quickly becomes still. The mind is compared to a serpent because it is both restless and a lover of music. Making the entire universe an object of forgetfulness, it becomes one-pointed, flowing only in the form of the Sound, and does not run toward any other sensory objects. Following this are verses describing **Samadhi** <note>an advanced state of meditative absorption</note>.

As stated in the *Vishnu Purana*:
> "Samadhi is defined as that grasping of the essential form by the mind which is free from imagination."

And according to the definition in the *Patanjali Yoga Sutras* [3.3]:
> "That same [meditation], when it shines forth as the object alone and is as if void of its own form, is Samadhi."

Through these, the state of <term>Cognitive Absorption</term> <note>original: *samprajñāta-samādhi*; a state where the mind is fully absorbed in a single object of focus</note> is explained. || 97 ||

Regarding the verse **Fire kindled...** [98]: Just as fire kindled in wood <note>original: *dāru*</note> is extinguished along with the wood, abandoning its flaming form and remaining only as the wood's essence, so the mind applied to the Sound dissolves along with the Sound. By the destruction of the qualities of **Restlessness** <note>original: *rajas*</note> and **Inertia** <note>original: *tamas*</note>, only **Purity** <note>original: *sattva*</note> and residual impressions remain. On this point, the *Maitrayaniya Upanishad* mantra says:

> "Just as fire without fuel is extinguished in its own source, so the mind, through the cessation of its fluctuations, is extinguished in its own source." || 98 ||

Regarding the verse **For the deer...** [99]: The sounds of bells and the like include the conch, the drum, the kettle-drum, thunder, and so on. These...

->|| VERNACULAR ||<-

Regarding the verse **Upon hearing the Sound...** [97]: By hearing the <term>Unstruck Sound</term>, the serpent of the internal mind quickly forgets everything and, becoming one-pointed in consciousness, does not wander into other sensory objects. || 97 ||

Regarding the verse **Fire kindled...** [98]: Fire active in wood, along with that wood, abandons its flaming form and attains peace; in the same way, the mind active in the Sound becomes absorbed along with that Sound. || 98 ||

Regarding the verse **For the deer...** [99]: For the deer of the internal mind, which is motionless because it is attached to the sounds of bells and the like...

<summary>This page explains how the inner sound (Nāda) acts as a captivating force that stills the restless mind, using metaphors of a snake charmer's music, fire consuming wood, and a hunter's bell luring a deer.</summary>
<keywords>Nāda Yoga, Samadhi, mind control, serpent metaphor, fire metaphor, Vishnu Purana, Patanjali Yoga Sutras, Unstruck Sound, internal mind</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, focusing on the practice of Nāda Yoga (Internal Sound). It concludes the commentary on verse 99, which used the metaphor of a deer being "hunted" by the yogi, and introduces verses 100 and 1 (of the final sequence), detailing the progression from the perception of sound to the absolute state of Brahman.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>FOURTH INSTRUCTION</header>

**ROOT TEXT: The vibration perceived from the Unstruck Sound is the Object of Knowledge. Within that Knowledge is the mind. The mind dissolves there; that is the highest state of Vishnu. || 100 ||**

**As long as the perception of sound continues, the conceptualization of Space remains. That which is soundless is the Supreme Brahman, sung of as the Supreme Soul. || 1 ||**

->|| COMMENTARY ||<-

...of those [bells], the **Inner Sound** <note>original: *nāda*</note> is attached to them. Therefore, the internal mind—which is like a **deer** <note>original: *hariṇa*</note>—becomes frozen and motionless. In the context of the mind, "striking" <note>original: *praharaṇa*</note> refers to the blocking of various mental fluctuations. In the context of the deer, "striking" means the act of killing. However, in the context of the breath <note>original: *vāyu*</note>, which moves as fast as an arrow, it refers to the skilled practitioner's ability to restrain it in the **Opening of Brahman** <note>original: *brahmarandhra*; the crown of the head</note> via the **Central Channel** <note>original: *suṣumnā-mārga*</note>. If one is proficient in "aiming"—meaning the application of the arrow to the bow—then this task is easily accomplished. || 99 ||

Regarding the verse **The vibration perceived...** [100]: The resonance <note>original: *nirhrāda*</note> that is heard or perceived from the **Unstruck Sound** <note>original: *anāhata-śabda*</note> contains within it the "Object of Knowledge." This Object is the **Light**—the self-luminous consciousness. Within that Knowledge is the internal mind, which has taken the very form of that knowledge. There, in that Object, the mind reaches dissolution <note>original: *vilaya*</note>; through **supreme detachment** <note>original: *para-vairāgya*</note>, it becomes void of all fluctuations, leaving only residual impressions. That is the "Highest State of Vishnu"—the all-pervading Self. It is called "The State" <note>original: *padam*</note> because it is "attained" <note>original: *padyate*</note> or realized by yogis when they are free from the limiting conditions <note>original: *upādhi*</note> of mental activity. || 100 ||

Regarding the verse **As long as...** [1]: As long as the sound—the vibration of the Unstruck—continues to be heard, the conceptualization of **Space** <note>original: *ākāśa*</note> remains complete. This is because sound is the specific quality of space, and there is no difference between a quality and that which possesses it. When the sound dissolves along with the mind, it becomes "Soundless." This soundless state, which is the meaning of the term "Supreme Brahman," is sung of as the **Supreme Soul** <note>original: *paramātmā*</note>. The essence is that when all fluctuations dissolve and one remains established in their true nature, that state is expressed by the words "Supreme Brahman" and "Supreme Soul." || 1 ||

->|| VERNACULAR TRANSLATION ||<-

Stopping the various fluctuations—the "striking" of the mind—is easy to do. || 99 ||

Regarding the verse **The vibration perceived...** [100]: Whoever hears the vibration of the Unstruck Sound finds within it the self-luminous consciousness. The mind within that consciousness becomes absorbed into it. That is the highest state of Vishnu, which is attained by yogis. || 100 ||

Regarding the verse **As long as...** [1]: As long as the internal resonance <note>original: *nāda*</note> is being heard, Space remains. When the sound dissolves along with the mind, and the resonance ceases, the consciousness stands firm in its own true nature. That which is without sound is Brahman; it is called the Supreme Soul. || 1 ||

<summary>This page describes the final stages of Nāda Yoga, where the practitioner moves from hearing internal sounds to a state of complete mental dissolution (samadhi). It identifies this soundless, absolute state as the "Highest State of Vishnu" and the "Supreme Soul."</summary>
<keywords>Anāhata (Unstruck Sound), Nāda Yoga, Samādhi, Vishnu's Padam, Brahman, Paramātmā, mental dissolution, Suṣumnā</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction on Samādhi (Absolute Absorption) from the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. Having previously discussed the internal sound (nāda), the text now identifies this sound with the primordial feminine energy (Shakti) and describes the ultimate goal of all yogic practices: the attainment of Raja Yoga, the state of supreme consciousness where the yogi transcends death.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA</header>

**ROOT TEXT: Whatever is heard in the form of Sound is nothing but Shakti (Divine Energy). That which is the "End of the Elements" and formless—that alone is the Supreme Lord. || 2 ||**

**All the methods of Hatha and Laya (Dissolution) Yoga are for the sake of achieving Raja Yoga. A person who has ascended to Raja Yoga becomes a "Cheater of Time" <note>This means they have conquered death and the limitations of temporal existence.</note>. || 3 ||**

**Reality (the mind) is the seed; Hatha is the field; indifference is the water. Through these three, the "Wish-Fulfilling Vine" <note>original: *kalpalatikā*; a celestial vine from Indian mythology that grants all desires.</note> of the Mindless State <note>original: *unmanī*; a state of consciousness beyond the reach of the mundane mind.</note> flourishes immediately. || 4 ||**

## || COMMENTARY ||

**Regarding the verse "Whatever...":** Whatever is heard or perceived in the form of Sound—specifically the **Unstruck Sound** <note>original: *anāhata-dhvani*; sound produced without two objects striking together, heard only in deep meditation.</note>—is **Shakti** <note>original: *śakti*; the kinetic power of the universe.</note> herself. That which is the **End of the Elements** <note>original: *tattvānta*</note> is the state in which all fundamental elements of reality dissolve. Such a state is **Formless** <note>original: *nirākāra*</note>, and that is the **Supreme Lord** <note>original: *parameśvara*</note>. This refers to the **Self** <note>original: *ātman*</note> that remains in its own essential nature when all mental fluctuations have ceased. Through verses such as "fire produced within wood" and others, the text describes **Objectless Samadhi** <note>original: *asamprajñāta samādhi*; the highest state of absorption where no individual thought remains</note>, which is a synonym for **Raja Yoga**. || 2 ||

**Regarding the verse "All...":** There are methods for both **Hatha** and **Laya**. The methods of Hatha consist of **Postures** <note>original: *āsana*</note>, **Breath Retention** <note>original: *kumbhaka*</note>, and **Seals** <note>original: *mudrā*</note>. The methods of Laya (Dissolution) include **Concentration on the Sound** <note>original: *nādānusandhāna*</note> and the **Shambhavi Seal** <note>original: *śāmbhavī-mudrā*; a technique of gazing internally while the eyes are open</note>. These have been taught for the **attainment**—meaning the successful completion—of **Raja Yoga**, which is characterized by the restraint of all mental fluctuations. A person who has **Ascended**—meaning one who has fully reached or obtained this state—is a **Cheater of Time**, meaning he deceives or conquers death. || 3 ||

**Regarding the verse "Reality...":** **Reality** <note>original: *tattva*</note> refers to the **Mind** <note>original: *citta*</note>, which is like a **Seed** because it transforms into the "sprout" of the Mindless State. **Hatha** refers to **Breath Control** <note>original: *prāṇāyāma*</note> characterized by the union of the inward and outward breaths; this is like the **Field**. In this field of Breath Control, the Wish-Fulfilling Vine of the Mindless State...

## || VERNACULAR EXPLANATION ||

<note>This section translates the Early Modern Hindi (Bhasha) commentary which summarizes the Sanskrit for a general audience.</note>

**Regarding the verse "Whatever...":** Whatever is heard in the form of Sound is Shakti. That in which the elements dissolve and which is formless is the Supreme Lord. || 2 ||

**Regarding the verse "All...":** All these methods of Hatha and Laya—Postures, Breath Retention, and Seals—are the methods of Hatha. Concentration on Sound and the Shambhavi Seal are the methods of Laya. These are spoken of for the sake of attaining Raja Yoga, which is the stopping of all mental fluctuations. A person who has attained Raja Yoga becomes a conqueror of death. || 3 ||

**Regarding the verse "Reality...":** In truth, the Mind is the Seed of the sprout of the Mindless State. Hatha (Breath Control) is the Field. Indifference (detachment) is the Water. Through these three, the Mindless State—which is the Wish-Fulfilling Vine that fulfills all spiritual desires—is produced quickly. || 4 ||

<summary>This page explains that meditative sound is a manifestation of divine energy (Shakti) and that all yogic techniques—postures, breathing, and concentration—are merely tools to reach the ultimate state of Raja Yoga, where the mind is transcended and death is conquered.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Nada, Shakti, Samadhi, Unmani, deathlessness, Hatha Yoga, Laya Yoga, breath control</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions on the practice of Nadanusandhana (meditation on the inner sound) and describes the resulting state of Samadhi, known here as the Unmani state.</meta>

<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>Fourth Instruction</header>
<sig>23</sig>

# **Main Verse:** By constant meditation on the inner sound, the accumulation of sins is destroyed. The mind and the breath certainly dissolve into the Stainless One. 5.
# **Main Verse:** He never hears the sound of the conch or the kettle-drum. Through the "no-mind" state, the body certainly becomes like a piece of wood. 6.
# **Main Verse:** Freed from all states and devoid of all anxieties, the yogi remains like a corpse. He is liberated; of this, there is no doubt. 7.

->॥ Commentary ॥<-

That very indifference is supreme detachment, which acts like water because it is the cause for the growth <note>of the state of Samadhi</note>. The specific mental impression caused by supreme detachment is "objectless meditation" <note>original: "asamprajnata"</note>, according to its definition. Through these three, the state of "no-mind" <term>Unmani|A high state of yoga where the mind ceases its habitual activity</term> or objectless meditation—which is like a wish-fulfilling vine <note>original: "kalpalatika"</note> because it accomplishes all desired goals—is immediately and quickly set in motion, or rather, it is produced. 4.

**By constant...** Always and at all times, through meditation on the inner sound <note>original: "nadanusandhana"</note>, the collections of sins or groups of demerits are destroyed and perish. In the quality-less <note>original: "nirguna"</note> consciousness, which is the Stainless One, the mind and the breath <note>original: "chitta-marutau"</note> certainly and surely become dissolved or merged. 5.

<margin>The state of the Yogi</margin> He describes the state of the yogi who has attained the "no-mind" state in eight verses. **Conch and kettle-drum...** The conch is a water-born shell, and the kettle-drum is a specific musical instrument; he never, at any time, hears the resonance or sound of these two. "Conch and kettle-drum" are used here as an illustration for all types of sound. Through the no-mind state, the body surely becomes like a piece of wood. This means it becomes motionless or inert. 6.

**All...** There are five "emergent" states characterized by waking, dreaming, deep sleep, fainting, and death. He is specially freed from or devoid of these. He is separated from all anxieties or memories. This yoga is the cessation of all mental fluctuations...

->॥ Vernacular Translation ॥<-

**By constant...** Through constant and perpetual meditation on the inner sound, the collection of sins reaches destruction. In the pure, quality-less consciousness, the mind and the vital air <note>breath</note>—these two—are surely dissolved. 5.

**Conch and kettle-drum...** Through the state of no-mind <note>unmani avastha</note>, the yogi's body becomes inert like a piece of wood. At that time, he does not hear the sound of conches or kettle-drums at any moment. 6.

**All...** One who is free from the five states of waking, dreaming, deep sleep, fainting, and death, and who is devoid of all anxiety, and who remains like a dead man—that yogi, possessing the "fourth state" <note>original: "turya avastha," referring to pure consciousness beyond the three common states</note>, is liberated even while living. 7.

<summary>This page describes how meditation on the inner sound leads to the 'Unmani' or no-mind state, where the yogi's mind and breath dissolve into pure consciousness. In this state of Samadhi, the yogi becomes unresponsive to external sounds and enters a condition of living liberation, transcending the five common states of existence.</summary>
<keywords>Nadanusandhana, Unmani, Samadhi, Chaitanya, liberation, inner sound, breath control, yogic states</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the yogi's state in Samadhi (the "no-mind" state or Unmani), emphasizing his immunity to death, karma, and sensory distractions. It continues the "Fourth Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>On the Light on Hatha Yoga</header>

# **Main Verse:** He is not consumed by Time, nor is he bound by his actions. The yogi united in Samadhi cannot be overcome by anyone or anything. 8.
# **Main Verse:** He perceives neither smell, taste, form, touch, nor sound. He knows neither himself nor others—the yogi united in Samadhi. 9.
# **Main Verse:** The mind is neither asleep nor awake; it is free from both memory and forgetting.

### ॥ Commentary ॥

<margin>The state of Living Liberation</margin>
The yogi who possesses the "restraint of the mind" <note>original: "chitta-nirodha"</note> is in the Fourth State <term>Turya|The state of pure consciousness that transcends waking, dreaming, and deep sleep</term>. He is "liberated while living" <note>original: "jivanmukta"</note>. Because all mental fluctuations have ceased, he remains in his own true nature. This is stated in Patanjali’s *Yoga Sutras*: "Then the Seer abides in his own nature" <note>original: "tada drashtuh svarupe'vasthanam" - Yoga Sutra 1.3</note>. The rest is clear. 7.

**He is not consumed...** The yogi united in Samadhi is not "consumed" or eaten by Time, meaning Death. This is the meaning: he is not killed. He is not "bound" or troubled by the results of his actions <note>original: "karma"</note>, whether good or bad, which normally cause the cycle of birth and death. As Patanjali’s *Yoga Sutras* state in the section on Samadhi: "From that, there is the cessation of afflictions and actions" <note>original: "tatah klesha-karma-nivritti" - Yoga Sutra 4.30</note>. Furthermore, he cannot be "overcome" or controlled by any other person, nor by mechanical devices, spells, or mantras. 8.

**Neither smell...** The yogi united in Samadhi does not perceive smell, whether pleasant or unpleasant. He does not perceive taste, which is of six kinds: sweet, sour, salty, pungent, astringent, and bitter. He does not perceive form, which is of seven kinds: white, blue, yellow, red, green, brown, and variegated. He does not perceive touch, whether cold, hot, or neutral. He does not perceive sound, whether internal or external, such as the sound of a conch, a kettle-drum, the ocean, or thunderclouds. He knows neither "himself" (the body) nor "others" (other people). This lack of perception applies to everything. The *Amarakosha* <note>a famous Sanskrit dictionary</note> notes: "The word *Atman* refers to the body, firmness, the soul, nature, and the Supreme Self." 9.

**The mind...** For such a yogi, the mind (the internal organ) is not asleep, because the darkness of ignorance that covers it is gone.

### ॥ Vernacular Explanation ॥

**He is not consumed...** Through Samadhi, the liberated yogi does not meet with destruction by the hand of Death. Because of the good and bad deeds he has done, he is not afflicted by the suffering of birth and death. No man, nor any machine, ritual, or spell, has the power to overcome him. 8.

**Neither smell...** The yogi joined in Samadhi does not know fragrance or foul odors. He does not know the sweet, bitter, astringent, pungent, salty, or sour tastes. He does not know forms such as white, blue, red, green, and yellow. He does not know touch, such as cold or heat. He does not know sounds, such as those of the conch, the drum, the ocean, or the clouds. He knows neither the "Self" (the body) nor other people. 9.

**The mind...** The mind of that yogi is neither sleeping nor waking, nor is there any memory...

<summary>This page describes the transcendental state of a yogi in Samadhi, who becomes immune to death (Time) and karma, and loses all external sensory awareness and ego-consciousness. The commentary references Patanjali's Yoga Sutras to validate this state of "Living Liberation" where the mind transcends ordinary states like sleep and wakefulness.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Turya, Karma, sensory withdrawal, Patanjali, Yoga Sutras, Jivanmukta, Immortality, Unmani</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the yogi's state in Samadhi from page 178, specifically focusing on the cessation of mental fluctuations and the physical signs of a "living liberated" soul (jivanmukta).</meta>

# **Fourth Instruction**
( 179 )

**Main Verse:** For him whose mind neither sets nor rises, he is truly liberated. 10.
**Main Verse:** The yogi united in Samadhi perceives neither cold nor heat, neither pain nor pleasure, nor honor nor dishonor. 11.
**Main Verse:** Established in himself, he who remains as if asleep while in the waking state, and who is without inhalation and exhalation, is certainly liberated. 12.

->॥ **Commentary** ॥<-

In the internal organ <term>Antahkarana|The "inner instrument" comprising the mind, intellect, and ego</term>, which is composed of the three qualities <term>Guna|The three fundamental qualities of nature: Sattva (light), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia)</term>, when the quality of darkness <note>original: "tamas"</note> emerges and overcomes the qualities of purity <note>original: "sattva"</note> and activity <note>original: "rajas" </note>, it covers all the senses. At that time, because the internal organ does not take the form of any external object, it is called "sleep." This state is not "waking," because there is no grasping of objects through the senses. It is described as being free from both memory <note>original: "smriti"</note> and forgetting <note>original: "vismriti"</note>. It is free from memory because there is an absence of general mental fluctuations <note>original: "vritti"</note> and an absence of anything to trigger them. It is free from forgetting because the mental impressions <note>original: "samskara"</note> that facilitate memory are absent. 

**It neither sets nor rises:** "Setting" means reaching destruction. The mind does not "set" because the mind-stuff still exists in the form of remaining impressions. It does not "rise" because no new mental fluctuations are produced. Such a person is "liberated while living" <note>original: "jivanmukta"</note>. 10.

**He perceives neither...** The yogi united in Samadhi does not perceive cold and heat—this is a compound term. He does not perceive cold or hot objects, nor does he perceive pain <note>original: "duhkha"</note>, such as the pain caused by others through beatings and the like. He does not perceive pleasure <note>original: "sukha"</note>, such as the means of pleasure like fragrant sandalwood paste. The word "likewise" <note>original: "tatha"</note> connects these ideas. He does not perceive honor, meaning respect shown by others, nor dishonor, meaning being treated with contempt. The verb "perceives" applies to each of these statements. 11.

**Established in himself...** "Established in himself" <note>original: "svastha"</note> means one whose senses and internal organ are clear and tranquil. This term excludes states like drowsiness or fainting. By saying "in the waking state," the states of dreaming and deep sleep are excluded. One who remains "as if asleep"—meaning the yogi stays in a state similar to sleep, where the activities of the physical senses are void—is established. <note>The commentator notes a grammatical point regarding the Sanskrit verb form 'avatisthate'.</note> "Without inhalation and exhalation": inhalation <note>original: "nihshvasa"</note> is taking external air into the abdomen, and exhalation <note>original: "uchhvasa"</note> is expelling the air residing in the abdomen. Being free of these two, he remains established; this phrase also connects here. He is "certainly," or without doubt, liberated. <meta>catchword: without doubt-</meta>

->॥ **Vernacular Language Version** ॥<-

Neither memory nor forgetting exists; he reaches neither destruction nor arising. Such a yogi is liberated while living. 10.
**He perceives neither...** The yogi joined in Samadhi does not know cold, heat, pleasure, honor, or dishonor. 11.
**Established in himself...** One whose internal organ is tranquil—such a yogi, staying in the waking state as if in sleep, and remaining without inhalation or exhalation, is liberated while living. 12.

<vocab>Living Liberation (Jivanmukta), Deep Absorption (Samadhi), Waking State (Jagrad-avastha), Established in Self (Svastha), Inhalation (Nishvasa), Exhalation (Uchvasa), Internal Organ (Antahkarana), Purity (Sattva), Activity (Rajas), Inertia (Tamas)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the state of a Jivanmukta (liberated soul) as one who is physically awake but mentally in a state beyond sleep, unaffected by sensory pairs of opposites like heat/cold or honor/dishonor. It provides a technical psychological explanation of how Samadhi differs from ordinary sleep through the balance of the three Gunas.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Jivanmukta, mental fluctuations, Gunas, sensory withdrawal, breathlessness, equanimity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, specifically expanding on Verse 12 regarding the state of a living liberated soul (jivanmukta) and moving into the invincibility of the yogi in Samadhi (Verse 13) and the obstacles they may face.</meta>

# **In the Light on Hatha Yoga**
( 180 )

**Main Verse: The yogi united in Samadhi is invincible to all weapons, cannot be overcome by any living being, and is beyond the reach of spells or mystical diagrams. 13.**

->॥ **Commentary** ॥<-

...truly liberated. The nature of one who is "liberated while living" <term>Jivanmukta|A person who has achieved spiritual liberation while still inhabiting a physical body</term> has been described by the sage Dattatreya: "One endowed with meditation on the Attributeless <note>original: 'nirguna'</note> should thereafter practice Samadhi. By practicing for only twelve days, one may attain Samadhi. By restraining the breath, the wise one surely becomes liberated while living." 12.

**Invincible:** This refers to the yogi united in Samadhi. In the phrase **to all weapons**, the genitive case <note>original: 'shasthi'</note> is used in a general sense to mean "by all weapons." The meaning is that he cannot be killed. In the phrase **by all living beings**, the genitive also implies "by"; he is **unconquerable**, meaning he cannot be overcome by the physical strength of any living creature. Regarding **mantras and yantras** <term>Mantra and Yantra|Sacred chants and geometric diagrams used in rituals, often for protection or to influence others</term>, he is **beyond reach**, meaning he cannot be controlled by spells or diagrams intended for subjugation, killing, or expulsion. 

Many obstacles <note>original: 'vighna'</note> arise for a yogi who has attained this state. Because knowledge of these is necessary to ward them off, they are shown here. Dattatreya states: "Laziness is the first obstacle; the second is said to be the company of the wicked; the third is the practice of spells <note>original: 'mantra-sadhanam'</note>; the fourth is alchemy <note>original: 'dhatuvada'</note>. Thus know the knowers of Yoga." 

In the *Markandeya Purana* <note>An ancient Indian scripture</note>: "Disturbances <note>original: 'upasarga'</note> arise when the Yogi sees the Self. I shall describe them to you in brief; listen to me. A person may desire ritual acts, or various desires; women, the fruits of charity, worldly knowledge, illusions, precious metals, wealth, or possessions. He may desire godhood, the status of the king of gods, the practice of rejuvenation <note>original: 'rasayana'</note>, or miraculous travels to Mount Meru. He may desire the fruits of ancestral rites <note>original: 'shraddha'</note>, the rewards of vows, or results from fasting, public works, and the worship of gods and ancestors. Disturbed by these, he seeks results from guests and rituals. In this way, obstacles arise where the non-yogi would turn back. But by keeping the mind attached to Brahman, one is freed from these disturbances." 

In the *Padma Purana*: "If his mind is not thrown off by these interruptions, then he attains the supreme Brahman, which is very difficult to reach." In the *Yoga Bhaskara*: "Supported by pure resolve <note>original: 'sattvic dhriti'</note>, the yogi remains steady in his essential being. Meditating on the Attributeless with his mind, he is freed from disturbances. Thus practicing yoga, indifferent even to the status of Indra <note>the King of the Gods</note>, and abandoning the desire for supernatural powers <note>original: 'siddhi'</note>, the sage becomes liberated while living. For fear of being too lengthy, many obstacles have not been mentioned. They should be warded off by the yogi through meditation on Vishnu and Hari <note>names for the Supreme Divine</note>." 13.

->॥ **Vernacular Translation** ॥<-

**Invincible:** The yogi united by the practice of Samadhi is impossible to destroy with any weapon, and is not capable of being mastered by the power of any living being, and is not susceptible to being controlled or killed by the use of mantras, yantras, or tantras. 13.

<summary>This page describes the invincibility and spiritual protection of a yogi in Samadhi, while also listing various worldly and supernatural obstacles that can distract a practitioner from reaching the ultimate state of Brahman.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Jivanmukta, Dattatreya, invincibility, obstacles, vighna, Markandeya Purana, alchemy, spells, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Instruction (Samadhi) of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. The previous page discussed the invincibility of a yogi in Samadhi and the obstacles they face. This page shifts focus to Verse 14, emphasizing that true spiritual knowledge is impossible without the mastery of breath, essence (bindu), and mind.</meta>

# Fourth Instruction ( 181 )

**Main Verse:**
## As long as the moving life-breath does not enter the central path,
## and as long as the essence does not become firm through the steady control of that breath...

### ॥ Commentary ॥

The author now refutes the supposed "knowledge" of those who are not yogis, stating that true knowledge belongs only to yogis. **As long as...** In the **central path**, meaning the *sushumna* <term>Sushumna|The primary energy channel in the subtle body that runs along the spinal column</term>, the **moving breath** <note>original: 'marutah'</note>—**as long as** it **does not enter**, meaning it does not thoroughly penetrate and reach the "opening of Brahman" <term>Brahmarandhra|The spiritual center at the crown of the head, considered the exit point for the soul</term>. This implies that because it has not stayed there, it has not become steady. Breath that does not travel through the *sushumna* is called "unperfected" <note>original: 'asiddha'</note>. As it is stated in the *Amritasiddhi*: 
> "As long as the breath is not motionless and does not travel through the middle path, know that breath to be unperfected, merely following the path of past actions."

The **life-breath** <note>original: 'pranavata'</note> is that which animates and gives life. Through its **steady control**, meaning its stabilization via breath retention <term>Kumbhaka|The practice of holding the breath to stabilize energy</term>, the **essence** <note>original: 'bindu'</note> or vital seed does not become **firm** or steady. It was stated earlier in this text that the stability of the essence depends on the stability of the breath: "When the mind is steady, the breath is steady; then the essence becomes steady." Without this stability, the yogi is considered unperfected. It is said in the *Amritasiddhi*: 
> "Until then, even if he practices restraints, the man is unperfected and considered a worldly being. As long as the 'king of essences' [semen/mercury], which is of the nature of Brahman, resides in the body, he is unperfected; know him as a mere spiritual student... afflicted by old age, death, and all types of suffering."

**As long as the reality** <note>original: 'tattvam'</note>, meaning the **mind** <note>original: 'chittam'</note> during meditation—the mind focused on the object of meditation—does not become **spontaneous** <note>original: 'sahaja-sadrisham'</note>... that is, the flow of mental states does not naturally take the form of the object of meditation. This failure to arise occurs because the breath has not been controlled. By the "logic of the lamp on the threshold" <note>original: 'dehali-dipa-nyaya'</note> <note>A logical principle where a single lamp placed on a door-frame illuminates both the room inside and the porch outside</note>, this point about breath control applies here as well. The stability of the mind follows the stability of the breath, as stated in the *Amritasiddhi*: 
> "When the breath 'dies' [becomes still] in the middle channel through central yoga, then the essence and the mind also 'die' along with the breath."

The unperfected state in the absence of this is also mentioned in the *Amritasiddhi*: 
> "As long as the mind flickers toward external or internal objects, know that mind to be unperfected and bound by the qualities of past actions."

**Until then, whatever knowledge**—referring to "book knowledge" or verbal knowledge <note>original: 'shabdam jnanam'</note>—someone speaks of, **how** can that knowledge be anything but **boastful, false talk**? It is "boastful" because the speaker thinks, "By speaking of knowledge, I will become worshipful in the world." It is "false talk" because it is a lie backed by vanity. Without the mastery of breath, essence, and mind, true knowledge cannot exist, and the cycle of rebirth <note>original: 'samsara'</note> remains unavoidable. As the *Amritasiddhi* says: 
> "When the breath moves, the essence is said to move. For one whose essence moves in the body, the mind is also flickering. When the essence, the mind, and the breath are all moving, people are born and die—this word is the truth of truths."

It is also said in the *Yogabija*: 
> "If the mind appears to be destroyed [stilled], then surely the breath is also destroyed. If this is not so, then there is no real scripture, no self-realization, no teacher, and no liberation."

By this, it is understood that only with the mastery of breath, essence, and mind does true knowledge...

### ॥ Vernacular Paraphrase ॥

**As long as...** As long as the life-breath traveling in the *sushumna* path does not enter the "opening of Brahman" at the crown of the head; and as long as the essence does not become steady through the stabilization of the breath via breath-retention...

<vocab>Life-breath [Prana-vayu], Central channel [Sushumna], Crown center [Brahmarandhra], The Perfection of Immortality [Amritasiddhi], Essence/Vital seed [Bindu], The Seed of Yoga [Yogabija], Breath retention [Kumbhaka], Knowledge [Jnana], Vitality [Virya], Mind [Chitta]</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that true spiritual knowledge is a physical and mental state achieved through Hatha Yoga, not just intellectual study. It argues that unless the breath enters the central channel (sushumna) and the mind and vital essence (bindu) are stabilized, any claim to enlightenment is merely boastful lying.</summary>
<keywords>Sushumna, Brahmarandhra, Bindu, Kumbhaka, Prana, Chitta, Amritasiddhi, Yogabija, false knowledge, Samadhi</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page concludes the specific discussion on the stabilization of breath and vital essence (bindu) before moving into the definitions of Samadhi. It addresses why Yoga is the primary path to liberation compared to the traditional paths of knowledge, action, and devotion, by arguing that those paths are actually components of Yoga itself.</meta>

( 182 )
-> In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika <-

**Root Verse:**
**As long as the Reality does not become as natural and spontaneous as one’s own being through meditation, so long does any talk of "knowledge" remain merely hypocritical, false chatter. ॥ 14 ॥**

**Thus ends the Fourth Instruction, titled "The Characteristics of Samadhi," in the *Hatha Pradipika*, composed by the king of yogis, Svatmarama, a gem in the lineage of the illustrious Sahajananda. Total verse count: 393. May this be offered to Lord Krishna.**

### || Commentary ||

Through this, it is indicated that liberation for the yogi is certain. It is stated in the *Amritasiddhi* <note>An influential 11th-century Vajrayana text on physical yoga</note>:
"Whatever state the breath <note>original: 'vayu'</note> reaches, the vital essence <note>original: 'bindu'</note> follows. Just as the breath is mastered, so is the refinement of the essence. When stilled, it removes disease; when matured, it grants the power of 'moving through space' <term>Khecharata|The yogic power of flight or spiritual transcendence</term>. When absorbed and motionless, it grants liberation. As is the state of the essence, so is the state of the mind."

Now, an objection is raised: "The Lord has spoken of three paths for the welfare of humanity: Knowledge <note>original: 'jnana'</note>, Action <note>original: 'karma'</note>, and Devotion <note>original: 'bhakti'</note>. He said there is no other way anywhere else. Since these three paths to liberation exist, why is Yoga alone stated here as the means to liberation?"

The answer is: "No, that is not so, for those paths are included within the 'limbs' of Yoga." 

To explain: The Vedas <note>original: 'shruti'</note> state: "The Self, indeed, is to be seen, heard, contemplated, and meditated upon." To achieve the supreme human goal of self-realization, the scriptures prescribe hearing <term>Shravana|The act of listening to sacred teachings</term>, contemplation <term>Manana|Reflecting deeply on what has been heard</term>, and profound meditation <term>Nididhyasana|Continuous, internalizing meditation</term>.

Among these, *hearing* and *contemplation* are included within "Self-study" <term>Svadhyaya|The fourth Niyama or observance in yoga, involving the study of sacred texts</term>, which is part of the Yogic observances <note>original: 'niyamas'</note>. "Self-study" refers to the study of scriptures concerning liberation. This study is only complete when the intended meaning is firmly decided; such decision-making regarding the meaning is achieved through hearing and contemplation. Thus, hearing and contemplation are included within Self-study. 

In his explanation of the observances, the sage Yajnavalkya clearly states that "hearing the established truths <note>original: 'siddhanta'</note> is called the 'hearing of Vedanta' by the wise." Through logical reasoning, the act of constant reflection—defined as "continuous practice"—is also included within the observances.

Furthermore, profound meditation <note>original: 'nididhyasana'</note>—which consists of a flow of consistent thoughts after blocking out inconsistent ones—is included under the definition of "meditation" <note>original: 'dhyana'</note>. It leads to liberation via self-realization through its perfected state, Samadhi. 

As for the Yoga of Action <note>original: 'karma yoga'</note>, which is the performance of selfless deeds with the mindset of offering them to the Lord, it is included within "Active Yoga" <term>Kriya Yoga|Practical yoga consisting of austerity, self-study, and devotion</term> as taught by Patanjali: "Austerity, self-study, and devotion to the Lord constitute Kriya Yoga." This is part of the Yogic observances. Regarding "Austerity" <note>original: 'tapas'</note>, it is described in the *Ishvara Gita*...

### || Vernacular Language ||

As long as the mind-stream does not take the form of the Infinite <note>original: 'brahma'</note> during the contemplation of the Reality, then any "knowledge" one speaks of—with the egoistic thought of "I shall become worthy of worship by speaking this knowledge"—that is... 
<meta>catchword: is...</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the formal instruction on Samadhi, emphasizing that spiritual talk is meaningless without actual meditative experience. It argues that Yoga is the ultimate path because it encompasses the traditional paths of knowledge, devotion, and action within its own practices.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, Svatmarama, Amritasiddhi, Bindu, Breath, Karma Yoga, Jnana Yoga, Bhakti Yoga, Kriya Yoga, Patanjali, Svadhyaya, Shravana, Manana</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the response to the objection that Knowledge, Action, and Devotion are separate paths from Yoga. The commentator Brahmananda is now explaining how these paths are integrated into the Yoga system, specifically referencing Patanjali’s Yoga Sutras to show that Devotion (Bhakti) is a component of the Eight-Limbed Yoga.</meta>

( 183 )
### Fourth Instruction

## || Commentary ||

"The ascetics say that the supreme austerity <term>Tapas|Disciplined practices of self-purification</term> is the drying up of the body through fasts such as the 'Paraka' and 'Chandrayana' <note>Traditional Vedic penances involving regulated fasting</note>." 

Self-study <term>Svadhyaya|Sacred recitation and study</term> is also described there: "The wise declare that self-study, which purifies the essence of a man, consists of the repetition <note>original: 'japa'</note> of the Vedanta, the Shatarudriya <note>A hymn to Shiva from the Yajurveda</note>, and the sacred syllable Om." 

Devotion to the Lord <term>Ishvarapranidhana|Complete surrender to God</term> is also described: "That unwavering devotion achieved through praise, remembrance, and worship, performed with speech, mind, and body—this is known as the worship of the Lord."

Patanjali has clarified in a subsequent sutra <note>Yoga Sutras 2.1-2</note> that this "Yoga of Action" <term>Kriya Yoga|The yoga of preliminary practice</term> is the cause of liberation, indirectly leading to the direct realization of the Self through the cultivation of meditative absorption <term>Samadhi</term> and the weakening of the mental afflictions <term>Kleshas</term>. 

By the grammatical derivation of "devotion" <note>original: 'bhakti'</note> as an instrument—meaning that by which the heart is made to serve or take the form of the Divine—it is referred to as the nine-fold **Practice-Devotion**: "Hearing, chanting, and remembering Vishnu; serving His feet, worshiping, bowing, servitude, friendship, and self-surrender." <note>A famous verse from the Bhagavata Purana</note>. 

This is included within the "Observances" <term>Niyamas|The second limb of Ashtanga Yoga</term> in the form of devotion to the Lord. Patanjali states that this is a cause for Samadhi: "Or [Samadhi is attained] through devotion to the Lord." <note>Yoga Sutras 1.23</note>. The meaning of this sutra is that the attainment of Samadhi—which is the fruit of practice—comes from a special kind of devotion, a profound placement of the self within the Lord.

If "devotion" is derived as a state of being—meaning the devotion that consists of the mind taking the very shape of the Divine—then it is called **Result-Devotion**. This is also termed **Love-Devotion** <term>Prema-bhakti|The highest state of ecstatic love for God</term>. 

Its definition has been given by Narayanatirtha: "The path of Love-Devotion is an uninterrupted, exclusive, and absolute flow of love directed toward the lotus feet of the Lord." 

Madhusudana Sarasvati <note>A renowned 16th-century philosopher of Advaita Vedanta</note> defines it as: "Devotion is a mental state where the mind, having first melted, takes on the form of the Lord." 

The fact that this leads to liberation through the direct realization of the Self is confirmed by Scripture: "Know Him through the yoga of faith, devotion, and meditation" <note>Shvetashvatara Upanishad 6.23</note>; and by Tradition: "Through devotion he comes to know Me in truth." <note>Bhagavad Gita 18.55</note>. 

However, the devotees of the Lord argue that since happiness itself is the ultimate goal of human life, then Love-Devotion—which is of the nature of unsurpassed bliss unmixed with any touch of suffering—is the ultimate goal in itself. This state is included within "Cognitive Absorption" <term>Samprajnata Samadhi|Meditation where an object of focus remains</term>.

Thus, it is proven that there is no means for attaining the supreme goal of human life that exists outside of the **Eight-Limbed Yoga**. ॥ 114 ॥

> For the wise, speech is only beneficial when it concerns the Absolute <note>original: 'Brahman'</note>; 
> Even if the phrasing is unrefined according to the standards of the time, 
> Just as a mother understands the protection requested 
> When a child cries out 'Ma!', though the word is imperfect. ॥ 1 ॥ 

> This work, which illuminates the true meaning and destroys the mass of darkness, 
> This 'Moonlight' <note>original: 'Jyotsna'</note> composed by Brahmananda, 
> Is hereby offered at the pair of auspicious feet of Lord Shiva. ॥ 2 ॥

Thus ends the Fourth Instruction, titled "The Definition of Samadhi," in the commentary named *Jyotsna* (The Moonlight), composed by Shri Brahmananda on the *Hatha Yoga Pradipika*. ॥ 4 ॥

-> Total verse count of the commentary: 2450 <-

---

## || Vernacular Summary ||

Understand that any talk of realization without the practice of Yoga is merely hypocritical and false. There is no means for the supreme goal of human life that is separate from the **Eight-Limbed Yoga**. ॥ 14 ॥

<summary>This page concludes the Hatha Yoga Pradipika commentary by integrating the paths of devotion and action into the framework of Patanjali's Eight-Limbed Yoga. It argues that Yoga is the comprehensive and essential path to liberation and the supreme bliss of Samadhi.</summary>

<keywords>Samadhi, Bhakti, Kriya Yoga, Patanjali, Prema-bhakti, Brahmananda, Jyotsna, Eight-Limbed Yoga, liberation, devotion</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the fourth chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika and begins a publisher's advertisement catalog. It transitions from the spiritual conclusion of the text to the commercial reality of 19th-century book production in Mumbai.</meta>

( 184 )
### In the Hatha Yoga Pradipika

Thus ends the fourth instruction, titled "The Definition of Samadhi" <term>Samadhi|The state of intense meditative absorption where the meditator and the object of meditation become one</term>, in the commentary named "The Desire of the Mind" <note>original: 'Manobhilashini'</note>, composed by Shridhara—the son of Jatashankara, born into the lineage of Dadhyank. || 4 || Total verse count for the vernacular text: 1657.

This book was printed, scholarly corrected, and brought to publication in the city of Mumbai at the press known as "Prabodha Ratnakara" <note>meaning "The Ocean of Awakening"</note> on Thursday, the first day of the dark fortnight in the month of Vaishakha <note>April-May</note>, in the year 1817 of the Shalivahana era and the year 1951 of the Vikramaditya era <note>approximately 1895 CE</note>.

May the Beloved of Radha <note>a title for the deity Krishna</note> be pleased by this effort. || || || || || ||
